## PART A — PARMAR: Classical Dance (book pp.440–443, PDF 473–476)

Chapter heading on page: "CLASSICAL DANCE". Layout: two-column pages, boxes, photos, Hindi "TRICK TO REMEMBER EXPONENTS" mnemonic blocks (Devanagari sentence + word→name mapping). Mnemonic sentences are kept in Devanagari as printed; each mapping row is kept.

### A.1 Indian Dance Forms (book p.440)
- Indian dance forms divide into two types: (1) **Classical Dance Forms**: have *particular steps* that need to be performed in particular events (mostly performed in temples). (2) **Folk Dance Forms**: *no particular steps* are defined; performed in a specific ritual.

### A.2 Classical Dance Forms — general (book p.440)
- There are **8 classical dances** according to **Sangeet Natak Akademi (HQ: New Delhi)**; the **Ministry of Culture recognises 9** classical dances (8 classical dances + **Chhau**).
- Origin: rooted in **Natya Shastra** (considered the "Fifth Veda"), written by **Bharat Muni**.
- **Abhinaya Darpana** by **Nandikeshvara** describes expressions, movements and gestures.
- **Nritta**: purest form of dance. **Nritya**: expressive form of dance (pure dance + expression).
- Movements/Stages of dance: **Alarippu** (flowers offering), **Jatiswaram, Shabdam, Varnam, Padam, Tillana, Mangalam**.
- Facts box: Tamil text **Tolkappiyam** was composed during the **2nd Sangam**. **Lasya**: feminine (Parvati). **Tandava**: masculine (Shiva). Bharatnatyam is also depicted in the temple **Gopuram (Chidambaram Temple)**.

### A.3 BHARATNATYAM (Tamil Nadu) (book p.440)
- Oldest classical dance of India; performed solo (by a single person) → known as **Ekaharya**.
- Earlier names: **Sadirattam, Thevarattam, Parathaiyar Attam**. Also earlier known as **Dasiattam** as it was traditionally performed by **Devadasis** (female dancers) in temples (last surviving devadasi: **R. Muthukannammal**).
- Symbolises: **Fire**.
- Found in Natya Shastra; associated with **Kalakshetra style**.
- Banned by the Britishers in **1910**, later revived. Revival by **E. Krishna Iyer** and **Rukmini Devi Arundale** → renamed the dance Sadirattam to Bharatnatyam.
- Forms of Classical Dance: **Shaivite (majority)** — dedicated to Lord Shiva (masculine); **Vaishnavite** — dedicated to Lord Vishnu (feminine).
- References in literature: **Cilappatikaram** (by Ilango Adigal) and **Manimegalai** (by Chithalai Chathanar).
- Main mudra: **Kataka Mukha Hasta**.
- Performed by certain families of the Tanjore district; these inheritors were known as **'Nattuvanar'**.
- Performance styles: **Pandanallur, Vazhuvoor, Kalakshetra, Melattur, Tanjore**.
- **TRICK TO REMEMBER EXPONENTS** (Hindi sentence): "भारत की अलार्म बाजी और उसने उठते ही अपनी माता रुक्मिणी देवी के पदम छुए और अपनी सुंदर दोस्त मीनाक्षी और अपने सारे भाइयों को लेकर बालकृष्ण लीला देखने चला गया और धन लाकर सो गया"
  - भारत → Bharatnatyam
  - अलार्म → Alarmel Velli
  - रुक्मिणी देवी → Rukmini Devi Arundale
  - पदम → Padma Subrmanyam (leading figure in Bharatnatyam)
  - सुंदर दोस्त मीनाक्षी → Meenakshi Sundaram Pillai
  - सारे भाइयों → Mrinalini Sarabhai (also an exponent of Kathakali dance), Mallika Sarabhai (also an exponent of Kuchipudi dance)
  - बाल → Bala Saraswati
  - कृष्ण → Yamini Krishnamurthy (also an exponent of Kuchipudi dance)
  - लीला → Leela Samson
  - धन → Shanta Dhananjayan / V P Dhananjayan
  - सो गया → Sonal Mansingh (also an exponent of Odissi dance)
  - Box: **Alarmel Valli is associated with Pandanallur style and is also an exponent of Odissa dance.**
- **Rukmini Devi Arundale**: first woman to be nominated in **Rajya Sabha**; founder of **Kalakshetra Dance School (Estd. 1936)**; received Padma Bhushan (1956).
- **Yamini Krishnamurthi**: **"Asthana Narthaki"** (Resident Dancer) title bestowed by **Tirumala Tirupathi Devasthanam Temple**; received Padma Vibhushan (2016), Padma Bhushan (2001), Padma Shri (1968), Sangeet Natak Academi Award (1977).
- **Sonal Mansingh**: youngest recipient of **Padma Vibhushan (2003)**.
- Other exponents (Bharatnatyam): Rama Vaidyanathan, Meenakshi Chittaranjan, Narthaki Nataraj, Urmila Satyanarayanan, M K Saroja, Radha Sridhar, Deepak Mazumdar, CV Chandrashekhar. (list spans p.440 → p.441)

### A.4 KUCHIPUDI (Andhra Pradesh) (book p.441)
- Earlier known as **Bhagavata Mela Dance** (Tanjore, Tamil Nadu).
- Modern form attributed to **Tirtha Narayanayati and Siddhendra Yogi** (developed as a dance-drama → **Bhama Kalpam**, story of Satyabhama, the charming but jealous wife of Lord Krishna).
- Symbolises: **Earth**.
- Elements: **Ganesha Vandana, Jatiswaram, Shabdam, Kalapam, Padam, Tarangam** (Tarangam is the concluding part of dance where the dancer dances on the **rim of a brass plate**).
- **Yakshagana** was performed in Andhra Pradesh in earlier times; traditionally performed from dusk till dawn.
- **TRICK (1) TO REMEMBER EXPONENTS**: "जिसके सरनेम में भी Reddy दिख जाए वह Kuchipudi से Related होगा" → Raja Reddy, Radha Reddy, Padmaja Reddy, Bhavana Reddy, Yamini Reddy. Other exponents: Yamini Krishnamurthy, Mallika Sarabhai.
- **TRICK (2) TO REMEMBER EXPONENTS**: "सत्यम और सतीश लक्ष्मी जी की सत्यनारायण कथा में गए जहाँ उन्होंने एक सुंदर लड़की शोभा के साथ कुच्ची पूड़ी खायी"
  - सत्यम → Vempata Chinna Satyam
  - सतीश → Aparna Satisham
  - लक्ष्मी → Lakshmi Narayan Shastri
  - सत्यनारायण → Vendantam Satyanarayan (Padma Shri Awardee)
  - सुंदर → Swapna Sundari
  - शोभा → Shobha Naidu
  - कुच्ची पूड़ी → Kuchipudi, Halim Khan
- Box: **Kuchipudi was initially performed by Brahmin boys.** **Sri Krishna Leela Tarangini authored by Narayan Teertha.**

### A.5 ODISSI (Odisha) (book p.441, continues p.441 col.2)
- Symbolises: **Water**.
- **Gotipua**: traditional dance form of Odisha originally performed by **Maharis** (female temple dancers); today young boys perform in the style of the Maharis; Gotipua is the **precursor** to the classical Odissi dance.
- **TRICK (1) TO REMEMBER**: "अगर नाम में Mohapatra या Mohanty दिख जाए तो Odissi से Related है"
  - **Kelucharan Mohapatra** (considered father of Odissi who popularised the dance; **Padma Vibhushan in 2000**), Sujata Mohapatra, Leela Mohanty, Niharika Mohanty.
  - "**Adiguru**" of Odissi dance: **Pankaj Charan Das**.
  - Other exponents: Kiran Sehgal, Mayadhar Raut, Indrani Rehman.
  - **Protima Gauri** founded **Nrityagram** (an institution in Karnataka) dance village and popularised Odissi.
- **TRICK (2) TO REMEMBER EXPONENTS**: "भूटान के प्रधान माधवी से मिलने उड़ीशा आए और यह बात विजय ने संजू और साहू को बता दी"
  - भूटान → Shagun Bhutani
  - प्रधान → Gangadhar Pradhan
  - माधवी → Madhavi Mudgal
  - उडीशा → Odissi
  - विजय → Bijayni Satpathy
  - संजू → Sanjukta Panigrahi
  - साहू → Sudhakar Sahu, Sutapa Talukdar
- Elements in Odissi: **Mangalacharan, Batu Nritya, Pallavi, Tharijham, Moksha**.

### A.6 KATHAK (Uttar Pradesh) (book p.441)
- Name derived from **"Katha" (storytelling)**.
- **The only classical dance to feature Persian elements**; the **Mughals** patronised it.
- Performed in Hindustani music and Urdu ghazals.
- **Wajid Ali Shah**, the last Nawab of Awadh, widely associated with patronage of Kathak.
- Elements: **Amad, Thaat (standing position), Tukra, Paran, Jugalbandhi, Tarana, Bhaav, Kramalaya Ghat**.
- Gharanas: **Lucknow Gharana (Founder: Ishwari Prasad)**, **Jaipur Gharana (Founder: Bhanuji Maharaj)**, **Banaras Gharana (Founder: Janaki Prasad)**.
- **TRICK TO REMEMBER EXPONENTS**: "अरे महाराज नारायण और देवी में आस्था रखो उनका प्रसाद खाओ विदया मिलेगी लखपति बनोगे और सब मंगल होगा"
  - महाराज → Birju Maharaj, Lacchu Maharaj, Acchan Maharaj
  - नारायण → Shovna Narayan
  - देवी → Sitara Devi
  - आस्था → Kamini Asthana, Nalini Asthana
  - प्रसाद → Narayan Prasad, Kalka Prasad, Ishwari Prasad
  - विद्या → Vidhya Gauri Adkar
  - लखपति → Kumudini Lakhia (mapping row continues at top of p.442)
  - मंगल → Aditi Mangal Das, Kumkum Dhar, Roshan Kumari
- **Birju Maharaj**: from **Kalka Bindadin Gharana (Lucknow)**; founded dance school **Kalashram in Delhi in 1998**; received **Padma Vibhushan (1986)**. Other exponents: Gopi Krishan, Rani Kasna, Geetanjali Lal, Maulik Shah.

### A.7 MOHINIYATTAM (Kerala) (book p.442)
- Associated with Hindu God **Vishnu** (his female avatar **"Mohini"**). Also called **"Dance of Enchanters"** (graceful dance).
- Symbolises: **Air**.
- Referenced in **"Vyavaharamala"** (ancient text) written by **Mazhamangalam Narayanan Namputiri**.
- No abrupt jerks — smooth performance. Known to have **24 hand gestures**.
- Elements: **Cholkettu, Jatiswaram, Padavarnam, Padam, Tillana, Slokam, Saptam**.
- Dance forms: **Tevitichiyattam, Nangai Natakam, Dasiyattam**.
- **King Swathi Thirunnal** not only revived Mohiniyattam but was also responsible for inculcating **Carnatic classical music** into the dance form.
- **TRICK TO REMEMBER EXPONENTS**: "मोहिनी गोपी के कान में सुना जय राधा कृष्णा की अम्मा"
  - मोहिनी → Mohiniyattam; गोपी → Gopika Verma; कान → Kanak Rele; सुना → Sunanda Nair; जय → Jayaprabha Menon; राधा → Radha Dutta; कृष्णा → Pallavi Krishnan; अम्मा → Kalyanikutty Amma
- **Kalyanikutty Amma**: played a major role in the revival; Sangeet Natak Akademi Award (1974); founder of **Kerala Kalyanam (Estd. 1952)**.
- **V N Menon**: helped repeal the **ban on temple dancing in Kerala**, thereby reviving Mohiniyattam.

### A.8 KATHAKALI (Kerala) (book p.442)
- **Katha = "Story"**, **Kali = "Performance"**.
- Performed by a **male performer**; usually performed in temples after **Sevakali** session.
- Box: **Kalaripayattu is a martial dance.**
- Vocal performance is in **Sanskritised Malayalam**. Based on epics **Ramayana and Mahabharata**.
- Uses facial expressions and **face colour** (face painted **green** → colour represents **strength**) → hence **"Mask Dance"**. Colours and meanings: **Green (strength)** and **Red (evil)**.
- **Vesham (5), Expression (9), Hand gesture (24).**
- Forms: **Natyam, Nritham, Nrithyam, Sangeetam, Vadya**.
- Elements: **Kelikottu, Thodayam, Purappadu, Tiranokku, Ilakiyattam, Kalasam**.
- **Aharya** = costume and makeup in Sanskrit.
- **Five major Veshams (makeup)**: **Pacha** — green, noble/heroic/generous characters; **Kathi** — knife, villainous characters; **Thadi** — beard, characters with beards; **Kari** — black, evil characters; **Minukku** — radiant, noble/virtuous female characters. (Photo captions also show "PAZHUPPU".)
- **TRICK TO REMEMBER EXPONENTS**: "बाल मिले ना मिले कुछ गोपी की कली मिल जाए"
  - बाल → P. V. Balkrishna; मिले → Milena Salvini; कुछ → Kunchu Kurup; गोपी → Gopi Nath; कली → Kottakal Nandakumaran Nair, Mrinalini Sarabai

### A.9 MANIPURI (Manipur) (book p.443)
- Introverted type of dance based on **Vaishnavism**. **Ashtapadis of Jayadeva's Gita Govinda** are sung in Manipuri.
- Forms of Manipuri Dance: (1) **Raas Leela** — depicts love story of Krishna and Radha; propounder **Rajarshi Bhagya Chandra**. (2) **Sankirtana Tradition** — **Jagoi** and **Cholom** are two parts; **Cholom** is the masculine aspect in which **pung (drum)** is used. (3) **Thang-Ta** — a martial dance.
- **TRICK TO REMEMBER EXPONENTS** (English): Surname with **Devi** is mostly associated with Manipuri: Kalavati Devi, Gambhini Devi, Bimbavati Devi. Exponents: **Jhaveri sisters**, Nirmala Mehta, **Guru Bipin Singh** (received the title **"Hanjaba"** from some Manipur King), Charu Mathur, Devyani Chalia, Aluna Kabuini. Other exponents: Rajkumar Singhajit Singh, Nileshwar Mukherjee.

### A.10 SATTRIYA (Assam) (book p.443)
- Vaishnavite form of dance **included under classical dance category in 2000**.
- Theme related to Lord Krishna. Founder: **Srimanta Sankaradeva**.
- **Ankiya Naat**: a "one-act play" written in **Brajavali**; in Assam called **"Bhaona"**, a traditional art form combining dance, music and visual art.
- Dance numbers: **Chali** (no theatre components), **Jhumura, Nadu Bhangi**.
- Exponents: Jatin Goswami, **Bhupen Hazarika (Bharat Ratna in 2019)**, Guru Ghanakanta Bora, Indira Bora, Maniram Dutta, Krishnakshi Kashyap, Meenakshi Medhi, Sharodi Saikia.

### A.11 VARIOUS ORGANISATIONS (book p.443, boxed right column)
1. **Sangeet Natak Akademi/Indian National School of Dance**: Delhi.
2. **Kalakshetra School of Dance**: Rukmini Devi Arundale (mother of Modern Bharatnatyam).
3. **National Institute of Kathak**: 1964 (New Delhi).
4. **Nehru Manipur Dance Academy**: 1954 (Imphal).
5. **Govindji Nrityalaya**: Manipur.
6. **Darpana Academy of Performing Arts** established by Mrinalini Sarabhai and Vikram Sarabhai: 1949 (Ahmedabad).
7. **Nalanda Nritya Kala Mahavidyalaya**: Mumbai.
8. **National Institute of Performing Arts**: Mumbai.
9. **Kalasharam Kathak School** established by Birju Maharaj: New Delhi.
10. **Kadam School of Dance and Music** founded by Kumudini Lakhia: 1967 (Ahmedabad).

### A.12 Notes on Part A
- ⚠ Printed as-is: the Bharatnatyam list "8 classical dances per Sangeet Natak Akademi" does not name the 8 — the book only names the 8 dances through the individual sections (Bharatnatyam, Kuchipudi, Odissi, Kathak, Mohiniyattam, Kathakali, Manipuri, Sattriya) and does not print a Chhau section under "Classical" here (Chhau appears under Martial Art Dances / Jharkhand / Odisha / West Bengal in Part B).
- ⚠ The page-440 photo caption/"Trick" block gives "Mrinalini Sarabhai ... Kathakali" and "Mallika Sarabhai ... Kuchipudi" as printed. The Manipuri "Cholom" description is printed as "masculine aspect".
- ⚠ Birju Maharaj is said to be from "Kalka Bindadin Gharana (Lucknow)" as printed.
## PART B — PARMAR: Folk Dances of India (book pp.444–458, PDF 477–491)

Chapter heading on pages: "FOLK DANCES OF INDIA". Structure: one block per state/UT, each opening with a Hindi/Hinglish **TRICK TO REMEMBER** sentence whose capitalised words are the first words of the dance names; then a 3-column table (TRICK word | Dance | Description). Where a dance row has no "TRICK" word in the source (dance not covered by the mnemonic), the TRICK cell is shown "—". Photos/captions noted where they carry a name.

### B.1 ANDHRA PRADESH (book p.444)
TRICK: "Veer Logon Ke Liye Dhimese Koyle Ki Bhaap Se Burra Banalu"

| Trick | Dance | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Veer | Veeranatyam | Known as the "Song of Bravery" or Dance of warriors. Performed by the Veeramusti community (believed to be followers of Veerabhadra (Lord Shiva)). |
| Logon | Lambadi | Banjara tribe |
| Dhimese | Dhimsa | Tribe of Araku valley. Performed by 15–20 women forming a chain. |
| Koyle | Kolattam | Stick dance performed in many southern states, including Andhra Pradesh, Tamil Nadu and Karnataka. Not restricted to one specific state. Mainly performed during the Pongal festival. |
| Bhaap | Bhamakalapam | Dance-drama, composed by Siddhendra Yogi. |
| Burra | Burrakatha | Tambura instrument is used in the performance of Burrakatha. |
| Banalu | Bonalu | Celebrated in honour of Goddess Mahakali. |
| — | Butta Bommalu | Mask dance. Performed in West Godavari region. Butta Bommalu means "Basket Toys". |
| — | Dappu Nrityam | Performed in Andhra Pradesh and Telangana by the Mala and Madiga communities playing the Dappu (dafli) drum. |
| — | Kolannalu | Stick dance of Andhra Pradesh. |
| — | Vilasini Natyam | Traditionally performed by devadasis in temples and courts. Revived from near extinction by Swapnasundari. |
| — | Gusadi | Performed by the Gond tribe. |
| — | Dandaria | Performed by the Gond tribe. |
| — | Yakshagana | Mainly from Karnataka, but also performed in AP. |
Photo captions: Dhimsa dance; Butta Bommalu Dance.

| Pas | Pasi Kongki | The Adi tribe performs this on Abhang songs. |
| Chalo | Chalo Loku | Nocte tribe performs in Oct–Nov. |
| Sham Ko | Bardo Chham | Dance of 12 zodiacs (Sherdukpen community); associated with Tibetan-Buddhist. |
| Mask | Mask | (no description printed) |
| Lion and Peacock | Lion and Peacock | Monpa tribe |
| — | Popir | Performed by Garo tribe in tribute to Mopin Ape (Goddess of prosperity and fertility). |
| — | Ponung | Adi tribe |
| — | Yak Chham | Mask dance. It is also known as Yak dance. |
| — | Tapu | Performed by the Adi tribe during the Solung festival. |
| — | Ojiyele | Performed by the Galo tribe during the Mopin festival. |
| — | Ozele | Wancho tribe |
Photo captions (p.445 top): Lion and Peacock Dance; Rikhampada Dance.

### B.3 ASSAM (book p.445)
TRICK: "Ali Bhor Main Deo Ke Pass Gaya, Hey Bhagawan Maths Main Ank Dila Dena Iss Pali Main Jhum Ke Nachunga"

| Trick | Dance | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Ali | Ali ai Ligang | The Mishing tribe performs Gumrag dance in this festival (observed on first Wednesday of Feb–March). |
| Bhor | Bhortal | Developed by Narhari Burha Bhakat and is performed by Satriya community in Barpeta. |
| Deo | Deodhani | Performed to pay tribute to Goddess Mansa (Snake Goddess). |
| Ank | Ankiya Naat | One-Act play. Centred around acts of Lord Krishna and teachings of Vaishnavite faith. |
| Pali | Ojapali | Semi-dramatic tradition folk dance. |
| Jhum | Jhumur | Performed by Kulis (people who work in tea gardens). |
| — | Bagarumba | Performed by Bodo tribe during Bwisaga festival (women wear Dokhona/Arunai). A butterfly dance performed by the women of the Bodo tribe in Assam during their New Year festival. Known as the "Bodo tribe's Bihu" and signifies nature (butterflies, birds). **In January 2026, over 10,000 participants performed this to create a Guinness World Record at the Arjun Bhogeswar Barua Stadium (Sarusajai Stadium) in Guwahati.** |
| — | Bihu | A dance and also a festival. A group of dancers performing in circles or rows, often accompanied by drums/pipes to celebrate the Bihu festival. Women wear a traditional costume called "Mekhela Chador". **Three types of Bihu:** Bohag Bihu / Rongali Bihu — celebrated as a New Year festival in Spring (March–April). Kati Bihu / Kongali Bihu — performed in October (Kartik month). Magh Bihu / Bhogali Bihu — performed in January (Magh month). |
| — | Baidima | Dimasa tribe |
| — | Bawang | Karbi Anglong tribe |
| — | Yak Chaam | Monpa tribe. |
| — | Rabha War Dance | Dhawa dance. A high-energy warrior dance performed by the Rabha community in the regions of Assam and West Bengal. |
Photo captions: Bihu Dance; Ojapali Dance.

### B.4 BIHAR (book p.445)
TRICK: "Do Natwe Jat-Jatin Apna Sohar Khila Rahe Hai Jhum Ke"

| Trick | Dance | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Natwe | Natwa | Chamar community |
| Jat Jatin | Jat-Jatin | Shows tragic love story of Jat and Jatin. Performed in Mithila region during moonlit nights (monsoon). |
| Sohar Khila | Sohar Khelwana | Performed during child birth. |
| Jhum Ke | Jhumri | Performed in Mithilanchal region during Karthik month (Oct–Nov). |
| — | Bidesia | A Bhojpuri folk dance created by **Bhikhari Thakur**, famously known as the **"Shakespeare of Bihar"**. Deals with many social issues, contradictory topics and conflict between the traditional and the modern, the urban and the rural, and the rich and the poor. Its main theme is to end discrimination. |
| — | Jharni | Juhula community |
| — | Jhijhiya | From the Mithilanchal region, performed mainly by women during Navratri for Goddess Durga (dancers carry a pot on their heads). |
| — | Kajari | Performed to welcome the arrival of the monsoon season. |
| — | Karma | Baiga tribe. An agrarian ritual performed during Bhadra Ekadashi; performed during the Karma festival. Also done in Jharkhand, Odisha and Chhattisgarh. |
| — | Launda Naach | Male performers cross-dressing as women, showcasing regional traditions and entertaining audiences during festivals. |
| — | Pari Khanda | Martial dance |
| — | Paika | Also done in Jharkhand and Odisha. Performance segments of Paika Nritya: blowing the war conch (shankha); shield–sword sparring drills; acrobatic somersaults; group victory shout of "Jai Jagannath". |
| — | Dhibia | Dhangar community |
| — | Domkach | Performed by women of the groom's family. |
Photo captions: Jhijhiya Dance; Bidesia Dance.

### B.5 CHHATTISGARH (book p.446)
TRICK: "Aksar Suwa Karma Na Karke Gendiya Marte Hai Pagalpanti Karte Hai Aur Pandavo Ke Saath Jhumur Ke Niche Saila Dance Karte Hai."

| Trick | Dance | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Aksar | Kaksar | Performed by the Abujh Maria tribe of Bastar region. |
| Suwa | Suwa Nacha | Kamar tribe. Suwa literally translates to parrot. Dancers perform by mimicking the movements of a parrot. |
| Karma | Karma Nacha | Performed during Karma festival around sacred karma tree. The ritual honours the Karam tree on Bhadra Ekadashi in Chhattisgarh. The drum most associated is the **Madal**. Also done in Jharkhand, Odisha and Bihar. |
| Gendiya | Gendi | Baiga tribe. A dance that involves balancing and dancing on bamboo sticks (stilts). |
| Marte Hai | Gaur Maria | Performed by the Bison Horn Maria community during occasions like births and marriages. |
| Pagalpanti | Panthi Nacha | Performed by Satnami community (followers of Satnam panth). Panthi dancers wear Dhoti during performance. The dance encodes the teachings of **Guru Ghasidas** from his compilation called **"Amritvani"**. |
| Pandavo | Pandavani | A style of folk singing which includes narration of tales from Mahabharata. "The dark horse of Chhattisgarh Folk dance." Portrays the account of the Pandavas — the main characters of an epic battle. Famous exponents: **Teejan Bai (Padma Vibhushan in 2019)** and **Usha Barle (Padma Shri in 2023)**. |
| Jhumur | Jhumair | Linked with the Karam Puja or Sohrai festival. |
| Saila Dance | Saila | Performed by boys of Bhil tribe during harvest season. Also known as a stick dance. Performed by the Gond tribe. A post-harvest dance performed in the month of Aghan (Nov–Dec). Also performed in Odisha and Madhya Pradesh. |
| — | Baiga Pardhoni | Energetic dance from Chhattisgarh and Madhya Pradesh. Performed by the Baiga tribe during marriages to welcome the groom's procession (Barat). Baiga tribes also perform karma, saila, and Faag. |
| — | Bison Horn Maria | Performed by the Maria / Dandami Maria tribe (a Gond sub-group) in the Bastar region of Chhattisgarh and parts of Jharkhand. Dancers mimic the movement of a wild bison, with men wearing horn headgear, while women generally wear ornaments made of brass or silver. **Ghotul**: a traditional centre of the Dandami Maria tribe used for imparting education and as a meeting place for unmarried couples. |
| — | Raut Nacha | Performed by the Yadav community, mainly at night. |
Photo captions: Suwa Nacha; Pandavani Dance.

| Dance | Description |
|---|---|
| Ottamthullal | A dance-drama introduced by **Kunchan Nambiar**. |
| Kaliyattam / Theyyam | Depicts religious rituals of sacred groves of Malabar coast. Known as the **"dance of Gods"** with intense performances, heavy makeup and elaborate costumes. Performed by the **Vannam and Malayan** communities in the North Malabar region (Kannur and Kasaragod districts). Vibrant costumes represent divine gods. Traditionally held from November to April during the kaliyattam festival. |
| Koodiyattam | Traditional **Sanskrit theatre** art dance. |
| Kolakali | Stick dance. Dancers move in a circle striking small sticks in rhythm with their steps. |
| Chakyar Koothu | A monologue performance where the performer narrates episodes from Hindu epics (Ramayana, Mahabharata). |
| Oppana | Performed by the Muslim (**Mapplia**) community of the Malabar region in Kerala during marriages. Two types: **Oppana Chayal** (no clapping) and **Oppana Murukkam** (continuous rhythmic clapping). |
| Mayil Attam | Also known as the "Peacock Dance". Dancers wear peacock feathers to honor Lord Murugan (Kartikeya). |
| Villu Pattu | Known as the **"Bow Song Dance"** because a large bow (arrow and bow style) is used during the performance. |
| Kummi | Performed by women in circles. |
| Kakkarissi Natakam | Folk theatre/dance form primarily focusing on satirical elements. |
| kolattam | Stick dance. Also performed in Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu. |
| Puli Kali | Performed during **Onam**, depicting Tiger Hunting. |
| Thiruvathira kali | Group dance performed mainly by women. Women wear white sarees called **"kasavu saree"**. They dance in circles around a central lamp (deep). Performed during the **Thiruvathira festival (Dhanu month) and Onam**. Commemorates the day Lord Shiva accepted Goddess Parvati as his wife. |
| Thappu kali | (no description printed) |
| Kooravara kali | Performed during local temple festivals (Utsavams). |
| Kalaripayattu | Martial dance |
| Velakali | Martial dance |
| Theeyattu | Solo dance-drama |

### B.24 TAMIL NADU (book p.455) — table has no TRICK column
| Dance | Description |

| Kolattam | Stick dance. Women use sticks to create rhythmic beats and perform intricate movements. Mainly performed during the Pongal festival. Performed in many southern states, including Andhra Pradesh, Kerala and Karnataka. |
| Silambattam / Silambam | Martial art dance. |
| Oyilattam | Performed in the Madurai region. |

| Kudirai Attam | **Poikkal Kuthirai Attam** Dance. Dancers wear a dummy horse structure while performing. |
| Kummi | Performed by women during Pongal festival (harvest festival). Performed by women dancing in a circle while clapping their hands to a rhythm. |
| Kavadi Attam | **Burden Dance**. Performed to worship **Lord Murugan**; devotees carry a bamboo structure (**Kavadi**) on their shoulders. Devotees often pierce their skin with a spear (Lord Murugan's symbol) and carry offerings of rice and milk to the temple. |
| Mayil Attam | Also known as the **"Peacock Dance"**. Dancers wear peacock feathers to honour Lord Murugan (Kartikeya). |
| Karakattam | Also known as **Karagam**. Performed in front of an image of the **Goddess Mariamman**. Goddess Mariamman is known as the Goddess of Rain. |
| Villu pattu | Known as the **"Bow Song Dance"** because a large bow (arrow and bow style) is used during the performance. |
| Sevai Attam | "Sevai" means service. A service/dance performed to honour **God Thirumal** (an avatar of Lord Vishnu) by the **Nayakar** community. |
| Bommalattam | **Puppet dance**. Depicts scenes from the Ramayana and Mahabharata. |
| Natyanjali | "Natya" means dance, "Anjali" means offering. Celebrated primarily on **Maha Shivaratri in Chidambaram, Tamil Nadu**. Dedicated to **Lord Nataraja**, the Cosmic dancer. |
Photo captions: Kolattam Dance; Devarattam Dance.

## PART F — CROSS-BOOK NOTES & ⚠ DOUBTS FOR THE DANCES SUB-TOPIC
1. **Parmar vs Lucent state lists differ in scope.** Parmar's tables carry description + trick for every dance; Lucent §32 is a bare name list per state and includes several names that Parmar does not list under that state (e.g. Lucent Jharkhand: Sarahul, Jat-Jatin, Danga, Bidesia, Sohrai, Bheja; Uttarakhand: Gadhwali, Kumayuni, Bhotia; Himachal: Jhali, Chharhi, Dhaman, Mahasu, Nati, Dangi, Chamba, Thali, Jhainta; Goa: Mandi, Jhagor, Khol, Dakni). Both are kept; neither is "the" answer if a question uses a name found in only one book.
2. **Same-name dances in several states** (Parmar B.18 vs B.26 Cheraw; B.23 vs B.29 Maruni; Karma in Odisha/Jharkhand/Chhattisgarh/Bihar; Paika in Odisha/Jharkhand/Bihar; Saila in Odisha/Chhattisgarh/MP).
3. **Parmar printing slips flagged in-line:** West Bengal "Rabha war" ("West Bengal and West Bengal"), Haka attributed to "Samoa tribe near New Zealand", Tripura trick word "Sang".
4. **Overlap with other subjects:** Classical dance & Sangeet Natak Akademi recognition → Static GK music/awards; Kalbeliya UNESCO 2010 (A.12/B.22). No existing project doc holds dances, so nothing to point back to. Chhau (UNESCO listed) and Baul (UNESCO) are also worth cross-checking against the Culture/UNESCO Intangible Heritage list when that file is built.

# STATIC GK — MUSIC: RAW EXTRACTION
Subject: Static GK → Music (round 1). **RAW EXTRACTION ONLY** — no test cross-referencing yet.

## Sources & page map
| Part | Source | Book pages | PDF pages |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | Parmar SSC Fatman (2nd Ed., 2026) — "Music and Paintings" chapter, music section (Indian classical music, thaats/ragas, gharanas, instruments & exponents, folk music of states) | 429–439 | 462–472 |
| B | Parmar — Paintings of Indian States table (same chapter; cross-file note) | 439 | 472 |
| C | Lucent's GK (2026) — Miscellany §30 (Instruments & exponents) + Karnatak note | 446–447 | 459–460 |
(Parmar PDF = book + 33 here; Lucent PDF = book + 13.)

## Method / disclosure
Read from native page images; OCR only as cross-check. Fact-complete, not word-for-word (all entries, names, numbers, awards and dates kept; only explanatory prose condensed). ⚠ = doubt / printing slip / internal inconsistency. Spellings as printed.

---

## PART A — PARMAR: "MUSIC AND PAINTINGS" chapter — MUSIC section (book pp.429–438; PDF 462–471)
Running head on pages: "MUSIC AND PAINTINGS". This file covers the music part; the paintings part of the same chapter is a separate topic (Architecture/Culture — round 2) and is noted in the build notes.

### A.1 Indian Classical Music (book p.429)
- Divided into two categories: **Hindustani music** and **Carnatic music**.
- **Hindustani Music (North India)** — sung in: **Hindi, Braj, Avadhi, Rajasthani, Marathi**.
- **Carnatic Music (South India)** — sung in: **Sanskrit, Kannada, Telugu, Malayalam, Tamil**.

**Common elements in both traditions**
1. **Raga**: melodic framework, each associated with a specific emotion or time of day. E.g. **Miyan ki Todi** (sung during late morning hours).
2. **Taal**: rhythmic framework based on cyclic time units.
3. **Sruti**: the smallest measurable difference between musical pitches.

**7 Basic Swaras (Sargam)**: Sa → Shadjam; Re → Rishabham; Ga → Gandharam; Ma → Madhyamam; Pa → Panchamam; Da → Dhaivatham; Ni → Nishadham.
Box: **Bhoop** corresponds to the **"Mohanam Ragam"** of Carnatic music.

### A.2 Vocal forms of Hindustani music (book p.429)
"3 Major Vocal Forms of Hindustani Music" (heading as printed): Dhrupad, Khayal, Tarana.
- **Dhrupad**: sung with **Tanpura and Pakhawaj**. First composed by **Miyan Tansen** (as printed). Propounded by the **Dagar family** and **Darbhanga Gharana**.
- **Khayal**: more lyrical/expressive form that **evolved from Dhrupad**; known for freedom of expression and romantic themes. First composed by **Amir Khusrau** (as printed).
- **Tarana**: performed towards the **end of a concert**; conveys the mood of elation. **Tillana of Carnatic music** is based on this.
- ** Lighter vocal forms of Hindustani classical music, often considered semi-classical: **Dhamar (associated with Dhrupad), Kajari, Tappa, Thumri, Ghazal, Bhajan, Tripal, Chaiti**.
(Photo on page: a sitar player.)
⚠ "Dhrupad first composed by Miyan Tansen" and "Khayal first composed by Amir Khusrau" are the book's statements as printed; other sources give different attributions (Dhrupad's origin is usually credited to Raja Man Singh Tomar / older tradition; Khayal's origin is conventionally credited to Amir Khusrau or Sultan Husain Sharqi). I do not have a verified source settling this — verify before relying on it.

### A.3 Major styles of Carnatic music (book p.429)
1. **Gitam**: simple melodic extension of the raga with a uniform tempo; an introductory piece.
2. **Svarajati**: mix of devotional and heroic elements; learnt after a course of Gitam.
3. **Kirtanam**: comprises devotional songs.
4. **Tillana**: short, crisp compositions, similar to **Tarana** in Hindustani music.
5. **Kriti**: highly evolved musical compositions; a development from Kirtana.
6. **Suladi**: an extension of raga-based compositions.
7. **Padam**: a love poem/tempo (as printed).

### A.4 Important personalities (book p.429)
**Hindustani**
- **Vishnu Narayan Bhatkhande** → categorised ragas into **10 basic musical scales called Thaats**.
- **Vishnu Digambar Paluskar** → founded **Gandharva Mahavidyalaya (1901, Lahore)**.
- Box: **There are 7 basic Talams in Carnatic music.**

**Carnatic**
- **Purandar Das** → considered the **Father of Carnatic Music**.
- **M. S. Subbulakshmi** → known as the **Queen of Carnatic Music**. Box: **M. S. Subbulakshmi was the first musician to receive Bharat Ratna in 1998.**
- **"Trinity of Carnatic Music"**, also "Three Jewels of Carnatic Music" (18th century) → **Tyagaraja, Muthuswami Dikshitar, Syama Sastri**.

### A.5 Hindustani Music vs Carnatic Music (book pp.429–430, table)
| Hindustani Music | Carnatic Music |
|---|---|
| Have influence of **Arab, Persian and Afghan** | **Indigenous** system of music |
| Scope provided to artists for **variation and improvisation** | **Less scope** of improvisation |
| Several sub-styles which lead to emergence of **'Gharanas'** | Only **one particular prescribed style** of singing |
| **6 major ragas** | **72 melakartas**, ragas and janya ragas |
| Instruments are **equally important** as vocals | **More emphasis on vocal** music |
| Main emphasis on **Ragas** | Mainly based on **Tala or Taal** |
| Mainly associated with **north India** | Mainly prevalent in **south India** |
| Major instruments: **Tabla, Sarangi, Sitar, Santoor** | Instruments: **Veena, Mridangam, Mandolin** |
| **Khayal, Dhrupad, Tappa** = vocal forms of Hindustani tradition | **Kriti, Varnam, Kirtan** = vocal forms of Carnatic tradition |
| **12 main Talas** in Hindustani music, which include **Keherwa, Dadara, Rupak, Dhumali, Matta, Jhapatal, Rudra Taal, Ekataal, Deepachandi, Chautal, Teentaal, Tilawada** | **7 main Talas (Saptatalas)** in Carnatic music: **Dhruva, Mathya, Rupaka, Jhampa, Triputa, Ata, Eka** |

### A.6 Thaats & Ragas (book p.430)
The **10 Thaats** were introduced and formalised by **Pandit Vishnu Narayan Bhatkhande**. A Thaat = parent scale/family for ragas; most ragas are classified under 10 structured Thaats.

| Thaat | Swaras (notes) | Time | Feature | Associated ragas |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bilawal | S R G M P D N | Morning | Light/joyful | Alhaiya Bilawal, Yamani Bilawal, Shankara, Hamsadhwani, Bihag |
| Kalyan | S R G M# P D N | Early night | Serene/devotional | Yaman, Bhupali, Hindol, Kedar, Kamod, Shyam Kalyan |
| Khamaj | S R G M P D n | Late night | Light-hearted/romantic | Khamaj, Desh, Tilak Kamod, Rageshri, Jaijaiwanti, Jhinjhoti |
| Kafi | S R g M P D n | Late evening | Gentle/romantic | Kafi, Bageshri, Bhimpalasi, Dhani, Shivranjani, Pilu |
| Bhairav | S r G M P d N | Early morning | Serious/devotional | Bhairav, Ahir Bhairav, Jogiya, Nat Bhairav, Gauri, Vibhas |
| Marwa | S r G M# D N | Twilight/dusk | Anxious/deep | Marwa, Puriya, Bhatiyaar, Sohoni, Bibhas |
| Poorvi | S r G M# d N | Late evening/twilight | Serious, devotional | Poorvi, Shree, Puriya Dhanashree, Basant, Lalit |
| Asavari | S R g M P d n | Early morning | Calm/devotional | Asavari, Jaunpuri, Desi, Darbari Kanada, Adana |
| Bhairavi | S r g M P d n | Morning | Often used to close concerts | Bhairavi, Malkauns, Bilaskhani Todi, Kaunsi Kanada |
| Todi | S r g M# d N | Morning | Intense/emotional | Todi, Gujari Todi, Madhuvanti, Multani |
(Table copied as printed; the printed Marwa row carries only six swara-letters "S r G M# D N" — Pa is absent, as in the standard Marwa scale. ⚠ Note the book prints Kafi's time as "Late evening" and Poorvi's as "Late evening/twilight".)

**Quick note on notation (book p.430)**
- Capital letters (S, R, G, M, P, D, N) = **Shuddha (natural)** notes.
- Lowercase letters (r, g, d, n) = **Komal (flat)** notes.
- **M#** = **Teevra (sharp) Ma**.
- Morning ragas: Bhairav, Todi, Ahir Bhairav, Nat Bhairav. Afternoon ragas: Bhimpalasi, Sarang, Multani. Ragas (evening, label missing in print): Yaman, Bageshri, Puriya Dhanashree. Night ragas: Darbari Kanada, Malkauns, Chandrakauns.
  ⚠ The third bullet has no time-of-day label in the printed page (label appears dropped) — evening is my reading, not the book's.

**Indian Classical Music Notation Guide (book p.430)**
| Note | Note name | Notation ID | Sol-fa | Full name |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | sa | S | sa | shadja |
| 2b | komal re | r | re | komal rishabha |
| 2 | shuddha re | R | re | shuddha rishabha |
| 3b | komal ga | g | ga | komal gandhaara |
| 3 | shuddha ga | G | ga | shuddha gandhaara |
| 4 | shuddha ma | m | ma | shuddha madhyama |
| 4# | tivra ma | M | ma | tivra madhyama |
| 5 | pa | P | pa | panchama |
| 6b | komal dha | d | dha | komal dhaivata |
| 6 | shuddha dha | D | dha | shuddha dhaivata |
| 7b | komal ni | n | ni | komal nishaada |
| 7 | shuddha ni | N | ni | shuddha nishaada |
| 8 | sa | S' | sa | shadja |
⚠ The notation guide's "shuddha ma = m, tivra ma = M" conflicts with the quick-note line above ("M# = Teevra Ma") — two different notation conventions are printed on the same page. Use the quick-note convention for the Thaat table.

### A.7 Ragas and raga-related detail (book p.431)
- **Raga Shankara**: performed at **night**, in the **second pahar** (typically 9 PM–12 AM or 10 PM–1 AM). Associated with the energy of **Lord Shiva**. Belongs to the **Bilawal Thaat**. Traditionally rendered conveying a **meditative and devotional** mood.
- **Raga Bageshree**: performed at night, **second pahar**; belongs to the **Kaafi Thaat**.
- **Raga Jaunpuri**: derived from the **Asavari (Asarvi) Thaat**; conveys emotional depth and devotion; performed in **late morning** sessions, gentle devotional mood.
- **Raga Bhairav**: derived from the **Bhairav Thaat**; performed in the **early morning**; known as the **"King of Morning Ragas"**; devotional, majestic and serious mood; associated with **Lord Shiva**.
- **Raga-based performances**: **Thumri, Dhrupad, Prabandha** are traditional forms of raga-based performance. **Thumri** — very lyrical; a **love song**, so textual beauty is very important. **Sonata** = a structural form associated with **Western classical** music.
- **Structure of Dhrupad**: four parts (tuks/segments): 1. **Sthayi (Asthayi)** — base/refrain covering lower and middle octaves; 2. **Antara** — middle and higher octaves; 3. **Sanchari** — development phase using all three octaves; 4. **Abhoga** — concluding section.
- **Raga Vivodha and Pandit Somnath**: in the text *Raga Vivodha*, **Pandit Somnath** defined a raga through two components: (1) **Devamaya Swarupa** — the ethos / spiritual-emotional form; (2) **Nadamaya Swarupa** (also **Swarlakshana**) — the tonal structure of the raga.
- **Achal and Chal Swaras**: in the 7 basic notes — **Achal (fixed): "Sa" and "Pa"**, always Shuddha, never change position. **Chal (movable)**: notes with variants **Komal (flat)** and **Teevra (sharp)** — all other swaras except Sa & Pa.
- **Types of Jati in Rhythm (Carnatic)** — 5 main Jatis: **Tishra** (3 beats, 1+2 or 3); **Chaturashra** (4 beats; default in many talas, 1+3 or 2+2); **Khanda** (5 beats, 2+3 or 5); **Mishra** (7 beats, 3+4 or 3+2+2); **Sankeerna** (9 beats, 4+5 or 3+2+4 — printed "3+2+4", ⚠ which sums to 9 as printed).
- **Jati in melodic structure (range classification; Hindustani)**: classifies ragas by number of notes — **Audav** (pentatonic, 5 notes); **Shadav** (hexatonic, 6); **Sampurna** (heptatonic, all 7); **Mixed (Mishra)** e.g. Audav-Sampurna (5 in aroh, 7 in avroh).
- **Rhythmic cycles (Talas)**: **Adi Tala** = 8 beats; **Mishra Chapu** = 7 beats; **Rupak Tala** = 3 or 6 beats (as printed).
- **Teentaal**: **16 beats (matras)**, divided into four sections of four (4-4-4-4). **Clap (Taali)** on **1st, 5th and 13th** beats; **empty beat (Khali)** on the **9th** beat.
### A.8 Other notation / theory notes (book p.432)
- **Saptak notation system**: dot above (S) = **Taar Saptak (high octave)**; dot below (S) = **Mandra Saptak (low octave)**; no dot (S) = **Madhya Saptak (middle octave)**.
- **Gamaka**: any graceful turn, curve or oscillation applied to a single note or between notes. Gliding from one note to another = **Meend** (common in Hindustani classical).
- **Khayal**: romantic and highly ornamented style of singing; compositions in Khayal are called **"Bandish"**.
- **Abhinaya Darpana**: composed by **Nandikeshwara**; famously the **"Mirror of Gesture"**. **Nritta** = pure dance (without expressive emotions); **Nritya** = expressive dance (emotions and expressions).
- **Venkat Mukhi Swami**: regarded as the grand theorist of Carnatic music; developed the **raga classification system and the Melakarta system**; famous composition **Chaturdandi Prakashika**.
- **Sarangadeva**: composed the **Sangeeta Ratnakara**; court scholar for **King Singhadeva II of the Yadava dynasty (capital Devagiri)**; Sangeeta Ratnakara is divided into **seven chapters, collectively called Saptadhyay**.
- **Annamacharya** wrote over **32,000 songs** promoting devotion and equality.
- In **Haripala's "Sangeeta Sudhakara"** (14th century AD) the terms **Carnatic and Hindustani** are found for the first time.
- The advent of **Purandaradasa in 1484** marked a very important landmark in the development of Carnatic music. (⚠ year as printed.)
- **Matanga** is the composer of the treatise **Brihaddesi**.

### A.9 GHARANAS (School of Music) (book pp.432–434)

**Gwalior Gharana** (p.432)
- Considered the **oldest and most influential Khayal Gharana**; emerged in the reign of Mughal Emperor **Akbar (16th century)**.
- **Ramtanu Pandey**, later known as **"Miyan Tansen"**, was one of the first and most famous vocalists of this Gharana (Akbar gave the title "Miyan").
- **Founders:** Hassu Khan, Haddu Khan & Nathoo Khan, Nathan Pir Baksh.
- **Exponents:** Raja Bhaiya Poonchwale, Krishnarao Shankar, Pt. Omkarnath Thakur, Veena Sahasrabuddhe, Vishnu Digambar Paluskar, Bade Mohammad Khan.
- Photo caption: Gwalior Gharana contemporary artist **Sharad Sathe** performing.
- Box: Tansen initially served in the court of **Raja Ramchandra Singh of Rewa**. **Guru of Tansen: Saint Haridas** (he started **Dagar Gharana**).

**Kirana Gharana (Uttar Pradesh)** (p.432)
- Known for **perfect note rendition (Sargam)**. **Founders: Abdul Karim Khan and Abdul Wahid Khan.**
- **Exponents: Pt. Bhimsen Joshi (from Karnataka)** — **Bharat Ratna (2008), Padma Vibhushan (1999), Padma Bhushan (1985)**; **Sawai Gandharva (Guru of Pt. Bhimsen Joshi)**, Gangubai Hangal, Prabha Atre, Manik Varma, Chhannulal Mishra.
- Box: **Kumara Gandharva (from Karnataka)** — original name **Shivaputra Siddaramaiah Komkalimath**; he **refused to be associated with any Gharana**.

**Patiala Gharana** (pp.432–433)
- Inspired and influenced by four Gharanas: **Delhi, Rewa, Gwalior, Jaipur**.
- Known for its **Ghazal, Khayal, Thumri** styles.
- Founder: **Mian Kallu** (he played Sarangi). "Later popularised by **Ustad Ali Baksh Khan and Ustad Fateh Ali Khan**."
- p.433 continuation: "Propounded by: **Ustad Bade Fateh Ali Khan and Ustad Ali Baksh Jarnail Khan**" (⚠ printed thus at the top of p.433; the heading of the Gharana it continues is Patiala from p.432).
- Exponents: **Bade Ghulam Ali Khan, Begum Akhtar, Ajay Chakraborty, Naina Devi, Parveen Sultana, Lakshmi Shankar, Amanat Ali Khan**. Photo: Ustad Bade Fateh Ali Khan.

**Mewati Gharana (Haryana)** (p.433)
- Known for **Tarana** form of singing. **Founder: Ghagge Nazir Khan.**
- **Exponents: Pt. Jasraj** — introduced **Jugalbandi** form of singing (**Padma Vibhushan in 2000**); Maniram, Kala Ramnath (associated with Violin), Tripti Mukherjee, Ratan Mohan Sharma, Arvind Thatte.

**Maihar Gharana** (p.433)
- **Founder: Ustad Allauddin Khan.** Unique feature: blending of **Hindustani and Carnatic** elements; influence on **Western music**.

Kishan Maharaj, Samta Prasad, Pt. Kanthe Maharaj, Gopal Mishra, Hanuman Prasad Mishra. (⚠ printed as-is: Ustad Bismillah Khan being listed under a *Tabla* Gharana is odd; kept as printed.)

**Dumrao Gharana** (p.434) — **Founder: Pt. Ghanarang Baba.** Exponents: Pt. Tilak Chand Dubey, Ramjee Mishra.

### A.10 Types of Musical Instruments of India (book p.434)
In **Natya Shastra**, **Bharat Muni** categorises Indian musical instruments into four types: **Avanaddha (membranophones), Ghana (idiophones), Sushira (aerophones), Tat (chordophones)**.
1. **Tantu Vadya (Chordophones) → String instruments** — sound by plucking or bowing stretched strings. E.g. **Sitar, Sarod, Santoor, Veena, Rudra Veena, Violin, Surbahar, Guitar, Mohan Veena**.
2. **Sushir Vadya (Aerophones) → Wind instruments** — sound by blowing air. E.g. **Flute, Shehnai, Nadaswaram**.
3. **Avanaddha Vadya (Membranophones) → Percussion instruments** — striking a stretched membrane (animal skin)/drumhead. E.g. **Tabla, Pakhawaj, Mridangam, Drums**.
   - Percussion instruments are of two types: (1) **Idiophones** — sound primarily by vibration of the instrument itself (no air flow, strings, membranes or electricity), e.g. **xylophones, bells**; (2) **Membranophones** — stretched membrane struck or vibrated, e.g. **Tabla, Mridangam**.
4. **Ghana Vadya (Idiophones) → Solid instruments** — striking or shaking solid materials. E.g. **Ghatam, Manjira, Kartal**.

**Some traditional instruments (p.434)**
- **Kamaicha**: bowed flute (as printed) played by **Manganiyars of West Rajasthan**.
- **Kartal**: wooden clapper from **Rajasthan**; comes under Idiophones.
- **Chinkara**: stringed instrument played popularly in **Madhya Pradesh** (printed "Chinkara").
- **Pena**: traditional stringed instrument played in **Manipur and Assam**.
⚠ Kamaicha is generally described as a bowed string instrument (not a flute) — the book's wording "bowed flute" is kept as printed; verify from another source.
### A.11 MUSICAL INSTRUMENTS AND EXPONENTS (book pp.434–437)
(Photos of exponents on the pages are recorded only by caption.)

**STRING INSTRUMENTS (Tantu Vadya)**

**Sitar** (p.434→435) — plucked string instrument used in Hindustani music.
- **Pt. Ravi Shankar**: **first Indian to win a Grammy** for the album **"West Meets East" (1968)**; also the Indian to win the **most Grammys (a total of 5)** (as printed); **second Indian musician to receive Bharat Ratna (1999)**. Other sitar exponents: Anoushka Shankar, Vilayat Khan, Imrat Khan, Nikhil Banerjee, Budhaditya Mukherjee, Pt. Harashankar Bhattacharya, Arvind Parikh, Anupama Bhagwat, Rajeeva Janardhan (Imdadkhani Gharana).
- Box: **First Grammy won by Mickey Hart in 1992** (as printed; ⚠ context unclear — printed without qualifier). **First female to win Grammy: Tanvi Shah** (as printed; ⚠ verify). **Father of Fusion dance (also known as "Hi" form of dance) → Pt. Uday Shankar (brother of Pt. Ravi Shankar).**
⚠ Grammy box claims ("first Grammy won by Mickey Hart in 1992", "first female to win Grammy: Tanvi Shah") are printed without context/qualification and I cannot verify them; treat as unverified.

**Sarod** (p.435) — stringed instrument known for deep, resonant sound.
- **Amjad Ali Khan** — known as **"Sarod Samrat"**; known for fast and clear **ekhara taan**; **Padma Bhushan (1991), Padma Vibhushan (2001)**.
- Others: **Alauddin Khan (Maihar Gharana), Ali Akbar Khan (Maihar Gharana), Hafiz Ali Khan (Padma Vibhushan 1998)** (as printed), **Rajeev Taranath (disciple of Ali Akbar Khan), Buddhadev Dasgupta.**

**Santoor** (p.435) — known as **"Shattantri Veena"** in Sanskrit; has **72 strings**.
- **Shivkumar Sharma** — popularised Santoor; **Sangeet Natak Akademi Award (1986), Padma Vibhushan (2001)**.
- Others: Rahul Sharma, Ulhas Bapat, Bhajan Sopori, **Tarun Bhattacharya (Sangeet Natak Akademi Award 2018)**.

**Sarangi** (p.435) — **bowed, short-necked** instrument often used in classical and folk music.
- **Pt. Ram Narayan (Padma Vibhushan in 2005)**, Ustad Sabri Khan, Bundu Khan, Binda Khan, Shakoor Khan, Abdul Latif Khan, Ustad Sultan Khan.

**Veena** (p.435) — ancient, revered stringed instrument. **Samudragupta (Gupta dynasty) is depicted playing Veena on his coins.** **Mughal Emperor Aurangzeb** and former PM **APJ Abdul Kalam** also played Veena. Box: **Saraswati Veenas are crafted from the wood of Jackfruit.**
- Exponents: S. Balachander, **Jayanthi Kumaresh (plays Saraswati Veena)**, Chitti Babu, E. Sankara Sastry, Doraiswamy Iyengar.

**Rudraveena** (p.435) — used in **Dhrupad** music. Exponents: **Asad Ali Khan, Zia Mohiuddin Dagar, Sadik Ali Khan.**

**Surbahar** (p.435) — plucked string instrument also known as **bass sitar**. Exponents: **Annapurna Devi, Ustad Imrat Khan** (also known as Surbahar instrument master), Wahid Khan, Imdad Khan.

**Violin** (p.435) — stringed instrument with **4 strings** held against the shoulder under the chin, played with a bow.
- **T.N. Krishnan (Padma Bhushan 1992)**, Nagai Muralidharan, **Lalgudi Gopala Iyer Jayaraman (Padma Bhushan 2001)**, Mohanan Chandrasekaran (awardee of **Madras Music Academy's Kalanidhi**), Dwaram Venkataswamy Naidu, V.G. Jog, N. Rajam, Ragini Shankar, Nandini Shankar, **M. Gopalkrishna (Padma Bhushan 2012)**, Ganesh and Kumaresh Rajagopalan, Lakshminarayana Subramaniam, **Mysore Nagaraj and Mysore Manjunath (Mysore Brothers)**.

**Mandolin** (p.435) — stringed instrument in the lute family, generally plucked with a pick. Exponents: **U. Srinivas**, S. Balamuralikrishna, Nagen Dey, Khagen Dey.

**Guitar** (p.436) — stringed instrument with **6 strings**, played by plucking or strumming. Exponents: **Brij Bhushan Kabra (Sangeet Natak Akademi 2005)**, Thakur Chakrapani Singh.

**Mohan Veena** (p.436) — a **modified Hawaiian guitar** with **20 strings**. **Pt. Vishwa Mohan Bhatt** — **Grammy (1994)** for album **"A Meeting by the River"**; **Sangeet Natak Akademi Award (1998)** and **Padma Bhushan (2017)**.

**Ektara** (p.436) — **single string** instrument made using **bamboo and a gourd**, mostly used by traditional folk singers in **Maharashtra**.

**WIND INSTRUMENTS (Sushir Vadya)**

**Flute (Bansuri)** (p.436) — wind instrument of a tube with **6 or 7 fingerholes**.
- **Hariprasad Chaurasiya** — born in **Prayagraj**; **Padma Bhushan (1992), Padma Vibhushan (2000)**. Box: **"Prasanna" surname associated with Flute.**
- Others: **Pannalal Ghosh (other name: Amal Jyoti Ghosh)**, Rajendra Prasanna, Rajendra Kulkarni, Rakesh Chaurasiya, Prabhat Sharma.

**Shehnai** (p.436) — made of wood, **7 fingerholes**; also known as **Mangala Vadya (auspicious instrument)**.
- **Ustad Bismillah Khan** — born in **Dumraon, Bihar**; played Shehnai at the **Red Fort on India's first Independence Day (15 Aug 1947)**; received **all four highest civilian awards: Bharat Ratna (2001), Padma Vibhushan (1980), Padma Bhushan (1968), Padma Shri (1961)**; the Iranian city of **Tehran** named an auditorium after him → **Tahar Mosique Ustaad Bismillah Khan** (as printed); the **3rd Indian musician to receive Bharat Ratna**.
- Others: **Raghunath Prasanna (also plays flute)**, S. Ballesh, Ali Ahmed, Bageswari Quamar, Krishna Chaudhary.
⚠ Bharat Ratna order among musicians is printed inconsistently across pages: Ravi Shankar "second (1999)", Bismillah Khan "3rd (2001)", M.S. Subbulakshmi "first musician to receive Bharat Ratna in 1998" (p.429). Book's ordering is as printed; not reconciled.

**Nadaswaram** (p.436) — **similar to Shehnai but larger in size**, mostly played in **South Indian temples**. Exponents: **T.N. Rajarathinam Pillai**, Rajratna Pillai, Neeru Swami Pillai, Sheikh Chinna Maulana.

**Harmonium** (p.436) — keyboard instrument producing sound by blowing air through **metal reeds**. Exponent: **Tulsidas Borkar**.

**PERCUSSION INSTRUMENTS (Avanaddha Vadya)**

**Tabla** (p.436) — **Zakir Hussain**: "Total Grammy received: 4" — has received a total of **3 Grammys (in 2024)** for **"Pashto", "As We Speak", "This Moment"** with fusion group **Shakti**; **4th Grammy** for a collaborative album **"Global Drum Project"**; **Padma Bhushan (2002), Padma Vibhushan (2023)**. (⚠ Punjab Gharana row on p.433 also says Padma Vibhushan 2023.)
- Others: **Nikhil Ghosh (Padma Bhushan 1990)**, Bickram Ghosh, **Ananda Gopal Bandopadhyay (Benaras Gharana)**, Anokhelal Mishra, Pt. Swapan Choudhary, Shubhankar Banerjee, Shreya Bhattacharjee.
- Box: **Pudukkotai Dakshinamurthi Pillai** is renowned for the **Kanjira**.

**Pakhawaj** (p.437) — used in **Dhrupad** music. Exponents: Kunal Patil, **Tota Ram Sharma**, Pt. Bhavani Shankar, **Ustad Rahman Khan**, Kelucharan Mohapatra.

**Mridangam** (p.437) — **double-headed drum** used in South Indian classical music.
- **Umayalpuram K. Sivaraman** — **Padma Bhushan (2003), Padma Vibhushan (2010), Sangeet Natak Akademi (1992)**. Others: V.K. Sivaraman, Rajna Swaminathan, Guruvayur Dorai, RV Raghavan.

**Ghatam** (p.437) — **clay pot** used as percussion instrument. **E. M. Subramaniyam** — title **"Kalaimamani" (2000)**; **Sangeet Natak Akademi Award (2011)**. **T.H. Vinaykram (Vikku Vinaykram)** — **Padma Bhushan (2014)**.

**Drums** (p.437) — make sound when struck. Exponent: **Sivamani**.

### A.12 Folk Music of Different States (book pp.437–438)
| State | Folk music / description |
|---|---|
| Andhra Pradesh | **Burrakatha**: a folk storytelling art from **Andhra Pradesh and Telangana**; famously performed during the **Perupalem Beach Festival**. |
| Assam | **Kirtan-Ghosha**: composed by the prominent Assamese saint and polymath **Srimanta Sankardev**. **Assamese Lullabies**: **Nichukoni Geet** are traditional lullabies. **Bihu-Geet**. **Zikir folk music**. |
| Bihar | **Biraha**: sad plays and songs popular in **19th-century Bihar's Bhojpur region**. **Mangal folk songs**: sung in the **Mithila region**. |
| Chhattisgarh | **Pathori Geet**: sung during **bidding farewell to a newly-wed bride**. **Dohe songs**: sung during **Diwali**. **Dewar Geet**: sung as **entertainment songs**. **Tambura**: instrument commonly used as a prop during performance of **Pandavani**. |
| Gujarat | The raga **"Sorathi"** derives its name from the region in Gujarat. The raga **"Bilaval"**, named after **Veraval**, is traditionally associated with Gujarat. |
| Goa | **Mando**: central theme of Mando music is **love and historical narratives**. A slow verse-and-refrain composition dealing with love, tragedy and both social injustice and political resistance. |
| Himachal Pradesh | **Hulki**: traditional drum from HP. **Narsingha**: traditional wind instrument with ornate curved hornpipe design, ceremonial use in HP folk music. |
| Haryana | **Ragani** folk songs. |
| Jammu & Kashmir | **Chakri**: popular folk music form of J&K (the intricate footwork in Rouf is also sometimes referred to as Chakri). **Wanawun**: vocal songs sung during the **Rouf dance**. **Sufiana music**: **Hafiz Nagma** is a dance based on the musical notes of Sufiana music. **Lal Ded (Lalleshwari)**: Kashmiri mystic of the Shaivism tradition who used poetic verses known as **Vak**. |
| Jharkhand | **Jhumti Song**: tied to the **"Jhumair"** folk dance. **Adharatiya**: a traditional folk song. |
### A.12 Folk Music of Different States — continued (book pp.438–439)
| State | Folk music / description |
|---|---|
| Kerala | **Sopana Sangeetham**: devotional classical music tradition originating from Kerala. "Sopana" translates to **steps** — traditionally performed on the **steps of temples** in Kerala. **Pulluvan pattu** folk music, **Ottanthullal** songs, **Kathakali music**, church choir, **Mappila songs** are some famous music forms of Kerala. Drums like **Timila and Maddalam** are mentioned in **Silappadikaram** and still used today. **Kangarange**: a drum from South India, associated with the Carnatic music tradition. |
| Madhya Pradesh | **Sanjha**: melodious folk song sung by young girls in groups, predominantly associated with the **Malwa region**. **Sant Singhaji**: known as the **"Kabir of Nimar"**; composed in the **Nirguni** style (formless God). |
| Mizoram | **Talhkhuang**: musical instrument of curved wooden pieces played with a wooden hammer; used **only at memorial stone ceremonies**, not taken into homes. **Darbu**: looks somewhat like a large Damru; struck to produce sound → **membranophone (Avanaddha Vadya)**. **Bengbung**: traditional musical instrument made of wood. **Khuang**: has a stretched membrane → **membranophone (Avanaddha Vadya)**. |
| Maharashtra | **Lavani**: combination of traditional song and dance, particularly performed to the beats of **Dholki**; noted for its powerful rhythm; contributed substantially to the development of **Marathi folk theatre**; in Maharashtra, southern MP and North Karnataka performed by female performers wearing **nine-yard long saris**. **Sangeet Ratnakara**: literally the **"Ocean of Music and Dance"**; a classical musical treatise composed in the **13th century CE by Sharangadeva** (Sarangadeva). **Abhang Vanis**: folk songs. **Narayanrao Rajhauns**: established his own theatre company, the **Gandharva Natak Mandali**; honoured with **Padma Bhushan in 1964**; the title **"Bal Gandharva" (Singer of Heaven)** was conferred upon him by freedom fighter **Lokmanya Bal Gangadhar Tilak**. |
| Nagaland | **Neuleu** songs. **Asem**: a traditional musical instrument of Nagaland; belongs to the drum. |
| Odisha | **Daskathia**: a form of **ballad singing**. |
| Rajasthan | Rajasthani music is generally based on/tied to nature, life and stories (printed "based on Tied to nature"). **Pankhida** songs: sung by peasants while working in the fields. **Kamaicha**: string instrument central to the **Manganiyar** community's musical tradition. **Ravanhatta**: traditional instrument from Rajasthan; specifically played by the **Bhopa** community. **Lotia** folk music: sung by women carrying water pots (Lota). **Maand** folk music: similar to Thumri or Ghazal. **Bhopa**: a form of folk music. |
| Sikkim | **Simikla Koom (Binayo)**: musical instrument; by mythology created by the deity's brothers: **Sawakemba, Samdang Khewa and Lingdang Khewa**. |
| Tamil Nadu | **Swathi Thirunal**: Maharaja of the **Travancore** kingdom (located near present-day Tamil Nadu); great patron of **Hindustani and Carnatic music and the Mohiniyattam** dance form. **Vamsi**: traditional instrument of Tamil Nadu — a **Flute**. |
| Uttar Pradesh | **Hori**: traditional music form sung in the **Braj region**. **Rasiya** folk songs: in the context of Rasiya folk songs of UP, **Bumb** refers to large drums. **Kajri**: sung by women during the **monsoon**, often with night-long group dancing. |
| Uttarakhand | **Chhopati** folk songs. **Bhankora**: an **aerophone (Sushir Vadya)** from the **Garhwal** region. **Mangal**: a folk tradition performed by the **Jaunsari tribe**. |
| West Bengal | **Padavali Kirtans**: sung in devotional gatherings. **Bhawaiya** folk songs: sung solo or in chorus. **Dotara**: traditional string instrument widely played in the Bengali tradition (West Bengal, Assam, Bangladesh). **Baul music**. |
| Punjab | **Tappa**: developed by **Shori Mian**; traditionally sung during joyous occasions; rapid, jumpy notes inspired by **camel riders' songs**. **Bhangra**: energetic folk music and dance style, typically performed during harvest seasons and festive occasions. |
| Ladakh | Known for **monastic music tradition** that includes sacred chants with drumbeats called **'Yang'**. A **monastic orchestra** that uses the instrument **'Gyaling' (oboe)** is associated with Ladakh. |
(The right-hand half of p.438 starts with a blank state cell over "Abhang Vanis / Narayanrao Rajhauns / Bal Gandharva" — it is the continuation of the Maharashtra block from the left column.)
⚠ Kamaicha is described here as a "string instrument" (p.438) but as a "bowed flute" on p.434 — internal inconsistency in Parmar.

Subbulakshmi 1998, Ravi Shankar 1999, Bismillah Khan 2001, Lata Mangeshkar 2001, Bhimsen Joshi 2008; Padma awards to Zakir Hussain, Hariprasad Chaurasia, Shivkumar Sharma, etc.) should be cross-checked against the Awards raw file (round 2) — no verification done here.

5. No existing project doc holds music; nearest related: Medieval History raw (Bhakti/Sufi; Amir Khusrau; Tansen in Mughal court) — `medieval-history-raw/delhi_sultanate_raw_extraction.md` §A.12 and `medieval-history-raw/mughal_empire_raw_extraction.md` §H.3 (cross-reference boxes only; not re-extracted).

# STATIC GK — FESTIVALS: RAW EXTRACTION
Subject: Static GK → Festivals (round 1). **RAW EXTRACTION ONLY** — no test cross-referencing yet.

## Sources & page map
| Part | Source | Book pages | PDF pages |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | Parmar SSC Fatman (2nd Ed., 2026) — "Festivals of India" (state-wise tables, Important Festivals, Indian New Year) | 459–473 | 492–506 |
| B | Lucent's GK (2026) — Art & Culture §4 "Festivals and Fairs" (+ Rangoli table on the same page) | 327–329 | 340–342 |
(Parmar PDF = book + 33; Lucent PDF = book + 13.)

## Method / disclosure
Read from native page images; OCR only as cross-check. Fact-complete, not word-for-word (every festival, date, tribe, number and trick sentence kept; explanatory prose condensed). ⚠ = doubt / printing slip / cross-book disagreement. Parmar trick sentences are kept as printed; trick-word→row mappings not tagged in the book are marked "—" or flagged.

---

## PART A — PARMAR: "FESTIVALS OF INDIA" (book pp.459–473, PDF 492–506)

Chapter running head: "FESTIVALS OF INDIA". Layout: state blocks (two-column pages), each usually with a Hinglish **TRICK TO REMEMBER** sentence (capitalised words = first words of festival names) and a table (Trick | Festival | Description). Where a festival has no trick word "—" is shown. Photo captions noted where they name a festival.

### A.1 ANDHRA PRADESH (book p.459) — no trick
| Festival | Description |
|---|---|
| Ugadi | **Telugu New Year**. Signifies start of the harvest season. Marks the beginning of a new **Hindu Lunisolar calendar**. |
| Tirupati Brahmotsavam | Annual festival of **Lord Venkateswara at Tirumala**, celebrated for **9 days**. |
| Durgamma | Celebrated in **Durga Pooja**. |
| Visakha Utsav | Held in **December**. |
| Yellama Jatara | Dedicated to **Goddess Yellamma**. |
| Sankranti | Also known as **Pedda Panduga**. A harvest festival celebrated in **Andhra Pradesh and Telangana for four days** with the **Bhogi bonfire**. |
| Bathukamma | **9-day floral festival** celebrated mainly by **women**. Large central arrangement of flowers (called Bathukamma). Starts on **Mahalaya Amavasya (New Moon day)**. **Goddess Gauri** and nature are worshipped. Takes place **September–October**. Celebrated in **Telangana and parts of Andhra Pradesh**. |
| Bonalu | Celebrated in Andhra Pradesh and Telangana with special prayers to **Goddess Kali**; involves local fairs. |
| Perupalem Beach Festival | Celebrated in **West Godavari district**, specifically at **Mogalthur**. **Inaugurated in 2020**. Primarily a tourism and cultural festival lasting about **2 days**. Events like **beach volleyball** showcased. The traditional **'Burrakatha'** dance (as printed) also performed. |
| Kanuma | Marks the celebration of the **victory of good over evil**. |
Photo captions: Bonalu; Tirupati Brahmotsavam.

### A.2 ARUNACHAL PRADESH (book pp.459–460)
TRICK: "Arun kya tere Sang Solun is Tree ke neeche Chalo Sir Meri Pin pakdo aur Paas aao"

| Trick | Festival | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Arun | — | Arunachal Pradesh |
| Sang | Sangken | **3-day** festival celebrated in **April**. **Water festival** of the **Singpho tribe**. |
| Solun | Solung | **10-day harvest** festival celebrated by the **Adi tribe**. **Apong** = traditional rice beer shared to promote social bonding. **Ponung dance** performed. Deities worshipped: **Goddess of Crop (Kine Nane)** and **God of Domestic Animals (Dadi Bote)** for prosperity. |
| Tree | Dree | Harvest festival celebrated by the **Apatani tribe** in **July**. |
| Chalo | Chalo Loku | Celebrated in the **Tirap district** by the **Nocte tribe** in **July/August**. 'Lofe' (from which Loku is derived) means **"to drive out"** (driving out the old season). **3-day** harvest festival around **Oct–Nov** at the end of the paddy harvest. **Day 1 (Famlam)**: omen-reading by elders to determine jhum plots before the buffalo slaughter; ceremonial animal slaughter (pigs, buffaloes). **Day 2 (Chamkatja)**: people visit monasteries/shrines ('Chaatam') to offer prayers. **Day 3 (Thanglangja)**: farewell day with communal dancing, fasting, omen-reading. |
| Lo Sir | Losar | **New Year festival of Tibetan Buddhism**, celebrated by the **Monpa tribe**. Marks the **1st day of the Tibetan calendar**. |
| Meri Pin | Mopin | Celebrated by the **Galo tribe** in **April**. **Popir dance** performed. |
| Paas aao | Pangsau | To honour the **war heroes who died in WW2**. |
| — | Ziro | Founded in **2012**. Celebrated in the **Ziro Valley**. |
| — | Boori-Boot | Celebrated by the **Nyishi tribe** in **Spring/Feb**. |
| — | Myoko | Celebrated by the **Apatani tribe** in the Ziro Valley. Based on **rotating village responsibility for rituals**. |
| — | Nyokum / Nyokum Yullo | Celebrated by the **Nyishi tribe in Feb.** Ritual invocation of deities for harmony and prosperity: **Nyo = earth, Kum = Collectiveness, Yullo = invocation**. Agricultural festival where **Donyi-Polo (Moon/Sun God)** is worshipped for a bountiful harvest. |
| — | Pongtu | Celebrated by the **Tutsa tribe**. Observed to seek blessings from the **Rangkhothak deity** before millet harvesting. **"Pongtu" = "retreating wind"**. Pre-harvest festival, also celebrated as a **New Year** festival. |
Photo captions: Losar; Solung.

### A.3 ASSAM (book p.460)
TRICK: "Assam ki Bahu ne Bai ko Patak patak ke mara kyunki usko Ali ke Abbu ke saath Majuli Festival dekhne jana tha."

| Trick | Festival | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Bahu | Bihu | Celebrated **thrice a year**: **Rongali/Bohag Bihu (April)** — Assam New Year; **Kati/Kongali Bihu (Oct–Nov)**; **Bhogali/Magh Bihu (Jan)**. **Uruka** before Magh Bihu features bonfires and feasting. **Manuh Bihu** includes gifting Gamucha and traditional dishes. |
| Baiko | Baikho | Pre-harvest festival celebrated by the **Rabha community in June**, featuring the **'Baranakai' fire**. |
| — | Baishagu | **New Year festival of the Bodo Kachari community**, celebrated in **mid-April**; prayers offered to **'Bathou' / Lord Shiva**. |
| Patak | Dehing Patkai | **3-day** annual festival held in **January** at **Lekhapani in Tinsukia district**. |
| Ali | Ali Ai Ligang | **Harvest festival of the Mishing tribe**. Seed-sowing festival in **Feb–March** by the Mising tribe, featuring the **'Gumrag' dance**. |
| Abbu | Ambubachi Mela | Held at **Kamakhya temple**. Marks the **menstrual phase of Goddess Kamakhya**. Also known as **Ameti/Tantric fertility festival**. Occurs around **mid-June**, signifies onset of the monsoon. 'Ambubachi' means **"spoken with water"**. Symbolises Mother Earth regaining her fertility. For **3 days the land is left completely empty** — no digging, plowing or sowing. |
| Majuli | Majuli | **3-day** festival held in **November**. |
| — | Rongker | Celebrated by the **Karbi tribe**. |
| — | Junbeel Mela | **Exchange of products through barter exchange**. |
| — | Makar Sankranti | Known as **Magh Bihu or Bhogali Bihu**. |
| — | Bushu | **Dimasa community**. |
Photo captions: Bihu; Ambubachi Mela.

### A.4 BIHAR (book pp.460–461)
TRICK: "Sonu Shaam ko Chhath par Bahu La"

| Trick | Festival | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Sonu | Sonepur Cattle Fair | **Largest fair for elephant trading in Asia** — actually the **Sonepur Mela**, held in **November in Patna, Bihar** (as printed). |
| Shaam | Sama Chakeva | Originates from the **Mithila region**. |
| Chhath | Chhath Pooja | Major festival of **Bihar and UP** dedicated to **"Chhathi Maiya" and the Sun God (Surya)**. Begins exactly on the **6th day after Diwali**. **4-day** festival during **Kartik month (around Oct/Nov)**. |
| Bahu La | Bihula | Celebrated in **August**; devotees pray to the **Serpent Goddess Mansa**. |
| — | Rajgir | **3-day** festival **started in 1986**. |
| — | Salhesh | Celebrated in the **Mithila region**. Honours **Baba Salhesh**, who belonged to the **Dusadh Dalit** community (primary group that celebrates it). The **'Bhagat' and 'Manarias'** narrate his dramatic stories and legends. |
| — | Makar Sankranti | Known as **Khichdi**. |
| — | Santhal Festivals (Erok Sim & Magh Sim) | Santhal community mainly found in **Jharkhand, Bihar, Odisha, West Bengal**. **Erok Sim** marks the **'beginning' of the agricultural season**; seed-sowing festival celebrated before sowing paddy; very similar to **Chapchar Kut of Mizoram**; also known as **'Harisim' or 'Harpod'**. **Magh Sim** is celebrated to express gratitude for the harvest and prepare for a new agricultural cycle. **Animal sacrifices** (generally a **rooster/chicken**) performed by the village priest, the **'Naike' (or Naiki)**, at a sacred grove known as **'Jaherthan'**. Deities: **'Jaher Era' and 'Marang Buru'**. |
Photo captions: Chhath Pooja; Sonepur Cattle Fair.

### A.5 CHHATTISGARH (book p.461)
TRICK: "36 Madai Soncha aur Bola Haveli me Basta leke Dussehra Manaenge."

| Trick | Festival | Description |
|---|---|---|
| 36 | — | Chhattisgarh |
| Madai | Madai (Phagun Madai) | Celebrated on the **eve of Holi** in the **Dantewada district**. Celebrated by the **Gond and Baiga tribes** during **Feb–March (Phagun)**. Takes place at the **Maa Danteshwari temple**; also called the **"Kumbh Mela of Tribal Culture"**. |
| Soncha | Goncha | **Chariot festival** celebrated in **Bastar (Jagdalpur)**, primarily by the **Maria and Muria tribe** for **9 days**. Marks the beginning of the famous **Bastar Dussehra (lasts 75 days)**. **Tupki** = playful mock gun made of wood/bamboo using **Goncha fruits** as bullets. Chariot procession in **June–July**, a tradition started by the **Chalukya rulers**. **Rath Yatra begins on Ashadh Shukla Dashmi and ends on Dutiya.** |
| Bola | Pola | Thanksgiving festival acknowledging **Bulls and Oxen**. Celebrated in **Madhya Pradesh (Balaghat), Maharashtra and Chhattisgarh**. During **Shravan (monsoon)** on an **Amavasya (New Moon) night**. Farmers bathe and decorate their bulls with oil, turmeric and paints. |
| Haveli | Hareli | **Harvest festival of Shravan month (July/August)** celebrated by farmers. |
(The trick words "Basta", "Dussehra", "Manaenge" continue on the next page — see "A.5 (continued)" below.)
### A.5 (continued) CHHATTISGARH (book p.462 top)
| Trick | Festival | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Basta Dussehra | Bastar Dussehra | To honour **Goddess Danteshwari**. |
| — | Karam | Harvest festival tribute to the **Karam tree**. |
| — | Champaran Mela | Annual fair held in **Champaran region, Raipur**. |
| — | Narayanpur Mela | **Muria tribe**. |

### A.6 GOA (book p.462)
TRICK: "3 Kings went to Goa's St. Francis Xavier church took some Grapes lekin Car Nahi mili aur Sunburn ho Gaya."

| Trick | Festival | Description |
|---|---|---|
| 3 Kings | 3 Kings Festival | Also known as the **Epiphany festival**. |
| St. Francis Xavier | Feast of Saint Francis Xavier | Celebrated on **3rd December** (death anniversary of Saint Francis). Takes place at the **Basilica of Bom Jesus** (associated with St. Francis Xavier). |
| Grapes | Grapes Escapade | **Wine festival**. |
| Car Nahi | Carnival | Celebrated **immediately before the Lent** (Christian). |
| Sunburn | Sunburn | **Asia's largest Electronic Dance Music festival**. |

| Pang Lhabsol | **Chu Faat** (sacred folk dance) performed by the **Lepcha tribe** of Sikkim. Signifies the worship of **5 sacred peaks: Mt. Kanchenjunga, Mt. Kabru, Mt. Simbrum, Mt. Pandim, Mt. Narshing**. Involves worship of Mount Kanchenjunga; prayers, masked dances, local fairs. Also known as **Pangtoed Cham**; takes place in **Aug–Sept**. |
| Saga Dawa | Also known as the **Triple Blessed festival**; **Sikkim's most sacred, month-long festival** commemorating **Lord Buddha's birth, enlightenment and death (Mahaparinirvana)**. |
| Tendong Lho Rumfaat | Celebrated by the **Lepcha** community in **July/August** to honour **Mount Tendong**. |
| Tihar | **Diwali is known as Tihar in Sikkim**. **Maruni dance** performed during Tihar. |
| Indra Jatra | Celebrated by the **Newar (Nawar) community** in Sikkim; lasts **8 days**; dedicated to the **Rain God (Lord Indra)**. |
| Bumchu | Buddhist festival at **Tashiding Monastery** in Sikkim, where a **sacred pot of holy water ("Bumchu")** is opened to predict the year's fortune. |
| Sonam Lochar | **New Year festival of the Tamang people**. |
| Tamu Lochar | **Gurung community**. |

### A.23 UTTARAKHAND (book p.470) — no trick
| Festival | Description |
|---|---|
| Harela | Celebrated in the **Kumaon region**. |
| Bhitauli | Celebrated in the **Kumaon region**. |
| Syaldey Bikhauti Mela | Celebrated at **Dwarahat, Almora district**; at the **Vimandeshwar temple** in **Baisakh (April–May)**; begins with the **"Odha Bhetna" (stone striking)** ceremony. |
| Phool Dei | Marks the **welcoming of Spring**; young girls gather first-blooming flowers and place them on doorsteps to wish prosperity; **unique to Kumaon Hills**. |
| Kale Kauva (Ghugutiya) | Celebrated on **Makar Sankranti**. |
| Kandali | Celebrated in the **Chaundas Valley of Pithoragarh**; by the **Rung tribe**; **once every 12 years**; starts with the blooming of the **Kangdali** plant flowers. |
| Ganga Dussehra | Celebrated in **May–June**, lasts **10 days**; Goddess **Ganga** descended from Heaven to Earth on this day. |
| Bisu Mela | **Week-long Jaunsari tribal fair** in **Chakrata**, thanking for harvest and honouring **Santoora Devi** (a Durga form). |
| Shakunkhar Mangal Geet | Associated with **Kumaon**; auspicious songs **sung exclusively by women** during childbirth, marriage, Ganesh puja. |
| Baithki Holi | Celebrated in **Kumaon** (Kumaoni Baithki Holi); mainly around **Basant Panchami (January)**. |
| Jauljibi Fair | **13-day trade fair** in **Pithoragarh** at the confluence of **Gori River and Kali River**, celebrated in **November**. |
Photo captions: Phool dei; Kandali.

### A.24 TAMIL NADU (book p.470) — no trick
| Festival | Description |
|---|---|
| Pongal | "Pongal" = **"to boil"** (traditional dish of boiled milk and rice). Harvest festival that marks the **Uttarayan (sun's northward movement / Makar Sankranti)**. **4-day** harvest festival (**13–16 Jan**): **Day 1 (Bhogi)** — cleaning the home and letting go of the old; **Day 2 (Thai/Surya)** — worshipping the Sun God; **Day 3 (Mattu)** — worshipping cattle (bull-taming sport **Jallikattu** held); **Day 4 (Kaanum)** — family reunions and outings. **Kolam**: Kolam Rangoli drawn at the entrance of homes during Pongal. |
| Jallikattu | Traditional **bull-taming** event of Tamil Nadu; during Pongal, specifically on **Mattu Pongal** (honours cattle). |
| Puthandu | **Tamil New Year**; ritual baths and temple visits. |
| Natyanjali | Annual classical dance festival dedicated to **Lord Shiva (Nataraja)**, held at **Chidambaram Temple**, where the **Cosmic dance (Tandava)** of Shiva is depicted. |
### A.24 (continued) TAMIL NADU (book p.471) — no trick
| Festival | Description |
|---|---|
| Karthigai Deepam | Celebrated in **Nov–Dec**; associated with the **Arunachaleshwar Temple**, generally linked to **Lord Shiva**; earthen oil lamps called **"Agal vilakku"** lit to mark the victory of good over evil. |
| Thaipusam | Worship of **Lord Murugan** where **Kavadi** is carried; celebrated on the **full moon of the Tamil month Thai**; commemorates the victory of Lord Murugan over the demon **Surapadma**. |
| Mahamaham | Known as the **"Kumbh of South"**; celebrated **once every 12 years**. |
| Kulasai Dasara | At the **Arulmigu Mutharamman Temple** in Tamil Nadu; about **10 days** during **Navratri (Sept–Oct)**; devotees wear demon-themed makeup and costumes. |
| Thiruvathirai | Women swing on **mango trees** to celebrate womanhood (as printed). |
| Manjal Neerattu Vizha | **Turmeric bathing ceremony** held when a girl achieves womanhood (first menstruation). |
| Modhwidsh Festival | **Toda tribe** is found in the **Nilgiri Hills**; Todas build special huts and worship the **buffalo** (sacred). **"Modhwidsh" = New Year festival** of the Toda tribe, around **Dec/Jan**. (Printed "Modhwidsh"/"Modhwidsh Festival".) |
| Karaga | Celebrated mainly in **Tamil Nadu** and also in **Karnataka**; lasts **11 days**; by the **Thigala** community to honour **Draupadi and Shakti**. |
Photo captions: Thaipusam; Jallikattu.

### A.25 UTTAR PRADESH (book p.471) — no trick
| Festival | Description |
|---|---|
| Taj Mahotsav | Celebrated **17–27 February**. |
| Holi | Celebrated on the **Full moon day (Purnima) of Phalgun**; popularly known as the **festival of Colors**. |
| Diwali | Celebrated on the **New moon day (Amavasya) of Kartika**; marks the **return of Lord Rama to Ayodhya after 14 years of exile**. |
| Kumbh Mela | **Major spiritual gathering**; held at four Tirthas: **Prayagraj, Haridwar, Nasik, Ujjain**; the places where **four drops of amrit (nectar)** fell during a scuffle between gods and demons. |
| Eid-ul-Fitr | Conclusion of **Ramadan**; fasting, prayers, communal feasts. |
| Makar Sankranti | Known as **Khichdi**. |
| Chhath Puja | Major festival of **Bihar and UP**; dedicated to **"Chhathi Maiya" and the Sun God (Surya)**; begins on the **6th day after Diwali**; **4-day** festival during **Kartik** (Oct/Nov). |
(⚠ The Diwali/Kumbh/Eid/Makar Sankranti/Chhath rows sit in the right-hand column of p.471 after the Tamil Nadu block, under the UP heading of the left column — as printed; treat them as UP-page items.)

### A.27 TELANGANA (book pp.471–472)
TRICK: "Bathua Banalu Sam me Padunga"

| Trick | Festival | Description |
|---|---|---|
| Bathua | Bathukamma | **9-day floral festival** celebrated mainly by **women**; large central arrangement of flowers; starts on **Mahalaya Amavasya**; **Goddess Gauri** and nature worshipped; **Sept–Oct**; celebrated in **Telangana and parts of Andhra Pradesh**. |
| Banalu | Bonalu | **Started in 1813** to eradicate the **plague (caused by Yersinia pestis bacteria)**. A **"Bonam"** (food offering) offered to **Goddess Mahakali**. Mainly celebrated in **Hyderabad and Secunderabad**; declared the **Official State Festival of Telangana in 2014**. **Potharaju**, the brother of Goddess Mahakali, is also worshipped. |
| Sam | Sammakka Jatara | Also known as **Medaram Jatara**; rituals performed by the **Koya tribe**. |
| Padunga | Dunnapothula Panduga | Also known as **Sadar festival**; celebrated by the **Yadava community**. |
| — | Makar Sankranti | Celebrated for **four days** with the **Bhogi bonfire**. |
| — | Ugadi | Signifies the start of the harvest season and the **official start of the New Year**. |
⚠ Bonalu "started in 1813" is as printed.

### A.28 TRIPURA (book p.472) — no trick
| Festival | Description |
|---|---|

| Hojagiri | Celebrated by the **Reang community** to worship **Goddess Mailuma (Laxmi)**. |
| Garia Puja | Traditional tribal dances and rituals after **jhum cultivation**. |
| Bijhu | Celebrated by the **Chakma tribe** to celebrate the **New Year**. |
| Garia Carnival | Celebrated in Tripura to honour **Baba Garia**. The **"Ochai"** acts as guide/priest for the entire festival; involves worship of a **bamboo pole**; celebrated in **Chaitra (March–April)**, lasts about **7 days**. |

| Losar | **Ladakhi New Year**; symbolic **dough models of the Sun, the Moon and the Ibex** placed in households. |
