### A.32 IMPORTANT FESTIVALS (all-India) (book p.473, left column + right column top)
| Festival | Description |
|---|---|
| Makar Sankranti | Celebrated across India; marks the **sun's northward movement (Uttarayan)**, entering **Capricorn (Makar)** at the **winter solstice** (as printed). |
| Paryushana | Involves **fasting, prayer and self-reflection**; observed in **Bhadrapada**; a **Jain** festival (Aug–Sept). |
| Shahidi Divas | Commemorates the martyrdom of **Guru Arjan Dev — 5th Sikh Guru**; executed by **Mughal Emperor Jahangir** for giving shelter to Jahangir's son **Khusrau**. |
| Buddha Purnima | Commemorates the **three major life events of Buddha: birth, enlightenment, death (Mahaparinirvana)**; also called the **"Thrice Blessed Festival"**. |
| Kumbh Mela | Major spiritual gathering; at four Tirthas **Prayagraj, Haridwar, Nasik, Ujjain**; four drops of amrit fell there. |
| Maha Shivaratri | Day of devotion to **Lord Shiva**; falls on the **14th day of the dark half of Phalguna**. |
| Karva Chauth | Observed with fasting by married women for husbands' well-being; typically in **Kartik**. |
| Bandi Chhor Divas | Commemorates **Guru Hargobind's release with 52 kings**. |
| Navroz festival | Celebrated by the **Parsi (Zoroastrian)** community. **Balban started the Navroz festival in India** (as printed). |
| Tulip Festival | **Asia's largest tulip festival** is organised in **Srinagar**. |
| Diwali | New moon day (Amavasya) of **Kartika**; return of **Lord Rama to Ayodhya after 14 years of exile**. |
| Holi | **Full moon day (Purnima) of Phalgun**; **festival of Colors**. |
| Navreh | Celebrated by **Kashmiri Pandits**; on the **first day of Chaitra** — the **Kashmiri New Year**; items like walnuts, almonds and raisins kept during the festival. |
| Lohri | Popular festival of **North India**; mainly associated with **Sugarcane harvesting**. |
| Muharram | **First month of the Islamic calendar**. |
| Eid-ul-Fitr | Conclusion of **Ramadan**; observed with fasting, prayers, communal feasts. |
| Barawafat (Eid-e-Milad-un-Nabi) | Commemorates the **birth anniversary of Prophet Muhammad**, on the **12th day (Barah Wafat) of Rabi' al-Awwal**. |
| Zakat al-Fitr | **Charity** is given during this festival. |
⚠ "Balban started the Navroz festival in India" is the book's statement (Delhi Sultanate context — cross-check `medieval-history-raw/delhi_sultanate_raw_extraction.md`; I have not verified it).

Map "When do Indians celebrate their New Year?" (p.473; small print, read as legibly as possible — ⚠ some labels are tiny): colour key = **Jan 1 (by Sun's path); April 13/14/15 (by Sun's path); March/April (by Moon's path); June/July (by Moon's path); Oct/Nov (by Moon's path, day after Diwali); December (by Moon's path)**. Labels include: Ladakh — Galdan Namchot; J&K — Navroz; Himachal — Basoa; Uttarakhand — Bikhoti; Punjab/Haryana — Baisakhi; Rajasthan, UP, MP, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand — Chaitra Navaratri; Bihar — Jur Sital; Assam — Rongali Bihu; Arunachal — Sangken; Nagaland — New Year; Manipur — Sajibu Cheiraoba; Meghalaya — Chad Suksa (⚠ illegible/uncertain); Tripura — Buisu Tring; Mizoram — New Year; Sikkim — Lhosar; Kutch — Ashadhi Bij; Gujarat — Nutan Varsh; Odisha — Bishuba Sankranti; West Bengal — Pohela Boishakh; Maharashtra — Gudi Padwa; Telangana, Karnataka, AP — Ugadi; Tamil Nadu — Puthandu; Kerala — Vishu; Konkan — Bisu Parba. By community: Parsi — 17/18/19 Aug (by Sun); Zoroastrian — 21 March (by Sun); Sindhis — Cheti Chand (March/April, by Moon).
## PART B — LUCENT'S GK (2026), "ART AND CULTURE" §4 "Festivals and Fairs" (book pp.327–329 = PDF 340–342)
Read from page images. Right-edge text on p.328 is partly cut off in the scan (marked ⚠).

### B.1 Introductory notes (p.327)
- Festivals are an integral part of Indian life; different religious communities (Hindus, Muslims, Christians, Sikhs, Jain, Buddhist etc.) and regions (Kashmir, Punjab, Delhi, UP, Bihar, Assam, Bengal, Tamil Nadu, Kerala, Goa etc.) have their own festivals.
- Secular festivals of all-India importance = **National Festivals**: **Republic Day (26 January), Independence Day (15 August), Gandhi Jayanti (2 October)**.

### B.2 Hindu Festivals (Lucent p.327)
| # | Festival | Date (occasion) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Shitala Ashtami | **Ashtami of Krishna Paksha of Chait** (celebrated by women in honour of **Shitala Mata**, goddess of chechak i.e. smallpox) |
| 2 | Ramnavami | **Navami of Shukla Paksha of Chait** (Lord Ram's birth anniversary) |
| 3 | Hanuman Jayanti | **Purnima of Chait** (Hanuman's birth anniversary) |
| 4 | Teej | **Tritiya of Shukla Paksha of Savan** (worship of **Lord Shiva by married women** for husbands' well-being) |
| 5 | Nag Panchami | **Panchami of Shukla Paksha of Savan** (worship to protect life from snakes) |
| 6 | Raksha Bandhan | **Purnima of Savan** (festival of brother and sister) |
| 7 | Ganesh Chaturthi | **Chaturthi of Krishna Paksha of Bhado** (worship of Lord Ganesh) (as printed; ⚠ commonly Shukla Paksha — printed "Krishna Paksha") |
| 8 | Krishna Janmashtami | **Ashtami of Krishna Paksha of Bhado** (Lord Krishna's birth anniversary) |
| 9 | Jeevatputrika / Jeetiya Puja (Jeemutvahan Puja) | **Ashtami of Krishna Paksha of Aashwin** (festival performed by mothers of sons for longevity and health of their sons) |
| 10 | Durga Puja | **First 9 days — Prathama to Navami — of Shukla Paksha of Aashwin** (worship of goddess Durga) |
| 11 | Vijaya Dasami | **Dasami of Shukla Paksha of Aashwin** (Ram's victory over Ravana; victory of good over evil) |
| 12 | Karva Chauth | **Chaturthi of Krishna Paksha of Kartik** (married women, long life of husbands; popular in North India) |
| 13 | Deepawali / Deewali | **Amavasya of Kartik** (Lord Ram's return to Ayodhya after 14 years of exile, Vanvas) |
| 14 | Chhath | **Shashthi of Shukla Paksha of Kartik** (worship of **Sun (Surya)**; esp. popular in **Bihar and Eastern UP**) |
| 15 | Makar Sankranti | **Chaturthi of Krishna Paksha of Magh** (printed), usually **14/15 January** (Sun's entry from Sagittarius (Dhanu Rashi) to Capricorn (Makar Rashi) — transition of Sun in the Northern hemisphere, **Uttarayan**) |
| 16 | Vasant Panchami | **Panchami of Shukla Paksha of Magh** (worship of goddess **Saraswati**, goddess of art & learning) |
| 17 | Shivaratri | **Chaturdashi of Krishna Paksha of Fagun** (Shiva-Parvati marriage) |
| 18 | Holi | **Purnima of Fagun** (Holika's burning and Prahlad's survival; victory of good over evil; festival of Abir/Gulal and Rang) |

**Notes (p.328)**
- A. **Names of months of Hindu calendar (Hindu Panchang):** 1 Chaitra (Chait), 2 Vaishakh (Baisakh), 3 Jyeshth (Jeth), 4 Aashadh (Asadh), 5 Shravan (Savan), 6 Bhadrapad (Bhado), 7 Aashwin (Aasin/Kwar), 8 Kartik (Katik), 9 Agrahayan (Agahan), 10 Paush (Pus), 11 Magh (Magh), 12 Falgun (Fagun).
- B. **30 days in a month** of the Hindu calendar; first 15 = **Krishna Paksh**, later 15 = **Shukla Paksha**. Last day (15th) of Krishna Paksha = **Amavasya**; last day (15th) of Shukla Paksha = **Purnima**. Other days share names in both pakshas: Prathama (1st), Dwitiya (2nd), Tritiya (3rd), Chaturthi (4th), Panchami (5th), Shashthi (6th), Saptami (7th), Ashtami (8th), Navami (9th), Dasami (10th), Ekadashi (11th), Dwadashi (12th), Trayodashi (13th), Chaturdashi (14th).
- C. **Hindu festivals are based on Vikram Samvat.**

**Kumbh Mela (Lucent p.328):** major pilgrimage and festival of Hindus; **world's largest public gathering**; devotees bathe in a sacred river; celebrated **four times over the course of 12 years**; sites: **Haridwar (Ganga), Ujjain (Shipra), Nashik (Godavari), Prayagraj/Allahabad (Ganga)**.

### B.3 Muslim Festivals (Lucent p.328)
| # | Festival | Date (occasion) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Muharram | **10th day of first month Moharram-ul-Haram** of Hijri calendar (mourning festival in memory of the martyrdom of Hazrat Muhammad's daughter Fatima's and son-in-law Ali's son **Hussain in Karbala**) |
| 2 | Milad-un-Navi (Milad-i-Sharif) | **12th day of third month Rabi-ul-Awwal** (Hazrat Muhammad's birth anniversary) |
| 3 | Vara-wafat (Vara = Barah i.e. twelve; wafat = death) | **12th day of Rabi-ul-Awwal** (Hazrat Muhammad's death anniversary) |
| 4 | Shab-i-Barat (Fate writing night) | **15th day of eighth month Shabaan** (Allah writes the fate of the coming year; prevalent only in Indian Muslims) |
| 5 | Ramzan / Ramdan | **Month-long fasting in the ninth month Ramzan-ul-Mubarik**; in memory of Muhammad's worship of Allah by staying hungry and thirsty inside a cave in Mecca for a month; Roza broken in the evening = **'Iftaar'**; after 30 days, Eid celebrated on the first day of next month Shawwal |
| 6 | Eid / Eid-ul-Fitr / Eid-i-Ramzan | **1st day of tenth month Shawwal** (festival of happiness to commemorate the revelation of Quran Sharif to Hazrat Muhammad) (⚠ right edge partly cut) |
| 7 | Bakarid / Eid-ul-Azha / Eid-i-Qurban | **10th day of 12th month Jil-Hija** (in memory of Hazrat Ibrahim's willingness to sacrifice his son Ismaeel by divine order and his resurrection) (⚠ right edge partly cut) |
Note: **Names of months of Hijri (Muslim) calendar:** 1 Moharram-ul-Haram, 2 Safar, 3 Rabi-ul-Awwal, 4 Rabi-ul-Thani, 5 Jumada-ul-Awwal, 6 Jumada-ul-Thaniyah, 7 Rajab, 8 Shabaan, 9 Ramzan-ul-Mabarik, 10 Shawwal, 11 Jikada (as printed), 12 Jil-Hija.
⚠ Lucent's row 3 ("Vara-wafat = Muhammad's death anniversary on 12th Rabi-ul-Awwal") differs from the Parmar p.473 gloss (Barawafat = Eid-e-Milad-un-Nabi = birth anniversary on 12th Rabi' al-Awwal): the two books explain "Barawafat" differently. Both as printed.

### B.4 Christian Festivals (Lucent p.328)
| # | Festival | Date (occasion) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Christmas | **25 December** (birth anniversary of Jesus Christ) |
| 2 | Palm Sunday | Month of **March**, **Sunday before Easter** (Jesus Christ's triumphal entry into Jerusalem; greeted with palm branches and cries of Hosanna) |
| 3 | Good Friday | **March/April** (mourning; crucifixion of Jesus Christ) |
| 4 | Easter | **Sunday just after Good Friday** (resurrection of Jesus Christ; per the Bible 3 days after the crucifixion) |

### B.5 Sikh, Jain and Buddhist Festivals (Lucent pp.328–329)
| # | Religion | Festival | Date (occasion) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Sikh | Guru Parv | **October/November** (birth anniversary of **Guru Nanak**, first Guru) |
| 1 | Sikh | Guru Gobind Singh Jayanti | **Saptami of Shukla Paksha of Paush** (Dec/Jan) (birth anniversary of Guru Gobind Singh, tenth & last Guru) |
| 1 | Sikh | Lohri | **13 January / 1 day before Makar Sankranti** (bid farewell to cold waves of winter; Lohri bonfires) |
| 1 | Sikh | Vaisakhi | **First date of Vaisakh** (to commemorate the **Khalsa panth founded by Guru Gobind Singh in 1699**) |
| 2 | Jain | Mahavir Jayanti (Vir Trayodashi) | **Trayodashi of Shukla Paksha of Chait** (birth anniversary of Mahavir Swami, 24th & last Teerthankar of Jains) (⚠ right edge cut) |
| 3 | Buddhist | Buddha Purnima | **Purnima of Baisakh** (Buddha born (jaati), enlightened (Sambodhi) and died (Mahaparinirvana) **on the same day of Baisakh Purnima**) |

### B.6 Regional Festivals and Fairs (Lucent p.329)
| # | State | Festivals / fairs |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Andhra Pradesh | Ugadi, Tirupati festival |
| 2 | Arunachal Pradesh | **Mopin of the Galos, Solung of the Adis, Losar of the Monpas, Dree of the Apatanis, Si-Donyi of the Tagnis, Nyokum of Nyishis, Reh of the Idu Mishmis** |
| 3 | Assam | Bihu (Rongali Bihu/Bohag Bihu, Bhogali Bihu/Magh Bihu & Kangali Bihu/Kati Bihu), Kherai Puja (puja of Bodos), Bathow (puja of Bodos), Ambubachi fair |
| 4 | Bihar | Chhath puja, Janaki Navami, **Sonepur cattle fair (Harihar kshetra mela)**, Sitamarhi cattle fair |
| 5 | Chhattisgarh | Hareli, Larukaj, Kaksar, Bambleshwari fair, Danteshwari fair |
| 6 | Goa | Zatra at Cansaulim, Feast of St. Francis Xavier, Carnival, Chikal kalo (Monsoon mud festival) |
| 7 | Gujarat | Garba, Dandia, **Ranotsav-Kutchh carnival**, Tarnetar fair, Madhavrai fair, Ambaji fair, Janmashtami, Dang Darbar, Shamlaji fair, Bhavnath fair, Chitra-vichitra fair |
| 8 | Haryana | Geeta Mahotsav, **Surajkund craft fair Faridabad** |

| 11 | Karnataka | Ugadi, **Mahamastakabhisheka (held once every 12 years)** |

| 13 | Madhya Pradesh | Teej, Gangaur, Khajuraho dance festival, Malwa festival |
| 14 | Maharashtra | Ganpati puja, Gudi Padwa, Chhatrapati Shivaji Maharaj Jayanti |
| 15 | Manipur | Lai Haraoba, Cheiraoba, Ningol Chak-Kouba, Imoinu Iratpa, Gaan-Ngai, Lui-Ngai, Sangai, Mera Houchongba |
| 16 | Meghalaya | **Pomblang Nongkrem (Khasis), Ka Shad Suk Mynsiem (Khasis), Beh Dienkhlam (Jaintias), Wangala (Garos)** |
| 17 | Mizoram | Chapchar Kut (Kut = festival), Mim Kut, Thalfavang Kut, Anthurium Kut |

## PART C — CROSS-BOOK NOTES & ⚠ DOUBTS (Festivals)
1. **Parmar is state-wise with descriptions; Lucent gives (a) date-based religion lists and (b) bare state lists** — both extracted separately. Lucent has festival names not in Parmar (e.g. Assam Kherai Puja/Bathow, Chhattisgarh Larukaj/Kaksar, Gujarat Tarnetar/Ambaji, Uttarakhand Devidhura/Purnagiri/Nanda Devi/Gauchar/Uttaraini melas, UP Nauchandi/Garh Mukteshwar, Tripura Ker Puja, Sikkim Losung/Namsung); Parmar has many festivals not in Lucent.
2. **Pola / Santhal / Bushu / Cheraw / Gangaur** repeat across several state blocks in Parmar — kept as printed in each state.
3. **Printed doubts** collected in-line: Ganesh Chaturthi "Krishna Paksha of Bhado" (Lucent B.2 #7), Makar Sankranti "Chaturthi of Krishna Paksha of Magh" (Lucent #15), Kamaicha/… (music file), Bonalu 1813, Raulane "5,000-year-old", Perupalem "Burrakatha dance", Aoleang tribe attribution, Parmar Kojagiri/Ambubachi details.
4. **Overlaps with other subjects:** Sonepur/Pushkar/Nagaur cattle fairs and Bihar/Rajasthan animal fairs → Geography/Economy (livestock) if such questions appear; Buddha Purnima (Ancient History §Buddhism raw file); Gurpurab/Baisakhi/Guru Gobind Singh (Medieval History — Sikh gurus); Khalsa founded 1699 — check against `medieval-history-raw/*`. No festival content exists in other subjects' raw files, so nothing was skipped.
5. **Dance–festival cross-refs:** many festivals have a named dance (Wangala, Cheraw–Chapchar Kut, Ponung–Solung, Chu Faat–Pang Lhabsol, Natyanjali–Chidambaram, Thabal Chongba–Yaoshang, Hojagiri, Bihu, Nuakhai–Sambalpuri, Bonalu, Gangaur, Ghoomar–Teej/Gangaur) — see `dances_raw_extraction.md`.

## Method / disclosure
Read from native page images; OCR only as cross-check. Author→book pairs are dense list content: **every pair is kept as printed** (including printed misspellings and duplicate rows, flagged ⚠ where dubious). Section headings that the book does not repeat across page breaks are noted in italics. This is a reference table, so it is reproduced as a table by design.

## PART C — CROSS-BOOK NOTES & ⚠ DOUBTS (Books & Authors)
1. **Same pair, two books:** Parmar is organised by author category (Sports / Political & Historical / Classical Old / Modern / Foreign / Music-Film / Other Autobiographies); Lucent by (A) Indian, (B) Foreign, (C) Latest, (D) "Through the Lens of Exams" — the (D) list is Lucent's own selection of exam-asked pairs and is likely the highest-yield for tests.
2. **Disagreements between the two books / within a book (as printed):** Swami Vivekananda in the West: New Discoveries → Marie Louise Burke (Parmar) vs "Sister Gargi" (Lucent); Hitopadesha "Narayan Pandit" (Lucent columns reversed); "Arundhati Rai" spelling in Lucent; Lucent "Ashish Roy" vs Parmar "Ashish Ray"; Shantaram entry in Parmar Music/Film table ("Shantaram — George David Robert") looks like a garbled/erroneous entry; Kazuo Ishiguro is repeated; "Firdausi — Shahnama (In Arabic)" (Parmar) vs Lucent "Firdausi — Shahnama"; "Hindu Civilization" attributed to J. M. Barrie (Lucent) — as printed and ⚠ suspect (I cannot verify).
3. **Booker/Nobel claims printed in these tables** are recorded without verification: Parmar "Clear Light of Day (Booker Prize 1980)", "The Promise (Booker Prize 2024)", "Flesh (Booker Prize 2025)", "Tomb of Sand (Booker Prize 2022)", "The Remains of the Day (Booker 1989)", "Alice Munro (Nobel 2013)"; Lucent "God of Small Things (Booker Prize winner)". These are recent-event risk items — check against the Awards raw file (round 2) when it is built.

No sub-content was skipped; books are only recorded here.

# STATIC GK — SPORTS: RAW EXTRACTION
Subject: Static GK → Sports (round 1). **RAW EXTRACTION ONLY** — no test cross-referencing yet.

## Sources & page map
| Part | Source | Book pages | PDF pages |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | Parmar SSC Fatman (2nd Ed., 2026) — "Sports" chapter (A.1 Olympics → A.19 Miscellaneous one-liners) | 488–497 | 521–530 |
| B | Lucent's GK (2026) — Miscellany: §53 Youth Affairs & Sports (Sports part: Khelo India, awards, SAI, LNIPE), §54 Olympic Games, §55 Paralympics & Winter Olympics, §56 Commonwealth Games, §57 Asian Games, §58 SAF Games, §59 Cricket, §60 Football, §61 Hockey, §62 Some Important Facts (Volleyball, TT, Basketball, Badminton, Tennis, Polo, Wrestling, Chess, Golf, Water Polo, Baseball, Billiards, Rifle Shooting, Kho-Kho, Swimming, Boxing, Marathon), §63 Cups & Trophies, §64 Famous Stadia, §65 National Games (world), §66 Court/Field, §67 Number of Players, §68 Nicknames, §69–71 Eminent Sportspersons (India / International female / male), §72 Special Awards in Sports + Pension + Welfare Fund, §73 Miscellaneous (mixed topics — see note) + Operation Sindoor box | 465–487 | 478–500 |

## Method / disclosure
Read from native page images; OCR only as cross-check. **Fact-complete, not word-for-word:** every entry, table row, name, number, date and mnemonic mapping is kept; explanatory prose is condensed. Tables (medal tallies, Olympics-at-a-glance, CWG/Asian Games tables, World Cup tables, India's CWG 2022 medallists, awards money, players lists, nicknames, stadia) are reproduced row by row. Spellings are kept **as printed** with [sic]/corrected forms in brackets where the slip is obvious. ⚠ marks doubts, printing slips, cross-book disagreements, and RECENT-EVENT items (2024–2026) that must be verified against primary sources before relying on them.
- **§73 Miscellaneous (Lucent)** is a mixed-topic grab-bag of one-liners; it is transcribed fully in Part B.26 with my own routing tags in [brackets] (Govt / Sci-Tech / Awards / Places / Dance / Music / Intl / Days) so the Round-2 files can point to it instead of re-extracting. The **Operation Sindoor** box (B.27) is likewise a Govt/Defence recent-events item.
- Lucent §49 (UNESCO Kalinga Prize), §51 (Committees) and other non-sports Misc sections are NOT in this file (Round 2).
- Cross-references to other subjects: Sports ↔ Awards (Khel Ratna/Arjuna/Dronacharya, Padma, Special Awards — Round 2 Awards file), Sports ↔ Books (sports personalities' autobiographies — `books_authors_raw_extraction.md` Part A "Sports personalities"), Youth Affairs/Khelo India/TOPS ↔ Govt Policies (Round 2).

## PART A — PARMAR SSC FATMAN (2nd Ed., 2026): "SPORTS" chapter (book pp.488–497, PDF 521–530)
Page running head: "SPORTS". Format: bullet-point revision notes with red-highlighted key terms (not captured, keys kept in text) and grey "fact boxes". Sports-related current-affairs facts (current office-holders, upcoming hosts, latest tallies) are flagged ⚠ as **recent-events items: verify from a current source** before using them.

### A.1 OLYMPICS — History of Olympics (book p.488, left column)
- **Governing body:** International Olympic Committee (**IOC**) → conducts Olympics.
- **Headquarters:** initially **Paris, France** → currently **Lausanne, Switzerland**.
- **Founded in: 1894** by **Pierre de Coubertin (2nd President of IOC)** and **Demetrius Vikelas (1st President of IOC)**.
- **Current President of IOC: Kirsty Coventry** (⚠ recent-events; verify).
- **Official motto:** **"Citius, Altius, Fortius – Communiter"** (Faster, Higher, Stronger – Together).
- **Symbol:** Olympic Rings (**Blue, Yellow, Black, Green, Red**) representing unity of the **five continents**.
- **Total member countries: 206** (⚠ figure as printed; verify).
- **Selection of host cities:** chosen through a **Secret Ballot**.
- Box: **The first Winter Paralympics Games held in Sweden in 1976.**

**Olympic Games Timeline**
- First Summer Olympics: **1896 (Athens, Greece)**.
- First Winter Olympics: **1924 (Chamonix, France)**.
- First Paralympics: **1960 (Rome, Italy)**.
- First Youth Summer Olympics: **2010 (Singapore)**.
- First Youth Winter Olympics: **2012 (Innsbruck, Austria)**.
- Second Olympic Games: **1900 (Paris, France)** → **first women participation** was seen here.
- Box: **2 years gap between Summer and Winter Olympics.**

**Upcoming Olympics Events** (⚠ as printed in 2026 edition; verify)
- 2026 Winter Olympics: **Milan and Cortina (Italy)**.
- 2028 Summer Olympics: **Los Angeles, California, USA**.
- 2030 Winter Olympics: **French Alps (France)**.
- 2032 Summer Olympics: **Brisbane (Australia)**.
- 2034 Winter Olympics: **Salt Lake City (USA)**.

**Hosting Cities**
- Most number of times hosted by: **USA (8 times → 4 Summer and 4 Winter Olympics)**.
- **London: 3 times. Paris: 3 times. Los Angeles: 3 times.**

### A.2 INDIA'S PERFORMANCE IN OLYMPICS (book p.488)
- **1900**: First participation of India in **Paris, France**.
- **First Indian medalist: Norman Pritchard (Anglo-Indian)** → won **2 silver** medals in 1900.
- **1920**: India's first-ever Olympic contingent, a **six-member team**, participated in **Belgium (Antwerp) Olympics** → **First flag bearer: Purma Banerjee**.
- **Total medals won by India: 41 medals** (⚠ figure as printed; verify).
- **10 Gold** (8 medals won by Indian Hockey Team and 2 individual medals won) **+ 10 Silver + 21 Bronze**. (⚠ 10+10+21 = 41 as printed.)

**In Hockey**
- The Indian Men's Hockey team is the most successful team in Olympics with a record of winning **8 gold + 1 silver + 4 bronze** till now (as printed).

**Participation of Indian Men's Hockey Team**
- 1916: No Olympics held due to **World War I (1914–1918)**.
- **1928: Amsterdam** Olympics → Captain of Indian Men's Hockey Team: **Jaipal Singh Munda**. **14 goals** scored by **Major Dhyanchand** ("**Wizard of Hockey**").
- **1932: Los Angeles** Olympics → Won from **US by 24-1**.
- **1936: Berlin** Olympics (Capital: Dhyanchand).
- 1940–1944: No Olympics due to **World War II (1939–1945)**.
- **1948: London** Olympics → Men's Hockey team led by **Kishan Lal**.
- **1952: Helsinki** Olympics → **Balbir Singh Sr.** scored **5 goals** (he is the **first sportsperson to receive Padma Shri** award).
- **1956: Melbourne** → **6th gold** medal won.
- **1960: Rome** → a **silver** medal won.
- **1964: Tokyo** → **7th gold** medal won by defeating **Pakistan**.
- India also won **2 Bronze medals** in: **1968 Mexico** (bronze), **1972 Munich** (bronze).
- **1976: Montreal** → India went home with **no medal**.
- **1980: Moscow** → **8th gold** medal won.

**Individual Gold Medals Won by India in Olympics**
- **2008: Beijing** → **Abhinav Bindra** in **10 m Air Rifle Shooting**.
- **2020: Tokyo** → **Neeraj Chopra** was the **first Indian to win gold in Track and Field** (record: **87.58 m**) on **7th August** (this day is now celebrated as **"National Javelin Day"**).
- Box: The first Winter Paralympics Games held in Sweden in 1976.

**Some Notable Olympic Medalists of India**
- **1952: Helsinki** → First individual bronze medal won by **K.D. Jadhav** in **Wrestling**.
- **1996: Atlanta** → **Leander Paes** won bronze medal in **Tennis**.
- **2000: Sydney** → **Karnam Malleswari** became the **first woman to win medal (bronze) in Olympics in weightlifting**.
- **2004: Athens** → **Silver** medal won by **Rajyavardhan Singh Rathore** in **shooting** (1st Indian to win medal in shooting).
- **2008: Beijing** → **Vijendra Singh** won **bronze** in **boxing** and **Sushil Kumar** won **bronze** in **wrestling**. Box: **Beijing is the first city to host both Summer and Winter Olympics editions.**

**2012 London Olympics**
- **Sushil Kumar**: silver in wrestling. **Vijay Kumar**: silver in shooting. **Saina Nehwal**: bronze — also the **first Indian to win a medal in Badminton**. **Mary Kom**: bronze (printed "silver"; ⚠ printed as "She won a silver medal") — **first Indian woman to win a medal in Boxing**. **Yogeshwar Dutt**: bronze in wrestling. **Gagan Narang**: bronze in **10m air rifle shooting** event.
  ⚠ Book says Mary Kom "won a *silver* medal" at London 2012 — as printed; my general knowledge is that Mary Kom's London 2012 medal was **bronze** and Saina Nehwal's was also bronze. Verify.

**2016 Rio Olympics**: **PV Sindhu** — silver in Badminton. **Sakshi Malik** — bronze in wrestling.

**2020 Tokyo Olympics**
- The phrase **"we have wings"** is associated with the **opening ceremony of the Tokyo 2020 Paralympic Games**.
- **Official mascot of 2020 Tokyo Paralympics: Someity.** Box: Someity is related to: Tokyo Paralympics 2020.
- **India's rank in Tokyo Olympics 2020: 48th.**
- **Mirabai Chanu**: silver in weightlifting. **Lovlina Borgohain**: bronze in boxing. **PV Sindhu**: bronze in badminton — "**double medallist**" at the Olympics. **Bajrang Punia**: bronze in wrestling. **Neeraj Chopra**: **gold** in javelin throw. **Men's Hockey Team**: **bronze**.

**2024 Paris Olympics** (book p.489)
- **Torch bearer: Abhinav Bindra.** **Flag bearer for opening ceremony: Achanta Sharath Kamal (table tennis) and PV Sindhu.** **Flag bearer for closing ceremony: PR Sreejesh and Manu Bhaker.** **Chef de Mission: Gagan Narang.**
- **Bilquis Mir**: the **first Indian woman to be jury member** in Olympics.

**Medals Tally of India in 2024 Paris Olympics** (p.489)
- India sent a contingent of **117 athletes**. India won **6 medals → 1 silver and 5 bronze**. **India's rank: 71 out of 206 nations**.
| Athlete | Event | Sport | Medal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Manu Bhaker | Women's 10m air pistol | Shooting | Bronze |
| Manu Bhaker & Sarabjot Singh | Mixed team 10m air pistol | Shooting | Bronze |
| Swapnil Kusale | Men's 50m rifle 3 positions | Shooting | Bronze |
| Team India | Men's event | Hockey | Bronze |
| Neeraj Chopra | Men's javelin throw | Athletics | Silver |
| Aman Sehrawat | Men's freestyle 57kg | Wrestling | Bronze |

**About Manu Bhaker:** the **first Indian woman to win an Olympic shooting medal**; also the **first Indian woman to win multiple medals in a single edition of the Olympics** (bronze in mixed team 10m pistol event with Sarabjot Singh).

**Medals Tally of India in 2024 Paris Paralympics**: First Paralympics held in **1960 in Rome, Italy**. Total medals won in 2024: **7 gold, 9 silver, 13 bronze → Total medals: 29** (box).
**Gold Medals Won by Athletes — Paralympics**
| Athlete | Event | Sport | Medal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Avani Lekhara | Women's 10m air rifle standing SH1 | Shooting | Gold |
| Nitesh Kumar | Men's single SL3 | Badminton | Gold |
| Sumit Antil | Javelin throw F64 | Athletics | Gold |
| Harvinder Singh | Men's individual recurve open | Archery | Gold |
| Dharambir | Men's club throw F51 | Athletics | Gold |
| Praveen Kumar | Men's high jump T64 | Athletics | Gold |
| Navdeep Singh | Men's javelin throw F41 | Athletics | Gold |

### A.3 INDIAN OLYMPIC ASSOCIATION (IOA) (book p.490)
- Established: **1927**. First President: **Dorabji Tata (1927)**. Second President: **Maharaja Bhupindra Singh (1928)**. Current President: **P.T. Usha** ("Payyoli Express") (⚠ recent-events; verify).
- **P.T. Usha Records in**: Asian Games in **1982 Delhi and 1986 Seoul**; in **1984 Olympics she lost bronze medal by 0.01 seconds**.

**Some Facts on Olympics**
- **Michael Phelps** is the most decorated Olympian of USA — **28 medals (23 golds)**.
- **Cricket** to be included in **2028 Los Angeles Olympics** along with other sports → **Football, Squash, Baseball, Softball, Lacrosse** (⚠ as printed; verify — football is already an Olympic sport).
- **Satish Kumar**: first Indian to participate in a **Super Heavyweight Boxing** event in the **2020 Olympic Games**.
- **Archery** event was hosted at **Lord's Cricket Ground (LCG)** at the **2012 Olympics**.
- **Thomas Hicks**: first **American** male athlete to win the **Marathon gold medal** in the Olympic Games.
- **Marathon Distance: 42.195 km**.
- **South Africa has never hosted Olympics.**
- **1992: Shiny Wilson** became the **first woman to carry India's flag** at the opening ceremony of the Summer Olympic Games.
- **Shabana Akhtar** became the **first female athlete to represent Pakistan in 1996 Atlanta Olympics**.
- Modern Olympic gold medals: major component is **silver, a minimum 92.5% silver**, plated with a minimum of **6 grams of pure gold**.

### A.4 COMMONWEALTH GAMES (book p.490)
- First held in: **1930, Hamilton, Canada**. Not held in **1942 and 1946** (WWII).
- Previously called: **British Empire Games (1930–1950)**.
- Current Head: **King Charles III**.
- Most hosted by: **Australia (5 times)**.
- India hosted in: **2010 (New Delhi)** → India finished **2nd**.
- **2022 Birmingham CWG**: India finished **4th**.
- **2026: Glasgow (Scotland)** to host for the **4th time** (⚠ recent-events).
- Commonwealth ratification: **May 1949**.
- Commonwealth Nations: **56 countries**.

### A.5 ASIAN GAMES (book p.490)
- Also known as **Asiad**; **quadrennial multi-sport** event for athletes from Asia; organised by **Olympic Council of Asia**.
- **First Asian Games: 1951, New Delhi, India.** India also hosted in **1982**.
- Most hosted by: **Thailand (4 times)** and **South Korea (3 times)**.
- **Upcoming Asian Games** (as printed): **2022: Hangzhou, China; 2026: Nagoya, Japan; 2030: Doha, Qatar.** (⚠ the Hangzhou edition was actually held in Sept–Oct 2023 after postponement — the book prints "2022" as the label.)
- Some facts: **Neeraj Chopra** first set the senior national record during the **2016 South Asian Games at Guwahati**. **India won 5 gold medals in 1986 Asian Games held in Seoul, South Korea.** (⚠ figure as printed.)

### A.6 CRICKET — governing bodies and World Cup (book p.490)
- Governing body (international): **ICC**; established as **Imperial Cricket Conference in 1909**, became ICC in **1989**. HQ: **Dubai, UAE**.
- Governing body (India): **BCCI**. HQ: **Mumbai**.
- **First Test Match: 1877** (Australia vs England, in England — ⚠ printed "in England"; first Test was played in Melbourne, Australia per general knowledge — verify). **First ODI: 1971** (Australia vs England, Melbourne Cricket Ground (MCG)).
- Box: **Sports Authority of India (SAI) was set up in 1984.**
- **Cricket World Cup**: First cricket World Cup: **Women (1973, in England)** and **Men (1975, in England) → Winner: West Indies**.
- **India has won World Cup two times: 1983 (under Kapil Dev's captaincy) by defeating West Indies, and 2011 (under MS Dhoni's captaincy) by defeating Sri Lanka.**
- **2023 World Cup (held in India)**: won by **Australia (6th title)** by defeating India. Australia holds the record for the **most ICC Men's Cricket World Cup titles**.
- Box: **Nick names of MS Dhoni: "Captain cool"; "Thala"; "Mahi".**
- **ICC Men's Cricket World Cups hosted by India: 1987 (India and Pakistan jointly hosted); 1996 (India, Sri Lanka and Pakistan jointly hosted).**
### A.6 (continued) CRICKET (book p.491)

Box: ***2027 World Cup: Will be hosted by South Africa, Zimbabwe, Namibia.**

**T20 Cricket World Cup**
- First Edition: **2007** (held in **South Africa** and won by **India**).
- Most titles: **England, West Indies and India (2 each)**.
- **2022**: Held in Australia and won by **England**.
- **India has won two times: in 2007 and 2024.**
- **2024 hosts: USA and West Indies** (India won by defeating **South Africa**).
- **2026 hosts: India and Sri Lanka** (India won by defeating **New Zealand**) (⚠ recent-events item as printed in the 2026 edition; verify).

**Champions Trophy**
- Inaugurated in **1998 (Host Bangladesh), Winner: South Africa**.
- Current Champion: **India (2025)**. Most successful teams: **India (3rd title)** and **Australia (2nd title)** (⚠ recent-events; verify).

**Asia Cup:** 1st held in **1984 (Sharjah, UAE)**. Most won by **India (8 times)**. Current Champion: **India** (⚠ recent-events).

**Dimensions**
- Cricket field shape: generally **oval or circular**.
- Weight of cricket ball: **156–163 g**.
- Cricket pitch length: **22 yards**. Distance between the stumps and the popping crease at the same end: **1.22 m**.
- Wicket (Stump) height: **28 inches**.
- In **ODIs**, a bowler can bowl a maximum of **10 overs**; in **T20**, maximum **4 overs**.
- Ball change is allowed only after **80 overs in test cricket**.
- **Strike rate** = (Total runs scored ÷ Total balls faced) × 100.
- In a test match, a team can play a minimum of **90 overs in a day**.
- Over rate in Test match is **15 overs per hour**; ODI match is **50 overs and 3.5 hrs**.

**Terms Associated**
- "Strike rate" best describes the average number of runs a batter makes per **100 deliveries**.
- If umpire raises an **index finger above the head** → **batsman is out**. If umpire extends **both hands horizontally** → **wide ball**.
- First slip fielding position is **behind the batsman**.
- **"Chinaman"** refers to a **leg-spin delivery by a left-arm bowler**.

**Associated Trophies:** Duleep Trophy; Ranji Trophy; ICC World Cups; ICC Champions Trophy; Asia Cup; Vijay Hazare Trophy & Syed Mushtaq Ali Trophy.

**Facts Associated with Cricket**
- India's ODI debut was in **1971 against England**.
- The **Maharaja of Vizianagaram** was the **first Indian cricketer to serve as President of BCCI**.
- Average circumference of standard cricket ball in international cricket: **8.81 inches–9 inches**.
- **Sachin Tendulkar** scored his first ODI century in his **79th match**; he has also featured in the most no. of international cricket matches.
- **Lala Amarnath** was the **first Test cricket captain of an independent Indian team**.
- **Narendra Modi Cricket Stadium** is the **largest cricket stadium in the world**, located at **Ahmedabad (India)**.

### A.7 FOOTBALL (also known as SOCCER) (book pp.491–492)
- Governing Body: **Fédération Internationale de Football Association (FIFA)**. HQ: **Zurich, Switzerland**. Box: Football World Cup started in **1930**.
- **First FIFA Match: 1872 (England vs Scotland)**. **First FIFA World Cup: 1930 held at Uruguay (winner: Uruguay)**.
- **Most Wins: Brazil (5 times)**. **2022 Winner: Argentina (held at Qatar)**.
- **Upcoming FIFA World Cups** (⚠ verify): **2026 Hosts: Canada, Mexico and USA**; **2030 Hosts: Morocco, Portugal, and Spain**.
- Box: **Indian Football Association was established in 1893.** Football leagues in India: **Indian Super League (ISL)** and 1st Edition was won by **Atletico de Kolkata**.
- **Ball:** circumference **68–70 cm**; weight **410–460 gm**.
- **About the Game:** Minimum players to start a game: **7**. Total no. of players in each team: **11**. Game duration: **90 minutes** (two 45-min halves). Tie-breaker: **30 minutes** (penalty shootout if needed). Substitutes allowed: **3**.
- **Famous Football Players:** India — Sunil Chhetri, Bhaichung Bhutia, P.K. Banerjee. World — Cristiano Ronaldo (Portuguese); Lionel Messi (Argentine); Neymar Jr (Brazilian); Aitana Bonmati (Spain).
- **Field Dimensions:** Length **91–120 m (Standard: 105 m)**; Width **45–91 m (Standard: 68 m)**; Goal post: Width **24 ft (7.32 m)** and Height **8 ft (2.44 m)**; distance between goal posts **7.32 m (24 ft)**; distance between **penalty mark** from midpoint of the goal post: **11 m**. (Field diagram also labels: centre circle radius **9.15 m**; penalty area **16.5 m (18 yds)**; goal area **5.5 m (6 yds)**; corner arc **1 m (1 yd)**.)
- **Cards in Football:** **Yellow card**: foul and misconduct (caution to the player). **Red card**: serious misconduct (player's dismissal from field).
- **Terms Associated:** Dribble (also used in hockey and basketball); Banana Kick, Bicycle Kick.
- **Trophies Associated:** India — **Durand Cup (founded 1888 in Shimla; the oldest football tournament in India)**, **Santosh Trophy**, **Rovers Cup (started in 1891)**. World — **Merdeka Cup, Scissors Cup, La Liga**.
- In football, when the ball strikes the frame of the goal or the referee and remains within the goal and touch lines, it is still in play.
- **Facts:** **Sunil Chhetri** is the second Indian footballer to play 100 international games after **Bhaichung Bhutia**. **Mohammed Salim** was the first player from the Indian subcontinent to play for a European Football club (**Celtic FC**). **Manisha Kalyan** is the first Indian female football player to play in **UEFA** women's champions league. The **Super Bowl**, an annual championship game of Football, is held in **USA**. (⚠ Super Bowl is American football, not association football — as printed.)

### A.8 HOCKEY (book pp.492–493)
- **Field Dimensions:** Length **91.44 m**; Width **55 m**; **Astroturf** is a synthetic surface where hockey is played. **Goalpost size:** Height **2.14 m**, Width **3.66 m**. (Diagram: field 91.40 m × 55 m; 23 m lines; shooting circle; penalty spot; goal post.)
- **Terms Associated:** Dribble; Scoop; Backstick; 16-yard hit.
- **About the Game:** Ball Weight: **156–163 gm**; Players: **11**; Substitutes: **No limit**; Game Duration: **60 minutes (four 15-min quarters)** — earlier a 70-min format with two 30-min halves.
- **Cards in Hockey:** **Green Card**: time penalty of **5 minutes**. **Yellow Card**: time penalty of **10 minutes**. **Red Card**: **permanent expulsion** from the game.
- **Hockey World Cup:** First World Cup: **1971 (Winner: Pakistan)**. Most titles: **Pakistan (4 times)**. **India won it once (1975)** → **hosted hockey 4 times** (most no. of times) (as printed).
- **FIH Hockey5s World Cup:** First Edition held at **2024, Muscat, Oman**. Winners: **Netherlands (Men & Women)**.
- Umpire signals diagram on p.493 (Free Hit, 16-yard hit, Long Hit, Penalty Corner, Advantage, Penalty Stroke, Side-in, Add Ten, Raised Ball, Stick Interference, Advancing, Dangerous Use of Stick, Pushing, Obstruction, No Goal, Third Party Obstruction, Dangerous Play, Time-out, Goal Scored, Bully, Play with Wrong Side of Stick) — names only.
- **Associated Trophies:** Nehru Trophy; Wellington Trophy; Aga Khan Cup; Maharaja Ranjit Singh Cup; Rangaswamy Cup; **Sultan Azlan Shah Cup (hosted in Malaysia)**; **Sultan of Johor Cup (hosted in Malaysia)**; Begum Rasul Trophy; Murugappa Gold Cup; **Beighton Cup (oldest Hockey tournament in India, established 1895)**.
- **Facts:** In **ice hockey**, **6 players** per team on the ice at one time. In hockey, **long corner** is awarded if the ball goes over the back line after last being touched by a defender. **Major Dhyan Chand** is known as **"Wizard of Hockey"**. **Rani Rampal** is the **first woman hockey player to receive the Rajiv Gandhi Khel Ratna Award**. **P. R. Sreejesh** was awarded **"Major Dhyan Chand Khel Ratna Award" in 2021**. The **Birsa Munda International Hockey Stadium**, the largest Hockey stadium in the country, is situated in **Rourkela, Odisha**.

### A.9 TENNIS (book p.493)
- **Field Dimensions:** Length **78 ft (23.77 m)**. Height/width: **27 feet (8.23 m) for singles and 36 feet (10.97 m) for doubles** (printed "Height"). **Net height: 3 feet (0.914 m) in the centre and 3 feet 6 inches (1.07 m) at the posts.**
- **Tennis Grand Slam Tournaments:**
| Tournament | Played in | First played on | Type of court |
|---|---|---|---|
| Wimbledon | July | 1877 | Grass |
| US Open | Aug–Sep | 1881 | Hard court |
| French Open | May–June | 1891 | Clay court |
| Australian Open | January | 1905 | Hard court |
- **Famous Players:** Roger Federer (Switzerland); Rafael Nadal (Spain); Leander Paes, Sania Mirza, Mahesh Bhupati, Rohan Bopanna, Vijay Amritraj, Anand Amritraj. Box: The French Open also known as **Roland-Garros**. Tennis was first introduced in the Olympics in **1896**.
- **Terms Associated:** **Deuce** (equality in points → 40-40); **Advantage Out** (score when the receiver wins the next point after deuce); **Love** (score of zero). Box: In tennis, **6-4 score is a complete set**. **Associated Trophies: Davis Cup**.
- **Facts:** **Leander Paes and Mahesh Bhupathi** became the first two Indians as a pair to reach the finals of all Grand Slams in a year in the Open era, winning the French Open and Wimbledon as top seeds. Box: **Leander Paes is the first Indian and Asian tennis player to win an Olympic medal** (⚠ as printed; the book's "first Indian" claim vs K.D. Jadhav's wrestling bronze — box is specific to tennis).
- (Court diagram: 78 feet (23.77 m); 36 ft (10.97 m); 27 ft (8.23 m); service box 21 ft (6.4 m); 4 ft 6 in (1.37 m) doubles alley.)
### A.10 TABLE TENNIS (book p.494)
- Also known as **"Ping Pong"**.
- **Dimensions:** Table **9 ft (2.74 m) long × 5 ft (1.525 m) wide × 2.5 ft (76 cm) high**. **Net height: 15.25 cm** (diagram: net overhang 15.25 cm).
- **Tournaments:** World Table Tennis Championships; ITTF World Cup; Olympic Games; Asian Games.
- **Ball Specifications:** Diameter **40 mm**; Weight **2.7 g**; Colour **White or orange** (matte finish).
- **Associated Trophies: Corbillon Cup.**
- **Famous Players:** **Achanta Sharath Kamal** (veteran, Arjuna Awardee); **Manika Batra** (India's top-ranked female TT player; also won bronze medal in the Asian Cup); **Sathiyan Gnanasekaran**; **Sreeja Akula**.
- **Facts:** period during which a ball is in play = **Rally**. **Unlimited lets** allowed in a row on a serve. **Shake hand grip** is used. First edition of the **World Para Table Tennis Championships** held in **Assen, Netherlands (1990)**.

### A.11 BADMINTON (book pp.494)
- **Governed by: Badminton World Federation (BWF).** Initially known as **"Poona"**. Box: **Badminton was introduced in Olympics in 1992.**
- **Court Dimensions:** Length **44 ft (13.40 m)**; Width **20 ft (6.10 m)**; **Net height 5 ft in middle and 5 ft 1 inch at the edges of the court.** (Diagram: 13.4 m (44 ft); service courts 6.1 m; etc.)
- **Shuttlecock weight: 4.74–5.50 g.**
- **Game format:** best of 3 games, each up to **21 points**; in case of deuce, win by at least 2 points, unless the score reaches **29-29**, in which case the next point decides the winner.
- **Cards:** **Yellow card**: official warning. **Red card**: fault (opponent is awarded a point). **Black card**: disqualification (for severe misconduct).
- **Associated Tournaments:** **Thomas Cup (China)**: Men's international team championship (1st Edition: **1949**); **Uber Cup (China)**: Women's international team championship; **Syed Modi International Badminton Championships (India)**; Narang Cup; Maharaja Ranjit Singh Gold Cup; Lady Ratan Tata Trophy; Yonex Cup. **India has won Thomas Cup only once (2022).**
- **Facts:** **Saina Nehwal** won the **first badminton medal** in the Olympics games from India in **2012 London Olympics**. India will organise the **World Badminton Championship event 2026** (⚠ as printed; verify). **Pullela Gopichand Badminton Academy** founded in **Hyderabad (2008)**. **K. Srikanth** is associated with Badminton.

### A.12 BALL BADMINTON (book p.494)
- Racket sport played with a **yellow ball**. **Origin: Tamil Nadu, India.**

### A.13 BASKETBALL (book pp.494–495)
- Basketball (India) governed by: **Basketball Federation of India (BFI)**.
- **Terms Associated:** Dribble; Pivot; Double dribble.
- **Court Dimensions:** Shape: rectangle; Length **94 feet (28.65 m)**; Height (width): **50 ft wide (15.24 m)**. (Diagram: court 28 m × 15 m; 3-point line; free throw lane (key); centre line; basket height/measurements as printed — labels small.)
- **Weight of the ball:** between **20 and 22 ounces (567 and 624 g)**.
- **Total players:** each team has **5 players** (a total of 10 players on the court).
- **Facts:** maximum points in a shot: **4** (printed; ⚠ standard maximum for a single shot is 3 points — as printed). **Free throw = 1 point.** The Basketball centre line is part of the **Back court**. A **FIBA**-sanctioned game typically consists of **4 quarters of 10 minutes** each.

### A.14 KABADDI (book p.495)
- **Team size: 7 players** on court (up to **5 substitutes**).
- **Match duration:** Men: **2 halves × 20 minutes (with 5-minute break)**; Women & Juniors: same duration.
- **Court Dimensions (Standard Kabaddi):** Men's Court **13 m × 10 m**; Women's Court **12 m × 8 m**. (Diagram lines: end line, baulk line, bonus line, middle line, lobby; 13000 × 10000 mm.)
- **Cards in Kabaddi:** **Green Card**: warning. **Yellow Card**: 2-minute suspension. **Red Card**: player is out of the match.
- **Terms Associated:** Raider; defender; raid; tackle; all-out; bonus; attack line; lona; bauk line.
- **Associated Tournaments:** **Pro Kabaddi League (PKL)**: franchise-based Indian league. **Kabaddi World Cup**: organised by **International Kabaddi Federation (IKF)**. **Asian Games Kabaddi**: introduced in **1990**. South Asian Games. **Senior National Kabaddi Championship**: held annually in India.
- **Facts:** The **first winner of the Arjuna Award in Kabaddi** is **Sahanand Mahadev Shetty**. The **Pro Kabaddi League** founded in India in **2014** (**Jaipur Pink Panthers** won the first season).

### A.15 KHO KHO (book p.495)
- **Origin: India.** Governing Body (India): **Kho Kho Federation of India (KKFI)**. Recognised by: **Sports Authority of India & School Games Federation**. Played between **two teams of 12 players (9 take the field at a time)**.
- **Kho Kho World Cup 2025:** Theme: **Speed, Agility, and Teamwork**. Mascot: **Tejas and Tara**.
- **Terms Associated:** Inning.

### A.16 BILLIARDS (book p.495)
- Ball weight: **160–170 g**. Famous players: **Pankaj Advani, Sourav Kothari**.

### A.17 CHESS (book pp.495–496)
- **Governing body: FIDE – Fédération Internationale des Échecs (World Chess Federation)**. Established in **1924**. HQ: **Lausanne, Switzerland**. Governing body (India): **All India Chess Federation (AICF)**. HQ: **New Delhi**.
- **Basic Setup:** Board **8 × 8 grid = 64 squares**. Each player starts with **16 pieces**: **1 King** (one square any direction); **1 Queen** (diagonal/horizontal/vertical); **2 Rook** (horizontal/vertical); **2 Bishop** (diagonal); **2 Knight** ('L-shape'); **8 Pawn** (one square forward; two on first move).
- **Terms Associated:** Stalemate; Checkmate; Gambit; Grandmaster (GM); Castling.
- **Associated Tournament: FTX Crypto Cup.** Box: **First woman GM: S. Vijayalakshmi** (printed "S. Vikayalakshmi"; ⚠ spelling as printed).
- **Famous Players:** **Viswanathan Anand**: 5-time World Champion, **India's first GM (1988)**; also the **1st recipient of Rajiv Gandhi Khel Ratna Award**. **Dibyendu Barua**: India's 2nd Grandmaster after Anand. **R Praggnanandhaa**: young prodigy; reached **2023 FIDE World Cup final**. **Gukesh D**: among youngest Super Grandmasters, broke into world top 10. **Top women players:** **Koneru Humpy** (1st Indian Grandmaster in Women's Chess to become World Rapid Champion); Tania Sachdev; Bhakti Kulkarni; R Vaishali. **Magnus Carlsen**: Norwegian Chess Grandmaster.
- Box: **The current World Chess Champion is Gukesh Dommaraju. He won the title by defeating Ding Liren in the 2024 World Chess Championship in Singapore. Gukesh became the youngest World Chess Champion ever at 18 years old.** (⚠ recent-events; verify.)
- **Facts:** **P. Harikrishna** was the first Indian to win the **Under-10 World Chess Championship** in **Menorca, Spain** in **1996** (as printed).

# PART B — LUCENT'S GENERAL KNOWLEDGE (2026): "Miscellany" — Youth Affairs & Sports; §54 Olympic Games onward (book pp.465–487, PDF 478–500)

### B.1 §53 YOUTH AFFAIRS AND SPORTS — SPORTS SECTION (book p.465, PDF 478)
(Top-left: tail of the Youth Affairs text — "…various dimensions of youth development, engaging in seminal research in the vital areas of youth development and conducting training/capacity building programmes in youth development, besides the extension and outreach initiatives across the country." — cross-ref Govt Policies extraction for the Youth Affairs body text.)

**Sports (intro)** — sports seen as integral to all-round development of personality; means of entertainment and physical fitness; generate spirit of healthy competition and bonding; achievements a source of national pride.

**Khelo India**
- Ministry of Youth Affairs and Sports implemented a Centrally Sponsored Scheme **Panchayat Yuva Krida aur Khel Abhiyan (PYKKA)** from **2008-09 to 2013-14**.
- A Central Sector Scheme **"Khelo India – National Programme for Development of Sports" (Khelo India Scheme)** was implemented in **2016-17**.
- Aim: creation of basic sports infrastructure at **village and Block Panchayat levels** through development and maintenance of playgrounds, and mass participation via annual sports competitions.
- Separately, **Urban Sports Infrastructure Scheme (USIS)** introduced in **2010-11**, continued up to **2015-16**.
- Khelo India = the **new umbrella scheme** after merger of on-going schemes **Rajiv Gandhi Khel Abhiyan (RGKA), USIS and National Sports Talent Search System Programme (NSTSSP)**.
- **Incentive Schemes for Sports persons:** various schemes to give incentives to sportspersons to take up sports are also implemented.

**National sports awards (Lucent text)**
| Award | Instituted | Purpose / eligibility | Cash / other | Limit |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Major Dhyan Chand Khel Ratna Award** | scheme launched **1991-92** | honour sportspersons to enhance general status, dignity and place of honour in society; for the most spectacular and outstanding performance over a period of **four years** immediately preceding the year of the award | **₹25 lakh (earlier ₹7.5 lakh)** | — |
| **Arjuna Award** | **1961** | consistently good performance for previous **four years** at international level with excellence for the year recommended, plus leadership, sportsmanship and sense of discipline | statuette, certificate, ceremonial dress and **₹15 lakh (earlier ₹5 lakh)** | **not more than 15** awards per year |
| **Dhyan Chand Award for Life Time Achievement** | **2002** | honour sportspersons who contributed by performance and continue to contribute to promotion of sports after retirement | statuette, certificate, ceremonial dress, **₹10 lakh (earlier ₹5 lakh)** | **not more than 3** per year |
| **Dronacharya Award** | **1985** | eminent coaches who successfully trained sportspersons/teams to achieve outstanding results in international competitions | statuette of Guru Dronacharya, certificate, ceremonial dress; **₹15 lakh in life-time category, ₹10 lakh in regular category** | **not more than 5** per year |
(⚠ Cross-book: Parmar's Sports chapter says P. R. Sreejesh got the "Major Dhyan Chand Khel Ratna" in 2021 (as printed there); the Awards raw file will hold Parmar's awards chapter — compare cash amounts there.)

**Sports Authority of India (SAI)**
- Established **1984** as a registered society, primarily to ensure effective maintenance and optimum utilisation of the sports infrastructure built in **Delhi during ASIAD 1982**.
- Now the **nodal agency** for broadbasing sports and for training of sportspersons to achieve excellence in national and international sports.
- **Society for National Institute of Physical Education and Sports (SNIPES)** merged with SAI in **1987**. Subsequently **Netaji Subhash National Institute of Sports (NSNIS), Patiala** and its allied centres at **Bengaluru, Kolkata and Gandhinagar**, and **Lakshmibai National College of Physical Education at Thiruvananthapuram** came under SAI.
- Regional centres now at **Bengaluru, Gandhinagar, Kolkata, Sonepat, Bhopal, Imphal, Chandigarh and Lucknow**. SAI also operates a **High Altitude Training Centre (HATC) at Shillaroo (HP)**.

**Lakshmibai National Institute of Physical Education (LNIPE)**
- Established initially as a college on **August 17, 1957** (centenary year of the first war of Independence). Located at **Gwalior (MP)**.
- Upgraded to **"Deemed University" in 1995**; fully funded by Government of India; attracts students from abroad.
- **North East Campus at Guwahati (Assam)** functioning since **2010-11**.

### B.2 §54 OLYMPIC GAMES (book pp.465–470, PDF 478–483)

**Ancient Games (p.465):** first held in honour of the Greek god **Zeus in 776 BC** in the plain of the kingdom of **Elis**, in a lush valley between the **Alpheus River and Mount Kronion**, 15 km from the Ionian Sea. Oldest sanctuary of Greece was there — the altar of the Great Mother of Gods, **Rhea (Earth)**.

**Modern Olympic Games — symbols (pp.465–466)**
- **Olympic Symbol:** five rings or circles linked together — sporting friendship of all people. Rings also symbolise the continents: Europe, Asia, Africa, Australia and America. Each ring a different colour: **blue, yellow, black, green and red**. Colour ↔ continent as printed: **Africa (black), America (red), Asia (yellow), Australia (green), Europe (Blue)**.
- **Olympic Flag:** created **1913** at suggestion of **Baron Pierre de Coubertin**; solemnly inaugurated in **Paris in June 1914**; raised over an Olympic stadium for the first time at the **Antwerp Games (Belgium) in 1920**. There is also a second flag used for the **Winter Games**. Made of **white silk** with the five intertwined rings; left to right: **blue, yellow, black, green and red**. (printed "riased" — typo.)
- **Olympic Flame:** first ceremonially lighted and burned in a giant torch at the stadium entrance at the **Amsterdam Games 1928**. Modern version (relay) adopted at **1936 Berlin Games**. Symbolises continuity between ancient and modern Games. Torch first lit by the **sun's rays at Olympia, Greece**, carried by relay of runners; ships and planes when necessary. **July 15, 1976**: space-age technology used to transport the flame from one continent to another.
- **Olympic Motto:** **"Citius–Altius–Fortius"** (faster, higher, stronger). **Rev. Father Didon (1840–1900)**, headmaster of a school near Paris and promoter of sports in French Catholic colleges, first used the motto (embroidered on pennants of his school clubs). Adopted at suggestion of his friend **Baron Pierre de Coubertin** (revived the Games nearly 1,500 years after the last ancient Games) at the International congress for the "Study and Propagation of the Principles of Amateurism" on **June 23, 1894** — same day the restoration of the Olympic Games and creation of the **IOC** were decided.
- **Olympic Prizes, Medals and Certificates:** ancient heroes got a crown of olive branches. Modern: **Gold (1st), Silver (2nd), Bronze (3rd)**; all athletes ranking **first to sixth** get a certificate. Each medal **60 mm diameter, 3 mm thick**. First and second place medals made of **92.5% silver**; the first-place medal is plated with **6 gram of fine gold** (not full gold). Third is bronze.

**Facts list (pp.466)**
- Olympic games started **776 B.C.** on **Mount Olympus** in honour of Greek God **Zeus** (⚠ as printed here: "Mount Olympus"; the para above on p.465 says plain of Elis / Mount Kronion — internal wording difference).
- **Modern Olympics started at Athens, Greece, on 6th April 1896**, with efforts by **Pierre de Coubertin of France**.
- Organised every **four years**. Flag recognised in **1913**, hoisted first time at **Antwerp 1920**.
- Tradition of lighting the Olympic flame started at **Amsterdam 1928**. Head Office of **IOC: Lusane [Lausanne] (Switzerland)** (printed "Lusane").
- Participation of **women** started in the **Second Olympic Games in 1900**.
- First Indian player in the Olympics: Anglo-Indian **Norman Pritchard** (printed "Prichard" here, "Pritchard" below). **Nilima Ghose** — 1st Indian woman participant, **15th Olympic Games 1952 at Helsinki**.
- **IOC founded 1894 at "Chakhon"** (⚠ as printed; the usual spelling/venue is Paris (Sorbonne) — verify). In the inaugural ceremony, team of **Greece** first, **host team last** in the March Past; others in alphabetical order of English alphabet.
- First woman referee in football: Canadian lady **Sonia Denancord** (Atlanta Olympics) (⚠ spelling as printed; not independently verified).
- Maximum gold medal winner sportswoman: **Larina Lavyanina [Larisa Latynina]** — **18 medals including 9 gold** (as printed).
- "Maximum gold medal winner sports woman" (second claim): **Christina Otty [Kristin Otto]** — **6 gold in swimming at Seoul Olympic of 1986 [sic — Seoul was 1988]** (⚠ printed "1986"; Seoul Olympics were held 1988).
- Maximum gold medal winner male: **Michael Phelps (USA)** — **8 gold in swimming at Beijing 2008**.
- **London** — first city to stage the Olympic Games **thrice**, after 1908 and 1948 (Summer) — i.e. 2012 was third.
- London 2012 inaugural ceremony had an Indian flavour: music composers **Ilayaraja and A. R. Rahman**.
- **Michael Phelps**: greatest Olympian of all time, **28 Olympic medals**, of which **6 each from London and Rio**; all-time record for Gold Medals (**23**); **5 Gold and 1 Silver in Rio 2016**.

**India in Olympics: 1900–2024 (pp.466–467)**
- India first represented at **1900 Paris**. Sprinter **Norman Pritchard**, the country's only athlete, won **two silver medals in men's 200 m and 200 m hurdles**.
- Men's hockey: **eight gold medals** (more than any other NOC); includes **six consecutive golds 1928–1956**.
- **K. D. (Khashaba Dadasaheb) Jadhav** — country's first individual medal in **Wrestling, 1952 Helsinki** (printed "first medal in Wrestling").
- Men's hockey: **silver at 1960 Rome** (lost final to **Pakistan**); reclaimed title at **Tokyo 1964**; **bronze in 1968 and 1972**; missed podium at **Montreal 1976**; **eighth hockey gold at Moscow 1980**. Low point at **London 2012** — lost all five group games.

**More India-in-Olympics facts (p.467, top)**
- Indian **women's hockey team** has competed at the Olympics only **twice**: placed **fourth on debut, round-robin tournament, 1980 Moscow**; then finished **bottom of Group B at Rio 2016**.
- Until then **Abhinav Bindra** (shooter) was the only Indian to have won Olympic **gold in a sport other than hockey** — **men's 10 m air rifle, 2008 Beijing**. Bindra finished **fourth** in the same event at **Rio 2016** (his **fifth** Games).
- Olympic appearance record for India: tennis player **Leander Paes — seven consecutive editions 1992–2016**. Best result: **bronze in men's singles, 1996 Atlanta**.

### B.4 RIO 2016 / TOKYO 2020 / PARIS 2024 (book p.468, PDF 481)
**Rio 2016**
- India won **two medals**. Freestyle wrestler **Sakshi Malik** — bronze, women's **58 kg**, the **fifth** time an Indian competitor had finished on the podium in wrestling. **PV Sindhu** — silver, women's singles, following **Saina Nehwal** who won India's first Olympic medal in Badminton — a **bronze in women's singles at London 2012**.
- Oldest Indian athlete: **Leander Paes (43 years)**; youngest Indian athlete: **Jisna Mathew (17 years)**.

**Tokyo 2020 (32nd)**
- **P. V. Sindhu**, silver in Rio, bagged **Bronze in Badminton in Tokyo 2020** — first Indian woman athlete to bag a silver medal in Olympics ever (as printed; Lucent's wording: "She has become the first Indian women athlete to bag the silver medal in Olympics ever" — refers to Rio).
- India ranked **48th** with **7 medals (1 Gold, 2 Silver, 4 Bronze)**.
- Tokyo Games inaugurated by **IOC President Thomas Bach** and the **Emperor of Japan** on **July 23, 2021**.
- **Miraitowa** — a type of robot — was the **mascot of the Tokyo Summer Olympic Games 2020**.

**PARIS OLYMPICS 2024 (July 26–August 11, 2024)**
- India ranked **71st** in the medals tally with **6 medals (1 Silver and 5 Bronze)** in the **33rd Olympics**, Paris (France).
- **Paris 2024: An Overview** — Date **26 July–11 August**; Host **France**; Participants/Athletes **10,714**; Teams **204 + EOR + AIN**; Events **329**; Opening ceremony duration **4 hours**; Opening ceremony **directed by Thomas Jolly**.
- Spectators on the banks of the Seine on 26 July: **3,26,000**, of which **2,22,000 free ticket holders** and **1,04,000 paying ticket holders**.
- Milestone: **first ever inauguration ceremony held outside a stadium**; the IOC had to amend **Article 54.1** of its Olympic charter.
- **Opening Ceremony:** inaugurated by French President **Emmanuel Macron** on **26 July 2024**, Paris (on the bank of the **Seine**). **78** Indian athletes and officials in the opening ceremony. Indian contingent led by **two flag-bearers — two-time Olympic medallist PV Sindhu and table tennis legend A. Sharath Kamal**.
- **The Phryges** — official mascots for Paris Olympics and Paralympics 2024: little figures modelled after traditional **Phrygian hats**, a symbol of the French Republic. The Olympic Phryge is a blue, white and red cap-like figure with the golden Paris 2024 logo across its chest (colours of the French flag). Paralympics mascot design largely the same but features a **prosthetic leg**. Aligned with "Paris 2024's vision" to "demonstrate that sport can change lives"; play "a major role by leading a revolution through sport". **Motto of the Phryges:** "Alone we go faster, but together we go further".
- **The Phrygian cap:** design/name chosen to represent allegorical figures of the French republic and symbols of freedom; Phrygian caps became symbols of **liberty during the French Revolution (1789–1799)**; also a sign of the French Republic — worn by the figure **Marianne** (embodiment of *l'espirit Francais*) in government buildings and public spaces; appear on French **coins and stamps**. Name derived from the ancient country of **Phrygia in Anatolia**; represented in ancient Greek art as headdress of people in Anatolia and regions to the east.

(Phrygian cap, p.469 top: "It is associated with the national allegorical figure of **Liberté** (Liberté, Egalité, Fraternité was the clarion call of the French Revolution and the founding principles of the French Republic)." — Source cited: *The Hindu*, July 27, 2024.)

### B.5 XXXIII OLYMPICS 2024 — LIST OF INDIA'S MEDAL WINNERS (book p.469, PDF 482)
- India ended with **six medals and no gold**, one less than Tokyo 2020.
- **Flag bearers at the Paris 2024 closing ceremony: Manu Bhaker and P.R. Sreejesh** (Indian Olympic medallists).
- Contributors: **Neeraj Chopra, Manu Bhaker, Sarabjot Singh, Swapnil Kusale, Aman Sehrawat and the Indian hockey team** — 1 silver + 5 bronze; **71st** place on the medals tally.
- Many near misses: **Lakshya Sen** (Badminton), **Saikhom Mirabhai Chanu** (Weightlifting), **Vinesh Phogat's** disqualification from the wrestling final.

| Medallist | Event | Medal | Details (as printed) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Neeraj Chopra** | Men's Javelin Throw | **Silver** | Lost to Pakistan's **Arshad Nadeem**, who bettered an Olympic record that had stood **16 years**, twice: first throw **92.97 m** (his second throw of the competition) = gold; second **91.79 m** (final attempt). Chopra's qualification throw **89.34 m** (biggest of season); second throw of the final **89.45 m** = silver. |
| **Manu Bhaker** | Women's **10 m air pistol** | **Bronze** | First Indian woman shooter on the Olympic podium; first Indian ever to win **twin medals in a single edition** of the Games. |
| **Manu Bhaker & Sarabjot Singh** | **10 m air pistol mixed team** | **Bronze** | Beat **South Korea 16–10** in the bronze medal match. |
| **Indian Men's Hockey team** | Field Hockey | **Bronze** | Bronze match vs **Spain** (Aug 8, 2024, Yves du Manoir Stadium, Paris). In the last half century India had not secured two consecutive Olympic medals; retained bronze in Paris (after Tokyo). **India's fourth bronze and 13th Olympic medal in hockey** (as printed: "fourth bronze and 13th Olympic medal") — a farewell for goalkeeper **P.R. Sreejesh**, who closed his two-decade-long international career. |
| **Sarabjot Singh** | see mixed team above | Bronze | World No. 14; hails from **Haryana**; individual and mixed team **World junior champion in 2021**; three World Cup gold medals in 2023 and 2024 (including one in **Munich in June**) apart from two Asian Games medals "last year". |
| **Swapnil Kusale** | **50 m rifle 3 positions** | **Bronze** (first Olympic; **third medal for India** in Paris) | Three positions across three stages — **20 shots each in kneeling, prone and standing**. |
| **Aman Sehrawat** | **57 kg freestyle wrestling** | **Bronze** | India's **youngest-ever Olympic medallist** (less than a month after his 21st birthday); beat Puerto Rico's **Darian Cruz 13–5**; **U-23 world champion**; India's lone male wrestler to qualify for the Paris Games. Indians have won wrestling medals at every Olympics since **2008** — streak unbroken. |

**Medals missed by a whisker (fourth-place finishes; six):**
1. **Arjun Babuta** — Men's 10 m Air Rifle Shooting; 2. **Dhiraj Bommadevara and Ankita Bhakat** — Mixed Team Archery; 3. **Manu Bhakar** — Women's 25 m Pistol Shooting; 4. **Lakshya Sen** — Men's Badminton Singles; 5. **Anantjeet Singh Naruka and Maheshwari Chauhan** — Skeet mixed team shooting; 6. **Saikhom Mirabai Chanu** — Women's Weightlifting 49 kg.

**Vinesh Phogat's disqualification (pp.469–470)**
- In her **third Olympics**; stunned **Japan's Yui Susaki** (World and Olympic champion) on her way to the **women's 50 kg final**; **disqualified after weighing 100 grams more** than permissible in the morning weigh-in ahead of the final.
- (p.470) She **appealed to the Court of Arbitration for Sports (CAS)** demanding a **joint silver medal**, and announced her **retirement** from the sport. Source cited: *The Hindu*, Aug 12, 2024. (⚠ Outcome of the CAS appeal is a recent-event item not stated in Lucent; verify.)

### B.7a §55 PARALYMPICS AND WINTER OLYMPICS (book pp.470–471, PDF 483–484)
- **First Games for disabled athletes: 1948, Stoke Mandeville, England**. On the day of the Opening Ceremony of the 1948 Olympic Games in London, the Stoke Mandeville Games were launched — first competition for **wheelchair-bound** athletes.
- Olympic-style Games for athletes with disability: first organised in **Rome in 1960**, immediately after the Olympic Games — considered the **first Paralympic Games**.
- Since then every four years, always in the same year as the Olympic Games.
- Other disability groups added in **Toronto (Canada) in 1976**; idea of merging different disability groups conceived. In the same year the **first Paralympic Winter Games took place in Sweden**.

**India in Paralympics**
- Most successful campaign: **2024 Paris — record-breaking 29 medals: 7 gold, 9 silver, 13 bronze.**
- **1968 Tel Aviv, Israel** — maiden appearance; delegation of **10 athletes (8 men + 2 women)**; no medals.
- **1972 Heidelberg (Germany)** — first success: para-swimmer **Murlikant Petkar** gold in **50 m freestyle swimming**, **world record 37.331 seconds**; India's only medal; finished **24th of 42** nations.
- India did not take part in **1976 and 1980**. **1984** Games: **four medals — 2 silver + 2 bronze**: **Bhimrao Kesarkar** silver in Men's Javelin Throw; **Joginder Singh Bedi** silver in Men's Shot Put plus two bronzes in Men's Javelin Throw and Men's Discus Throw.
- **2004 Athens** (20 years later): **Devendra Jhajharia** gold Men's Javelin Throw; **Rajinder Singh Rahelu** bronze Men's Powerlifting **56 kg** — 2 medals.
- **2012 London:** **Girisha N Gowda** — lone silver. **2016 Rio:** four medals. **2020 Tokyo:** **19 medals**.
- **Paris 2024 Paralympic Games:** **August 28 to September 8, 2024**; **4,400 athletes**, **22 sports**; hosted across some of Paris's …(continues on p.471)
  (p.471 continues:) …Paris's most iconic venues, including the **Eiffel Tower, Château de Versailles and the Grand Palais**.
- For India, Paris 2024 Paralympics = most successful Games; **record 84 athletes in 12 sports**; supported by **Khelo India** and the **Target Olympic Podium Scheme (TOPS)** — core group athletes receive an **Out-of-Pocket Allowance (OPA) of ₹50,000 per month**, plus full funding for training plans approved by the **Mission Olympic Cell (MOC)**.
- Medal highlights (as printed): **Avani Lekhara** — gold, **Women's 10 m Air Rifle Standing**; **Nitesh Kumar** — gold, **Men's Singles badminton**; **Sumit Antil** — gold, **Men's Javelin Throw**; **Dharambir** — gold, **Men's Club Throw**; **Harvinder Singh** — gold, **Men's Individual Recurve Open** (archery); **Navdeep Singh** — gold, **Men's Javelin Throw** (F41 implied; not printed); **Suhas Yathiraj** — silver, Men's Singles Badminton; **Nishad Kumar** — silver, Men's High Jump; **Rakesh Kumar and Sheetal Devi** — bronze, Archery **Mixed Team Compound Open**. Source cited: MoYES, PIB, 09.09.2024. (⚠ Lucent's paragraph names 9 medallists but the total is 29 — it is a highlights list only.)

### B.8 WINTER OLYMPIC GAMES (book pp.471–472, PDF 484–485)
- Started in **1924 AD**; first Games at **Chamonix, France**, followed by **St. Moritz, Switzerland (1928 & 1948); Lake Placid, New York (1932 & 1980); Garmisch-Partenkirchen, Germany (1936); Oslo, Norway (1952); Cortina d'Ampezzo, Italy (1956); Squaw Valley, California (1960); Innsbruck, Austria (1964 & 1976); Grenoble, France (1968); Sapporo, Japan (1972); Sarajevo, Yugoslavia (1984); Calgary, Canada (1988); Albertville, France (1992)**. **XVII Winter Games: Lillehammer (Norway), February 1994** — first in accordance with the IOC's new cycle of Winter and Summer Games **two years apart** (instead of same year, as had been tradition since 1924).
- **XIX Winter Games — Salt Lake City (USA), February 2002**: **Germany** topped with **35 medals (12 Gold)**; **Norway** runner-up **24 medals (11 Gold)**.
- **XX — Turin (Italy), February 2006**: Germany again topped the tally. Four-member Indian team led by **luge athlete Shiva Keshavan**.
- **XXI — Vancouver, Canada, Feb 12–28, 2010**: 2700 players of 82 countries.
- **XXII — Sochi (Russia), Feb 7–23, 2014**: 17-day, costliest Olympics ever (**$51 billion**); three Indian athletes: luger **Shiva Keshawan** (fifth consecutive participation), Alpine skier **Himanshu Thakur**, cross-country skier **Nadeem Iqbal**.
- **XXIII — Pyeong Chang (S. Korea), 9–25 Feb 2018**: inaugurated Feb 9 at Pyeong Chang Olympic Plaza; two Indian athletes: **Jagdish Singh** (Cross-Country Skiing, Men's 15 km Free) and **Shiva Keshavan** (Luge, Men's Singles).
- **XXIV — Beijing 2022 overview:** Date **4–20 February 2022**; Host **China**; Athletes **2,834**; Teams **91**; Events **109**. **India at 2022:** competitors **1 (1 man, 0 women)**; flag bearer (opening) **Arif Khan**; flag bearer (closing) **Volunteer**; Medals: none (G-0, S-0, B-0, Total-0).

| Germany | 12 | 10 | 5 | 27 |

### B.9 §56 COMMONWEALTH GAMES (book p.472, PDF 485)
- After Olympics, the **second largest** sports festival in the world; held **once in four years, only in between the Olympic years**. Originally known as the **British Empire Games**.
- **1st: 1930 at Hamilton, Canada**.
- **10th: Christchurch, New Zealand, 1974**; **15th: Victoria (Canada), 1994** — about **3,350 athletes from 64 nations** (including **South Africa**, which rejoined the family of Commonwealth athletes after **36 years**).
- **Namibia** (independent 1990) made its debut; **Hong Kong** made its final appearance before being ceded to China in **1997**.
- India first participated in the **second** Commonwealth Games, **London 1934**.

### B.10 §57 ASIAN GAMES (book pp.473–474, PDF 486–487)
- **First Asian Games began March 4, 1951 in New Delhi.**
- The Asian Games Association chose **shining sun** as its symbol.
- **AGF (Asian Games Federation)** adopted **'Ever Onward'** — given by **Pt. Jawaharlal Nehru** — as the motto.
- Emblem: a **'bright full rising sun' with interlocking rings**.
- The **Maharaja of Patiala** presented the **Torch and the Flag** for the first Asian Games; since then carried from country to country.

**XIX Asian Games (Sept 23–Oct 8, 2023) — Hangzhou (p.474)**
- 19th Asian Games held in **Hangzhou, Zhejiang Province, China** from **Sept 23 to Oct 8, 2023**. Originally scheduled **10–25 September 2022**; postponed to 2023 on **6 May 2022** (COVID-19); new dates announced **19 July 2022**. Hangzhou is the **third Chinese city** to host the Asian Games after **Beijing 1990** and **Guangzhou 2010**.
- **Emblem: "Tides Surging"** — two parts; designed to resemble a hand fan, a running track, the **Qiantang River** and radio waves (wireless connectivity). Organising committee: reflects "the great cause of socialism with Chinese characteristics gathering momentum in the new era" and "the unity, solidarity and development of the OCA".
- **Mascots:** group of robots proclaiming host city's heritage and vibrancy — **Congcong, Lianlian and Chenchen**, collectively **"Memories of Jiangnan"**, unveiled **3 April 2020**; robotic superheroes originating from the **Archaeological Ruins of Liangzhu City, the West Lake and the Grand Canal** respectively.
- **Slogan:** **"Heart to Heart, @Future"** — announced **15 December 2019** to mark **1,000 days** before the opening ceremonies; symbolises connectivity between Asian countries.
- **Theme song:** **"With You and Me"** — released with music video **27 April 2023**; performed by **Angela Zhang, Jackson Wang, Sunnee and JC-T**; selected via a global competition; originally created by a team from Thailand, **Nadao Music**; original title **"Let's Celebrate" or "Puean Kan" in Thai**, sung by Ice Paris, Billkin, PP Krit, Nana Sawanya.
- **Disciplines** (printed "Desciplines"): Competitive Sports – **24**, Ball Sports – **18**, Adversary Sports – **9**, Water Sports – **10**.
- **Schedule:** **40 sports** (swimming, archery, athletics, badminton, baseball and softball, basketball, boxing, etc.) and **61 disciplines**; 15 competition days; **483 gold medals**.

**Highlights of XIX Asian Games (p.475)**
- A **655-member** Indian contingent at Hangzhou, **Sept 23–Oct 8, 2023**. 19th edition of the continental quadrennial multisport meet, originally scheduled 2022, postponed a year (COVID-19).
- India: **record 107 medals — 28 gold, 38 silver, 41 bronze**. Surpassed the previous record at **Jakarta 2018** (**570-strong squad, 70 medals — 16 gold, 23 silver, 31 bronze**).
- **China** led with **201 gold**, ahead of **Japan (52)** and **Republic of Korea (42)** (printed twice). **Only four countries won over 100 medals** in Hangzhou.
- **Shooting** led for India with an unprecedented **22 medals incl. 7 gold**. **Athletics** (headlined by **Neeraj Chopra's** successful title defence in javelin) second: **6 gold, 14 silver, 9 bronze**.
- India's **first Asian Games badminton gold**: men's doubles pair **Chirag Shetty and Satwiksairaj Rankireddy**.
- **Squash, tennis and equestrian** completed the **28 gold** medals.
- Hangzhou offered **74 Paris 2024 Olympic quotas** — 6 in archery, 10 in artistic swimming, 34 in boxing, 2 in breaking, 2 in hockey, 10 in modern pentathlon, 6 in sailing, 2 in tennis, 2 in water polo. India secured **six quotas** — **4 in boxing (Nikhat Zareen, Preeti Pawar, Parveen Hooda, Lovlina Borgohain)** and one each in **athletics (Kishore Jena)** and **men's hockey**.

### B.11 §58 SAF GAMES (book pp.475–476, PDF 488–489)
- **South Asian Federation Games (SAF Games)** — sport festival of South Asian countries. **South Asian Sports Federation** (India, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, Bangladesh, Nepal, Bhutan, Maldives) formed in **New Delhi on November 26, 1982**.
- First SAF Games: **Kathmandu 1984**; then **Dhaka (1985), Kolkata (1987), Islamabad (1989), Colombo (1991), Dhaka (1993), Chennai (1995)**.
- **Eighth SAF Games: Sept 25–Oct 4, 1999, Kathmandu**.
- **10th SAF Games (18–28 August 2006): Sri Lanka**; India retained the crown with **118 Gold, 69 Silver, 47 Bronze**; Pakistan (43-44-71) and hosts Sri Lanka (3-63-78) second and third.
- **Flag and Motto:** flag includes a **dove** (desire for peace); motto **'Peace, Prosperity and Progress'**.
- **New name:** **South Asian Games (SAG)** — decision of the South Asian Sports Federation at its **32nd meeting, Islamabad, April 2, 2004**.
- **13th South Asian Games 2019 (box):** Host city **Kathmandu, Pokhara & Janakpur**; Host country **Nepal**; Nations **7**; Athletes **2715**; Events **317 in 26 sports**; Opening **1 December**; Closing **10 December**; Officially opened by **Bidhya Devi Bhandari (President of Nepal)**; Athlete's Oath **Paras Khadka (cricket)**; Judge's Oath **Deepak Thapa (badminton)**; Torch lighter **Deepak Bista**; Main venue **Dasarath Rangasala Stadium**.
- Officially the XIII South Asian Games; originally slated **9–18 March 2019** (Kathmandu and Pokhara); postponed; held **1–10 December 2019** (coincidentally same time as **2019 Southeast Asian Games**, Philippines). New dates confirmed at the **South Asian Olympic Council Executive Board meeting in Bangkok, 1 March 2019**. Dasarath Stadium hosted the opening ceremony and men's football; renovation after the **2015 earthquake** completed in under 10 months; capacity **20,000**.
- **Logo & Mascot (p.476):** unveiled by Nepal's Ministry of Youth and Sports on **13 May 2019**; mascot — a pair of **blackbucks (Krishnashar)** (only extant member of genus *Antelope*; found in southern Nepal; endangered in Nepal); **logo — a flying pigeon** embodying the colour of the South Asia Olympic Council logo, with world heritage sites and mountains of Nepal in the background.
- **Participating nations:** seven; **Afghanistan did not compete** (first time since 1999, after leaving the South Asia Olympic Council in 2016). Athletes: **Bangladesh (470), Bhutan (116), India (487), Maldives (216), Nepal (596), Pakistan (263), Sri Lanka (564)** (total 2,715 — printed: sum of listed = 2,712; ⚠ small mismatch as printed).
- **Sports:** 26 sports; **cricket returned after 8 years**; **golf and karate** added by hosts; each event needs minimum **4 participating teams**; **Paragliding** removed (only Nepal and Pakistan had registered).
- **New Records:** high jumper **Sarvesh Anil Kushare** — **2.21 m** (broke 2.20 m of Sri Lanka's Manjula Kumara, 2004). **Karthik Unnikrishnan** — triple jump **16.47 m** (broke 16.45 m of Renjith Maheshwary). **Tejinder Pal Singh** — shot put **20.03 m** (broke 19.15 m of Bahadur Singh Sagoo, 1999).
