### B.12 §59 CRICKET (Lucent, book p.476, PDF 489)
- Believed started in **England in 1300 AD**; game of shepherds; popular among other classes in **18th century AD**.
- **Marylebone Cricket Club (M.C.C.)** formed at **Lords, London**. Cricket popular in **Australia** due to British influence.
- **First official Test match: 1877, Australia vs England, Melbourne**.
- **Imperial Cricket Conference formed 1909** → gave birth to **International Cricket Conference in 1956**.
- **First One Day International: 1971, England vs Australia, Melbourne**.
- **First one-day World Cup: 1975, London — West Indies won**, beating **Australia by 17 runs**.
- Apex body: **ICC**; HQ now in **Dubai from 1 August 2005** (earlier Lords, England).
- **Australia** has won maximum of **five World Cups till 2015**.
- In India, cricket introduced by British royalty. **Parsee community** first to take part, **1848**. Parsee team visited England **1886**. Matches between European and Parsee teams, called **Presidency matches**, started in **Poona (Pune) and Bombay (Mumbai)**.
- **Raja Bhupindra Singh of Patiala** donated the **Ranji Trophy in 1934** (national championship). **Board of Control for Cricket in India formed 1927**.
- **The ICC World Test Championship** intended to become premier Test championship; will replace the **ICC Champions Trophy**, "held in 2013 for the last time" (⚠ as printed; contradicted by Parmar's Champions Trophy 2025 note in Part A — cross-book inconsistency).
- **Measurements in cricket:** Pitch length **22 yards (20.11 m)**; Crease length **1.22–1.83 metres (4 ft)**; Ball weight **155 to 163 gram** (Parmar: 156–163 g); Ball circumference **22.4–22.9 cm (9 inch)**; Bat length **96.5 cm (38 inch)**; Bat width **10.8 cm (4.25 inch)**; Stumps length **71.1 cm (28 inch)**; Bails "Length of Bells" **11.1 cm (each bell)** [sic — bails].
- **Winners of World Cup Cricket since 1975 (table begins):** 1975 England — West Indies (runners-up Australia); 1979 England — West Indies (runners-up England); 1983 England — India (runners-up West Indies). (continues on p.477)

**Cricket World Cup 2011, 2015, 2019 and 2023 (p.477)**
- **India was main host of WC 2011.** Initially India, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, Bangladesh were to host jointly; after the terrorist attack on the Sri Lankan team in Pakistan, **Pakistan was cancelled from the hosts' list by ICC**.
- **M.S. Dhoni** (captain) = Man of the Match (final); **Yuvraj Singh** = Man of the Tournament (2011). India beat **Australia in QF, Pakistan in SF, Sri Lanka in Final**.
- **2015:** hosted jointly by Australia and NZ. **Australia beat New Zealand by 7 wickets on March 29, 2015 at Melbourne.** **Michael Clarke** = fourth Australian captain to win ICC WC.
- **2019** in England: England defeated New Zealand in final.
- **2023** in India (**Oct 5–Nov 19, 2023**): Australia defeated India **by 6 wickets**.
- **Terminologies list:** Played on, Appeal, Bye, Leg Bye, Power Play, Follow on, Dusra, Beamer, Hoober Shot [sic], Lost Ball, Duck worthLuis [sic — Duckworth-Lewis], Retired Hurt, Chinaman, Batsman, Bowler, Wicket Keeper, Fielder, LBW (Leg Before Wicket), Catch, Hit wicket, Throw, Maiden over, Four, Sixer, Wide, Swing, Stroke, Cover, Mid on, Mid Off, Mid wicket, Over the wicket, Round the wicket, Leg spinner, Off spinner, Over throw, Over Slip, Gulley, Cover point, Silly point, Long off, Long on, Third man, Short pitch, Hook, Dead ball, Run out, Popping crease, Pitch, Bouncer (or Bumper), Full Toss, Yorker, Yorked, Googly, Wicket Maiden, Snick, Duck, Hat-Trick, Rubber, The Ashes, Scoring a Ton etc.

**ICC Twenty-20 Cricket World Cup (p.477)**
- **1st: South Africa, 11–24 Sept 2007**; final at **Wanderers, Johannesburg** — **India beat Pakistan by 5 runs**.
- **2nd (2009): Pakistan** beat Sri Lanka by 8 wickets in the final, **London, 21 June 2009**.
- **3rd (2010): England** beat Australia by 7 wickets, final in **Bridgetown, Barbados**, 16 May 2010.
- **4th (2012):** **West Indies** defeated host **Sri Lanka** in final at **Colombo, Oct 7, 2012**.
- **5th (2014):** Bangladesh, March 16–April 6; **Sri Lanka defeated India by 6 wickets** at **Sher-e-Bangla Stadium, Dhaka**; **Virat Kohli 'Man of the Series'**.
- **6th (2016):** India, **March 8–April 3**; **West Indies (captain Darren Sammy)** beat **England (2010 champion) by 4 wickets**; Virat Kohli again Man of the Series.
- In all six T20 WCs till 2016, **M.S. Dhoni** captained India. **Dhoni and Suresh Raina** announced retirement from international cricket on **15 Aug 2020**. (Side-box: **M.S. Dhoni inducted in the ICC Hall of Fame on 9 June 2025**.)
- **7th (2021 edition of ICC Men's T20 WC):** UAE and Oman, **17 Oct–14 Nov 2021**; **Australia** beat **New Zealand by 8 wickets**, final in **Dubai**; **Mitchell Marsh** Player of the Match; **David Warner** Player of the Tournament.
- **8th:** Australia, **16 Oct–13 Nov 2022**; **England** beat **Pakistan by 5 wickets**, final at **MCG, Melbourne**; **Virat Kohli Top scorer**, **Sam Curran Player of the Series**.
- **Retirements (recent-event items, p.477 — ⚠ verify):** **Virat Kohli** retired from Test cricket **May 2025**, continues in ODIs; retired from T20Is in 2024. **Rohit Sharma** retired from Test cricket **May 2025**; earlier from T20Is after the 2024 T20 WC; continues with ODIs. **Piyush Chawla** retired from all forms of cricket **June 2025**.
- **ICC Men's T20 World Cup 2024 (box):** Host **USA & West Indies**; Final **June 29, 2024, Bridgetown**; Champions **India**; Runners-up **South Africa**. India won by **7 runs**. **Note:** next (**10th edition**) WC to be held **Feb–March 2026 (India/Sri Lanka)**. (⚠ Lucent's own counts: 9th = 2024; Parmar's Part A lists 2026 as held/won by India vs NZ — check consistency; both recent-event items.)

**India to Host the ICC Women's ODI World Cup 2025 (p.478)**
- ICC approved **Bangladesh, India, England and Sri Lanka** as the four host countries of the ICC Women's white-ball events **2024-27** on **July 26, 2022 in Birmingham**.
- Next ICC Women's CWC (ODI) in **2025** to be staged by **India** and **Sri Lanka** to host ICC Women's T20 Champions Trophy 2027 subject to qualifying.
- India to host the Women's ODI World Cup for the **fifth time** in 2025; first global women's tournament in India since **2016** (T20 WC ran parallel to men's event). (⚠ recent event — as of today the tournament has been played; result not in Lucent — verify.)

### B.13 §60 FOOTBALL (Lucent, book p.478, PDF 491)
- Believed British origin; a similar game played in **500 BC by Greeks of Sparta — "Harpaston"**. First football club: **Sheffield Football Club, 1857, England**.
- Introduced in India by British in **1848**; first football club of India: **Dalhousie Club**. **Indian Football Association**, oldest football association in the east, formed **1878** (⚠ Parmar: IFA established **1893** — cross-book disagreement).
- Apex: **FIFA — formed by seven countries on May 21, 1904**; HQ **Paris (France)** (printed; ⚠ Parmar says HQ Zurich, Switzerland — cross-book disagreement; FIFA HQ is in Zurich as far as I know; verify).
- To distinguish from the carrying-cum-kicking game (Rugby), given name **"Soccer"** by **London Football Association, formed 1863**. FIFA World Cup = biggest competition.
- Football included as competitive game in Olympic Games officially in **1908**. India took part in the World Olympic Football Competition in **1948 in London**.
- World Cup Championships planned by two Frenchmen: **M. Jules Rimet and Henry Delaunay**. **Jules Rimet** was president of the French Football Federation and FIFA president **1924–1954**.
- **First World Cup at Monte Video (Uruguay), 1930**.
- Trophy named **"Jules Rimet Cup"**; became permanent property of **Brazil** (world title thrice: **1958, 1962, 1970**). From **1974** (Xth championship, Germany) trophy = **"The FIFA World Cup"** — new trophy cast in **18 ct. gold**.
- In India **Indian Football Association (IFA)** organises the National Football Championship; the trophy is the **Santosh Trophy**, donated in memory of **Manmath Nath Roy Chaudhary of Santosh (now part of Bangladesh)**.
- **Durand Cup**: oldest football tournament of India and **second oldest in the world**, started **1888**; first organised at **Shimla**, held in **Delhi since 1940**.
- **Football Players' Association (FPA) of India** launched in **Kolkata on August 13, 2006**.
- FIFA announced (**April 17, 2007**) assistance of **$1 million** to Indian football via **'Win in India with India'** project, initially for four years. FIFA President **Joseph S. Blatter** visited India April 2007; hinted at another grant of **$400,000** for the third **'FIFA Goal Project'**. **AIFF** started 'Goal Project' in **Manipur**, second in **Delhi**; FIFA and **AFC** identified **Sikkim** as third site.
- FIFA World Cup played every **four years**.

**Football — continuation (book p.479, PDF 492)**
- **Brazil, five wins**, has won the FIFA World Cup (Men) the maximum number of times and is the **only team to have played in every tournament**.
- **Hakan Sukur** holds the record of the **fastest goal — 11th second** — in a FIFA World Cup match.
- **Measurements of the playground:** Length **91 to 120 m**; Width **45 to 91 m**; Weight of ball **396 to 453 g**; Circumference of ball **68 to 71 cm** (⚠ Parmar: weight 410–460 g, circumference 68–70 cm — cross-book difference).
- **Terminologies of Football:** Abbey [sic], Dribble, Extra time, Full back, Half back, Striker, Centre Forward, Penalty kick, Free kick, Scissor Kick, Goal Kick, Direct Kick, Corner Kick, Referee, Tie breaker, Hat trick, Hand ball, Sweeper, Back, Throw in, Hand-ball (fault), Touch line, Place Kick or Kick off, Direct Free Kick, Indirect Free Kick, Tackle, Off side, Sliding Tackle, Drop Ball, Sudden death, Penalty shoot out etc.

**22nd FIFA World Cup Football — 2022 (20 Nov–18 Dec 2022)**
- Official Tagline **"Now is all"**; Mascot **La'eeb** (Arabic — "super skilled player").
- Opening ceremony **20 Nov 2022** at **Al Bayt Stadium, Al Khor, Qatar**. Host **Qatar**; Duration 20 Nov–18 Dec 2022; Total Matches **64**; Teams **32**; Goals Scored **172**; Venues **8**.
- **Final standings:** Winner **ARGENTINA (3rd title)**; Runners-up **FRANCE**.
- **Awards:** Golden Ball **Lionel Messi**; Golden Boot **Kylian Mbappe**; Golden Glove **Emiliano Martinez**; Best Young Player Award **Enzo Fernandez**; FIFA Fair Play Award **ENGLAND**.

### B.14 §61 HOCKEY (Lucent, pp.479–480, PDF 492–493)
- **Blackheath Rugby and Hockey Club** — first hockey club in world, **1861, England**. **London** first city in England to popularise it in the **1870s**. **Hockey Association of England** formed **1886, London**.
- Apex: **Federation Internationale de Hockey (FIH)** — established **January 7, 1924**, HQ at **Vienna (Austria)**, later shifted to **Paris** (⚠ as printed; FIH HQ is now in Lausanne, Switzerland as far as I know; verify). First International Hockey Match: **Wales vs Ireland at Rayle [Rhyl], June 26, 1895**.
- Hockey introduced in the Olympic Games first in **1908 in London**.
- **Indian Hockey Federation (IHF)** formed **7 November 1925 at Gwalior**. Since **1944**, National Hockey Championship organised by IHF every year. **All India Women's Hockey Federation** formed **1947**.
- India first participated in the Olympics (hockey) in **1928 (Amsterdam)**. India has won the hockey title a maximum of **8 times**. **First World Cup Hockey: Barcelona, 1971**.

**World Cup Hockey (Lucent table, p.479)** — Year | Place | Winner | Runners-up | India's position
| Year | Place | Winner | Runners-up | India's position |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1971 | Barcelona | Pakistan | Spain | Third |
| 1972 | Amsterdam | Holland | India | Second |
| 1975 | Kuala Lumpur | India | Pakistan | First |
| 1978 | Buenos Aires | Pakistan | Holland | Sixth |
| 1982 | Mumbai | Pakistan | West Germany | Fifth |
| 1986 | London | Australia | England | Twelfth |
| 1990 | Lahore | Holland | Pakistan | Tenth |
| 1994 | Sydney | Pakistan | Holland | Fifth |
| 1998 | Utrecht | Holland | Spain | Ninth |
| 2002 | Kuala Lumpur | Germany | Australia | Tenth |
| 2006 | Monchengladbach (Germany) | Germany | Australia | Eleventh |
| 2010 | New Delhi | Australia | Germany | Eighth |
| 2014 | The Hegue [The Hague] | Australia | Netherlands | Ninth |
| 2018 | Bhubaneswar (India) | Belgium | Netherlands | Sixth |
| 2023 | Odisha | Germany | Belgium | Ninth |
(⚠ The 1972 row is printed as a World Cup in Amsterdam — not counted as a World Cup edition in Parmar's chapter, which says the first World Cup was 1971; kept as printed. Parmar says "Pakistan 4 titles" (1971, 78, 82, 94) — consistent with this table.)
- Hosts of next Hockey World Cups — **Belgium and the Netherlands** jointly host men's and women's FIH Hockey World Cups in **2026**. (⚠ tense as printed; those events were due Aug 2026 — verify results.)

**Odisha Hockey Men's World Cup 2023 (p.479)** — **Harmanpreet Singh** led the squad; **Amit Rohidas** vice-captain of the 18-man squad; held **January 13 to 29, 2023** at **Bhubaneswar and Rourkela**. **Germany defeated Belgium in the final in a penalty shoot-out 5–4** (printed "short out"). **Mascot: Olly, the Olive Ridley turtle.** **Format:** 44 games across two venues; 16 teams in four pools — **Pool A:** Australia, South Africa, France, Argentina; **Pool B:** Belgium, Japan, Korea, Germany; **Pool C:** Netherlands, Chile, Malaysia, New Zealand; **Pool D:** India, Wales, Spain, England.

**Hockey — continuation (p.480 top):** The **highest Astroturf Hockey Ground of India** is at **Shilaroo (Himachal Pradesh)**. **Measurements:** 11 players each team; length of field **91.44 m**; width **50 to 55 m**; ball weight **155–163 g**; circumference **223–224 cm** [sic — printed "cm"; actually mm]; ball colour **White**; hockey stick weight **280 g (max)**. **Terminology:** Advantage, Back-stick, Bully, Carry, Dribble, Dodge, Goal line, Green Card, Flick, Free hit, Face of stick, Jab Stroke, Lung Stroke, Melee, Off side, Penalty shoot out, Short Corner, Striking circle or Shooting circle, Square pass, Tackling, Through pass, Under cutting, Stick, Penalty stroke, Scoop, Side line, Tie breaker, Penalty, Under cutting, Volley, Centre forward, Roll in, Push in, Shooting, Half volley, Full back etc.

### B.15 §62 SOME IMPORTANT FACTS (book p.480 onward, PDF 493+)
**Volleyball**
- Invented **1895** at **Holyoke Y.M.C.A. Gymnasium, USA**, by Physical Director **William J. Morgan**. First name **'MINTONNETTE'**; renamed Volleyball by **Dr. A.T. Halsted of Springfield College**.
- **International Volleyball Association** formed **1947**, HQ **Paris (France)**. First **World Volleyball Championship 1949, Prague**. First **Asian Volleyball Championship 1955, Tokyo** (**India beat Japan in the final**). **Volleyball Federation of India formed 1950**.
- **Measurements:** court length **18 m**, width **9 m**; ball weight **250–260 g**; circumference **65–67 cm**; net **1 m (±3 cm) wide and 9.5 m long**; net height **2.43 m (men)** and **2.24 m (women)**; ball internal pressure **0.40–0.45 kg/cm** [sic].
- **Terminology:** Antennae, Attack hit, Back zone players, Dribbling, Libero, Front zone players, Blocking, Smash, Rotation, Boosting, Net fault, Volley pass, Forearm pass, Service, Hook serve, Set up, Referee etc.

**Table Tennis**
- Started in **England in 1880s**. **International Table Tennis Association** established **1926**. First World Championship match **1927**. Introduced in the Olympics **1988, Seoul**. **Table Tennis Association of India** formed **1938**.
- **Measurements:** table length **2.74 m (9 ft)**, width **1.52 m (5 ft)**, height **76 cm**; ball weight **2.4 to 2.53 g**; diameter **37.2–38.2 mm**; colour **white or yellow**.
- **Terminology:** Foil, End line, Late control, Flat hit, Block stroke, Service, Penholder grip, Back spin, Centre line, Half court, Side spin, Swing stroke, Push stroke, Rally, Let, Reverse, Top spin, Drop shot, Lob, Chopped return, Counter hitting etc.

**Basketball**
- Invented by **Dr. James Naismith of U.S.A. in 1891 at Springfield College**. **International Basketball Federation set up 1932**. **Basketball Federation of India formed 1950**; **its first World Championship played 1950** (as printed).
- **Measurements:** court length **28 m**, width **15 m**, basket height **3.05 m**, ball weight **600 to 650 g**.
- **Terminology:** Dribbling, Front court, Second dribble, Two count stop, Travelling or shifting, Pivoting, Held ball, Jump ball, Violation, Foul, Feinting or Dodging, Shooting, Set shot, Ring, Guard point, Dead ball, Basket Rudnick [sic], Hook pass, Goal, Centre line, Free throw line, Onsted [sic], Fast break, Lay-up shot, Man to man defence, Pack, Three point, Turn over, Assist, Throw, Goal Tending, Steal, Tap etc.

**Badminton**
- Modern badminton probably developed in **17th century**, named from **'Badminton' in Gloucestershire (England)**. **International Badminton Federation (IBF)** established **1934**. **Badminton Association of India formed 1934**.
- **Thomas Cup** (men) named after the first president of the IBF, **Sir George Thomas** — started **1948–49**. **Uber Cup** (women) started **1956**. **World Badminton Championship started 1977**.
- **Measurements:** court length **44 ft**, width **20 ft (doubles)** and **17 ft (singles)**, net height **5 feet**, shuttlecock **4.74 to 5.50 g**, racket overall length **680 mm (2.5 ft) maximum**, width **230 mm (9 in) maximum**, head length **290 mm (11.5 in) maximum**, racket weight **85 to 140 g**.
- **Terminology:** Badminton Court, End, Trans Lines, Back gallery, Service Court, Let, Forward Stroke, Backward Stroke, Toss or Lob, Clear, Smash, Net strokes, Rally, Setting, First hand or Second hand, Side out, Base of operation, Rotation, Long service, Net fault, Double fault, Foot Fault, Service break, Match point, Set point, High service, Cross shot, Service change, Drive, Drop shot, Duce, Advance etc.

**Lawn Tennis (p.480 end)**
- Introduced by **Major Wingfield in Wales in 1870**. **All England Championship (popularly Wimbledon)** started **1877** for men only; women's competitions in Wimbledon introduced **1884**.
- **Measurements:** court length **78 ft or 23.77 m (singles)**, width **27 ft or 8.23 m (singles)**, … (continues on p.481)
  (p.481 continues:) width **36 ft or 10.97 m (doubles)**; net height **3 ft (0.914 m)**; ball weight **56.0 to 59.4 g**; maximum length of racket frame **32 in (81.28 cm)**; ball colour **white or yellow**. **Terminology:** Ace, Advantage, A Let, Back hand drive, Deuce, Chip Shot, Volley, Half volley, Let fault, Foot fault, Double fault, Smash, Service, Grand slam, Singles sticks, Love, Slice, Tie Breaker, Luv, Change, Set, In, Out.
- **Important Championships (Grand Slams) — Lucent box:** **Australian Open** (January) — Melbourne, from **1905**; **French Open** (also Roland Garros) (May–June) — Paris, from **1891**; **Wimbledon** (June–July) — London, from **1877**; **U.S. Open** (August–September) — New York, from **1881**. (Parmar table: Wimbledon "July", first played 1877 — consistent; Lucent adds June–July.)

**Polo:** field length **300 yards**, width **150 yards**, distance between goals **250 yards**, distance between goal posts **8 yards**. Terminology: Bunker, Chukker, Mallet, Chukkas, Angle shot etc.

**Wrestling:** international competitions — circular area **9 m diameter** with a **1 m diameter** circle in centre; played on a mattress, mattress **1:1 m diameter high** (as printed). Terminology: Heave, Half, Nelson, Rebuts, Hold sager, Timekeeper, Active, Attack, Hold, Head Lock etc.

**Chess:** squares on chessboard **64**; colour **white and black**; number of same-colour squares **16** [sic — 32 of each colour; printed "16" refers to pieces of the same colour]. Terminology: Bishop, Gambit, Checkmate, Stalemate, Pawn, Grandmaster etc.
- **45th Chess Olympiad 2024 (box):** held in **Budapest (Hungary), Sept 10–23, 2024**. World's largest team chess competition, **381 teams (incl. 184 teams in the Women's Championship)**. **Team India won gold for the first time, scoring double gold in both open and women's championship.**

**Golf:** ball weight **45.9 g**, circumference **4.27 cm** [sic — likely diameter], field **125–175 Acres**; diameter of the hole **4 inches**. Terminology: Bogie, Fore some, Stymie, T, Putt, Put hole, Nib lick, Caddy, Limns, Iron, Putting, The green, Bunker etc.

**Water Polo:** field **30 × 20 yards**. Terminology: 2 metre line, 4 metre line, Goal line, Caps, Personal, Fault, Ball under etc.

**Baseball:** distance of each base **90 feet**; base-to-base along the hypotenuse **127 feet**. Terminology: Home, Diamond, Pitcher, Put out, Home run, Strike, Ant rubber [sic] etc.

**Billiards:** Terminology: Cue, Jigger, Pot, Break Pot, In luck, In off, Cans, Bolting, Hazard, Long etc.

**Rifle Shooting:** Terminology: Target, Bulls eye, Muzzle flub [sic] etc.

**Kho-Kho:** Terminology: Chasers, Active Chaser, Runners, Diving, Taking a direction, To recede, Tapping, Trapping, Dupe turn, Late Kho, Giving Kho, Fake Kho etc.

**Swimming:** Terminology: Crawl, Breast Stroke, Spring Board, Twist, Butter fly, Front Crawl, Stroke, Back Stroke.

**Boxing:** ring size minimum **4.9 × 4.9 m²**, maximum **6.10 × 6.10 m²**. Terminology: Punch, Upper cut, Round, Jab, Hook, Knock down, Knock out, Hitting willow [sic], Ring, Break, Bell, Belt, Blow, Bounce, Bout, Bleeder.

**Marathon:** **26 mile 385 yards or 42.195 km**.
- N.B.: **Vallamkalli** — traditional boat race in **Kerala**, held on **Onam** in the **Pampa river** near **Alappuzha** at the beginning of monsoon on the occasion of installation of the idol of Krishna. (⚠ as printed; the well-known Onam snake-boat races are often described differently; verify.)

### B.25 §72 SPECIAL AWARDS IN SPORTS (book p.484–485, PDF 497–498)
**Special Awards to winners and their Coaches in International Sports Events** — According to **INDIA 2024**, this Scheme was introduced in **1986** to encourage and motivate outstanding sportspersons for higher achievements and to attract the younger generation to take up sports as a career. Special awards to sportspersons and coaches for winning medals in recognised international events held in a year at the following prize money:

**(a) Category: Open Category Sports**
| S. | Event | Gold | Silver | Bronze |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Olympic Games (Summer & Winter) | 75 lakh | 50 lakh | 30 lakh |
| 2 | Asian Games | 30 lakh | 20 lakh | 10 lakh |
| 3 | Commonwealth Games | 30 lakh | 20 lakh | 10 lakh |
| 4 | World Championships or World Cup (conducted in Four Year Cycle) / All England Championship of Badminton | 40 lakh | 25 lakh | 15 lakh |
| 5 | World Championships/World Cup (held once in two years) | 20 lakh | 14 lakh | 8 lakh |
| 6 | World Championships/World Cup (held annually) | 10 lakh | 7 lakh | 4 lakh |
| 7 | Asian Championships (held once in 4 years) | 15 lakh | 10 lakh | 5 lakh |
| 8 | Asian Championships (held once in 2 years) | 7.5 lakh | 5 lakh | 2.5 lakh |
| 9 | Asian Championships (held annually) | 3.75 lakh | 2.5 lakh | 1.25 lakh |
| 10 | Commonwealth Championships (held once in 4 years) | 15 lakh | 10 lakh | 5 lakh |
| 11 | Commonwealth Championships (held once in 2 years) | 7.5 lakh | 5 lakh | 2.5 lakh |
| 12 | Commonwealth Championships (held once in 1 year) | 3.75 lakh | 2.5 lakh | 1.25 lakh |
| 13 | World University Games | 3.75 lakh | 2.5 lakh | 1.25 lakh |

**(b) Category: Para Sports**
| S. | Event | Gold | Silver | Bronze |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Paralympic Games (Summer & Winter) | 75 lakh | 50 lakh | 30 lakh |
| 2 | Para Asian Games | 30 lakh | 20 lakh | 10 lakh |
| 3 | Commonwealth Games (Para Athletes) | 30 lakh | 20 lakh | 10 lakh |
| 4 | IPC World Cup/Championship (held biennially) | 20 lakh | 14 lakh | 8 lakh |
| 5 | IPC World Cup/Championship (held annually) | 10 lakh | 7 lakh | 4 lakh |

**(c) Category: Blind Sports**
| S. | Event | Gold | Silver | Bronze |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | IBSA World Championship | 10 lakh | 7 lakh | 4 lakh |
(continues on p.485)

(§72 continued, book p.485, PDF 498)
**(d) Category: Sports for Deaf** — Deaflympics: Gold **75 lakh**, Silver **50 lakh**, Bronze **30 lakh**.
**(e) Category: Special Olympics – Sports** — Special Olympics (Summer/Winter): Gold **5 lakh**, Silver **3 lakh**, Bronze **1 lakh**.
**(f) Category: Blind Cricket World Cup** — Blind Cricket World Cup (held in 4 years): **5 lakh** (single amount printed).
- **Cash award to coaches** who trained the medal winners for at least **180 days** immediately preceding the tournament. Coach's award = **50%** of the award money given to the sportsperson coached; if more than one coach, distributed equally.

**Scheme of Pension to Meritorious Sportspersons** — per INDIA 2024, launched **1994**. Sportspersons who are Indian citizens and have won gold, silver and bronze medals in **Olympic Games, World Cup/World Championships, Asian Games, Commonwealth Games, Para-Olympics and Para-Asian Games**, have attained the age of **30 years** and retired from active sports career are eligible for pension **for life**. Rates of pension **doubled from April 2018**.
| S. | Category of Meritorious Sportspersons | Rate of pension (₹/month) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Medalists at the Olympic Games / Para Olympic Games | 20,000 |
| 2 | Gold medalists at the World Cup / World Championship* in Olympic and Asian Games disciplines | 16,000 |
| 3 | Silver and Bronze medalists at the World Cup in Olympic and Asian Games disciplines | 14,000 |
| 4 | Gold medalists of the Asian/Commonwealth Games / Para Asian Games | 14,000 |
| 5 | Silver and Bronze medallists of the Asian/Commonwealth Games / Para Asian Games | 12,000 |
* World Cup/World Championship held once in four years shall be considered.

**Pandit Deendayal Upadhyay National Welfare Fund** — name of the National Welfare Fund for sportspersons changed to Pandit Deendayal Upadhyay National Welfare Fund for sportspersons from **2017**. Set up in **1982** to assist outstanding sportspersons of yesteryears living in indigent circumstances. Financial assistance to: sportsperson sustaining injury other than fatal; families of sportspersons in indigent circumstances; medical treatment; sports promoters (referees, coaches, umpires) in indigent circumstances. Reviewed and revised in **2016**; annual income limit for eligibility raised from **₹2 lakh to ₹4 lakh**. (Footer printed: "BHARAT 2025".)

### B.27 OPERATION SINDOOR (Lucent box, p.487) [Govt/Defence/Days & Events — recent event; ⚠ verify against primary sources]
- Launched by Indian Armed Forces in the midnight of **6th–7th May, 2025**, to destroy terror infrastructure across the border; punish perpetrators and planners of terror. Launched after the **April 22 terror attack on tourists at Pahalgam, Kashmir, resulting in 26 deaths**.
- First press briefing **May 7**, led by **Colonel Sophia Quraishi and Wing Commander Vyomika Singh**: response focused, measured, non-escalatory; Pakistani military establishments not targeted; any attack on military targets in India will invite a suitable response.
- After Operation started and Pakistan escalated using drones and shelling to target religious sites in India, India conducted retaliatory strikes on **radar installations in Lahore and Gujranwala**.
- India launched coordinated missile strikes on **9 terrorist bases (4 in Pakistan and 5 in Pakistan-occupied Kashmir)**, key command centres for **Jaish-e-Mohammed (JeM) and Lashkar-e-Taiba (LeT)**. Over **100 terrorists killed**.
- **Kamikaze drones** deployed to neutralise Pakistan's air defence capabilities, including disabling **Lahore's air defence system**.
- Non-military measures: **Indus Waters Treaty Suspension, Border Closure and Trade Suspension, Visa Revocation and Deportation, Cultural Ban, Diplomatic Isolation, Reduction of High Commission Strength** etc.
- Operation Sindoor redefined rules of engagement; established a new strategic red line; enforced equal punishment for terrorists and their sponsors; India "does not require anyone's permission to defend its people"; reframed the Kashmir narrative purely through counter-terrorism. (Section/book closes with ★★★ marker on p.487.)

1. **Champions Trophy:** Lucent (§59) says the ICC Champions Trophy was "held in 2013 for the last time" (to be replaced by the WTC); Parmar (A.6) lists Champions Trophy 2025 (India champion). Cross-book/edition inconsistency — Parmar's is the newer statement; verify.
2. **Men's World Cup 2027/2031/2032:** Lucent table lists 2027 (South Africa, Zimbabwe, Namibia) and **2031 (India and Bangladesh)**; Parmar A.6 says "2032: India (upcoming)". Verify against ICC.
3. **T20 World Cup:** Lucent ends at 2024 (India beat South Africa by 7 runs) and says the 10th edition is Feb–Mar 2026 (India/Sri Lanka); Parmar states India won 2026 (beat New Zealand) — a 2026 recent-event claim; verify. Lucent also counts editions (1st 2007 … 8th 2022; 9th 2024).
4. **FIFA HQ:** Lucent says Paris (France); Parmar says Zurich, Switzerland (as far as I know, Zurich is correct). **IFA (Indian Football Association):** Lucent 1878 vs Parmar 1893. **FIH HQ:** Lucent Vienna→Paris; the current HQ is in Lausanne as far as I know. **Football weights:** Parmar 410–460 g / 68–70 cm; Lucent 396–453 g / 68–71 cm.
5. **Olympics facts in Lucent with slips:** "Athens (Egypt)" for 2004; "Seoul Olympic of 1986" (Seoul = 1988) in the Christina Otty/Kristin Otto line; "IOC founded at 'Chakhon'" (unclear); "Mount Olympus" vs Elis/Kronion; "Larina Lavyanina" (Larisa Latynina); "Sonia Denancord" as first woman football referee at Atlanta (unverified). Lucent "Norman Prichard/Pritchard" spelling both forms.
6. **CWG 2022 India tally:** Lucent's top-five table shows India 3rd with 26-32-34 = 92 and Canada 22-16-23 = 61, but Lucent's own list of India's medallists sums to 22 G + 16 S + 23 B = 61 — the India/Canada rows look swapped (as far as I know, India was 4th with 61 medals). See note in B.9/B.10.
7. **Paralympics:** Lucent p.470 says Murlikant Petkar won India's first Paralympic gold (1972) but the p.471 box says "First Ever Gold for India: Devendra (Athens 2004)". Athletes count 4,400 (text) vs 4,433 (box).
8. **Awards money:** Lucent Khel Ratna ₹25 lakh; Arjuna ₹15 lakh; Dhyan Chand Award ₹10 lakh; Dronacharya ₹15/10 lakh. Parmar A.19/A.3 statements about Sreejesh ("Major Dhyan Chand Khel Ratna 2021") and Rani Rampal ("first woman hockey player to get Rajiv Gandhi Khel Ratna") appear in both books (Lucent §73). Compare against the Awards file (Round 2).
9. **National sport of India = Hockey:** printed as such in Lucent §65; India has no officially declared national sport as far as I know — verify with MoYAS/RTI.
10. **Chess:** Lucent p.481 "Number of same colour Chess — 16" is a slip (32 squares of each colour; 16 pieces of each colour). "Golf ball circumference 4.27 cm" likely a diameter. "Hockey ball circumference 223–224 cm" should be mm. Kept as printed.
11. **Parmar-side flags** (see the ⚠ notes inside Part A): Super Bowl under football; T20 WC 2026; Champions Trophy 2025; Gukesh world champion; Hangzhou Asian Games "2022"; IOC President Kirsty Coventry; IOA President P.T. Usha; 2026 and later hosts.

Painting (pp.322–327) [PDF = book + 13, PDF 319–340], plus Miscellany §7 Important Monuments/Structures (World) (p.434). Chapter 07 §4 Festivals and Fairs = already in `festivals_raw_extraction.md`. Parmar's SSC Fatman has no Architecture chapter (its Static GK covers music/dance, folk, festivals, census, days, books, sports, organisations, awards). Test cross-referencing NOT done (raw extraction only).
Method / disclosure: "fact-complete" extraction (all entries, table rows, names, numbers, dates kept; explanatory prose condensed, not word-for-word). Pages read from images (OCR only as an aid). ⚠ = printing slip / doubt / verify. Spellings kept as printed with corrections in brackets. Today's date when extracted: 2026-09-24.
Cross-references to project raw files: Harappan buildings/sculpture → `ancient-history-raw/indus_valley_raw_extraction.md`; Mauryan/Ashokan pillars, Buddhist stupas/caves → `ancient-history-raw/mauryan_empire_raw_extraction.md`, `jainism_buddhism_raw_extraction.md`; Gupta temples → `ancient-history-raw/gupta_dynasty_raw_extraction.md`; Post-Maurya schools (Gandhar/Mathura/Amaravati) → `ancient-history-raw/post_maurya_raw_extraction.md`; Pallava/Chola/Pandya → `ancient-history-raw/tripartite_cholas_sangam_raw_extraction.md`; Delhi Sultanate/Mughal/Vijayanagar/Bahmani/Rajput buildings & painting → `medieval-history-raw/*`; British-era buildings → `modern-history-raw/*`; Rangoli/folk painting ↔ dances/festivals files; UNESCO WHS ↔ Lucent Misc §20 (Famous People/Places file).
Contents: PART A Architecture (A.1–A.15), PART B Sculpture (B.1–B.6), PART C Painting (C.1–C.5), PART D §7 World Monuments, PART E doubts.

## PART A — LUCENT'S GENERAL KNOWLEDGE (2026): Chapter 07 "Art and Culture" — 1. ARCHITECTURE (book pp.306–318, PDF 319–331)

### A.1 Introduction and Harappan architecture (p.306)
- The art of building = **'Architecture'**. In India the story of architecture begins with the **Harappan (Indus Valley) Civilization**.
- Important Harappan buildings (in **descending order of size**):

| Name | Located at |
|---|---|
| Great Bath | Mohenjodaro |
| Official Residence / Priest Residence | Mohenjodaro |
| Giant Reservoir | Dholavira |
| Great Granery | Harappa |
| Dockyard | Lothal |
| Assembly Hall | Mohenjodaro |

- **Burnt bricks** = base material (absence of stone building); joined with **mud (in most cases), gypsum (in drainage) and bitumen (in the Great Bath)**. No knowledge of sand, cement and red lime for joining bricks.
- Buildings solid and durable rather than beautiful. **A.L. Basham**: "The utilitarian brick buildings of the Harappa Culture, strong and competent they were, had apparently the little aesthetic merit."
- Except the **wall of Rajgrih (modern Rajgir, Nalanda district, Bihar)**, no significant architectural remains between the Harappan and Mauryan periods (few buildings of stone).

### A.2 Mauryan architecture (p.306)
- Pre-Ashokan architecture mostly **wood and perishable materials** → no remains. **General use of stone began with Ashokan time.** Replacement of wood by stone may have been partly due to contact with the **Achaemenid empire of Persia**, partly to denudation of forest in the Gangetic plains.
- Mauryan remains in three forms: **Palaces, Stupas & Caves**.
- **1. Palaces:** Megasthenes and other Greek writers refer to magnificent palaces and halls at **Patliputra**, finest as **Susa & Ecbatana** (two famous cities of Persia). Chinese pilgrims **Fahien, Hiuen Tsang** were wonder-struck. All perished; 20th-century excavations laid bare ruins, most wonderful = the **80-pillared hall from Kumharar (block of modern Patna, Bihar)**.
- **2. Stupas:** originated in India **prior to 5th century BC** as tombs of holy men, evolving into sacred sites dedicated to the Buddha. **Clay-built stupa of Lauriya Nandangarh (West Champaran district, Bihar)** and **brick-built stupa of Piprahwa (Siddharthnagar district, UP)** = specimens of **Pre-Mauryan Stupas** (forerunners of Mauryan Buddhist Stupas).
- Stupa (memorial) = one of the **three major forms of Buddhist architecture** (others: **Chaitya** = worship place; **Vihara** = monastery and learning centre). Stupa literally = **heap (mound) of clay**; initially clay, later bricks and stone, over the grave/residues (ash, bone, tooth, nail, hair) of a holy man (Gautam Buddha, Buddhist monks).
- **Basic features of a Stupa:** (1) hemispherical dome-like **solid** structure, cannot be entered; (2) built over residues kept in a **casket at base or top of dome**; (3) **circumambulatory passage/walkway (Pradakshina Patha)** enclosed by railing (**Vedika** — Sanskrit for outer railing around a sacred monument) around the base of the dome; usually bears low-relief inscriptions (protective deities, mythological events, iconography); (4) **four gateways (toranas)** at the cardinal points (Sanchi stupa has four gateways E, W, N, S); Torana = ceremonial gateway, origin traced to **gram-dwar** (village gateway) of Vedic period; (5) at the top of the dome a square-like structure, **Harmika**; from it rises a stick (**Yashti**) holding the **triple umbrella (Chhatras)** — sign of royalty and dignity.

### A.3 Stupa diagram legend and further Mauryan/post-Mauryan (p.307)
- **Sanchi Stupa diagram legend:** Chhatra = umbrella-like structure; Harmika = small square fencing; Anda = semicircular dome; Pradakshinapatha = circumambulatory passage/walkway; Medhi = circular lane; Vedika = railing; Torana = gateway.
- Per Buddhist tradition **84,000 stupas** built by **Ashoka** — exaggerated. Fahien and Hiuen Tsang saw many; majority perished. **Stupas of Sanchi, Sarnath, Bharhut, Tuxila (Taxila) etc.** originally built by Ashoka, extended and enlarged by post-Mauryan patrons; **Sanchi Stupa** = typical example.
- **3. Caves:** Mauryan caves = **earliest examples of rock-cut architecture**; **Sudama Cave is the oldest**. Hewn from solid rock, interior walls polished like mirrors. Built by **Ashoka and his grandson Dasharatha**, donated to **Ajivikas (followers of Makkhali Goshala)** and Buddhist monks. Located in the twin hills of **Barabar (4 caves, built by Ashoka)** and **Nagarjuni (3 caves, built by Dasharatha)**, **Jehanabad district, Bihar**.
  - **Barabar group:** 1. **Sudama Cave** (donated to Ajivikas); 2. **Vishwa Jhonpari Cave** (Ajivikas); 3. **Karna Chopar** (monks); 4. **Lomash Rishi Cave**.
  - **Nagarjuni group:** 1. **Gopi Cave** (Ajivikas); 2. **Vadathi Cave** (Ajivikas); 3. **Vapia Cave/Mirza Mandi** (Ajivikas).
  - Details of **Sudama Cave & Lomash Rishi Cave** show clear influence of **wooden architecture** on rock-cut architecture. Mauryan rock-cut architecture begins a tradition spanning the next **1,000 years**.
- **Post-Mauryan architecture:** 3 phases — **Sunga, Satavahana, Kushan**.
- **Sunga:** Buddhist texts depict Sunga rulers as persecutors of Buddhism but they were not. Remains: **Bharhut Stupa**; stone-built stupa & railing in place of brick-built at **Sanchi**; railing at **Bodhgaya Stupa**; stupa, chaitya & vihara at **Bhaja (near Pune)**.
- **Satavahana:** Stupa, Chaitya, Vihara = 3 main forms. Buddhist architecture remarkably developed in **Satavahana–Ikshawaku period** (Ikshawaku = successor dynasty of Satavahana in Andhra Pradesh).
  - **Stupas:** 1. **Amaravati** (Guntoor dist., AP); 2. **Nagarjunikonda** (Guntoor); 3. **Bhattiprolu** (Guntoor); 4. **Goli** (Guntoor); 5. **Jagayyapeta** (Krishna dist.); 6. **Ghantsal** (Krishna dist.); 7. **Peddaganjam** (Prakasham dist.). Note: **Nagarjunikonda stupa built in Ikshawaku period; the rest in Satavahana period.**
  - **Chaityas & Viharas:** 1. **Bhaja, Bedsa, Junnar & Karle** (Pune dist., Maharashtra); 2. **Kanheri** (Mumbai dist.); 3. **Kondana** (Kolaba dist.); 4. **Nasik** (Nasik dist.); 5. **Pitalkhora & Ajanta** (Aurangabad dist.). Note: few inscriptions name artisans — **Kanha at Pitalkhora** and his disciple **Balaka at Kondana cave**.
- **Kushan:** **Kanishka** was a great patron. Remains: **Purushpur (modern Peshawar) Stupa (now identified as Shahji ke Dheri)**, **Kanishkpur town (Kashmir)**, **Sirmukh (part of Taxila city)**, **Devkula temple & Naga temple of Mathura** etc.
- **Gupta architecture:** temples, rock-cut caves etc. Temples = best specimens; **remains of temples start being found in India from Gupta age**. Gupta = **first dynasty to build permanent free-standing (structural) temples**, beginning the long tradition of Indian temple architecture. Temples built on a high platform (**Jagati/Adhisthan**); principal shrine in centre reached by steps on all four sides; four lesser shrines at the corners. Roofs initially flat, later **Shikharas** (superstructure). A rectangular/square room (idol room) — continues next page.

### A.4 Gupta temples (pp.307–308)
- The idol room = **Garbhagriha (sanctum sanctorum)**, most important part; often walled on three sides with a gate on one. Figures of **Makarvahini Ganga and Kurmavahini Yamuna** engraved on the door-frame of the Garbhagriha = **speciality of the Gupta period** (tradition of purifying by bathing before entering).
- **Covered Pradakshina Patha** around the Garbhagriha. **Pradakshina = Pra + Dakshina** = moving towards the right (traditions found in almost every religion).
- Earlier a **pillared pavilion (Mandap)** was built in front of the Garbhagriha; later it began to be built around it.

| Temples of Gupta period | Location |
|---|---|
| Budh Dev Temple of Sanchi | Sanchi, Raisen dist., Madhya Pradesh |
| Vishnu Temple of Tigawa | Tigawa, Jabalpur dist., MP |
| Kankali Devi Temple of Tigawa | Tigawa, Jabalpur dist., MP |
| Vishnu Temple of Eran | Eran, Sagar dist., MP |
| Parvati Temple of Nachana-Kuthara | Nachana-Kuthara, Panna dist., MP |
| Shiv Temple of Bhumara | Bhumara, Satna dist., MP |
| Vishnu Temple of Piparia | Piparia, Satna dist., MP |
| Dashawatar Temple of Deogarh* | Deogarh, Lalitpur dist., Uttar Pradesh |
| Krishna Temple of Bhitargaon | Bhitargaon, Kanpur dist., UP |
| Luxman Temple of Sirpur | Sirpur, Raipur dist., Chhattisgarh |
| Temple of Mukund Darra | Mukund Darra, Kota dist., Rajasthan |
| Shiv Temple of Nagod | Nagod, Satna dist., MP |
| Shiv Temple of Khoh | Khoh, Satna dist., MP |
| Temple of Madhi | Madhi, Jabalpur dist., MP |
| Mani Nag Temple (Maniyar Math) of Rajgir | Rajgir, Nalanda dist., Bihar |

- Footnote (*, on Dashawatar Temple): **first example of Panchayatan style, first example of Shikhara style**. (⚠ the asterisk sits at the Dashawatar/Deogarh row region in the printed table; the book's note is attached to the table, likely to the Deogarh temple, and p.308 later says Dashawatar is the first example of Nagar style.)
- **Panchayatan style:** five shrines (1 principal + 4 lesser), each with its own Shikhara.

### A.5 Early Medieval architecture; temple-making styles (p.308)
- All-round development in the early medieval period; from the point of temple construction, it can be called the **golden period** of Indian history.
- On geographical basis three styles: **Nagar, Vesar, Dravida**.

| Style (time period) | Area of prevalence | Concerned dynasties |
|---|---|---|
| **Nagar** (Arya/Shikhara) style, 7th–13th century | From Himalaya to Vindhya, i.e., Northern India | Rajputa (Rajput) dynasties: Chauhan, Guhilot, Pratihar, Parmar, Chandela, Kalchuri, Solanki, Godhwal/Rathore; Regional dynasties: Kashmir, Pala & Sen of Bengal, Som/Kesari & Eastern Gang of Odisha |
| **Vesar** (Chalukya/Karnat-Dravida), mid 8th century–13th century | From Vindhya mountain to Krishna river, i.e., Upper South (esp. Kannada region) | Early Chalukya of Badami, Rashtrakuta of Manykhet (Malkhed), Eastern Chalukya of Vengi, Western Chalukya of Kalyani, Kakatiya of Varangal, Yadav of Deogiri, Hoysala of Dwarsamudra |
| **Dravida**, 7th–18th century | From Krishna river to Kanyakumari, i.e., Dakshinavarta/Lower South | Pallava of Kanchi, Chola of Tanjore, Pandya of Madurai, Vijaynagar empire, Nayak of Madurai |

- Note: styles sometimes transcend their area — e.g. **Vaishnav Temple of Vrindavan** built in Dravida style; some South Indian temples in Nagar style, like **Papanath Temple of Pattadakal**.

**Nagar style — main points (p.308)**
1. **Square ground-plan** (Swastikaar Aadhar Aayojan) and **curvilinear tower (Vakrarekhiy Shikhara)** = basic features.
2. Idol room = **Pratima Kaksh or Garbhagrih or Deval (Sanctum)**.
3. Curvilinear tower (Shikhara) over the main idol-room, thinner towards the top as height increased; side part (Anga-Shikhara) also reached the peak; vertical line predominated → **linear tower (Rekhiy Shikhara)**.
4. **Amalak** (looks like ribbed Amla/gooseberry) on top of the Shikhara in which the **urn (Kalash) and flag-pole (Dhwajdand)** were installed.
5. Credit for establishment of Nagar style goes to the **Gupta rulers**.
6. **Dashawatar temple (Deogarh, Lalitpur dist., UP)** = first example of Nagar style.
7. Style reached its **climax during Rajput rulers**.
- Note 1: shrines are of 3 types — **Sandhara** (with Pradakshina Patha), **Nirandhara** (without Pradakshina patha), **Sarvatobhadra** (can be accessed from all sides).
- Note 2: Synonyms of Devalaya (temple): **Devkula, Mandir, Kovil, Deol, Devasthanam, Devgrih, Devayatan, Prasada, Kshetram**.

### A.6 Dravida style; Nagar vs Dravida; Vesar (p.309)
**Dravida style — main points**
1. **Rectangular ground-plan** (Aayatakaar Aadhar Aayojan) and **pyramidal tower (Piramidiya Vimana)**.
2. Room for idol installation.
3. Pyramidal tower over the idol-room = **Vimana**; built in increasingly smaller sections in order of height.
4. Over the Vimana, **domed stupa or stupika** installed.
5. Later huge monumental **Gopurams (gateways)** added; in some temples the Gopuram is so high that even the main Vimana is not visible from the front.
6. Credit for establishing Dravida style goes to the **Pallava rulers of Kanchi**.
7. Climax during the **Chola rulers**; after **Rajendra-I** it lost architectural force and became more ornate.

| Feature | Nagar Style | Dravida Style |
|---|---|---|
| Superstructure | Curvilinear tower (Shikhara) | Pyramidal tower (Vimana) |
| Ground-plan | Square-shape (Vargakaar) | Rectangular-shape (Aayatakaar) |
| Gateway | Simple gateway (Saral Praveshdwar) | Huge gateway (Vishal Gopuram) |

**Vesar style — main points**
1. **Vesar** (Sanskrit) literally = **Khachchar (mule)** → **hybrid style (Dravida + Nagar)**.
2. Basically a modified form of Dravida style.
3. Basic planning and design = Dravida (Vimana pyramidal tower, Stupika, Mandapa) but decoration, representation, new symbols influenced by Nagar; elements of both mixed → also called **Mishra/Mishrit Shailee (mixed style)**.
4. Credit goes to **early Chalukyas (Chalukyas of Badami)** → also known as **Chalukya style**. Art historian **Adam Hardy** says **"Karnat-Dravida style"** is more appropriate for Chalukya temples of the Deccan rather than Vesar.
5. Reached its climax under **Hoysala rulers**; **stellate/star-shape ground plan** became popular in Hoysala period.
- Note: **Western Chalukya rulers of Kalyani** were the first to use stellate/star-shape ground-plan. Earliest example of stellate ground-plan = **Dodda Basappa temple (Dambal, Koppam (Koppal) district, Karnataka)** built by Western Chalukya ruler of Kalyani. Best example of Vesar style = **Hoysaleshwar temple of Halebid**.

**Temples of Nagar style (p.309)**
- States where Nagar temples prominent: **Odisha, Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan, Gujarat** etc.
- **Odisha** = richest state in Nagar temples, mainly at **Bhubaneshwar, Puri and Konark**, built **8th–13th century AD**. Three very famous: Lingaraj (Bhubaneshwar), Jagannath (Puri), Sun (Konark).
  1. **Lingaraj Temple** — Bhubaneshwar, **Khurda district**; main deity **Tribhubaneshwar Shiva = Lingaraj**; built by **Kesari ruler in 11th century AD**.
  2. **Jagannath Temple** — **Puri**, Puri district; deities **Jagannath (Krishna), brother Balbhadra (Balram), sister Subhadra**; built by **Eastern Gang king Anantverma Chodgang (1076–1147)**; other name **White Pagoda** (called by European mariners as a landmark).
  3. **Sun Temple** — **Konark, Puri district**; deity **Surya**; built by **Eastern Gang king Narsimhadeo (1238–64) during 1243–55**; other name **Black Pagoda** (European mariners, landmark).
- **Temples of Madhya Pradesh — 1. Khajuraho group:** **Khajuraho (Chhatarpur district)**; built by **Chandela kings 9th–12th century AD**; local tradition **85 temples**, now only **25** left; concerned with **Shaivite, Vaishnavite, Shakt and Jain** religion. Among Shaivite temples **Kandariya Mahadev temple** very famous (gateway looks like a Kandara/cave; main deity Mahadev); built by **Chandela king Dhangdeo** (continues next page).
### A.7 Khajuraho (cont.), other MP, Rajasthan, Gujarat temples (p.310)
- **Kandariya Mahadev** built by Chandela king **Dhangdeo in 1209 AD** (as printed; ⚠ Dhanga's reign is generally dated c. 950–1002 and Kandariya Mahadev c. 1030 — the printed "1209 AD" looks like a slip; verify).
- **Vaikunth Vaishnav or Luxman temple** (Vaishnavite) — built by Chandela king **Yashovarman in 939 AD**. Shakt temple **Mahisasurmardini** — **954 AD**.
- Other Khajuraho group temples (dates as printed): **64 Yogini (885), Lalgun Mahadev (900), Hanuman (922), Brahma (925), Varah (950), Ghantai — Jain (960), Vishwanath (999), Matangeshwar — Shaiv (1000), Ganesh (1000), Garud (1000), Devi Jagadambi (1023), Chitragupta (1023), Rishabhdeo/Adinath (1027), Vaman (1062), Javeri — Vaishnav (1090), Chaturbhuj (Vishnu) (1110), Dauladeo — Shaiv (1125)**.
- Apart from Gods/Goddesses, idols of Digpalas, Apsaras, animals and birds; influence of **Tantric ideology** visible in the erotic idols.
- **2. Other temples of MP:** **Keshavnarayan temple & Matsyendranath (Machhendranath) temple of Amarkantak (Anooppur district)**; **64 Yogini temple of Bheraghat (Jabalpur district)**; **Neelkanth/Udayeshwar temple of Udaypur gaon (Vidisha district)** built by **Parmar ruler Udayaditya in 11th century AD**.
- **Temples of Rajasthan — Dilwara's Jain temples** = most famous; on **Mount Abu (Arbud Parvat)**, Dilwara, **Sirohi district**; made of **white marble of Makrana**; the **Taj Mahal** is said to be inspired (from the point of art) by Dilwara's Jain temple. **5 temples**, 2 built in pre-medieval and 3 in medieval period:
  1. **Vimal Vasati / Rishabhdeo Temple (1031)** — built by **Vimal Shah (minister of Solanki ruler Bhim)**; deity **Rishabhdeo/Adinath**; fine carving; **earliest example of Jain architecture** (vasati/vasadi = jain temple).
  2. **Lun Vasati / Tejpal-Vastupal / Devarani-Jethani / Neminath Temple (1230)** — built by **Tejpal & Vastupal (two Jain ministers of Solanki ruler Vir Dhawal)** to commemorate late brother **Lun**; statues of **Devarani and Jethani** (spouses) at two sides of the door; deity **Neminath**.
  3. **Parshwanath / Chaumukh Temple (1458)** — built by **Mandalik (a Jain follower)**; deity Parshwanath; **the highest temple** in Dilwara.
  4. **Rishabhdeo / Adinath temple (1468)** — built by **Bhima Shah (minister of Mehmud Begara, Sultan of Ahmedabad)**; idol of Rishabhdeo in **Panchdhatu** (main metal Pital).
  5. **Mahavir Swami Temple (1582)** — deity **Mahavir Swami, 24th and last Teerthankar**.
- **Osian (Jodhpur district)** — famous for Jain and Brahmanic (Hindu) temples, e.g. **Mahavir Swami temple, Surya (Sun) temple** etc.
- **Temples of Gujarat:** Nagar-style temples built in early medieval period mostly by **Solanki rulers of Anihalwara**:
  1. **Surya (Sun) temple of Modhera (Mehsana dist.)** — built by Solanki ruler **Bhim in 1026–27**.
  2. **Someshwar Mahadev Temple of Somnath (Junagarh dist.)** — rebuilt by Solanki ruler Bhim.
  3. **Neelkanth Mahadev Temple of Sunak (Mehsana dist.)** — **Solanki ruler Jaisingh Siddhraj, 1095**.
  4. **Rudramahalaya / Rudramal Temple of Siddhpur (Patan dist.)** — Jaisingh Siddhraj.
  5. **Neminath & Mallinath Temple of Girnar parvat / Raiwatgiri / Raiwatak parvat (Junagarh dist.)** — Neminath temple built **1128–29**; Mallinath built by **Vastupal & Tejpal**.
  6. **Chaumukh Adinath Temple at Satrunjay Pahari of Palitana (Bhavnagar dist.)** — built by a rich merchant in **1618**.

### A.8 Temples of Vesar and Dravida style (pp.310–311)
- **Vesar temples** built by **Early Chalukya of Badami (543–757), Rashtrakuta of Manykhet (752–973), Western Chalukya of Kalyani (973–1190), Hoysala of Dwarsamudra (12th–14th century)**.
- Under Early Chalukya of Badami, **Nagar and Dravida styles built simultaneously at Aihole and Pattadkal in 7th–8th century** — e.g. **Papnath temple (Nagar)**, **Sagameshwar & Virupaksh temples (Dravida)** → new **Vesar style** combining both.
- **Rashtrakuta Architecture (752–973):** cave temples of **Ellora and Elephanta**. At Ellora: **Ravan ki Khai, Dashawatar, Kailashnath temple**. At Elephanta: **Trimurti of Maheshwar, Shiv Tandav (Natraj), Shiv Parvati Vivah**.
- Western Chalukya (Kalyani) and Hoysala notable temples: **Kalleshwar (Kuknoor, Koppal dist., Karnataka), Lakkundi/Laukikgundi Jain temple (Dharwad dist.), Mukteshwar, Siddheshwar, Someshwar & Siddh Rameshwar temples (Dharwad dist.), Mahadev temple (Ittagi), Kashi Vishweshwar temple (Kakkundi), Mallikarjuna temple (Bellary dist.), Hoysaleshwar temple (Halebid)**.
- **Dravida temples** built by **Pallava of Kanchi, Chola of Tanjore, Pandya of Madurai** in early medieval period.
- **Pallava Architecture (575–897):** Pallava rulers of Kanchi laid the foundation of Dravida style; **four styles** during the Pallava period:

| Style (ruler) | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Mahendravarman** | Bhairavkot temple (Arcot dist.), Anantshayi Vishnu temple at Undavalli (Guntoor dist.) etc. |
| **Mammal (Narsinghvarman / Narasimhavarman Mamalla)** | Mandap temples (Varah, Mahisasurmardini, Panch Pandav temple); Rath temples (Dharmraj, Bhim, Arjun, Sahdeo, Ganesh, Pirari, Valaiyamkutti temple) |
| **Rajsingh (Narsinghvarman-II)** | Vaikunth Perumal of Kanchi; Kailashnath/Rajsingheshwar temple of Kanchi; Shore temples (Shore, Ishwar, Mukund temple) of Mammalpuram |
| **Aparajit** | Mukteshwar temple of Kanchi, Matangeshwar temple of Kanchi, Parshurameshwar temple of Guddimallam etc. |

- "It is called Shore Temple because it is located near sea-shore."
- **Chola Architecture (850–1279):** two very famous — **Vrihadeshwar/Rajrajeshwar temple of Tanjore by Rajraj-I** and **Temple of Gangaikondacholapuram built by Rajendra-I**.
- **Pandya Architecture (1190–1311):** Dravida continued but **no important temple**; boundary walls built around temples with grand, huge, ornate **Gopurams** — speciality is the Gopurams (**Tirumalai temple, Chidambaram temple, Kumbkonam temple**).

### A.9 SULTANATE ARCHITECTURE — Delhi Sultanate (pp.311 onwards)
- Style developed under Turk-Afghan rulers = **Indo-Islamic style**: synthesis of **Hindustani Shahteeri (Trabeate tradition, based on beam)** and **Islamic Mehrabi (Arcuate tradition, based on arch)**.
- Features derived from both Hindu and Muslim sources; in Delhi Sultanate Islamic influence predominant, but not so in provincial architecture (**Bengal, Bijapur, Gujarat**).
- **Characteristic features:**
  1. **Arch (Mehrab), Dome (Gumbad):** Muslims credited with introducing **true arches** (replaced flat lintels/corbelled ceilings); also introduced **dome** (convex covering, usually hemispherical/semi-elliptical, over circular/polygonal space; replaced pyramidal roof/spire); also **vaults** (chamber roofed with arch-shaped ceiling) and **underground chamber** (burial/storage).
  2. **Mosque (Masjid)** — Arabic, place where Muslims prostrate (Sajda); prayer chamber spacious, lighted and open (collective namaz) vs small, dark, closed Hindu temple sanctum.
  3. **Tower (Minar)** — usually circular, great height; initially part of mosque for the Muezzin's call; later built independently to preserve memory of victory.
  4. **Tomb (Maqbara)** — Arabic, building over grave (**Qabr**); burial vs Hindu cremation; usually **domed (Huzara)** with **Mihrab in the western wall** and real grave in an underground chamber; bigger tombs also have a mosque, structure in the middle of a sprawling lawn.
  5. **New theme of ornamentation:** Hindu = naturalistic (human/animal forms, luxuriant vegetation); Muslims = representation of living beings taboo → **geometrical and Arabian patterns, ornamental writing (calligraphy), inlay work, glazed tiles, carved screens, formal plant/floral representation**.
  6. **New materials:** Muslims made extensive use of **concrete and lime mortar** (earlier mud for brickwork; large stone blocks held by iron clamps); lime as plaster and base for decoration.
- **Sultan's contribution:** Sultanate architecture in **4 phases — Early Turk, Khalji, Tughlaq, Sayyed-Lodi**.
- **Early Turk Phase (1206–90):** **Qutubuddin Aibak (1206–10)** — declared himself Sultan 1206; began architecture with the conquest of Delhi (**1193**); converted **Qila Rai Pithaura** into the capital; a mosque built in the centre of the city initially known as **Jami** (continues next page).
### A.10 Early Turk phase (cont.), Khalji and Tughlaq phases (p.312)
- Aibak's central mosque = initially **Jami Masjid**, later **Qubbat-ul-Islam (Dome of Islam)** or **Quwwat-ul-Islam (Might of Islam)**; also built **Dhai Din ka Jhonpara (Ajmer)** and began **Qutub Minar (Delhi)**.
  1. **Quwwat-ul-Islam Masjid** — **Mehrauli, Delhi**; **first building of Indo-Islamic architectural style**; on the occasion of Delhi conquest (per **John Marshall**); built from **dismantled remains of temples**; built **1193–98**; later parts added by **Iltutmish & Alauddin Khalji**.
  2. **Adhai Dhin Ka Jhonpara Masjid** — **Ajmer**; name from a fair held for **Adhai Din (2½ days)**; built **1195–97** from dismantled temple remains; excavated stone plates inscribe parts of Vigrahraj's **Harikeli Natak** and Somdeo's **Lalit Vigrahraj** (Sanskrit drama) (kept in Ajmer Museum).
  3. **Qutub Minar** — **Mehrauli, Delhi**; purpose: to declare power of Islam to inhabitants of conquered territories (per **Percy Brown**); named after Sufi **Khwaja Qutubuddin Bakhtiyar Kaki**; built **1199–1236**; **first storey by Qutubuddin Aibak; 2nd, 3rd, 4th storey by Iltutmish**; fourth storey struck by lightning **1370**, then **Firoz Tughlaq** replaced it with **two more storeys**; **first building in which no dismantled temple remains were used** (first outlined and constructed Islamic building).
- **Iltutmish (1211–36):** completed Quwwat-ul-Islam, Adhai Din ka Jhonpara & Qutub Minar; built **Sultanghari/Sultangarhi (Tomb of Nasiruddin Mehmud)**, **Tomb of Iltutmish** etc.; known as **'Father/Founder of Maqbara (Tomb/Mausoleum) Architecture'**.
  1. **Sultanghari / Sultangarhi / Tomb of Nasiruddin Mehmud** — **Malkapur, Delhi**; **first example of Maqbara**; in memory of Prince Nasiruddin Mehmud; **1231–32**.
  2. **Tomb of Iltutmish** — north-east of Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque, Mehrauli; begun **1235**.
  3. Other buildings: **Shamsi Idgah, Badayun; Jama Masjid, Badayun; Hauz-i-Shamshi, Mehrauli; Atarkin Darwaja, Nagaur [inspiration of Buland Darwaja (Akbar)]; Khwaja Moinuddin Chisti Dargah, Ajmer**.
- **Balban (1266–87):** built **Tomb of Balban** and **Tomb of Khan Shaheed**.
  1. **Tomb of Balban** — **Qila Rai Pithaura**, built **1287**; **first building in which true arch (Mehrab) was used**; dome has not survived — **Alai Darwaja is the first building whose dome survived**.
  2. **Tomb of Khan Shaheed** — near Balban's tomb; **Muhammad Khan**, elder son of Balban, called Khan Shaheed for dying fighting the **Mongols in 1285**.
- **Khalji phase (1290–1320):** second phase; Islamic influence very clear; ruled ~30 years so few buildings — **Alai Darwaja, Jamatkhana Masjid** notable.
  - **Alauddin Khalji (1296–1316):** very fond of architecture.
  1. **Siri (new city)** — founded **1300** at Siri village (north of Qutub area, Delhi); palace **Hazarsutun Mahal (1,000 pillars)**; giant pond **Hauz-i-Khas / Hauz-i-Alai** on the western side.
  2. **Alai Darwaja** — southern gate of Quwwat-ul-Islam masjid; completed **1310–11**; **horseshoe-shaped arch; earliest example of true arch in India**; "real monument of Alauddin Khalji's pride".
  3. **Alai Minar** — meant to be twice the height of Qutub Minar; only **one storey** built when he died; incomplete.
  4. **Jamatkhana Masjid** — campus of **Nizamuddin Auliya Dargah**; **earliest example of a Masjid completely based on Islamic ideals**.
  - Successors' buildings: **Nizamuddin Auliya Dargah** (built by **Khizr Khan**, son of Alauddin Khalji), **Ukha Masjid** (**Bayana, Bharatpur**, built by **Malik Kafur in 1320** during reign of **Kutbuddin Mubarak Khalji**).
- **Tughlaq phase (1320–1414):** third phase; dense decoration replaced by Islamic simplicity and seriousness. Own features: **sloping/battered walls, octagonal tombs, fortified settlements**.
  - **Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq (1320–25)** — founder; founded **Tughlaqabad**, built his own tomb.

### A.11 Tughlaq phase (cont.), Sayyed-Lodi phase, provincial (p.313)
1. **Tughlaqabad City (1321)** — east of Qutub region; fort **Chhappan Kot**; walls **inclined inwards like Egyptian pyramid — 75° angle** with ground (general walls 90°) = speciality of Tughlaq buildings.
2. **Tomb of Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq** — middle of an artificial lake inside Tughlaqabad; inward-inclined walls; burj/minar at every corner; huge **marble dome (Gumbaj)**.
3. **Tomb of Shekh Ruknuddin Abul Fataeh (Rukn-e-Alam)** — **Multan**; sufi saint patronised by Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq.
- **Muhammad-bin-Tughlaq (1325–51; printed "1325-11")** — ambitious and experimental; most buildings ruined; remains: **Adilabad Fort** (also **Muhammadabad Fort**, north-east of Tughlaqabad) and **Jahanpanah City** (only **Satpula (bridge)** and **Vijay Mandal (Palace)** remain).
- **Firoz Tughlaq (1351–88)** — great builder; founded **Firozabad, Fatehabad, Hisar Firoza, Jaunpur** etc.; **Firozabad** most notable (between mountain part (North) and **Hauz-i-Khas (South)** of Delhi); famous buildings: **Jama Masjid, Kushk-i-Shikar palace, Kotla Firozabad (palace)**. Two tombs: **Tomb of Khan-i-Jahan Telangani (1370–71)** and **Tomb of Firoz Tughlaq (1388)**.
- **Tomb of Khan-i-Jahan Telangani (1370–71):** built by **Jauna Shah**, son of Malik Maqbul Khan-i-Jahan Telangani (Wazir of Firoz Tughlaq), in memory of his father; **first building of Delhi Sultanate based on octagonal ground-plan (Ashtbujakar/Athpahlu adhar-ayojan)**; forerunner of octagonal tombs of Sayyed-Lodi period.
- **Sayyed-Lodi phase (1414–1526):** fourth phase.
  - **Sayyed Sultans (1414–50):** founded **Khizrabad (Khizr Khan)** and **Mubarkabad (Mubarak Shah)**; tombs of **Mubarak Shah** and **Muhammad Shah Sayyed** — octagonal ground plan.
  - **Lodi Sultans (1451–1526):** interest in **tombs (Maqbaras)** → "period of tombs" (per **Percy Brown**). **Sikandar Lodi** founded **Agra City (1504)** and shifted the capital from Delhi to Agra.
  - Lodi tombs in two groups: **Square-shaped / Quadrilateral (Chaukor / Chaturbhuji)** and **Octagonal (Athpathu / Ashtbhuji)**.

| Type | Examples (as printed) |
|---|---|
| Quadrilateral tombs | **Kale Khan Gumbaj** (Mubarak Khan Lohani, **1481**, Mubarakpur); **Bahlol Lodi Tomb** (Sikandar Lodi, **1489**, Chirag Delhi); **Shihabuddin Taj Khan / Bagh-i-Alam Gumbad** (**1501**, Hauz Khas); **Ibrahim Lodi Tomb** (Panipat, Haryana) |
| Octagonal tombs | **Mubark Shah Tomb** (Mubark Shah, **1434**, Kotla Mubarakpur, Delhi); **Muhammad Shah Sayyed Tomb** (Alauddin Alam Shah, son of Muhammad Shah Sayyed, **1414**, Lodi Garden, Delhi); **Sikandar Lodi Tomb** (Ibrahim Lodi, **1517–18**, Lodi Garden); **Hasan Khan Sur Tomb** (Shershah, **1535**, Sasaram, Bihar); **Shershah Tomb** (begun by Shershah Sur, completed by son **Islam/Salim Shah Sur, 1545**, Sasaram, Bihar); **Islam/Salim Shah Sur Tomb** (begun, not completed due to his death in **1554**, Sasaram); **Isha Khan Niyazi Tomb** (minister (Amir) of Islam/Salim Shah Sur, **1547**, Nizamuddin, Delhi) |
| Mosques | **Bada Gumbad Masjid** (1484–94, Lodi Garden, Delhi); **Khairpur Masjid** (Khairpur gaon, Mehrauli, Delhi); **Moth Masjid** (Miyan Bhuwa, minister of Sikandar Lodi, **1505**, South Delhi); **Qila-i-Quhan / Purana Qila Masjid** (Shershah Sur, **1541**, Purana Qila, Delhi) |

**II. The Provincial Architecture** (Sultanate period 1206–1526)

| Province (period) | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Kashmir (1003–1540)** | **Shah-i-Hamdani Masjid** (**1395**, Sultan Sikandar); Tomb of **Jainulabidin's mother** and **Tomb of Madin Shah** (built by Jainulabidin) |
| **Mewar (1326–1680)** | **Annapurna Temple** (Chittor, Hammir); **Pichhaula Lake** (Udaypur, Laksh Singh); **Vijay Stambh (Tower of Victory), Kumbha Swami Temple, Chaturmukh Vihar Temple, 32 Forts of Mewar** (Rana Kumbha); **Rajsamand Lake** (Raj Singh) |
| **Bengal (1345–1576)** | **Adina Masjid** (Pandua, **Sikandar Shah, 1368**); **Chhota Sona Masjid, Bara Sona Masjid & Kadam Rasul Masjid** (Gaur, Nusarat Shah); **Dakhil Darwaja** (**1459–74**, Barbak Shah); **Firoz/Chirag Minar** (**1485–89**, Saifuddin Firoz) |
| **Jaunpur (1399–1484)** | **Atala Devi's Masjid** (Ibrahim Shah Sharqi, **1408**); **Jhanjhari (perforated) Masjid** (Ibrahim Shah Sharqi, **1430**); **Jami Masjid** (**1438–78**); **Lal Darwaja Masjid** (**1457**) |
| **Malwa (1401–1562)** | **Kamal Maula Masjid** (Dhar, **1400**); **Lal Masjid** (Dhar, **1405**); **Dilawar Khan's Masjid** (Mandu, **1405**); **Jami Masjid** (Mandu, Sultan Hushang, **1405–35**); **Sultan Hushang's Maqbara, Ashrafi Mahal, Hindola Mahal (looks like a railway bridge), Jahaz Mahal (looks like a ship)** [Baz Bahadur: **1552–62**]; **Tarkin Dargah** (Nagaur, memory of Sufi saint **Shekh Hamiduddin Nagauri / Sultan-ul-Tarkin**, a title given by his pir Moinuddin Chisti); **Shams Masjid** (Nagaur) |

(⚠ Dates and attributions as printed; e.g. "Jahaz Mahal … [built by Baz Bahadur 1552–62]" looks like a bracket referring to the Mandu group — the Jahaz Mahal is usually attributed to Ghiyath Shah; verify.)
### A.12 Provincial (cont.): Gujarat, Deccan; Mughal architecture — features; Babur (p.314)
| Province / style (period) | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Gujarat (1407–1573)** | **Haibat Khan's Masjid** (Ahmedabad); **Sayyed Alam's Masjid & Jami Masjid** (Ahmed Shah, **1411–12**); **Champaner, Junagarh & Kheda city** (founded by **Mahmud Begarha: 1459–1511**); **Jami Masjid & Nagina Masjid** (Champaner, Mahmud Begarha); **Rauza of Rani Sipri/Sabrai** (Ahmedabad, **1514**, Muzaffar Shah II: 1511–26); **Sidi Sayyed Masjid** (**1515**, Muzaffar Shah II) |
| **Deccan style — Vijayanagar (1336–1665)** | **Vithal Swami Temple & Hazarrama Temple** (Hampi); **Tadapatri & Parvati Temple** (Chidambaram); **Varadraja & Ekambarnath Temple** (Kanchipuram) |
| **Madurai/Nayak (1529–1736)** | **Minakshi Temple** (Madurai); **Rangnath Temple** (Srirangam) |
| **Bahmani (1347–1526)** | **Bala Hisar & Jami Masjid** (Gulbarga); **Sola Khamba Masjid** (Bidar); **Mahmud Gawan's Madrasa** (Bidar, **1472**; Mahmud Gawan = prime minister of Bahmani Sultans) |
| **Adilshahi (Bijapur)** | **Jami Masjid** (Bijapur); **Gagan Mahal** (**1560**, Ali Adil Shah); **Ibrahim Rauza** (**1627**, Ibrahim Adil Shah); **Muhammad Adil Shah's Maqbara (Gol Gumbaj, 1656–60, famous for whispering gallery)** |
| **Nizam Shahi (Ahmednagar)** | Ahmednagar Fort; **Qasim Khan's palace & Bagh-i-hast-bihisht** (Malik Ahmed Nizam Shah: **1490–1510**); **Tomb of Salabat Khan / Chand Bibi's Palace** |
| **Qutub Shahi (Golconda)** | **Char Minar** (**1591**, Quli Qutub Shah); **Muhammad Quli Qutub Shah's Maqbara (1625)** |
| **Barid Shahi (Bidar)** | **Amir Barid Shah's Maqbara** (Bidar) |

**Mughal architecture**
- Climax of Indo-Islamic architecture that began in the 13th century (Sultanate). Per **Percy Brown**, reasons for all-round development: aesthetic nature of Mughal rulers, personal interest of rulers, wealth and power of the Empire, relatively settled conditions, intimate association between crown and craftsmen.
- **Characteristic features:**
  1. **Large scale construction and superior material** — initially sandstone, later marble (before Mughals, gravel/broken stone/brick used).
  2. **Outlining and enactment in a planned manner** — detailed plans, especially by **Akbar & Shahjahan**; emperor supervised.
  3. **Diversity in design, construction and decoration** — special attention to **plaster and stucco**; **Pietra Dura** first used (coloured stone on white marble, floral design) — in India started by **Jahangir**, climax in **Shahjahan's** time.
  4. **Harmony and balance between built and empty spaces** — perfect harmony with surroundings; multi-storey flat constructions and vertical towers kept minimal.
  5. **Introduction of Char Bagh system** — Persian; quadrilateral gardens divided into four square parts, symbolic of **four parts of heaven (Jannat/Firdaus)** in Quran; used in Mughal tombs/mausoleums; to give divine status to the deceased and lineage.
- **Percy Brown:** "The monument produced during the Mughal period may be regarded as representing 'Indian summer' of Moslem art and architecture…" → Mughal period = most important period of art and architecture.

**Babur (1526–30)** — cultured, aesthetic; did not appreciate Turk-Afghan buildings; sent for pupils of famous **Albanian architect Sinan** to work with Indian craftsmen whose skill he admired in the palaces of **Man Singh and Vikramajit at Gwalior**; fond of building but time did not permit.
- **Baghs:** 1. **Aaram Bagh of Agra (1528, the oldest Mughal Bagh)**; 2. **Panipat Bagh** (Panipat dist., Haryana).
- **Masjids:** 1. **Kabuli Bagh Masjid, Panipat (1527)**, named after Babur's wife **Kabuli Beghum**, **first Masjid built by Mughals**; 2. **Jamali-Kamali Masjid, Mehrauli** (begun by Babur **1528–29**, completed by Humayun in **1536**; named after Sufi poet & saint **Shekh Fazallullah Jamali** and his brother Kamali).

### A.13 Humayun and Akbar (p.315)
**Humayun (1530–40, 1555–56)** — inherited taste for fine arts, hardly found time. Ascended 1530; **1540** Shershah drove him out; after 15-year exile in Persia returned, defeated **Sikandar Shah Sur** (nephew of Shershah), throne **July 1555**, died after six months (**January 1556**).
- **City:** **Din Panah** — founded **1533**; destroyed by Shershah, who established **Purana Qila**; **Sher Mandal** (in Purana Qila) used as library by Humayun; he fell on the stairs after hearing the Muezzin and died of injuries, January 1556. **Lane Poole:** "Humayun tumbled through life and he tumbled out of it".
- **Mosques:** 1. Masjid at **Agra (1530)**; 2. Masjid at **Fatehabad (1540)**.
- **Tombs:**
  1. **Humayun's Tomb** — **Delhi**; **1562–72**; commissioned by **Hamida Banu Beghum**, built by **Bega Beghum (Hazi Beghum)**; **architect Mirak Mirza Ghiyas**; specialities: (a) **first Mughal tomb, first Indian tomb with Char Bagh system**; (b) **first example of double dome in India**; (c) **first Mughal building using white marble**; (d) synthesis of Indian and Persian traditions; (e) **inspiration of Taj Mahal**; (f) later buried in premises: Bega Beghum, Hamida Banu Beghum, **Dara Shikoh, Jahandar Shah, Farrukhsiyar, Rafi-ud-Darzat, Rafi-ud-Daula, Alamghir-II**; (g) last Mughal ruler **Bahadur Shah 'Zafar'** hid here during the **revolt of 1857**, captured and sent to Rangoon.
  2. **Sufi Muhammad Gaus's Tomb, Hajira, Gwalior** — contemporary of Humayun's tomb.
  - Note: these tombs concern Humayun but were built in **Akbar's** time — so Akbar = **'Father of Mughal Maqbara system'** (as printed).

**Akbar (1556–1605)** — inherited taste from Humayun and Babur; order and prosperity of long reign helped building. **Abul Fazal**: emperor planned beautiful buildings. Most buildings of **red sandstone** → **'Red Sandstone Age'**; white marble in some places.
- Capital: **Delhi & Agra 1556–64**, **Agra 1564–73**, **Fatehpur Sikri 1573–85**, **Lahore 1585–98**, **Agra 1598–1605**.
- **Prominent forts:** **Agra Fort (1564), Lahore Fort (1566), Ajmer Fort (1570), Attock Fort (1581), Allahabad Fort (1585)**.
- Buildings in three groups: Delhi, Agra Fort, Fatehpur Sikri.
- **(A) Delhi:** 1. **Khair-ul-Manazil Masjid** (1561–62, by **Maham Anaga**); 2. **Adham Khan's Maqbara** (1562, by Akbar); 3. **Humayun's Maqbara** (1565–72, by Bega Beghum / Hazi Beghum); 4. **Ataga Khan's Maqbara** (1566–67; husband of **Jiji Anaga**).
- **(B) Agra Fort:** **first fort built by Akbar**; **1564–80**; originally 4 gates, 2 closed; remaining **Delhi Dwar** (also **Hathiya Paur/Hathiya Pol**, two elephant statues) and **Lahore Dwar** (also **Akbar Dwar**; renamed **'Amar Singh Dwar'** by Shahjahan). Per **Abul Fazal** Akbar built **500 buildings** in Agra Fort; remaining: **Akbari Mahal** (two-storey, c.**1569**) and **Jahangiri Mahal** (built **1570**; **Hauz-i-Jahangir** = Jahangir's monolithic portable bath-tub now at main gate; inspired by **Man Mandir of Gwalior**).
- **(C) Fatehpur Sikri:** Sikri was a village where **Shekh Salim Chisti** was born; Akbar got son **Salim (Jahangir)** with his blessing → built fort **1571–85**; capital shifted from Agra **1573**; renamed **Fatehpur** to commemorate **Gujarat victory**; **abandoned 1585** (water shortage or shift to Lahore — undecided). Main buildings listed from **Buland Darwaja** inward (continues next page).
### A.14 Fatehpur Sikri buildings; Jahangir; Shahjahan (p.316)
**Fatehpur Sikri — main buildings (from Buland Darwaja inward)**
1. **Buland Darwaja** — built **1575–76** to commemorate **Gujarat victory (1573)**; **15-storey**; Persian inscriptions: (i) saying of **Jesus Christ (Isa)**: "The world is a bridge, pass over it, but build no houses upon it"; (ii) saying of Prophet Mohammad: Ayats of Quran.
2. **Jama Masjid** — replica of Masjid of Mecca but fusion of Islamic and Hindu style; **Badshahi Darwaja** at eastern part; **Tomb of Shekh Salim Chisti** and his grandson **Islam Khan** (built by **Jahangir in 1617**).
3. **Mariam-uj-Jamani Mahal** — biggest palace of Sikri Fort; residence of Mariam-uj-Jamani (mother of world).
4. **Birbal Mahal** — original name of Birbal **Mahesh Das**, jewel of Akbar's Navratna.
5. **Mariam Palace** — residence of Akbar's **Goan wife Mariam**.
6. **Hawa Mahal (Panch Mahal)** — **5-storey** building; inspired by **Buddhist Vihara**.
7. **Tansen Mahal** — Tansen's original name **Ramtanu Pandey**, jewel of Navratna.
8. Other buildings: Turkey Sultana Mahal, **Diwan-i-Khas, Diwan-i-Aam, Naubat Khan/Naqqarkhana, Khan Mahal, Ibadatkhana, Abul Faizi Mahal, Abul Fazal Mahal, Raja Todarmal Mahal, Shahi Ghursal, Shahi Taksal, Shahi Khazana, Carvan Sarai, Hiran Minar, Naumahal, Aankh-Michauli, Pachisi Prangan, Mina Bazar** etc.

**Jahangir (1605–28)** — more interest in painting than architecture; most significant feature = **introduction of Pietra Dura**.

| Gardens (Baghs) | |
|---|---|
| 1 | **Shalimar Bagh, Srinagar** — built **1619** |
| 2 | **Dilkusha Bagh, Shahdara, Lahore** — originally by Akbar; enlarged by Jahangir; Jahangir, **Nurjahan & Asaf Khan (Nurjahan's brother)** buried here |

| Tombs (Maqbaras) | |
|---|---|
| 1 | **Akbar's Maqbara, Sikandara, Agra** — begun by Akbar, completed by Jahangir **1613**; fusion of Islamic, Hindu, Buddhist & Christian styles |
| 2 | **Mariam-uj-Jamani's Maqbara, Sikandara, Agra** — Jahangir, for his mother |
| 3 | **Itmad-ud-Daula's Maqbara, Agra** — **1622–28**; by **Nurjahan** for her father (**Mirza Ghiyas Beg, title Itmad-ud-Daula**) and mother (**Asmat Beghum**); **first Mughal building wholly of marble** and **first Mughal building with Pietra Dura**; also 'Laghu Taj' (small Taj) / 'Bachcha Taj' (child Taj) |
| 4 | **Abdurrahim Khan-i-khana's Maqbara, Delhi** — by Abdurrahim Khan-i-khana (jewel of Akbar's Navratna); graves of him and wife |
| 5 | **So-called Anarkali's Maqbara, Lahore** — actually the Maqbara of **Sahib-i-Jamal (wife of Jahangir)**; the **Anarkali story was fabricated by English William Finch, Edward Terry** etc. (as printed) |

- (⚠ In the printed Itmad-ud-Daula entry the items "3." appears twice; I merged.)

**Shahjahan (1628–58)** — brought Mughal architecture to climax; **Percy Brown**: 'the **Golden Age of Mughal Architecture**'; called **'Engineer King'** and **'Prince of Builders'**.
- Chief characteristics: 1. Extensive use of **marble** (available from **Makrana (Jodhpur) and Jaipur**); 2. **square-shape base**; 3. squar(e) structure (dome etc.); 4. **onion-like dome with thin neck**; 5. **double dome**; 6. **foliated arches** etc.
- Five groups of buildings:
  - **(A) Agra Fort:** 1. **Khas Mahal** (for Beghums of harem); 2. **Shish Mahal** (glass inlay; suited to summer); 3. **Musamman Burj / Shah Burj / Chameli Mahal** — **6-storey**; Shahjahan spent last **8 years (1658–66)** in captivity and died here; 4. **Diwan-i-Aam**; 5. **Diwan-i-Khas**.
  - **(B) Taj Mahal of Agra:** Taj (crown) of Mahals; **1631–55 (22 years)**; built by Shahjahan in memory of **Mumtaz Mahal** (note: named 'Taj' with 'J' vs Mumtaz with 'Z'); land — per **Abdul Hamid and Mulla Muhammad Saleh**, **Jai Singh (grandson of Man Singh)** was given other land in exchange; the claim it was originally a Hindu palace is called a lie/rumour by the book; **Outliner: Ustad Ahmed Khan Lahori**; **Builder: Ustad Ishakhan (Isha Khan)** (as printed); material marble with Pietra Dura. **Rabindranath Tagore**: "Taj — an immortal poem of love and indelible tear-drop, the beloved's vow on the cheek of eternity" (⚠ quote as printed in book; I cannot confirm it is verbatim); **UNESCO World Heritage 1983**.
  - **(C) Red Fort (Lal Qila), Delhi:** begins on p.316–317.

### A.15 Red Fort, Jama Masjid, Aurangzeb, Mughal Baghs, Modern architecture (p.317)
- Shahjahan founded **Shahjahanabad**; new fort built **1639–48** under architects **Hamid and Ahmed**; called **Red Fort (Lal Qila)** for the red stone boundary wall; also **Qila-i-Mubark**. Two gateways: **Delhi Gate (Dehli Darwaja) south** and **Lahori Gate (Lahori Darwaja) west**.
- Notable buildings: **Naubat Khana/Naqqar Khana; Diwan-i-Aam; Diwan-i-Khas; Khas Mahal; Rang Mahal/Shesh Mahal; Moti Masjid** (by **Aurangzeb in 1662**); Mumtaz Mahal; Zafar Mahal; **Musamman Burj/Shah Burj**.
- **(D) Jama Masjid and other Delhi buildings:** **Jama Masjid** — one km west of Red Fort; **1650–56**; originally **'Masjid-i Jahannuma'**; last construction by Shahjahan; **largest mosque in India**. **Fatehpuri Masjid** — built by Shahjahan's wife **Fatehpuri Beghum**.
- **(E) Lahore:** Shahjahan built **Diwan-i-Aam, Shah Burj, Sheesh Burj, Naulakha Mahal, Khwabgah** in Lahore Fort; **Asaf Khan's Maqbara, Shahdara, Lahore** (Asif Khan = PM of Shahjahan).
- **Aurangzeb (1658–1707) and After:** Mughal architecture deteriorated after Shahjahan; little interest, constant wars/economic troubles. Buildings: **Moti Masjid at Red Fort (1662)**; **Bibi's Maqbara / Rabia-ud-Durrani's Maqbara, Aurangabad (1668–78)**; **Badshahi Masjid, Lahore (1674)**; **Aurangzeb's Maqbara at Shekh Jainuddin Dargah campus (1707)**.
- **Bibi's Maqbara / Rabia-ud-Durrani's Maqbara (Aurangabad, Maharashtra):** (a) in memory of first and beloved wife **Dilras Banu Beghum** (died **1657**; title **Rabia-ud-Durrani**); (b) approved **1660**; entrusted to **Attaullah Khan** (son of Ustad Ahmed Khan Lahori, architect of Taj Mahal), built in 10 years (**1668–78**); (c) on the pattern of Taj Mahal — **half the size**, **black marbles** (as printed; ⚠ commonly described as white marble apart from the dome; verify) → poor imitation; (d) **'Taj of Deccan'** / **'Poor Taj'** (low budget); (e) official name **'Rabia-ud-Durrani's Maqbara'**, public knows as **Bibi's Maqbara**; (f) shows the declining phase of Mughal architecture.
- **Safdarjung's Maqbara, Delhi** — built by **Shuja-ud-Daula** (son of Mirza Mukim Abul Mansur Khan, title **Safdarjung**, wazir of the Mughal emperor) in **1753–54**; described as **'the extinguishing flame of the lamp of Mughal architecture'**.

**Mughal Baghs**

| No. | Bagh | Built by |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Aaram Bagh at Agra (1528) | Babur |
| 2 | Panipat Bagh at Panipat | Babur |
| 3 | Dilkusha Bagh at Lahore (1557) | Akbar |
| 4 | Shalimar Bagh at Srinagar (1619) | Jahangir |
| 5 | Nishat Bagh at Srinagar (1633) | Asaf Khan (Nurjahan's brother) |
| 6 | Shalimar Bagh at Lahore (1642) | Shahjahan |
| 7 | Shalimar Bagh / Aizzabad Bagh at Delhi (1642) | Aizzabad Beghum (Shahjahan's wife) |
