| 8 | Hayatbaksh Bagh at Red Fort, Delhi | Shahjahan |
| 9 | Maahtab Bagh at the bank of Yamuna behind Taj Mahal, Agra (not survived at present) | Shahjahan |
| 10 | Wazir Bagh at Kashmir | Dara Shikoh |
| 11 | Mughal Bagh at Pinjore, Haryana | Muzaffar Hussain Koka alias Nawab Fidai Khan Koka (Aurangzeb's cousin brother) |
| 12 | Chaharburji Bagh at Lahore | Jebunnisa (Aurangzeb's daughter) |

(⚠ Book's entry for #3 says Dilkusha Bagh, Lahore (1557) by Akbar while the Jahangir section says Dilkusha Bagh Shahdara Lahore "originally built by Akbar" — consistent.)

**Modern India Architecture** — British powerful during Mughal decline; ruled **1757–1947**.

| Centre | Architectural specimens |
|---|---|
| **Bombay (Mumbai)** | Mumbai University (**1878**); **Chhatrapati Shivaji Terminus–CST (1887)**; **Gateway of India (1924**; to commemorate arrival (**1911**) of **King George V**) |
| **Madras (Chennai)** | **Fort Saint George (1641)**; **Chepauk Palace (1768)**; Chennai Railway Station (**1873**); Madras University (**1857**); General Post Office (**1884**); **Rippon Bhawan (1913)** |
| **Calcutta (Kolkata)** | **Fort William (1702)**; **Writers' Building (1777)**; **Government House (1803, now Raj Bhawan)**; Town Hall (**1813**); **Indian Museum (1814)**; **National Library (1836)**; **Howrah Railway Station (1854)**; Calcutta High Court (**1862**); GPO (**1868**); **Victoria Memorial (1921)** etc. |
**Modern India architecture table — continued (book p.318 top)**

| Centre | Architectural specimens |
|---|---|
| **Delhi** | **Connaught Place (1774 as printed ⚠, renamed Rajeev Chowk in 2013)**; **Metcalfe House (1835)**; **Saint James Church (1836)**; **Town Hall (1866)**; **Viceregal Lodge Estate (1902, later converted into Delhi University)**; **Viceroy's House (1929, now Rashtrapati Bhawan, architect Edwin Lutyen)**; **Sansad Bhawan (1921–27, architects Edwin Lutyen & Herbert Baker)**; **India Gate (1931, war memorial, architect Lutyen)** |

(⚠ "Connaught Place (1774)" is printed thus; the Connaught Place complex was built in the 1920s–30s — I believe 1774 is a slip; verify.)

## PART B — LUCENT: 2. SCULPTURE (book pp.318–322, PDF 331–335)

### B.1 Introduction; Harappan sculpture (p.318)
- Art of making objects from metal, stone, wood, clay etc. = **Sculpture**. Study of icons (images/statues) = **Iconography**. In India, sculpture begins with the Harappan civilization.
- **Important Harappan sculpture:**

| Type | Site : objects |
|---|---|
| **Metal figures** | **Mohenjodaro**: Dancing girl, standing woman, buffalo, ram etc. **Harappa**: Bullock cart, one-bull cart, dog attacking deer etc. **Chanhudaro**: Bullock cart, one-bull cart etc. **Lothal**: Ox, dog, rabbit, bird etc. **Kalibangan**: Bull etc. |
| **Stone statues** | **Mohenjodaro**: Priest/Bearded man, male statue, ram etc. **Harappa**: Male torso, male dancer |
| **Terracottas** | Subject: man-woman (viz. **Matridevi = Mother Goddess**), animals-birds (viz. Bull, Buffalo, Duck, Peacock) |

- Note: the **bronze dancing girl (Mohenjodaro)** shows that Harappans knew **bronze casting by CIRE PERDUE (lost wax process)** — figure made in wax, covered with clay coating, heated to run out the wax, hollow filled with molten metal.

### B.2 Mauryan sculpture (pp.318–319)
- Specimens: **pillars, stone statues, terracottas**.
- **1. Pillars / Ashokan Pillars (Ashok's Lat):** best example of Mauryan art — monolithic pillar with animal figure on top; admired for polish and animal figures. **Number would have been 30; 15 remain and 15 ruined.** Two types: with inscription & without.
  - **With inscriptions:** **Meerut-Delhi, Topara-Delhi, Allahabad, Laurianandangarh, Lauriaareraj, Sanchi, Sarnath, Rummindei, Nigali sagar (Nigliva)**.
  - **Without inscriptions:** **Rampurva, Kosambi, Basarh** etc.
  - **Parts:** two — **Tapering Shaft & Capital**.
  - **Tapering shaft (column):** carved from a single stone (monolithic); fine polish still shines like a mirror.
  - **Capital:** made separately, joined to the round and tapering shaft by a **copper dowel**; generally three parts — **bell-shaped inverted lotus (base), abacus (middle), crown (top)**; various animals, birds and **Dharma Chakra (Wheel of Law)** engraved on abacus; above the ornamented abacus animal figures (lion, bull, elephant etc.) crowned. Beauty mainly due to animal figure.
  - **Lion capital of Sarnath** = best; adopted as **State Emblem (Raj Chihn)** by Govt of India. The **wheel (Chakra)**, encarved at the middle part (abacus) of the capital (below lion figures and above inverted lotus) = **National Symbol (Rashtriya Pratik)**.
  - **National Emblem box:** capital of Sarnath originally had 4 parts: (1) **lotus bell base**; (2) **abacus** with **4 chakras (wheels) and 4 animals [Bull & Horse (visible); Lion & Elephant (invisible)] proceeding clockwise**; (3) **4 lions back to back facing 4 cardinal directions**; (4) crowning **Chakra (large wheel)** — pillar broken, kept in **Sarnath museum**. **Two parts rejected (lotus base and crowning wheel); two parts (abacus and 4 lions) selected for the National Emblem.** **'Satyameva Jayate'** (truth alone triumphs) taken from **Mundak Upanishad**, placed below the abacus = **National Motto**. **National Emblem adopted 26 January 1950.**
  - Pillars placed in sacred enclosures or in vicinity of towns; made mostly of **brown sandstone from Chunar (Mirzapur dist., UP)**; few in red & white sandstone from **Mathura**.
- **2. Stone statues:** **Yaksha–Yakshini** statues (**Yaksha = demi-god, protector of nature; Yakshini = demi-goddess**). Finding places: **Kurukshetra, Mehrauli, Besnagar, Vidisha, Mathura, Varanasi, Patna, Sishupalgarh (Odisha)** etc.; notable: **Yaksha 'Manibhadra' of Parkham village (Mathura)**; **Timukha Yaksha (Varanasi)**; **Yakshini/Yakshi of Besnagar (Vidisha, MP)**; **Chanwarbeerer (Chauri-bearer) Yakshini of Didarganj (Patna, Bihar)**. **Rock-cut statue of elephant from Dhauli (Odisha).**
- **3. Terracottas:** many clay figurines of animals, birds, men & women, made by fingers or moulds; remarkable: **Laughing boy from Didarganj (Patna)** and **Female Dancer from Bulandibagh (Patna)**.

### B.3 Post-Mauryan sculpture: three styles (p.319)
- Three styles/schools: **Amaravati, Gandhar, Mathura**.
- **1. Amaravati style (Satvahana):** Amaravati on the **Krishna river, Guntoor dist., AP**; old name **Dhanykatakam / Dharnikot**; developed in **Eastern Deccan (between Krishna & Godavari)**; emerged **200 BC**, flourished ~6 centuries (**200 BC–400 AD**); patronised by **Satvahanas** then **Ikshvakus** and chieftains, officers, businessmen. Prime example = sculpture of **Amaravati Buddhist stupa** (distinct place among religious sculptures of the world); figures concern Buddha's life and **Jataka** stories — frequently: **Chhadanta, Mahakavi, Vidurpundita, Ruru, Sibi, Vessantara, Sama Jataka**. Stupa now in ruins; remains preserved in museums (**Kolkata, Madras, London** etc.). **Discovered by Colin Mackenzie in 1797.** **White marble** as base material. Buddha mostly expressed through **symbols (Swastik, Agnistambh etc.)**; only in two or three places in human form = **earliest example of statue of Buddha in human form** (made **100 BC–100 AD**). Depicts co-existence of man and nature. Marked the transition between earlier **Sanchi-Bharhut** art style and later **Gandhar-Mathura** art style.
- **2. Gandhar style (Saka-Kushana):** Gandhar region (north-western region to part of Afghanistan); **50 BC–5th century AD**; patrons **Saka and Kushana rulers**, climax under **Kushana**; Indian themes in Greek way with Roman influence → **Indo-Greek / Indo-Helenistic / Graeco-Roman style**. **7 main centres:** **Tuxila (Taxila), Pushkalawati, Nagarhar, Swat valley, Kapishi, Bamiyan, Bactria**; later centres **Mohammoradu, Jaulian** etc. **Grey stone and slate** as base material. Statues of **Buddha & Bodhisatva** (based on Buddha's life events and Jatak Katha); most Bodhisatva statues are **Maitreya's**, some **Awalokiteshwar and Padmpani**. **Bodhisattva (Bodhi + Sattva)** = one on the path to becoming enlightened; in Buddhism, refers to previous births of Gautam Buddha. Goddess statues: **Hariti** (Buddhist goddess protector of child), **Ethina (Athena)** (Roman goddess of art & learning — as printed), **Roma** (patron goddess of Rome). Accurate depiction of the human body; Buddha like a Greek god — **curly hair, sometimes moustache/turban, resembles Apollo**; thick **Roman toga-like robe**; excessively ornamented; **halo plain and non-decorated** — **making of Halo = notable contribution of Gandhar style**. Fusion of Indian and Greek art; **parent of Buddhist art of Chinese Turkistan, Mongolia, China, Korea & Japan**.
- **3. Mathura style (Saka–Kushana):** Mathura region; credit for beginning goes to **Jain followers**; **1st century AD–3rd century AD**; **red sand stone (spotted)** as base material (continues).
### B.4 Mathura style (cont.), Gupta sculpture (p.320)
- **Mathura subject matter:** Buddha and Bodhisatva, **Jain Teerthankaras** and **Hindu deities** (Mathura = common centre of Buddhist, Jain, Brahmanic religions); statues of contemporary rulers also.
- **Buddhist:** Buddha statues in two forms — **Yakshmurti (standing)** and **Yogimurti (sitting)**.
- **Jain:** Teerthankara statues of two types — **Kayotsarg mudra (standing)** and **Padmasan mudra (sitting)**; **Aayagpatt** = votive stone slabs established in Jain temple for offering/worship of Teerthankaras — special form of Mathura sculpture. **Kankali Tila near Mathura** = Jain site; most Jain statues found there.
- **Brahmanic (Hindu):** (a) Vaishnavite: Vishnu, Luxmi, **Panch Vrishniveer**, Sankarshan, Balram; (b) Shaivite: Shiva, Parvati, Ganesh, Kartikeya (Skand), Nag; (c) Others: Brahma, Surya, Kuber, Gajluxmi, **Sapt-Matrika**, Katyayani, Mahisasurmardini, Vasundhara, Agni, Yaksha, Ayudhpurush.
- **Rulers:** **Chastan (Saka), Vim Kadphisas (Kushana), Kanishka (Kushana)**; the **headless statue of Kanishka** remarkable — standing, military attire, knee-length coat, full boots, right hand on mace, left hand holds hilt of sword.
- Overall: Gandhar = **realistic (materialistic & rationalist)**; Mathura = **idealistic (spiritual & emotionalist)**; Mathura almost free from foreign influence, carried forward earlier **Sanchi–Bharhut** style.
- **Gupta sculpture:** Indian sculpture developed greatly; Gupta art = **'Classical Art'**; statues concern Brahmanic and Buddhist religion.
  - **Vaishnavite:** Gupta rulers supporters of Vaishnavism; Vishnu idols **four-armed (Chaturbhuji)**; notable at **Mathura, Eran, Deogarh** — **Dasawatar temple (Deogarh)** most famous; **Varah incarnation** statues; giant Varah idol at **Varah cave temple of Udaygiri** (Varah lifting the earth with his teeth).
  - **Shaivite:** **Ardhanarishwar** (half male–half female) first made in this period; **Ekmukhi (one-faced) and Chaturmukhi (four-faced) Linga** first made — Ekmukhi Shiv linga found at **Bhumara and Khoh**, Chaturmukhi found in **Karamdanda (Faizabad)**; such idols = **Mukhlinga**.
  - **Buddhist:** Gupta Buddha statues free from Gandhar influence; Kushana halo plain, **Gupta halo ornamented**. Three famous: (1) stone Buddha from **Mathura** (Mathura Museum); (2) stone Buddha from **Sarnath** (Sarnath museum); (3) **copper statue of Buddha from Sultanganj, Bihar** (now in **Birmingham museum, England**).
- **Medieval sculpture — I. North India:**
  - **Pala & Sen (Eastern style):** Bengal & Bihar, **8th–12th century**; fine icons, many in **black basalt**; fine finish, decorated and polished, look metal-made.
  - **Odisha:** Bhubaneshwar and Konark carving shows deep sensuous appreciation; finest in the courtyard of **Sun temple at Konark** (forceful horses; elephant crushing a male figure — as printed).
  - **Chandela (Khajuraho):** figures of divinities and lovers.
  - **Solanki (Gujarat–Rajasthan):** **10th–12th century**; **Jain** fine icons, mostly white marble; found at **Mount Abu, Girnar, Palitana**.
- **II. South India:** **Chalukya** — temples of **Aihole, Badami, Pattadakal**; Gupta influence with elongation tendency (perhaps from Amaravati style). **Pallava** — graceful depiction of gods, humans, animals; most remarkable = sculptures of **Mammalapuram (Mahabalipuram)** (continues).

### B.5 South Indian, Mughal sculpture (p.321)
- Mahabalipuram: most striking = **relief of the descent of the Ganga**; also idealised portrait of **Mahendravarman and his queens**, **Narsinghvarman** and free-standing animal figures.
- **Rashtrakuta:** rock-cut cave temples at **Ellora and Elephanta**; Ellora — **Ravan's ditch (Ravan ki Khai), Dasawatar, Kailashnath**; Elephanta — **Trimurti (Bhairava–fearful form, Shiva–calm form & Uma–feminine form) of Maheshwar, Shiv Tandav (Natraj), Shiv Parvati marriage**.
- **Gomateshwar (Bahubali) statue, Shravanbelgola:** town in **Hassan district, Karnataka**; Jain pilgrimage; between **Vindhyagiri/Indragiri/Dohabetta** and **Chandragiri/Chikkabetta** hills; huge statue in **Kayotsarga mudra (standing)**; Gomateshwar (Bahubali) = one of the two sons of **Rishabhdev/Adinath (first Jain Teerthankar)**; built by **Chamund/Chawand Rai (minister of Gang ruler Rajmall-II of Mysore)**; **57 feet (17.4 m)** high; single block of **black stone** (as printed); **Mahamastakabhishek of Balaubali every 12 years**.
- **Chola sculpture:** distinct place; early influenced by Pallava, **from 10th century Chola distinctiveness**; stone sculpture subsidiary to architecture; metal sculpture an independent art.
  1. **Stone:** (a) **Brihadeshwar/Rajrajeshwar Temple of Tanjore** — forms of Shiva & Vishnu; folk statues; **108 mudras of dance** through dancing girls; (b) **Brihadeshwar (Shiva) Temple of Gangaikondacholapuram** — fewer but more artful; **Chandesh Anugrah statue of Shiva** most popular; (c) **Airateshwar Temple of Darasuram** — all mudras of **Bharat Muni's Natyashastra**.
  2. **Metal (bronze):** more than stone; more beautiful; found in museums worldwide and South India temples.
  - **Natraj (Shiva)** — ultimate in bronze; Tandav dance; **right leg on the back of dwarf Malayak (symbol of ignorance & evil)**; **left leg raised = Bhujangtrasit mudra**; four arms — **upper right: Damru (creation)**; **upper left: flame (destruction)**; **lower right: Abhay mudra (assurance)**; **lower left: Gajhast/Tirobhava (disappearance)**. Natraj = **'Cultural epitome of Chola period'**. Huge bronze from **Tirubharangakulam, Trichanapally district, TN** (now in Delhi museum); **Ardhanarishwar** from **Tiruvalankudu** (Chennai museum).
- **Hoysala:** **Chennakeshava temple (Belur)** and **Hoysaleshwar temple (Halebid)**; statues of king **Vithigdev/Vishnuvardhan** and queen **Santala** in Chennakeshava temple.
- **Vijaynagar:** **monolithic Nandi at Lepakshi temple campus** — **largest single stone Nandi in India**; bronze life-size statues of **Krishnadev Raya and queens Tirumalamba & Chinnadevi, Venkat-I, Achyut Raya** in **Venkateshwar temple of Tirupati**; statues of **Cholamdevi from Kalahasti**; metal statue of **Ramanujam from Sriperambdur**.
- **Madurai/Nayak:** **Tirumal Nayak** made statue of himself & wife.
- **Mughal sculpture:** setback with 13th-century Turk conquest (Islam prohibits images of living beings) → geometric, floral and calligraphic motifs. Akbar and Jahangir less fanatic: **Akbar** ordered statues of **Jaimal and Fatta** (heroes of **Chittor battle 1568**) at the main gate of Agra Fort — removed by **Aurangzeb in 1669**; **Jahangir** commissioned statues of **Rana Amar Singh and his son Prince Karan of Udaipur**; sculpture suffered under **Shahjahan and Aurangzeb**.
### B.6 Modern India sculpture (p.322)
- British ruled India **1757–1947**; statues by British rulers limited to British emperors/viceroys (to show dominance) = specimens of British sculpture style.
- **Deviprasad Raychowdhury (1899–1975)** — disciple of **Abanindra Nath Tagore**; notable works: **Triumph of Labour (1954, Madras)** and **Martyrs' Memorial (1956, Patna)**.
- **Ram Kinkar Baij (1906–80)** — notable works: **Santhal Family (1938, Shanti Niketan)** & **Yaksha-Yakshi (RBI)**.
- **Ram Vanji (R.V.) Sutar (born 1925)** — notable works: **Statue of Unity (2018, Narmada, statue of Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel)**; **Statue of Prosperity (2022, Bengaluru)**. (⚠ Ram V. Sutar's birth year printed 1925; 'born-1925' as printed; verify. Recent-date items.)
- Other sculptors: **Sudhir Ranjan Khastgir, Sankho Choudhury** etc.
- **Main centres of sculpture:** Jaipur, Chennai, Tanjore, Mumbai, Varanasi etc.

## PART C — LUCENT: 3. PAINTING (book pp.322–327, PDF 335–340)

### C.1 Introduction and ancient painting (p.322)
- Art of paint = **Painting**; among architecture, sculpture, painting — painting easiest, first art form. In India, story of painting begins with the **prehistoric period**.
- **Four forms:** **Wall Painting/Mural (Bhitti Chitra)** — on walls/ceilings; **Canvas Painting/Roll-Painting (Chitrapata)** — on cloth or leather, rollable; **Board Painting (Chitraphalaka Chitrakala)** — on board of wood, stone or metal; **Miniature Painting (Laghuchitra Chitrakala)** — small size on paper, book pages or cloth.
- **Ancient painting:** painting = oldest art form; cave walls as canvas = **Rock paintings**.
- **First discovery of rock paintings at Sohagighat in the sandstone hills of Vindhya (Mirzapur dist., UP) in 1867–78 by Archibold Carlleyle**; John Cockburn, C.W. Anderson, Rajendralal Mitra and Manomjan (Manmohan) Ghosh = early archaeologists who discovered many sites.
- Earliest paintings in India reported from the **Upper Palaeolithic (Later Old Stone Age)**. Remains of rock paintings in caves of **MP, UP, AP, Karnataka, Bihar**; richest from **Vindhya ranges of MP and the Kaimurean extensions into UP**. Subjects: human figures, activities, geometric designs, animal symbols; **hunting scenes predominate**.
- **Centres of ancient painting:** Indian painting began with wall paintings (murals); found in caves of **Bhimbetka, Jogimara, Ajanta, Bagh, Badami, Sittanvasal, Ellora, Elephanta** etc. Prehistoric rock-painting art = result of magical rituals or religious belief; notable places: **Bhimbetka caves (MP), Mirzapur hills (UP), Bellary hills (Karnataka)**; figures as simple lines (symbolic line-drawing); Bhimbetka mostly hunting and dancing.
  - **Bhimbetka** — **Raisen district, MP**; discovered **1957–58 by Vishnu Sridhar (V.S.) Wakankar**; ~**800 rock shelters, 500 with paintings**; **oldest known rock paintings in India**; themes: hunting, animal fighting, horse & elephant riders, honey collection, dancing, music, body decoration, household scenes; many colours but **white and red favourite** (**white from limestone; green from chalcedony**); **UNESCO World Heritage 2003**.
  - **Jogimara** — **Ramgarh hills, Puta village, Sarguja district, Chhattisgarh**; **3rd century BC–2nd century BC**; **8 panels of mural**; humans, animals.
  - **Ajanta** — Ajanta village, **Aurangabad district, Maharashtra**; rock-cut caves in the slope of a high hill in **Baghora river** valley in horse-shoe shape; **29 caves; caves no. 16 & 17 = Gupta period**; theme **Buddhism**; notable paintings — **dying princess, the great departure, Rahul presenting (mother & child)** (Rahul & Yashodhara); method **fresco & tempera**; **UNESCO World Heritage 1983**.
  - **Fresco** = painting on freshly plastered wall or ceiling. **Tempera** = paint in which pigment is tempered (mixed) with a water-based binding medium.

### C.2 Ancient painting centres (cont.) and medieval painting (p.323)
- **Bagh** — bank of **Bagh river, Dhar district, MP**; **Gupta period 319–550**; 9 caves, **6 have paintings**; folk life; discoverer **Lieutenant Dangerfield**.
- **Badami** — **Bagalkot district, Karnataka**; rock-cut caves under early Chalukyan king **Kirtivarman: 566–97**; of 4 caves **3 Hindu (Vaishnavite & Shavite), 1 Jain**.
- **Sittanvasal** — Pudukottai district, **Tamil Nadu**; patrons **Mahendravarman & son Narsinghvarman (Pallava kings)**; **Jain** religion theme.
- **Kanchipuram** — walls of **Vaikunthperumal temple**; patron **Nandivarman (Pallava King)**.
- **Ellora** — Aurangabad district; **Rashtrakuta** rulers; notable: **Kailashnath, Ganesh, Indrasabha, Lankeshwar**.
- **Elephanta** — island **Gharapuri (land of caves)**, **10 km east of Mumbai**; Portuguese named it **Elefante**; notable: Lord Sankar's leelas.
- **Tanjore** — walls of **Brihadeshwar temple**; patron **Rajaraja-I (Chola)**; puranic subjects (in one painting Rajaraja-I worshipping Shiva).
- **Medieval painting:** two categories — **Central/Imperial Style** and **Regional/Provincial Style**.
- **I. Central/Imperial — Painting in Sultanate period:** conservative Muslims consider depiction of living beings irreligious; Sultans did not completely disregard; limited **wall-paintings/murals, manuscript-paintings, portraits (Shabeeh = painting of a person), patachitra (on clothes)** — remains very rare.
- **Vijaynagar style:** continuation of **Chola style**; **Krishnadeva Raya** himself a good painter and patron; found in **Anegundi, Tadpatri, Kanchipuram, Tirupati, Chidambaram, Srirangam, Lepakshi**; **Lepakshi temple (Anantpur district, AP; 1538, Achyutdeva Raya)** known as **'Shaivites' Ajanta'**; themes: Ramayana, Mahabharata, Puranas, saints, rulers, soldiers, musicians, dancers.
- **Mughal style (1526–1707):** rise and fall corresponded to Mughal Empire; two categories: **portrait (Chhavichitra)** (single picture of specific persons/creatures) and **miniature (Laghuchitra)** (illustrations in manuscripts).
  - **Characteristics:** 1. speciality = **curved lines**; 2. fame due to subtle motifs, decorative painting, fine drawing, **golden colours on clothes**; 3. **green, red, blue, golden** colours; 4. themes: royal court and nature scenes; lacks lives of common people; 5. Mughal painters introduced scenes of royal court, prey chasing and battle scenes.
  - **Babur (1526–30):** desired to invite **Behzad (1450–1535)** (master miniature painter from Herat) but died; **Humayun** brought Behzad's disciples.
  - **Humayun (1530–40, 1555–56):** credited with starting Mughal painting; **'the Father of Mughal painting style'**; brought two Iranian painters — **Mir Sayyed Ali Tarbizi** (title **'Nadir-ul-Asr'**) & **Khwaja Abd-us-Samad Sheeraji** (title **'Sheereen Qalam'**); founded **Nigaar Khana** (painting workshop, part of his library); died Jan 1556.
  - **Akbar (1556–1605):** real development; ordered manuscript **'Dastan-i-Amir Hamza' (Hamzanama)** painted in **1567**; completed by **100 painters in 15 years** (continues).
### C.3 Mughal painting (cont.), Regional/Provincial styles (p.324)
- **Hamzanama:** painted **1567–82** under guidance of **Sayyed Ali (earlier) and Abd-us-Samad (later)**; on brightly coloured **cloth (linen)**; **14 volumes, 1400 pages**.
- Akbar appointed painting-ustad **Abdussamad** head of **Nigaar Khana (painting workshop) at Sikri**; ~**100 painters**; **Hindu painters:** Dashawant, Basavan, Lal, Kesu, Mukund, Harivansh etc.; **Muslim painters:** Abdussamad, Sayyed Ali, Miskin, Harrukh Begh, Farukh Begh etc. Paintings on manuscripts of **Hamzanama, Tareekh-i-Khandaan-e-Taimur, Ramayan, Razmnama (Persian translation of Mahabharata), Akbarnama, Baburnama** etc. Four categories: **non-Indian stories, Indian stories, historical paintings, portraits**.
- **Jahangir (1605–28):** proficient painter, connoisseur; in **Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri** prides on discriminating one artist's work from another's; Mughal painting reached **climax** → **'the Golden period of Mughal painting'**. Prominent painters: **Aaka/Aagha Riza** (Head of Nigaar Khana at Agra), **Abul Hasan** (title **Nadir-uz-Zaman**), **Bishandas** (title **Nadir-ul-Asr**; sent to Persia/Iran to make portraits of Persian king), **Manohar, Govardhan, Madhav, Vichitra**. He encouraged **portraits and albums (Murakka)** rather than manuscript paintings.
- **Shahjahan (1628–58):** more interested in architecture; painting flourished but heyday under Jahangir.
- **Aurangzeb (1628–1707 as printed ⚠; his reign was 1658–1707):** puritan; no encouragement; painters left for better centres.
- **II. Regional/Provincial Style:** **Rajput** prominent; others: **Jain (Western), Gurjar, Gond, Malwa, Deccan, Jaunpur, Sikh** styles.
- **Rajput style (16th–19th century):** term **'Rajput Paintings' coined by art historian Anand Coomaraswamy in 1916**; now 'Rajput School' obsolete → two schools: **Rajasthani & Pahari**. Specialised in illustrating **Hindu epic legends** — Ram, Krishna, **Bhagavata Puran, Geet Gobinda**.

**Rajasthani style: 16th–19th century**

| Style | Centres | Patrons & painters |
|---|---|---|
| Mewar | Udaypur | Patrons: Jagat Singh, Rana Pratap, Raj Singh, Jai Singh; Painters: Nisardin, Sahibdin, Manohar, Jagannath |
| Bikaner | Bikaner | Patrons: Rai Singh, Karan Singh, Anoop Singh; Painters: Ustad Ali Raza, Ruknuddin, Ibrahim, Nathu, Isha |
| Kisangarh | Jaipur, Jodhpur & Ajmer | Patrons: **Bishan Singh; Samant Singh alias Nagari Das (beloved's name — Bani Thani)**; Painters: **Nihal Chanda (Bani Thani)**, Amar Chanda, Sitar-am, Bhawani Das; Painting: **Bani Thani (known as 'Indian Monalisa')** |
| Kota–Bundi | Kota, Bundi, Jhalawar | Patrons: Umed Singh, Ram Singh, Chhatra Singh; Painter: Sheikh Hasan, Sheikh Ali, Sheikh Hatim, Utkal Ram |
| Jodhpur | Jodhpur | Patrons: Jaswant Singh, Ajeet Singh; Painter: Veerji, Dana |
| Jaipur | Jaipur | Patrons: Sawai Jai Singh, Sawai Ishwar Singh, Sawai Madho Singh, Sawai Pratap Singh; Painter: Sahibram, Guman |

**Pahari style: 17th–19th century**
- Paintings developed in **Pahar (hills)**; prevalent in **Himalayan foot-hills, Jammu to Garhwal**; began at **Basohli** with coarsely flamboyant style, blossomed into **Kangra School** via **Guler or pre-Kangra phase**.

| Style | Centres | Patrons & painters |
|---|---|---|
| Basohli | Basohli | Patron: **Kripal Pal**; Specimen: **Shangri Ramayana** (Shangri = place of residence of royal family) |
| Guler | Haripur (capital of Guler) | Patrons: Dileep Singh, Govardhan Chandra; Painters: **Pandit Sen & his sons — Manak (Manaku) & Nainsukh**; Khushala, Fattu & Gondhu |
| Kangra | Kangara | Patrons: **Sansarchand, Aniruddhachand**; Painters: Fattu, Purkhu, Khushala |
| Jammu | Jammu | Patron: Ranajit Dev |
| Kullu | Kullu | — |
| Chamba | Chamba | Patron: Aniruddh Chandra |
| Garhwal | Srinagar (capital of Garhwal) | Patron: **Prithapal Singh**; Painter: **Molaram** |

### C.4 Differences; Modern India painting (p.325)
**Differences between Rajput style & Mughal style**
1. Rajput: **Indian environment, common people, folk elements**; Mughal: **Persian environment & European impact, elite persons, court elements**.
2. Rajput: women portrayed in multiple ways; Mughal: negligibly.
3. Rajput: **no arabesque in margins**; Mughal: arabesque in plenty.
4. Rajput: animals/birds **symbolic**; Mughal: for **recreation**.
5. Rajput: often **single painter**; Mughal: at least **four painters** per painting.
6. Rajput: **spiritual and idealistic**; Mughal: **materialistic and realistic**.
7. Time: Rajput **16th–19th century**; Mughal **16th–18th century**.

**Modern India painting — Company Style (1757–1858)**
- Traditional Indian painting declined with East India Company rule (**1757–1858**); **European oil painting** replaced murals and miniatures; English officers commissioned local artists (some migrated from courts of **Delhi, Lucknow, Murshidabad**); painters largely on paper; mix of traditional and European style = **Company School (Company Style)**; popular in Britain too (albums).
- Prominent painters: **Ghulam Ali Khan ('Group of courtesans'), Mazhar Ali Khan, Lalchand, Gopal Chand, Sevakram, Jhumaklal** etc.
- **Patna Kalam:** centred in **Patna (Bihar)**, mid-18th to mid-19th century; offshoot of **Mughal and Company style**; theme: everyday life of a common man.
- **Raja Ravi Varma (1848–1906):** Govt of India established **Art schools at Madras (1850), Calcutta (1854), Bombay (1857)**; western oil painting produced painters such as Ravi Varma; from **princely family of Travancore**, knowledge of Sanskrit; scenes from **Ramayana and Mahabharata**; copied as **oleograph** and calendar images → **'the Father of Calendar Art in India'** (as printed). Notable paintings: **'Rama Vanquishing the pride of the Ocean', 'Krishna as envoy', 'Shakuntala patra-lekhan', 'Hans Damyanti', 'Nair Lady'**. By late 19th century nationalism, his academic style looked down upon as foreign.
- **Bengal School:** inspired by **E.B. Havell (1861–1934)** (Principal, Govt School of Art, Calcutta) and led by **Abanindra Nath Tagore (1871–1951)** (Vice-Principal) → **'Neo Art Movement'** (continues).
### C.5 Bengal School, After Bengal School, Folk paintings, Rangoli (pp.326–327)
**Bengal School (cont., p.326)**
- Revivalist art movement, originated in **Calcutta** (capital of Bengal Presidency); came to be known as **'Calcutta School' or 'Bengal School'**; **traditionalist in theme, nationalist in inspiration**.
- **Notable books by E.B. Havell:** *Indian Sculpture and Painting*, *Indian Architecture*, *Ideals of Indian Art*.
- **Notable paintings by A.N. Tagore:** 'Journey's End', 'Shahjahan Looking at the Taj', 'Buddha and Sujata', 'Tear Drops on a Lotus Leaf', 'Queen of the Forest', 'Bharat Mata' etc.
- **Prominent painters of Bengal School:** **Nandalal Basu** ('Uma's Tapasya', 'Gandhi's Dandi March/Bapuji', 'Dhaki', 'Tiller of the soil'), **Surendra Nath Gangooly, Sarada Charan Ukil** ('Arjun detach from war'), **Hakim Khan, K. Venkatappa** ('Rama's marriage'), **Asit Kumar Haldar, Kshitindra Nath Mazumdar** ('Rasa-Lila'), **Abdur Rahman Chaghtai** ('Radhika'), **Mukul Chandra Dey, Gaganendra Nath Tagore** ('City in the Night', 'A Cubist City', 'Magician').
- **Rabindra Nath Tagore (1861–1941)** — innovative experiments in painting but Western influence evident; **Modernism of Picasso style** attempted in an Indian background.
- **Jamini Roy** — notable paintings: 'Woman with Child', 'Black Horse'.
- **Amrita Shergil (1913–41)** — born in **Hungary**; pioneer of modern movement; style contrasted with Bengal School; died at **28**; notable: 'Camels', 'Haldi Grinder', 'Elephants bathing in green pool', 'Hill side', 'Professional model', Three girls.
- **After Bengal School: 1943 onwards** — after **Bengal Famine (1943)** Bengal School lost reputation; **1943, Prodosh Das Gupta** led young artists forming the **'Calcutta Group'** (universal art, free of older values). **1946**: **'The Progressives'** group formed in **Bombay** — leader **Francis Newton (F.N.) Souza**; members: **Maqbool Fida (M.F.) Husain** ('Farmer's Family', 'Mother Teresa'), **K.H. Ara, S.A. Bakre, H.A. Gade, S.H. Raza** ('Ma'). M.F. Husain largely figurative; **S.H. Raza** moved towards **abstraction**.
- Abstraction wide appeal in 1960s–70s; in South India **K.C.S. Paniker** ('The Dog'; 'Words and Symbols') founded **Cholamandalam**, artist village near Madras — pioneer in abstraction. 1990s liberalisation: globalisation impact; mobile medium (painting, photography, videography); "art of today speaks a language of its own".

**Folk Paintings (p.326–327)**

| Painting | Region | Remarks |
|---|---|---|
| **Mithila / Madhubani** | Mithila (esp. Madhubani), Bihar | Painters: **Jagdamba Devi, Baua Devi, Bharti Dayal, Ganga Devi, Mahasundari Devi, Sita Devi** etc.; mainly women; three forms — **Bhittichitra (wall painting), Pattachitra (canvas painting) & Aripan (ground/floor painting)** |
| **Cheriyal Scroll** | Telangana | Theme: **Hindu Epic & Puranic stories** |
| **Gond** | Central India | Gond tribe; themes nature, religious (esp. Krishnakatha) |
| **Manjusha** | Bhagalpur, Bihar | Painting on boxes of **jute & paper** |
| **Kalamkari** | **Srikalahasti & Machilipatnam, Andhra Pradesh** | Painting on **cotton fabric by pen (kalam)** |
| **Paitkar** | Jharkhand | Tribal people of Jharkhand |
| **Patua Art** | Bengal | Painting on **pats (scrolls)**; themes **Mangal Kavyas** & auspicious stories of Hindu deities |
| **Pattachitra** | Odisha | Painting on **patta (canvas cloth)** |
| **Phad** | Rajasthan | Painting on **phad (scroll)**; theme local deities (**Pabuji & Devnarayan**) |
| **Pithora** | Gujarat & Madhya Pradesh | Painting on walls on auspicious occasion |
| **Saura** | Odisha | **Saura tribe**; wall-painting; theme **Idital**, the deity of Saura tribe |
| **Thangka** (Thangka = plain canvas) | North-East | Originally **Tibetan** art; on canvas/cotton; theme **Buddha's life events**; practised by Buddhist monks |
| **Warli** | Gujarat–Maharashtra border | A type of wall-painting |

**Rangoli in different States of India**

| Name of the Rangoli | Concerned states |
|---|---|
| Alpana | West Bengal |
| Jhoti/Chita | Odisha |
| Rangoli | Karnataka, Gujarat, Maharashtra |
| Sanskar Bharati | Maharashtra |
| Chouk Purna | Chhattisgarh, Uttar Pradesh |
| Kolam | Tamil Nadu |
| Sathiya (Partner) | Gujarat |
| Aripan | Bihar |
| Aipan | Uttarakhand |
| Muggu | Andhra Pradesh |
| Mandana | Rajasthan |

(⚠ "Sathiya (Partner)" as printed.)
- **4. Festivals and Fairs** starts on p.327 — extracted separately in `festivals_raw_extraction.md` (Lucent Art & Culture §4, book pp.327–329; Hindu festivals table on p.327 begins with Shitala Ashtami).

1. Kandariya Mahadev "1209 AD" by Dhangdeo (A.7) — looks like a printing slip (Dhanga ruled c.950–1002; temple c.1030); verify.
2. Dashawatar (Deogarh) — footnote calls it first example of Panchayatan and Shikhara style; text says first example of Nagar style. Table asterisk placement ambiguous (A.4).
3. Bibi ka Maqbara "black marbles" (A.15); "Ustad Ishakhan" as Taj builder (A.14); Anarkali story "fabricated by Finch, Terry" (as printed).

5. Aurangzeb printed "(1628–1707)" in painting section; reign 1658–1707.
6. Mughal "Jahaz Mahal … built by Baz Bahadur" bracket ambiguity (A.11).
7. R.V. Sutar birth-year and Padma awards; Statue of Unity dates (recent) — verify.
8. Tagore's Taj quote is as printed; verbatim accuracy not verified.
9. Malayak/Bhujangtrasit, mudra details — as printed.

## PART A — PARMAR SSC FATMAN (2nd Ed., 2026): "IMPORTANT DAYS" chapter (book pp.477–479, PDF 510–512)
Running head "IMPORTANT DAYS". Month-wise bullet list in two columns per page, with shaded fact boxes (kept below in italics-style "Box:" lines). Format: "date: day(s)". The chapter closes with the red ■■■ marker on p.479. Entries are kept as printed (incl. odd ones flagged ⚠).

### JANUARY (p.477)
- 1st: English New Year / Global Family Day.
- 4th: World Braille Day.
- 5th: National Birds Day.
- 6th: World War Orphans Day.
- 9th: Pravasi Bhartiya Diwas → Non-Resident Indian (NRI) Day.
- 10th: World Hindi Diwas. Box: Hindi was first spoken at the UN General Assembly (UNGA) in 1949. This date is also commemorated as "World Hindi Day".
- 11th: Death Anniversary of Lal Bahadur Shastri.
- 12th: National Youth Day → On 12 January 1863, Swami Vivekananda was born.
- 15th: Army Day. Box "Reasons for celebration of Army Day": to commemorate the appointment of Field Marshal K.M. Cariappa as the first Indian Commander-in-Chief of the Indian Army.
- 16th: Startup Diwas.
- 23rd: Netaji's Birthday (S.C. Bose) → also celebrated as Parakram Divas. Box: ECI was established on 25th Jan 1950.
- 24th: National Girl Child Day.
- 25th: National Voters Day.
- 26th: International Customs Day.
- 27th: International Holocaust Day; International Tourism Day.
- 28th: Birth Anniversary of Lala Lajpat Rai; Data Protection Day.
- 30th: Martyr's Day (also known as Mahatma Gandhi's martyrdom day); World Leprosy Eradication Day.
- 31st: International Zebra Day.
- Box (top of p.477): International Days are recognised by United Nations.

### FEBRUARY (pp.477)
- 1st: Indian Coast Guard Day.
- 2nd: World Wetlands Day → On this day, Ramsar Convention was adopted in 1971 in Ramsar, Iran.
- 4th: World Cancer Day.
- 6th: International Day of Zero tolerance for Female Genital Mutilation.
- 6th–12th Feb: International Development Week.
- 8th: Safer Internet Day.
- 10th: National Deworming Day; World Pulses Day.
- 11th: World Day of the Sick; Int. Day of Women and Girls in Science.
- 21st: International Mother Language Day.
- 22nd: World Thinking Day.
- 24th: Central Excise Day.
- 27th: World NGO Day.
- 28th: National Science Day.
- Box: Sarva Dava Sewan is a program that aims to eliminate lymphatic Filariasis by 2027. National Filaria Control Programme (NFCP) was launched in 1955 to eliminate lymphatic Filariasis. 28 February 1928, Sir C. V. Raman and his student K. S. Krishnan discovered the Raman effect for which Raman received Nobel Prize in Physics in 1930 and Bharat Ratna in 1954.

### MARCH (p.477)
- 1st: Zero Discrimination Day.
- 3rd: World Wildlife Day.
- 4th: National Safety Day.
- 8th: International Women's Day.
- 2nd Wednesday of March: No Smoking Day.
- 10th: CISF Raising Day.
- 14th: Pi Day.
- 15th: World Consumer's Right Day.
- 16th: National Vaccination Day.
- 20th: International Day of Happiness; World Sparrow Day.
- 21st: International Day of Forests.
- 22nd: World Water Day.
- 23rd: World Meteorological Day.
- 24th: World Tuberculosis (TB) Day.
- Box: The last Saturday of March: Earth's Hour Day → celebrated from 8:30 pm to 9:30 pm local time, encouraging people to turn off non-essential lights for an hour to raise awareness about environmental issues.

### APRIL (pp.477–478)
- 1st: Odisha Foundation Day; April Fool's Day.
- 1st–7th: Prevention of Blindness Week.
- 2nd: World Autism Awareness Day.
- 5th: National Maritime Day.
- 7th: World Health Day → On 1948, World Health Organization (WHO) was established.
- 10th: World Homoeopathy Day.
- 13th: Jallianwala Bagh Massacre Day.
- 14th: B.R Ambedkar Remembrance Day.
- 17th: World Haemophilia Day. Box: In haemophilia, blood does not clot due to deficiency of certain clotting factors.
- 18th: World Heritage Day.
- 21st: National Civil Services Day.
- 22nd: World Earth Day.
- 24th: National Panchayati Raj Day → 73rd CA 1992 constitutionalised Panchayati Raj system in India.
- 25th: World Malaria Day.
- 26th: World Intellectual Property Day.
- 28th: World Day for Safety and Health at Work.

### MAY (p.478)
- 1st: International Labour Day; Maharashtra Day; Gujarat Day.
- 3rd: Press Freedom Day. Box: World Laughter Day (First Sunday of May).
- 7th: World Athletics Day.
- 8th: World Red Cross Day; World Thalassemia Day (Thalassemia occurs when body does not make enough hemoglobin).
- 9th: Rabindranath Tagore Jayanti.
- 11th: National Technology Day.
- 12th: International Nurses Day → Florence Nightingale was the founder of modern nursing (lady with a lamp).
- 16th: Buddha Jayanti or Buddha Purnima.
- 17th: World Telecommunication Day, World Hypertension Day.
- 18th: International Museum Day.
- 21st: National Anti-Terrorism day/Tea Day.
- 25th: Thyroid Day. Box: Mother's Day (Second Sunday of May).
- 31st: Anti-Tobacco Day.

### JUNE (p.478)
- 1st: World Milk Day.
- 2nd: International Sex Workers' Day.
- 2nd: Telangana Formation Day.
- 5th: World Environment Day.
- 7th: World Food Safety Day.
- 8th: World Oceans Day.

### AUGUST (p.478)
- 6th: Hiroshima Day (On 1945, an atomic bomb named "Little Boy" was dropped on Hiroshima, Japan).
- 7th: National Handloom Day; National Javelin Day.

- 20th: World Mosquito Day; Sadbhavna Diwas.

### SEPTEMBER (pp.478–479)
- 1st–7th: National Nutrition Week.
- 5th: International Day of Charity; Teachers' Day (honours the birth anniversary of Dr. Sarvepalli Radhakrishnan in India).
- 8th: International Literacy Day.

- 15th: Engineer's Day (India); International Day of Democracy.
- 16th: World Ozone Day → In 1987, at Montreal, Canada, Ozone Convention was held. (⚠ as printed: "Ozone Convention" — the Montreal Protocol was signed 16 Sep 1987; the Vienna Convention is 1985; verify.)
- 21st: International Day of Peace (UN); World Alzheimer's Day.
- 22nd: Rose Day → celebrated for the welfare of Cancer patients.
- 26th: Kargil Vijay Diwas. (⚠ printed under September at p.479; Kargil Vijay Diwas is 26 July as printed above — duplicate/slip.)
- 28th: World Hepatitis Day; Nature Conservation Day. (⚠ printed in the September list — duplicate of the July 28 entry.)
- 29th: International Tiger Day. (⚠ same duplicate slip — July 29 above.)
- 22nd: World Rhino Day. (printed after 29th, out of order; ⚠ 22 Sept.)
- 27th: World Tourism Day.
- Box: World Maritime Day → celebrated on the last Thursday of September every year.

### OCTOBER (p.479)

- 1st: Formation Day of 8 States (Kerala, Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, Haryana, Punjab, Chhattisgarh, Madhya Pradesh). (⚠ as printed; Tamil Nadu's Formation Day is officially 1 Nov in the state's own observance; Haryana 1966, Punjab (reorganised) 1966 — verify.)

### DECEMBER (p.479)

Visweshwaraiya) — 15 Sep; Sanchayika Diwas — 15 Sep; World Ozone Day/International Day for the Preservation of the Ozone Layer — 16 Sep; Railway Police Force (RPF) Foundation Day — 20 Sep; International Day of Peace, Alzheimer's Day — 21 Sep; World Tourism Day — 27 Sep; World Heart Day — 29 Sep; World Deaf Day — Last Sunday.
**October:** International Day for Older Persons — 1 Oct; Mahatma Gandhi's Birthday (International Day of Non-Violence) — 2 Oct; Birthday of Lal Bahadur Shastri — 2 Oct; World Habitat Day — 1st Monday; World Animal Welfare Day — 4 Oct; World Teacher's Day — 5 Oct; World Wild Animal Day — 6 Oct; Indian Air Force Day — 8 Oct; World Post Day — 9 Oct; World Sight Day — 10 Oct; International Day of the Girl Child — 11 Oct; Birthday of Loknayak Jay Prakash Narayan — 11 Oct; UN International Day for Natural Disaster Reduction — 2nd Wednesday; World Standard Day — 14 Oct; World Food Day — 16 Oct; International Day for the Eradication of Poverty — 17 Oct; World Statistics Day/World Osteoporosis Day — 20 Oct; World Iodine Shortage Day — 21 Oct; UN Day, World Development Information Day — 24 Oct; World Savings Day — 31 Oct; Death Anniversary of Indira Gandhi — 31 Oct.
**November:** International Day for Preventing the Exploitation of the Environment in War & Armed Conflict — 6 Nov; National Cancer Awareness Day — 7 Nov; National Legal Services Day — 9 Nov; World Science Day for Peace and Development — 10 Nov; National Education Day (Birthday of Maulana Azad) — 11 Nov; World Kindness Day — 13 Nov; Children's Day (Birth anniversary of Jawaharlal Nehru) — 14 Nov; World Diabetes Day — 14 Nov; International Day for Tolerance — 16 Nov; World Adult Day — 18 Nov; World Citizen Day, International Men's Day, World Toilet Day — 19 Nov; Universal Children's Day, Africa Industrialization Day — 20 Nov; World Fisheries Day — 21 Nov; World Philosophy Day — 3rd Thursday; International Day for the Elimination of Violence Against Women — 25 Nov; National Law Day/Constitution Day (India) — 26 Nov; International Day of Solidarity with the Palestinian People — 29 Nov.
**December:** World AIDS Day — 1 Dec; International Day for the Abolition of Slavery — 2 Dec; World Disabled Day/International Day of Persons with Disabilities, Dr. Rajendra Prasad's Birth Day India — 3 Dec; Navy Day (India) — 4 Dec; International Volunteers Day — 5 Dec; International Civil Aviation Day — 7 Dec; Armed Forces Flag Day — 7 Dec; International Anti-Corruption Day — 9 Dec; International Human Rights Day — 10 Dec; World Children's Fund Day — 11 Dec; International Mountain Day — 11 Dec; World Asthma Day — 11 Dec (⚠ as printed; World Asthma Day is usually in May — verify); National Energy Conservation Day — 14 Dec; International Migrants Day — 18 Dec; Goa Liberation Day — 19 Dec; International Human Solidarity Day — 20 Dec; Kisan Divas (Birthday of Chaudhary Charan Singh) — 23 Dec; National Consumer Day — 24 Dec; X-mas Day — 25 Dec; Good Governance Day — 25 Dec.
(⚠ Lucent's list has Statistics Day (India) 29 June and Engineer's Day = birthday of M. Visweshwaraiya 15 Sep — consistent with Parmar. Lucent p.438 lists World Hindi Day 10 Jan; Parmar the same. Lucent has no Veer Bal Diwas line here — see §73 Misc (Sports file B.26): Veer Baal Diwas 26 December.)

### B.5 CROSS-BOOK NOTES (Days & Events)
1. Parmar's month-wise list (Part A) is a subset-plus-additions of Lucent §19; Parmar adds many "reason" boxes (Army Day ← K.M. Cariappa; Ramsar Convention 1971; WHO 1948; 73rd CA 1992 for Panchayati Raj Day; St. Petersburg Declaration 29 July 2010; Hiroshima "Little Boy"/Nagasaki "Fat Man"; Vijay Diwas 16 Dec 1971; Goa Liberation Day/Operation Vijay 1961; Ramanujan Day; Veer Bal Diwas etc.).
2. Differences: Parmar 23 Jan = Netaji birthday/Parakram Divas; Lucent has no Parakram Divas. Parmar 25 Jan National Voters Day (ECI est. 25 Jan 1950); Lucent has "Tourism Day (India)" on 25 Jan. Parmar 24 Jan National Girl Child Day = Lucent "Rashtriya Balika Diwas". Parmar 31 Oct Rashtriya Ekta Diwas = Sardar Patel birth anniversary — Lucent 31 Oct lists Death Anniversary of Indira Gandhi and World Savings Day. Parmar 19 Dec = Goa Liberation Day + National Integration Day (⚠ slip).
3. Parmar "National Sports Day 29 Aug", "Teachers' Day 5 Sep", "Engineer's Day 15 Sep", "Statistics Day 29 June (P C Mahalanobis)" agree with Lucent.
4. Recent-event items for this sub-topic: Operation Sindoor (6–7 May 2025) — in Sports file B.27; Veer Baal Diwas (26 Dec) — Parmar p.479 and Sports file B.26; International Year of Volunteers for Sustainable Development 2026 (Lucent §18); further current-affairs "events" (summits, Census) — see Round-2 Govt Policies/Census notes once extracted.

# STATIC GK — AWARDS AND HONOURS — RAW EXTRACTION
Source books: Parmar's SSC Fatman (2nd Ed., 2026) — "Awards and Honours" chapter (book pp.505–509, PDF 538–542; PDF = book + 33). Lucent's General Knowledge (2026) — Miscellany §38–§49, §51–§52 (book pp.449–463, PDF 462–476; PDF = book + 13). Test cross-referencing NOT done (raw extraction only, as instructed).

Method / disclosure: extraction is "fact-complete" — all entries, table rows, names, years, numbers and mnemonic mappings kept; explanatory prose is condensed, not word-for-word. Scanned pages were read from page images (OCR only as an aid).
Conventions: ⚠ = doubt, printing slip, cross-book disagreement or RECENT-EVENT item (2024–2026; verify). Spellings kept as printed, corrections in brackets. Today's date when extracted: 2026-09-24 — anything printed as "latest" may since have changed.

Contents: PART A Parmar (A.1 Civilian, A.2 Gallantry, A.3 Sports, A.4 Literary, A.5 Films, A.6 Global). PART B Lucent (B.1 §38 table, B.2 §39 Padma/Bharat Ratna, B.3 §40 Gallantry, B.4 §41 Jnanpith, B.5 §42 Phalke, B.6 §43 Nobel intro, B.7 Nobel facts/records, B.8 §44 Oscar, B.9 §45 Grammy, B.10 §46 Booker, B.11 §47 Magsaysay, B.12 §48 Gandhi Peace Prize, B.13 §49 Kalinga, B.14 §50 Books (pointer), B.15 §51 Committees, B.16 §52 Commissions, B.17 §53 Youth pointer). PART C cross-book doubts.
Cross-references (other files): Sports awards (Khel Ratna, Arjuna, Dronacharya, Special Awards, pension) → `sports_raw_extraction.md`; Books/authors and Booker/Nobel author claims → `books_authors_raw_extraction.md`; Committees → Economy raw files.

## PART A — PARMAR SSC FATMAN (2nd Ed., 2026): "AWARDS AND HONOURS" chapter (book pp.505–509, PDF 538–542)
Running head "AWARDS AND HONOURS". Sections: Civilian Awards; Gallantry Awards; Sports Awards; Literary Awards; Films and Entertainment Awards; Global Awards. Recent-year tables (2024/2025 awardees; 70th National Film Awards; Filmfare 2024; Oscars 2025) are RECENT-EVENT items — kept as printed, ⚠ verify from primary sources.

### A.1 CIVILIAN AWARDS (book p.505)
- India's Civilian Awards recognise outstanding contributions in arts, literature, science, public service, social work. **Announced every year on Republic Day (26th January)** and **conferred by the President of India at Rashtrapati Bhavan**.

**1. Bharat Ratna (Highest Civilian Award)**
- Eligibility: any individual, irrespective of nationality. Instituted: **1954**. Total recipients till date: **53 (as of 2024)**.
- First recipients: **C. Rajagopalachari, Dr. S. Radhakrishnan and Dr. C.V. Raman (1954)**.
- First female recipient: **Indira Gandhi (1971)**. First posthumous recipient: **Lal Bahadur Shastri (1966)**.
- First sportsperson recipient: **Sachin Tendulkar (2014)** (also the youngest recipient: at age 40) (⚠ "youngest recipient" as printed; verify).
- Non-Indian recipients: **Khan Abdul Ghaffar Khan (1987), Nelson Mandela (1990)**.
- **Bharat Ratna Awardees for 2024:**
| Name | Field of Service |
|---|---|
| Karpoori Thakur (Posthumous) | Public Affairs (Politics) |
| M.S. Swaminathan (Posthumous) | Science & Engineering (Agriculture) |
| P.V. Narasimha Rao (Posthumous) | Public Affairs (Politics) |
| L.K. Advani | Public Affairs (Politics) |
| Chaudhary Charan Singh (Posthumous) | Public Affairs (Politics) |

**2. Padma Awards** — Instituted **1954**. Fields: arts, literature, education, science, industry, sports, medicine, social work, public affairs.
- **i. Padma Vibhushan (Second-Highest Civilian Award)** — First recipients: **Satyendra Nath Bose, Nandalal Bose, Zakir Hussain, Balasaheb Gangadhar Kher, V.K. Krishna Menon, Jigme Dorji Wangchuk**. (Cartoon quiz box: "Can you recall name of the 5th state of matter?" — no answer printed.)
- **Padma Vibhushan Awardees for 2025:**
| Name | Field of Service | State |
|---|---|---|
| Dr. D. Nageshwar Reddy | Medicine | Telangana |
| Justice (Retd.) Jagdish Singh Khehar | Public Affairs | Chandigarh |
| Smt. Kumudini Rajnikant Lakhia | Art | Gujarat |
| Shri Lakshminarayana Subramaniam | Art | Karnataka |
| Shri M. T. Vasudevan Nair (Posthumous) | Literature and Education | Kerala |
| Shri Osamu Suzuki (Posthumous) | Trade and Industry | Japan |
| Smt. Sharda Sinha (Posthumous) | Art | Bihar |
- **ii. Padma Bhushan (Third-Highest Civilian Award)** — First recipients (1954): **V.K. Krishna Menon** — Public Affairs; **Homi J. Bhabha** — Science & Engineering; **Shanti Swaroop Bhatnagar** — Science & Engineering; **Mahadeva Iyer Subramania Iyer** — Civil Service; **M.S. Subbulakshmi** — Arts (Music) [first female recipient]. (⚠ V.K. Krishna Menon appears in both the Padma Vibhushan and Padma Bhushan first-recipient lists as printed.)
- **iii. Padma Shri (Fourth-Highest Civilian Award)** — heading only; no further content printed on p.505 (⚠ nothing follows in the book).

### A.2 GALLANTRY AWARDS (book p.505)
- Presented to military personnel and civilians for acts of bravery and valor in the face of extreme danger; divided into wartime and peacetime.
- **Wartime:** **Param Vir Chakra** (Highest), **Mahavir Chakra** (Second-Highest), **Vir Chakra** (Third-Highest).
- **Peacetime:** **Ashoka Chakra** (Highest), **Kirti Chakra** (Second-Highest), **Shaurya Chakra** (Third-Highest).

### A.3 SPORTS AWARDS (book pp.505–507)
Intro (p.505): India's Sports Awards recognise excellence in various sporting disciplines; presented annually by the President of India.
**1. Major Dhyan Chand Khel Ratna Award (Highest Sporting Honour)** — Instituted **1991-92**; **₹25 Lakhs** awarded by Ministry of Youth Affairs & Sports to each recipient; first recipient **Viswanathan Anand (Chess) — 1991-92**. Box: **In August 2021, Rajiv Gandhi Khel Ratna Award got renamed to Major Dhyan Chand Khel Ratna Award.**
- **Khel Ratna Awardees for 2024:**

| Chess | Gukesh D |
| Hockey | Harmanpreet Singh |
| Para-Athletics | Praveen Kumar |
| Shooting | Manu Bhaker |
**2. Arjuna Award (Second highest Sporting honour)** — Instituted **1961**; **15 Lakh** given to recipients. First recipients (1961): **P.G. Sethi — Golf; Bajarangi Prasad — Swimming; Karni Singh — Shooting; Krishna Das — Archery; Udey Chand — Wrestling; P.K. Banerjee — Football; Kumari Ann Lumsden — Hockey; Salim Durani — Cricket; Gurbachan Singh Randhawa — Athletics.** Box: **1st Woman to get Arjuna Award: Meena Shah (1962) for Badminton. 1st Arjuna Award recipient for Kabaddi: Sahanand Mahadev Shetty (1972).**
- **Arjuna Awardees, 2024:**

**3. Dronacharya Award** — Given to coaches for producing outstanding sportspersons at the international level. Instituted **1985**. First recipients (1985): **Bhalchandra Bhaskar Bhagwat (Wrestling), O.M. Nambiar (Athletics), Om Prakash Bharadwaj (Boxing)**. First female recipient: **Hansa Sharma (Wrestling) in 2000**.
- **Dronacharya Awardees for 2024:**

**4. Dhyan Chand Award (or Dhyan Chand Award for Lifetime Achievement Award in Sports & Games)** (p.507) — Given to retired sportspersons for their lifetime contribution to sports. Instituted **2002**. First recipients: **Shahuraj Birajdar (Boxing), Ashok Diwan (Hockey), Aparna Ghosh (Basketball)**.

### A.4 LITERARY AWARDS (book p.507)
**1. Jnanpith Award (Highest Literary Award)** — for literary excellence in any of the **22 languages of India listed in the 8th Schedule**. Given by **The Bhartiya Jnanpith**. Prize amount **Rs. 11 lakhs**, a citation and a **bronze replica of Goddess Saraswati**. Instituted **1961**. First recipient (**1965**): **G. Sankara Kurup** for **Odakkuzhal (Malayalam)** = "The Bamboo Flute", published 1950. First female recipient (**1976**): **Ashapoorana Devi** for **Pratham Pratishruti**. **59th Jnanpith awardee (2024): Vinod Kumar Shukla**. **Damodar Mauzo**, "the first Indian writer in English", won the **57th Jnanpith** (⚠ as printed — Mauzo writes in Konkani; the "English" claim looks like a slip; verify).
**2. Sahitya Akademi Award** — for literary excellence in one of the **24 recognised Indian languages including English and Hindi**. Instituted **1954**. First recipient in Hindi category, **1955**: **Makhan Lal Chaturvedi** for **"Him-Tarangini"** (poetry). First recipient in English category, **1960**: **R.K. Narayan** for **"The Guide"**.
**3. Saraswati Samman** — annual award for outstanding literary works in any of India's **22 official languages** (8th Schedule). Instituted **1991**; given by **K.K. Birla Foundation**; first recipient **Harivansh Rai Bachchan (Hindi) — 1991**.
**4. Vyas Samman** — annual award for outstanding literary works in **Hindi**. Instituted **1991**; given by **K.K. Birla Foundation**; first recipient (1991) **Ram Vilas Sharma** for **"Bharat ke Pracheen Bhasha Parivar aur Hindi"**.
**5. Kalidas Samman** — awarded by **Government of Madhya Pradesh**; fields: Classical Music, Classical Dance, Theatre, Fine Arts; instituted **1980**; named after Classical Sanskrit poet **Kalidasa**; first recipients (1980): **Semmangudi Srinivasa Iyer and Mallikarjun Mansur**; first female recipient **M. S. Subbulakshmi — Classical Music (1988)**. Latest awardees (2023-24): **Pandit Uday Bhawalkar** — classical music; **Raghupathi Bhat** — for reviving the **Ganjifa** miniature paintings.

### A.5 FILMS AND ENTERTAINMENT AWARDS (book p.507)
**1. Dadasaheb Phalke Award (Highest Film Honour)** — lifetime contribution to Indian cinema. First recipient **Devika Rani (1969)**. **Dadasaheb Phalke Awardee for 2024 — Mithun Chakraborty**.
**2. National Film Awards — 70th National Film Awards, 2024:** Best Feature Film: **Aattam (The Play)**; Best Hindi Film: **Gulmohar**; Best Animation Film: **Brahmastra: Part 1 – Shiva**; Best Actor: **Rishabh Shetty – Kantara**; Best Actress: **Nithya Menen (Thiruchitrambalam), Mansi Parekh (Kutch Express)**; Best Popular Film Providing Wholesome Entertainment: **Kantara**; Best Director: **Sooraj R. Barjatya – Uunchai**; Best Supporting Actress: **Neena Gupta – Uunchai**; Best Supporting Actor: **Pavan Malhotra – Fouja**.
**3. Filmfare Awards 2024:** Best Film: **12th Fail**; Best Actor: **Ranbir Kapoor – Animal**; Best Actress: **Alia Bhatt – Rocky Aur Rani Ki Prem Kahani**; Best Director: **Vidhu Vinod Chopra – 12th Fail**.

### A.6 GLOBAL AWARDS (book pp.508–509)
**1. Nobel Prize (p.508)**
- Instituted **1901** by the **Nobel Foundation (Sweden/Norway)**. **6 categories:** Peace, Literature, Physics, Chemistry, Medicine, Economics. Given in memory of **Alfred Nobel (discoverer of Dynamite)**. All Nobel prizes awarded in **Stockholm, Sweden** while the **Nobel Peace Prize in Oslo, Norway**.
- First recipient: **Wilhelm Conrad Röntgen (Physics, 1901)** — discovery of X-rays. First female recipient: **Marie Curie (1903)** — only woman to win 2 Nobels: shared 1st Nobel with husband **Pierre Curie & Henri Becquerel**; won 2nd Nobel in **1911** for discovery of **Radium & Polonium**.
- First Indian recipient: **Rabindranath Tagore (Literature) 1913** for **Geetanjali**. Other Indian recipients: **C.V. Raman** – Physics, 1930; **Mother Teresa** – Peace, 1979; **Amartya Sen** – Economic Sciences, 1998; **Venkatraman Ramakrishnan** – Chemistry, 2009; **Kailash Satyarthi** – Peace (struggle against child labour), 2014; **Abhijit Banerjee** – Economic Sciences (work in poverty alleviation), 2019.
- Boxes: **Abel Prize is considered as Nobel Prize of Maths.** **Nobel Prize for Economics was established in 1968 by Sweden's Central Bank; originally known as Sveriges Riksbank Prize in Economic Sciences.** Quiz box: "Can you recall what is Raman effect?" (no answer printed here; cf. Days file: 28 Feb 1928 Raman effect).
**2. Ramon Magsaysay Award** — Named after **Ramon Magsaysay, former President of Philippines** (known for integrity and public service). Instituted **1957** by the **Rockefeller Brothers Fund (Philippines)**. First recipient **Wu San Lien** (Public Service, 1958) (⚠ as printed). First Indian recipient **Acharya Vinoba Bhave, 1958**. Other Indian recipients: **Kiran Bedi** – Government Service, 1994; **Aruna Roy** – Community Leadership, 2000; **Bezwada Wilson** – Emergent Leadership, 2016; **Ravish Kumar** – Ethical Journalism, 2019 (photo caption "Darr ka Mahaul hai."); **Dr. Ravi Kannan** – Public service in medical field, 2023.
**3. Booker Prize** — Instituted **1969 (UK)**; best original novel written in English and published in the UK or Ireland. First recipient: **P.H. Newby – Something to Answer For, 1969**. First Indian recipient: **Arundhati Roy – The God of Small Things, 1997**. Other Indian recipients: **Kiran Desai** – The Inheritance of Loss, 2006; **Aravind Adiga** – The White Tiger, 2008; **Geetanjali Shree** – Tomb of Sand (International Booker), 2022. Note: **International Booker Prize is awarded for a book translated into English and shared between the author and the translator.** (⚠ "First Indian recipient Arundhati Roy" — Salman Rushdie (Midnight's Children, 1981) was Indian-born; as printed.)
**4. Pulitzer Prize** — Instituted **1917** by the will of **Joseph Pulitzer (USA)**; awarded by **Columbia University, New York**; categories: Journalism, Literature, Drama, Music, etc. First recipient: **Herbert Bayard Swope (Reporting, 1917)**. First Indian recipient: **Gobind Behari Lal – Journalism (Science Reporting), 1937**. Other Indian recipients: **Jhumpa Lahiri** – Interpreter of Maladies (Fiction), 2000; **Geeta Anand** – Wall Street Journal investigative reporting, 2003; **Siddhartha Mukherjee** – The Emperor of All Maladies (General Nonfiction), 2011; **Indian Staff of Reuters** – Photography, 2020 (coverage of protests in Hong Kong) (⚠ as printed; Reuters photographers, incl. Indian staff, 2020 was for Hong Kong protests, as far as I know — verify).
**5. Oscar Awards (also The Academy Awards)** (p.509) — Instituted **1929**. First recipient: **Emil Jannings (Best Actor) & Janet Gaynor (Best Actress)**. First Indian recipient: **Bhanu Athaiya (Best Costume Design, Gandhi 1983)**. **Satyajit Ray** — first Indian to receive an Honorary Oscar (**1992**). **A. R. Rahman** — first Indian to win two Oscars in the same year (**2009**). **The Elephant Whisperers (2023)** — India's first win in the Documentary Short Film category.
- **Oscar Awards 2025 Winners:** Best Picture **Anora**; Best Director **Sean Baker (Anora)**; Best Actor **Adrien Brody (The Brutalist)**; Best Actress **Mikey Madison (Anora)**; Best Supporting Actor **Kieran Culkin (A Real Pain)**; Best Supporting Actress **Zoe Saldana (Emilia Perez)**; Best Animated Feature **Flow**; Best International Feature Film **I'm Still Here**; Best Original Screenplay **Anora**; Best Adapted Screenplay **Conclave**.
- Box: **Emmy awards are given in Television Industry and Grammy Awards are given in Music Industry.** (■■■ end marker.)

### B.2 §39 PADMA AWARDS (book pp.449–450)
- **Republic Day Awards:** Bharat Ratna, Padma Vibhushan and Padma Shree are given for exceptional service towards the advancement of Art, Literature and Science and in recognition of public service of a high (or the highest) order.
- **Param Vir Chakra:** the highest Gallantry Award — extraordinary act of bravery in the field of Naval, Air and Army. **Mahavir Chakra:** second highest Gallantry Award. **Vir Chakra:** third highest Gallantry Award.

**Bharat Ratna (p.449 right column)**
- Highest civilian award — for exceptional service in the advancement of art, literature and science, and recognition of public service of the highest order.
- **Decoration:** in the form of a **peepal leaf**, about **5.8 cm long, 4.7 cm wide, 3.1 mm thick**; toned bronze; obverse — a replica of the **sun, 1.6 cm in diameter**, below which the words "Bharat Ratna" in Hindi; reverse — State emblem and motto in Hindi; the emblem, the sun and the rim are of **platinum**; inscriptions in burnished bronze.
- First three recipients (**1954**): **Dr. S. Radhakrishnan, C. Rajagopalachari and Dr. C.V. Raman**; **Khan Abdul Ghaffar Khan** — first foreigner (**1987**).

**Recipients of Bharat Ratna (Lucent list, pp.449–450)**
| Year | Recipient(s) |
|---|---|
| 1954 | Chakravarti Rajagopalachari, Dr. Sarvapalli Radhakrishnan, Dr. Chandrasekhar Venkat Raman |
| 1955 | Dr. Bhagwan Das, Dr. Mokshagundam Visvesvaraiya, Pt. Jawaharlal Nehru |
| 1957 | Pt. Govind Ballabh Pant |
| 1958 | Dr. Dhondo Keshave Karve |
| 1961 | Dr. Bidhan Chandra (B.C.) Roy, Rajarshi Purushottam Das Tandon |
| 1962 | Dr. Rajendra Prasad |
| 1963 | Dr. Pandurang Vaman (P.V.) Kane, Dr. Zakir Hussain |
| 1966 | Lal Bahadur Shastri (Posthumous) |
| 1972 | Mrs. Indira Gandhi (⚠ Parmar: 1971) |

- **Note (Lucent p.450):** 1. Lal Bahadur Shastri = first person honoured with Bharat Ratna **posthumously**; Indira Gandhi = first **woman** recipient. 2. **S. Radhakrishnan, Dr. Zakir Hussain and A.P.J. Abdul Kalam** got Bharat Ratna **before becoming President** of India.

**Padma Awards (p.450)**
- One of the highest civilian honours; announced annually on the eve of **Republic Day**. Three categories: **Padma Vibhushan** (exceptional and distinguished service), **Padma Bhushan** (distinguished service of higher order), **Padma Shri** (distinguished service).
- Conferred on recommendations of the **Padma Awards Committee**, constituted by the **Prime Minister** every year; nomination process open to the public; **self-nomination** allowed.
- Govt of India instituted two civilian awards — **Bharat Ratna & Padma Vibhushan in 1954**. The latter had three classes: **Pahela Varg, Dusra Varg, Tisra Varg**; renamed Padma Vibhushan, Padma Bhushan and Padma Shri by **Presidential Notification of January 8, 1955**.
- Announced every year on Republic Day except brief interruptions in **1978 and 1979 and 1993 to 1997**.
- All persons without distinction of race, occupation, position or sex are eligible; Government servants (excluding those in PSUs, except doctors and scientists) are **not** eligible.
- Fields (illustrative): **Art** (Music, Painting, Sculpture, Photography, Cinema, Theatre); **Social work**; **Public Affairs** (Law, Public Life, Politics); **Science & Engineering** (Space Engineering, Nuclear Science, IT, R&D); **Trade & Industry** (Banking, Economic Activities, Management, Tourism, Business); **Medicine** (Ayurveda, Homeopathy, Sidha, Allopathy, Naturopathy); **Literature & Education** (Journalism, Teaching, Book composing, Poetry, Promotion of education, literacy, Education Reforms); **Civil Service**; **Sports** (popular Sports, Athletics, Adventure, Mountaineering, Yoga); **Others** (propagation of Indian Culture, protection of Human Rights, Wild Life protection/conservation).
- Awards presented by the **President of India usually in March/April**; awardees receive a **Sanad (certificate) signed by the President and a medallion**; a small replica of the medallion; names published in the **Gazette of India** on the day of presentation.
- Total number of awards in a year (excluding posthumous awards and to NRI/foreigners/OCIs) **not more than 120**. The award does **not** amount to a title and cannot be used as suffix or prefix.
- **Padma Vibhushan:** exceptional and distinguished services in any field. Decoration **circular, diameter 4.4 cm, thickness ≈ 0.6 mm** [sic]; obverse — lotus flower, "Padma" above and "Vibhushan" below (Hindi); reverse — State emblem and motto in Hindi; toned bronze; embossing in **white gold**.
- **Padma Bhushan:** distinguished service of a high order. Same design ("Padma" above, "Bhushan" below); embossing in **standard gold**.
- **Padma Shri:** distinguished service. "Padma" above, "Shri" below; embossing in **stainless steel**.

### B.3 §40 GALLANTRY AWARDS (book p.450 onward)
- **Param Vir Chakra:** highest decoration for valour — most conspicuous bravery or daring/pre-eminent act of valour or self-sacrifice in the presence of the enemy, on land, at sea or in the air. Decoration **bronze, circular**; obverse — **four replicas of "Indra's Vajra"** round the State emblem in centre; reverse — the words "Param Vir Chakra" in **Hindi and English** with **two lotus flowers** in the middle. (continues on p.451)
  (p.451 continues:) The decoration is worn on the left breast with a plain **purple** coloured riband about **3.2 cm** in width.
- **Mahavir Chakra** — **second highest** decoration; for acts of conspicuous gallantry in the presence of the enemy, on land, at sea or in the air. Made of **standard silver**, circular; obverse — **five pointed heraldic star** with domed centre-piece bearing the **gilded State emblem**; reverse — words "Mahavir Chakra" in Hindi and English with **two lotus flowers**. Worn on left breast with a **half-white and half-orange riband**, about 3.2 cm, **orange near the left shoulder**.
- **Vir Chakra** — **third** in the order; for gallantry in the presence of the enemy. **Standard silver**, circular; obverse — five pointed heraldic star which has an **Ashoka Chakra** in the centre; within this chakra a domed centre-piece bearing the gilded State emblem; reverse — "Vir Chakra" in Hindi and English with two lotus flowers. Worn with a **half-blue and half-orange riband**, about 3.2 cm, orange nearer the left shoulder.
- **Ashok Chakra** — the country's **highest peacetime gallantry award**, equivalent to Param Vir Chakra. Made of **gilt gold**, circular; obverse — replica of **Ashok Chakra surrounded by a lotus wreath**, edge pattern of lotus leaves, flowers and buds; reverse — words "Ashok Chakra" in Hindi and English with lotus flowers. Worn on the left breast with a **green silk riband**, 3.2 cm, divided into **two equal segments by an orange vertical line**.
- **Kirti Chakra** — awarded for conspicuous gallantry; **standard silver**, circular; obverse and reverse exactly as Ashok Chakra; **green silk riband** 3.2 cm divided equally into two by orange vertical lines (as printed).
- **Shaurya Chakra** — awarded for an act of gallantry; exactly like Ashok Chakra except made of **bronze**; **green silk riband** 3.2 cm divided into **four equal segments by three orange vertical lines**.
- **Param Vishisht Seva Medal (PVSM), Ati Vishisht Seva Medal (AVSM), Vishisht Seva Medal (VSM):** awarded to personnel of **all three services** for distinguished service of the "**most exceptional**", "**exceptional**" and "**high**" order respectively. **PVSM — gold; AVSM — standard silver; VSM — bronze**; all circular, **3.5 cm** diameter. Each medal has on obverse **five pointed stars** and on reverse the **Lion Capital**. Ribbon golden with **one dark-blue stripe** down the centre for PVSM, **two dark-blue stripes** dividing it into three equal parts for AVSM, **three dark-blue stripes** dividing it into four equal parts for VSM.
(⚠ Cross-book: Parmar A.2 lists only the six gallantry awards' order; Lucent adds decoration/ribbon details.)

### B.6 §43 NOBEL PRIZE (Lucent, book pp.452–455, PDF 465–468)
- Any of the prizes (**five in number until 1969**, when a **sixth** was added) awarded annually from a fund bequeathed by Swedish inventor and industrialist **Alfred Nobel**.
- **First distribution: December 10, 1901**, the fifth anniversary of Nobel's death. **Sveriges Riksbank Prize in Economic Sciences in Memory of Alfred Nobel** — established **1968** by the **Bank of Sweden**, first awarded **1969**; not technically a Nobel Prize but identified with it; winners announced with the Nobel recipients; presented at the Nobel Prize Award Ceremony.
- After Nobel's death, the **Nobel Foundation** was set up to carry out his will and administer funds. Four institutions — **three Swedish and one Norwegian** — award: from Stockholm the **Royal Swedish Academy of Sciences** — physics, chemistry and economics; **Karolinska Institute** — physiology or medicine; **Swedish Academy** — literature; the **Norwegian Nobel Committee based in Oslo** — peace. Nobel Foundation = legal owner and functional administrator of funds; not concerned with deliberations.
- **Selection process:** winners announced in **October and November**; selection begins in early autumn of the preceding year when institutions invite **more than 6,000** individuals to nominate. During September and early October the Nobel Committees submit recommendations; final decision by **November 15**; prizes distributed every year on **10th December** (death anniversary of Alfred Nobel). Prizes may be given only to **individuals, except the Peace Prize**, which may also go to an institution.
(Nobel recipient tables continue on pp.453–455 — see B.7 below.)

### B.7 §43 NOBEL PRIZE — key facts, statistics, records (Lucent book pp.453–454, PDF 466–467)

**Posthumous nomination (p.453 top)**
- An individual may **not be nominated posthumously**, but a winner who **dies before receiving the prize may be awarded it posthumously**: **Dag Hammarskjöld (Peace, 1961)**, **Erik Axel Karlfeldt (Literature, 1931)**, **Ralph M. Steinman (Physiology or Medicine, 2011)**.

**Some Important Facts**
- **27 November 1895**: Alfred Nobel signed his last will and testament → largest share of fortune to a series of prizes in **Physics, Chemistry, Physiology or Medicine, Literature and Peace** = the Nobel Prizes.
- **1968**: **Sveriges Riksbank (Sweden's central bank)** established the **Sveriges Riksbank Prize in Economic Sciences in Memory of Alfred Nobel**.
- Between **1901 and 2024**, the Nobel Prizes and the Riksbank Prize in Economic Sciences were awarded **627 times to 1012 people and organisations**.

| Nobel Prize | No. of Prizes | No. of Laureates | Awarded to one Laureate | Shared by two | Shared by three |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Physics | 118 | 227 | 47 | 33 | 38 |
| Chemistry | 116 | 197 | 63 | 25 | 28 |
| Medicine | 115 | 229 | 40 | 36 | 39 |
| Literature | 117 | 121 | 113 | 4 | 0 |
| Peace | 105 | 111 + 31 | 71 | 31 | 3 |
| Economic Sciences | 56 | 96 | 26 | 20 | 10 |
| **Total** | **627** | **1012** | **360** | **149** | **118** |

(Arithmetic check done: rows and column totals add up. Peace "111 + 31" = 111 individuals + 31 organisations is my reading; the book just prints "111 + 31" ⚠. 227+197+229+121+142+96 = 1012 only if Peace counts as 142.)

- Nobel Foundation statutes (quoted in book): "A prize amount may be equally divided between two works, each of which is considered to merit a prize. If a work that is being rewarded has been produced by two or three persons, the prize shall be awarded to them jointly. **In no case may a prize amount be divided between more than three persons.**"

**Years without Nobel Prizes**
- Since 1901 there are years with no award: total **49 times**, most during **World War I (1914–1918)** and **II (1939–1945)**.
- Statute: if none of the works is found important enough, prize money is **reserved until the following year**; if still not awarded, the amount is added to the **Foundation's restricted funds**.

| Prize | Years without a prize |
|---|---|
| Physics | 1916, 1931, 1934, 1940, 1941, 1942 |
| Chemistry | 1916, 1917, 1919, 1924, 1933, 1940, 1941, 1942 |
| Medicine | 1915, 1916, 1917, 1918, 1921, 1925, 1940, 1941, 1942 |
| Literature | 1914, 1918, 1935, 1940, 1941, 1942, 1943 |
| Peace | 1914, 1915, 1916, 1918, 1923, 1924, 1928, 1932, 1939, 1940, 1941, 1942, 1943, 1948, 1955, 1956, 1966, 1967, 1972 |
| Economic Sciences | (none listed — heading only; prize started 1969) |

(Count check: 6+8+9+7+19 = 49 ✓.)

- Between 1901 and 2024 the Nobel Prize and Prize in Economic Sciences have been awarded **66 times to women** (⚠ as printed; I cannot confirm this number — verify at nobelprize.org).
- **June** is the month in which most Nobel Laureates celebrate a birthday.
