(⚠ Cross-check within the page: Hopfield's Physics 2024 age is printed 91 and he is listed 3rd, but Hurwicz/Manabe are 90 — consistent. Not verified against outside sources.)

**Declined / forced to decline / arrested**
- **Jean-Paul Sartre**: 1964 Nobel Prize in Literature — **declined** because he had consistently declined all official honours.
- **Le Duc Tho**: **1973 Nobel Peace Prize jointly with US Secretary of State Henry Kissinger** (for negotiating the **Vietnam peace accord**). Le Duc Tho said he was not in a position to accept the Prize, citing the situation in Vietnam.
- **Four** Nobel Laureates were **forced by authorities to decline**: **Adolf Hitler forbade three German laureates — Richard Kuhn, Adolf Butenandt, Gerhard Domagk** — from accepting; all could later receive the **Diploma and Medal, but not the prize amount**. (The fourth = Pasternak below.)
- **Boris Pasternak** — **1958 Literature**; initially accepted but was later **coerced by the authorities of the Soviet Union** to decline.
- **Three** Nobel Laureates were **under arrest at the time of the award, all Peace laureates**: German pacifist/journalist **Carl von Ossietzky**; Burmese politician **Aung San Suu Kyi**; Chinese human-rights activist **Liu Xiaobo**.
- **ICRC** (International Committee of the Red Cross) honoured with a Peace Prize **three times**; its founder **Henry Dunant** got the **first Nobel Peace Prize in 1901**.
- **Linus Pauling** = the only person awarded **two unshared Nobel Prizes** — **1954 Chemistry** and **1962 Peace**.

**Posthumous Nobel Prizes**
- **From 1974** the Statutes stipulate a Prize cannot be awarded posthumously, **unless death has occurred after the announcement** of the Prize. Before 1974, only twice posthumously: **Dag Hammarskjöld (Peace 1961)** and **Erik Axel Karlfeldt (Literature 1931)**.
- **2011 Medicine**: after the announcement it was discovered that **Ralph Steinman had died three days earlier**; Nobel Foundation Board concluded he **remains a Laureate** because the **Nobel Assembly at Karolinska Institutet** had announced without knowing of his death.

**Double Nobel Laureates**

| Name | Country | Subject | Year | Contribution |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Marie Sklodowska Curie (Madam Curie) (printed "Curiem") | Poland | Physics | 1903 | Discovery of the phenomenon of radioactivity |
| | | Chemistry | 1911 | Discovery of the radioactive elements polonium and radium |
| Linus Pauling | USA | Chemistry | 1954 | Research into the nature of chemical bonding |
| | | Peace | 1962 | Militant pacifism during the Cold War |
| John Bardeen | USA | Physics | 1956 | With William Shockley and Walter Brattain for invention of the transistor |
| | | Physics | (1972 — year not printed) | With Leon N Cooper and John Robert Schrieffer for the fundamental theory of conventional superconductivity, the **BCS theory**. **Only person to be awarded the Physics Nobel twice** |
| Frederick Sanger | Britain | Chemistry | 1958 | His work on structure of proteins, especially insulin. **Only person awarded the Chemistry Nobel twice and second person after John Bardeen to get two Nobels in the same subject** |
| | | Chemistry | 1980 | Walter Gilbert and Sanger shared half of the chemistry prize "for their contributions concerning the determination of base sequences in nucleic acids" |
| K. Barry Sharpless | USA | Chemistry | 2021 | His work on chirally catalysed oxidation reaction (⚠ book's wording; the 2021 Chemistry prize went to List & MacMillan — verify; Sharpless's 2001 prize was for chirally catalysed oxidation reactions) |
| | | Chemistry | 2022 | His work for the development of click chemistry and bioorthogonal chemistry |

(⚠ In the Bardeen row the second year is blank in the printed table; I note that the BCS-theory prize was 1972 from general knowledge — not printed. Sharpless: **book prints 2021 for the first prize; I believe it was 2001 — likely a printing slip; verify.** Similarly the Curie row printed "Curiem".)

- **Note (printed):** **Mahatma Gandhi** was **nominated for the Nobel Peace Prize 5 times (1937, 1938, 1939, 1947, 1948)** but could not get it even once.

**Family Nobel Laureates**
- The **Curies** = a very successful 'Nobel Prize family'; Marie Curie herself was awarded two.

| Married couples | Father & Son | Mother & Daughter | Father & Daughter | Brothers |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Marie Curie & Pierre Curie | William Bragg & Lawrence Bragg | Marie Curie & Irène Joliot-Curie | Pierre Curie & Irène Joliot-Curie | Jan Tinbergen & Nikolaas Tinbergen |
| Irène Joliot-Curie & Frédéric Joliot | Niels Bohr & Aage N. Bohr | | | |
| Gerty Cori & Carl Cori | Hans von Euler-Chelpin & Ulf von Euler | | | |
| May-Britt Moser & Edvard I. Moser | Arthur Kornberg & Roger D. Kornberg | | | |
| Alva Myrdal & Gunnar Myrdal | Manne Siegbahn & Kai M. Siegbahn | | | |
| Abhijit Banerjee & Esthur (sic; Esther) Duflo | J. J. Thomson & George Paget Thomson | | | |
| | Sune Bergstrom & Svante Paabo (⚠ printed as Father & Son; Pääbo is Bergström's son) | | | |

**The Nobel Prize Insignias (p.454, continues on next page)**
- At the Award Ceremonies on **10 December**, laureates receive **three things**: a **Nobel Diploma**, a **Nobel Medal**, and a **document confirming the Nobel Prize amount**.
- Each Diploma = unique work of art by foremost **Swedish and Norwegian** artists and calligraphers. Medals are handmade, in **18 carat recycled gold**.
- Medals in Physics, Chemistry, Physiology or Medicine and Literature are **identical on the face:** (continues on p.455).

**(Nobel Insignias, continued from p.454 — book p.455, PDF 468)**
- Medal face shows the **image of Alfred Nobel and the years of his birth and death (1833–1896)**. His portrait also appears on the **Peace Prize Medal** and the **Medal for Economic Sciences**, with a slightly different design. The **image on the reverse varies** by the institution awarding the prize.

**The Nobel Prize Amount**
- Alfred Nobel left most of his estate, **more than SEK 31 million** (today approx. **SEK 1,702 million**) to be converted into a fund invested in "safe securities"; income "distributed annually in the form of prizes to those who during the preceding year have conferred the greatest benefit to mankind."
- Nobel Prize amount (**as on 12 June 2025**) = **SEK 11 million per full Nobel Prize** (⚠ recent figure as printed; verify).
- Why "Nobel **Laureates**"? The word refers to the **laurel wreath**; in Greek mythology **Apollo** is shown wearing a laurel wreath; wreath = circular crown of branches/leaves of the **bay laurel (Latin: Laurus nobilis)**; in Ancient Greece laurel wreaths were awarded to victors in athletic competitions and poetic meets.

**The Nobel Prize Awarding Institutions**

| Prize awarder | For |
|---|---|
| The Royal Swedish Academy of Sciences | Physics and Chemistry, Economic Sciences |
| The Nobel Assembly at Karolinska Institutet | Physiology or Medicine |
| The Swedish Academy | Literature |
| The Norwegian Nobel Committee | Peace |

**Nobel Laureates of India / Indian Origin (as printed)**
- **Rabindra Nath Tagore** — 1910 British poet **W.B. Yeats** translated Tagore's **'Geetanjali'** into English (and wrote its preface); Tagore awarded the Nobel Prize (Literature) in **1913**.
- **C.V. Raman** — Physics **1930**, discovery of **'Raman effect'**.
- **Hargobind Khorana** — Medicine **1968**, interpretation of the genetic code and its function in protein synthesis.
- **Mother Teresa** — Peace **1979**, humanitarian work with the poor.
- **S. Chandrasekhar** — Physics **1983**, theoretical studies of the physical processes of importance to the structure and evolution of stars; prize **divided equally between S. Chandrasekhar and William Alfred Fowler (USA)**.
- **Amartya Sen** — **'Sveriges Riksbank Prize 1998'** in Economic Sciences in memory of Alfred Nobel, "his contribution to welfare economics".
- **V.S. Naipaul** — Sir Vidiadhar Suraj Prasad Naipaul, Literature **2001**, "for having united perceptive narrative and incorruptible scrutiny in works that compel us to see the presence of suppressed histories".
- **Venkatraman Ramakrishnan** — Chemistry **2009**, jointly with **Thomas A. Steitz (USA)** and **Ada E. Yonath (Israel)**, "studies of the structure and function of the ribosome".
- **Kailash Satyarthi** — Peace **2014**, jointly with **Malala Yousafzai**, "their struggle against the suppression of children and young people and for the right of all children to education".
- **Abhijit Vinayak Banerjee** — Mumbai-born economist, Economic Sciences **2019**, shared with his economist wife **Esther Duflo** and US-based economist **Michael Kremer**, for their experimental approach to alleviating global poverty.
- (Book's list on this page does not include Har Gobind Khorana's/others' nationality notes, nor **Ronald Ross 1902, Rabindranath..** etc.; nothing else printed. ⚠ Printed list = 10 names: Tagore, Raman, Khorana, Mother Teresa, Chandrasekhar, Sen, Naipaul, Ramakrishnan, Satyarthi, Banerjee. Dalai Lama/Mother Teresa etc. not further annotated.)

### B.8 §44 OSCAR / ACADEMY AWARD (Motion-Picture Award) (Lucent pp.455–456, PDF 468–469)
- **Academy Award of Merit, by name Oscar** — presented annually by the **Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences**, located in **Beverly Hills, California, USA**, to recognise achievement in the film industry.
- First presented **1929**; winners get a **gold-plated statuette** called **Oscar**; at present chosen from **24 categories**.
- Academy also presents scientific & technical awards, special achievement awards, honorary awards, the **Jean Hersholt Humanitarian Award**, the **Irving G. Thalberg Memorial Award (excellence in producing)**, and the **Gordon E. Sawyer Award (technological contributions)** — not necessarily annual.
- Only **members of the Academy** may nominate and vote for candidates.
- Grand ceremony held each year at **Kodak Theatre, Hollywood (Los Angeles, USA)** in **February** (⚠ as printed; venue since renamed Dolby Theatre — book prints Kodak; verify current).
- **History:** Academy founded **1927**; **May 1928** approved committee's suggestion to present Academy Awards of Merit in **12 categories**; first awards covered films released **1 Aug 1927 – 31 Jul 1928**; presented **16 May 1929**, ceremony at the **Hollywood Roosevelt Hotel**; by the **second ceremony, 3 April 1930** (films of second half 1928 and 1929) categories reduced to **seven**.
- **Statuette:** design credited to **MGM art director Cedric Gibbons** — **a knight standing on a reel of film holding a sword**; sculptor **George Stanley** created the original statuette. Cast in **bronze with 24-karat gold plating** for many years; **plaster** during WWII (metal shortage); now **gold-plated britannium**. Design unchanged except **pedestal base height increased in 1945**. Statuette **13.5 inches (34.3 cm)** tall, **8.5 pounds (3.8 kg)**.

**Oscar facts**
- **George Bernard Shaw** — first person to get both **Nobel (Literature, 1925)** and **Oscar (Best Screenplay, 1938)**.
- **Bob Dylan** — second person to get both: **Oscar (Best Original Song, 'Things Have Changed', 2000)**; **Nobel (Literature, 2016)** "for having created new poetic expressions within the great American song tradition".
- **Mother India (Mehboob Khan)** — first Indian film nominated in **Best Foreign Language Film** category, **1957**; lost to **'Nights of Cabiria' by a single vote**.
- **Bhanu Athaiya** — first Indian to get Oscar for **Costume Designing**, Richard Attenborough's **'Gandhi'** (1982).
- **Satyajit Ray** — first Indian to get **Honorary Academy Award (Oscar Lifetime Achievement)**, **1992**.
- **Gulzar** — first Indian lyricist to receive an Academy Award, **'Jai Ho'** (2009).
- **A.R. Rahman** — Oscar for **Best Original Score** and jointly with Gulzar **Best Original Song** — **'Slumdog Millionaire'** (dir. **Danny Boyle**), 2009.
- **Resul Pookutty** — Academy Award 2009, **Best Sound Mixing**, 'Slumdog Millionaire'.
- **'Naatu Naatu'** (Telugu, **RRR**, dir. **S.S. Rajamouli**) — first Indian film song to win an Oscar; **M.M. Keeravani**, **Best Original Song at the 95th Academy Awards** (2023), beating Lady Gaga and Rihanna; lyrics **Chandrabose**; sung by **Rahul Sipligunj and Kaala Bhairava**; choreographed by **Prem Rakshit**; hook step on **N.T. Rama Rao Jr. and Ram Charan Teja**; earlier in 2023 it won the **Golden Globe** in the same category.
- **'The Elephant Whisperers'** — documentary directed by **Kartiki Gonsalves**, produced by **Guneet Monga**; Oscar **2023** (two abandoned elephants and their keepers).

List of all Oscar Awards won by India (as printed): Bhanu Athaiya (1982); Satyajit Ray (1992); Resul Pookutty (2009); Gulzar (2009); A.R. Rahman (2009); Kartiki Gonsalves and Guneet Monga, The Elephant Whisperers (2023); M.M. Keeravani for 'Naatu Naatu' song–RRR (2023).
(⚠ Book's list omits Chandrabose (lyricist co-credit is normally on the Oscar for the song) — printed as is. Cross-check: Parmar Awards Part A Oscar box.)

**Indian Films Nominated in Oscar:** Mother India (1957); Salaam Bombay (1988); Lagaan (2001).

### B.9 §45 GRAMMY AWARD (American Music Award) (Lucent p.456, PDF 469)
- Presented annually in the USA by **National Academy of Recording Arts & Sciences (NARAS; the Recording Academy)** or the **Latin Academy of Recording Arts & Sciences (LARAS; Latin Recording Academy)**.
- **Pt. Ravi Shankar** got a Grammy first in **1967** for **"West Meets East"** in **Best Chamber Music Performance**.
- Winners from **more than 25 fields**, **more than 75 awards**; honourees get a **golden statuette of a gramophone**.
- First presented by NARAS in **Los Angeles, 1959**, **28 prizes**; winners included **Ella Fitzgerald, Frank Sinatra, Kingston Trio**.
- **Rock** first recognised as a genre at the **1980** ceremony; **rap** at **1989**; **best music video** first given **1982**.
- **2011** NARAS restructured categories, reduced from **109 to 78**; gender-based categories and solo/group distinctions eliminated; Hawaiian, Native American and zydeco folded into "regional roots music".

**Pt. Ravi Shankar (Grammy)**
- **First Indian to win Grammy Awards**; **nominated 10 times, won 4 Grammys in 1967, 1972, 2001, 2012** ⚠ (see table: table says 2013 for the 4th = Lifetime Achievement; text says 2012 — internal mismatch as printed).
- Indian with max Grammys: **4 Grammy + 1 Lifetime Achievement Award (2013) = total 5**.

| Year | Annual Grammy | Category | For the album |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1967 | 10th | Best Chamber Music Performance | West Meets East |
| 1972 | 15th | Album of the Year | The Concert for Bangladesh |
| 2001 | 44th | Best World Music Album | Full Circle–Carnegie Hall 2000 |
| 2013 | 56th | Lifetime Achievement Award-2013 | — |

(⚠ Text says wins in "1967, 1972, 2001 and 2012"; table row 4 = "2013 / 56th / Lifetime Achievement" — the book's own text says "4 Grammy +1 Lifetime Achievement in 2013 = 5", so the table lists only 3 Grammys + the Lifetime award; the 2012 (55th) win is not in the table. Book inconsistency; verify.)

**(Grammy — continued at top of book p.457, PDF 470)**
- **Shankar Mahadevan's** music band **'Shakti'** won the **'Best Global Music Album Award'** at the **Grammys 2024** for the album **"This Moment"** (⚠ recent item as printed).
- Indian-American vocalist **Chandrika Tandon** won the Grammy for album **'Triveni'** in **Best New Age, Ambient or Chant Album** category on **2 February 2025**, Los Angeles (⚠ recent item; verify).

### B.10 §46 BOOKER PRIZE (British Literary Award) (Lucent p.457, PDF 470)
- Alternative titles: **Booker McConnell Prize, Booker Prize for Fiction, Man Booker Prize**.
- Prestigious **British** award given annually to a **full-length novel in English**. **Booker McConnell**, a multinational company, established it in **1968** as a counterpart to the **Prix Goncourt (France)**. Initially only English-language writers from **UK, Republic of Ireland and Commonwealth** were eligible; in **2013** it was announced it would be **open to English-language writers worldwide from 2014**.
- Controversies: in **1984 Salman Rushdie** (winner **1981**, *Midnight's Children*) called the judges "**Killjoyces**" and "**Anti-Prousts**" after the chairman said he had not read James Joyce and Marcel Proust did not want to award such writers.
- Rushdie won the **Booker of Bookers [1993]** (25th anniversary) and the **Best of the Booker [2008]** (40th anniversary).
- Administered by the **Book Trust until 2002**, then **Man Group PLC** (investment management firm).
- Well-known recipients: V.S. Naipaul, Nadine Gordimer, Ruth Prawer Jhabvala, Iris Murdoch, J.M. Coetzee, A.S. Byatt, Kingsley Amis, Penelope Lively, Ben Okri, Michael Ondaatje, Ian McEwan, Peter Carey, Kiran Desai, Hilary Mantel.
- **Booker Russian Novel Prize** set up **1992** (contemporary Russian authors); disassociated from the other Bookers **1999**.
- **Biennial Man Booker International Prize** established **2005** as lifetime achievement award; **from 2016 annual**, for a novel or short-story collection **in English translation**.
- **Man Asian Prize** established **2007**; Man Group announced in **2012** it was withdrawing sponsorship.
- **Shehan Karunatilaka** — **2022 Booker Prize** for **"The Seven Moons of Maali Almeida"**.
- **1969 and 1970**: prize to a novel published in the year previous; **1971**: novel published that same year (Jan–Nov). Rule change precluded eligibility of 1970 novels → one-off **Lost Man Booker Prize (devised 2010)**; winner by public vote: **Troubles by J.G. Farrell**.
- **Geetanjali Shree** = **first Indian writer to win the International Booker Prize for an Indian-language book — *Ret Samadhi* (Tomb of Sand)**.

**Indian Winners of the Booker Prize (as printed)**

| S. | Author | Book | Year |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | V.S. Naipaul | In A Free State | 1971 |

Deepa Bhasthi is the translator (Heart Lamp, Kannada). 2025 = recent item; verify. Cross-check with `books_authors_raw_extraction.md`.)

### B.11 §47 RAMON MAGSAYSAY AWARD (Lucent pp.457–458, PDF 470–471)
- **Asia's premier prize and highest honour**, celebrates greatness of spirit and transformative leadership in Asia; over **300** men, women and organisations in the past five decades ("over three hundred" as printed).
- Trustees of the **Ramon Magsaysay Award Foundation** select annually. Awardees get a **certificate and a medallion** with embossed image of Ramon Magsaysay **facing right in profile**. Presented in **Manila, Philippines on 31 August** = birth anniversary of Philippine President Ramon Magsaysay; **award created 1957**.
- Six categories, **five discontinued effectively in 2009**:
  - Government Service (1958–2008)
  - Public Service (1958–2008)
  - Community Leadership (1958–2008)
  - Journalism, Literature and Creative Communication Arts (1958–2008)
  - Peace and International Understanding (1958–2008)
  - Emergent Leadership (2001– )
  - Uncategorized (2009– )
  (⚠ as printed the list shows 7 lines (5 discontinued + Emergent Leadership + Uncategorized) although the text says "six categories".)
- Note: **Since 2001, Emergent Leadership** is given with the help of the **Ford Foundation**.

### B.12 §48 GANDHI PEACE PRIZE (Lucent p.458, PDF 471)
- Named after Mahatma Gandhi; **awarded annually by Government of India from 1995**.
- Given to **individuals and institutions** for contributions to **social, economic and political transformation through non-violence and other Gandhian methods**.
- Carries **₹1 Crore (10 million)** in cash (convertible in any currency), a **plaque and a citation**; open to all persons regardless of **nationality, race, creed or sex**.
- **Jury:** **Prime Minister of India, Leader of Opposition in Lok Sabha, Chief Justice of India and two other eminent persons**.

### B.13 §49 UNESCO KALINGA PRIZE (Lucent p.459, PDF 472)
- **Kalinga Prize for the Popularization of Science** — awarded by **UNESCO** for exceptional skill in presenting scientific ideas to lay people. Created **1952**, following a **donation from Biju Patnaik**, Founder President of the **Kalinga Foundation Trust** (India).
- Annual award; recipient must have shown, during a brilliant career as writer, editor, lecturer, film producer, radio/TV programme director or presenter, talent in interpreting science and technology for the public; striven to emphasise the international importance of S&T and their contribution to public welfare, enriching cultural heritage and solving problems facing humanity. Many winners scientists; others journalists, educators, writers.
- Laureate selected by the **Director-General of UNESCO** on the recommendation of a **four-member jury** designated by him: **three members** from different countries (equitable geographical distribution) and the **fourth on the recommendation of the Kalinga Foundation Trust**.

- Full Youth Affairs text (NYP-2014/2024, flagship schemes, RGNIYD etc., pp.463–465) is extracted in the Govt Policies file (Round 2). Only the p.463 opening is noted here: 'BHARAT 2025' says India has the largest youth population; youth (15–29) **more than 27.5%** of population (⚠ as printed); **NYP-2014** launched 2014, replacing NYP-2003, defines 'Youth' as **15–29 years**; **NYP-2024** to "synergise the hopes and aspirations of youth"; Department of Youth Affairs consolidated its schemes into **3 flagship schemes**. Sports part of §53 = `sports_raw_extraction.md` Part B.

# PART C — CROSS-BOOK DOUBTS AND VERIFY LIST (Awards)
1. Bharat Ratna count: Parmar "53 recipients (as of 2024)"; Lucent's table has 56 names (see B.2 note). Count basis (posthumous, non-citizen, 2024 batch of five) differs — verify.
2. Indira Gandhi's Bharat Ratna year: Parmar 1971 vs Lucent 1972 (see B.2).
3. Jnanpith cash: Parmar Rs. 11 lakh vs Lucent ₹21 lakh (B.4). Lucent's later figure is probably the current one — verify.
4. Dadasaheb Phalke: Mithun Chakraborty — Parmar "for 2024" vs Lucent "54th, 2022 row" (B.5). Lucent's table 1969–2022 uses a different year labelling from Parmar's announcement year — verify.
5. Booker: Parmar "instituted 1969 (UK)", first winner P.H. Newby 1969; Lucent "established 1968 by Booker McConnell" (first awarded 1969 — consistent if 1968 = founding). "First Indian recipient": Parmar Arundhati Roy 1997; Lucent's Indian list starts with V.S. Naipaul 1971 (British/Trinidad-born) — different definition. Lucent lists Geetanjali Shree (2022) and Banu Mushtaq/Deepa Bhasthi (2025) under "Booker" although these are International Booker (translated) — book's own text says International. The Books file also carries "Flesh (Booker 2025)" and "The Promise (Booker 2024)" (Galgut won 2021 as far as I know) — see the Books file doubts.
6. Oscar: Bhanu Athaiya — Parmar "Gandhi 1983", Lucent "1982" (ceremony year vs film year) — both printed. Lucent's list of Indian Oscars does not name Chandrabose (lyricist of Naatu Naatu); the Academy credits the song to Keeravani and Chandrabose — verify.
7. Grammy: Lucent text says Ravi Shankar won 4 Grammys "1967, 1972, 2001, 2012" but the table shows 1967, 1972, 2001 + Lifetime Achievement 2013 (56th). Internal mismatch.
8. Nobel: Lucent table prints Sharpless's first prize as "2021" (I believe 2001 — verify). Bardeen's second physics year is blank in the printed table. "Sartre etc." fine. "66 times to women" — verify. Nobel amount SEK 11 million (12 June 2025) — recent, verify. Kodak Theatre named as Oscar venue — verify current.
9. Lucent Nobel table: Peace laureates "111 + 31" printed; my reading is 111 persons + 31 organisations.
10. Ramon Magsaysay list: covers to 2023 only (2020–2022 blank as printed); "six categories" text vs seven listed; Chaturvedi/Gupta cell merged.
11. Gandhi Peace Prize: table has large gaps; 2022+ not printed (Gita Press 2021 is the last row).
12. Padma Shri heading in Parmar has no content (A.1).
13. All 2024/2025 awardee tables (Padma 2025, Bharat Ratna 2024, 70th National Film Awards, Filmfare 2024, Oscars 2025, Grammy 2024/25, Magsaysay 2023) are RECENT-EVENT items: verify from primary sources; awards since (2026 Padma, Bharat Ratna, National Film Awards, Nobel 2025, etc.) are not in the books.

# STATIC GK — FAMOUS PEOPLE / PLACES / FIRSTS & SUPERLATIVES — RAW EXTRACTION
(Project: Testbook Notes · SSC CGL Tier-I 2026 · Round 2)

**Sources:** Lucent's General Knowledge (2026) Chapter 11 "MISCELLANY" §1–§13 (book pp.430–434, PDF 443–447), §20–§21 (pp.440–442), §34–§37 (pp.447–449). Parmar SSC Fatman (2nd Ed., 2026) has **no separate Famous People/Places chapter** in its Static GK section (its contents: dances/music/folk, festivals, census, important days, books, sports, international/national organisations, awards) — nothing to add from Parmar; overlaps are in the other files (see pointers).
**Method:** read directly from page images (tesseract OCR only as cross-check). Fact-complete extraction: every table row, name, number, date kept; explanatory prose condensed. Not word-for-word.
**Conventions:** ⚠ = doubt / printing slip / cross-book disagreement / recent-event (2024–2026) item to verify; spellings as printed with [sic] or bracketed corrections.
**No test cross-referencing done** (per instruction).

**Contents**
- Part A — §1–§6 Firsts in India / World (male, female), Superlatives India / World; then §6 cont. p.434, Statue of Unity, §7 Monuments (World), §8 National Emblems, §9 National Animals, §10 Parliaments, §11 Signs/Symbols, §12 Official Books, §13 Newspapers, §14 UN opening points
- Part B — §20 WHS India, §21 Tourist Spots, §34 Places associated with eminent persons, §35 Crematoria, §36 Nicknames, §37 Great Works, pointer for §38–§39

**Cross-references (do not re-extract):** §7 World Monuments also appears in `architecture_raw_extraction.md`; Awards §38–§53 → `awards_raw_extraction.md`; Days/UN weeks → `days_events_raw_extraction.md`; India geography (rivers, dams, peaks, parks) → `claude/geography-raw/*`.

**Statue of Unity (p.434)**
- Statue of 'Iron Man of India' **Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel**, first **Deputy Prime Minister and Home Minister** of independent India.
- Located at **Sadhubet, on Narmada River, in Kevadiya Colony, Narmada District**, facing **Sardar Sarovar Dam (100 km.)**, Gujarat.
- Project first announced **2010**; construction started **October 2013** by **Larsen & Toubro**; total cost **₹2,989 crore (₹29.89 billion; US$437 million)**; designed by sculptor **Padma Bhushan Ram Vanji Sutar**; inaugurated by the PM on **31 October 2018**, the **143rd anniversary of Sardar Patel's birth**. Statue height **182 m**; complete structure **240 m** high. (⚠ Sutar's award is printed as Padma Bhushan; verify.)

### §11 SOME IMPORTANT SIGNS OR SYMBOLS (p.435)
- Pen — Symbol of Culture & Civilization; Lotus — Culture and Civilization; Red Cross — Red Cross Society; **Red Flag** — Revolution; also sign of danger; **Black Flag** — Symbol of protest; **Yellow Flag** — flown on ships/vehicles carrying patients suffering from infectious diseases; **Flag flown upside down** — Symbol of Distress; **Flag at half mast** — Symbol of National mourning; **White Flag** — Symbol of Truce; **Red Tringle (Triangle)** — Sign of Family Planning; **Pegion (Pigeon) or Dove** — Symbol of Peace; **Red Light** — Traffic sign of 'Stop', also 'Danger' or 'Emergency'; **Green Light** — Line clear signal / 'Go'; **A blindfolded woman holding a balanced scale** — Symbol of Justice; **Black strip on fore arm** — Sign of mourning or protest; **One skull on two bones crossing each other diagonally** — Sign of 'Danger'; **Wheel (Chakra)** — Symbol of progress; **Olive Branch** — Symbol of peace; **Tricolour** — National Flag of India; **Union Jack** — National Flag of the U.K.; **Stars and Stripes** — National Flag of the U.S.A.

### §12 SOME OFFICIAL BOOKS (WORLD) (p.435)
- **Green Book** — official reports/publications of **Italy & Iran**; **White Book** — official publications of **Portugal, China and Germany**; **Blue Book** — any official report of the **British government**; **Yellow Book** — report/publication of the **French government**; **Orange Book** — official report of the government of the **Netherlands**; **White Paper** — authoritative recital of facts issued by the government stating its views on a matter; **Grey Book** — report of the government of **Belgium and Japan**; **Joint Paper** — joint report of two or more governments; **Black Book** — book containing a list of secret contacts or of names of people to be punished.

### §14 UNITED NATIONS (p.435, continues on p.436)
- Name 'United Nations' adopted on suggestion of then US President **F.D. Roosevelt**.
- Meeting of prominent countries **21 August – 7 October 1944 at Dumbarton Oaks (printed "Dumbarton Ox") building, Washington** to prepare the UN format.
- **UNO formed 24 October 1945.** Charter signed **26 June 1945** by representatives of **50 nations**; founder members **51** who attended the **San Francisco Conference**; **Poland** signed later → 51st founder member.
- **At present 193 countries are members; South Sudan is the latest (193rd)** member.
- **UN Charter came into force 24 October 1945**, when governments of **China, France, UK, Soviet Union and USA** and a majority of other countries had ratified it.

## PART B — LUCENT'S GENERAL KNOWLEDGE (2026): Chapter 11 Miscellany §14–§16 UNITED NATIONS AND WORLD ORGANISATIONS (book pp.435–438, PDF 448–451)

### B.1 §14 UNITED NATIONS (pp.435–437)
- (Opening points on p.435: name 'United Nations' adopted on suggestion of US President F.D. Roosevelt; Dumbarton Oaks meeting 21 Aug–7 Oct 1944; UNO formed 24 Oct 1945; Charter signed 26 June 1945 by 50 nations (51 founder members; Poland 51st); 193 members, South Sudan latest (193rd); Charter in force 24 Oct 1945 — full text in `famous_people_places_raw_extraction.md` Part A §14 block.)
- The **Preamble** to the Charter was the work of **Field Marshal Smuts**.
- **Head Quarter** of the UN: **New York (USA)**; **John D. Rockefeller** donated **17 acres of land in Manhattan island**, on which a **39-storeyed secretariat building** constructed. Main office built **1952**, where first meeting of the General Assembly was held in **1952** (⚠ as printed).
- **UN Charter = Constitution of the UN**.
- **Flag of the UN:** white UN emblem (**two bent olive branches open at the top, between them world map**) on **light blue** background.
- **Languages:** official — **English, French, Chinese, Russian, Arabic, Spanish**; working languages — **English and French only**.
- Proverb **'Policeman of the world'** used for the **Security Council**. Only **permanent members** have the right to **'veto'**; five permanent members: **USA, Russia, UK, France, China**.
- **Main organs:** General Assembly, Security Council, Economic and Social Council, Trusteeship Council, International Court of Justice, UN Secretariat — all established **1945**.
- **General Assembly:** main deliberative, policymaking and representative organ; all **193** Member States represented (only UN body with universal representation). Annual session each **September** in New York; important questions (peace & security, admission of new members, budgetary) need **two-thirds majority**; others simple majority; elects a **GA President for a one-year term**.
- **Security Council:** primary responsibility for peace and security; **15 members (5 permanent, 10 non-permanent)**; each has one vote; all Member States obliged to comply with decisions; may impose sanctions or authorise use of force; **Presidency rotates every month**.
- **Economic and Social Council (ECOSOC):** principal body for coordination, policy review, dialogue on economic, social, environmental issues; **54 Members**, elected by GA for **overlapping three-year terms**.
- **Trusteeship Council:** established **1945** under **UN Charter Chapter XIII** to supervise **11 Trust Territories** administered by **seven Member States**; by **1994** all attained self-government/independence; **suspended operation on 1 November 1994**; resolution of **25 May 1994** amended rules — meets as occasion requires.
- **Secretariat:** Secretary-General + tens of thousands of staff; **Secretary-General** = chief administrative officer, appointed by GA on recommendation of Security Council for a **five-year, renewable term**.
- **International Court of Justice (ICJ):** principal judicial organ; seat **Peace Palace, The Hague (Netherlands)**; only principal organ not located in New York; sometimes called **World Court**; settles disputes between states and gives advisory opinions; successor of **Permanent Court of International Justice (PCIJ, established by the League of Nations in 1920)**; **15 Judges** elected by GA and Security Council for **nine-year terms**; official working languages **English and French**; jurisdiction: contentious cases (binding) and advisory opinions (non-binding).
- **Indian Jurists in ICJ:**

| Judge | Tenure |
|---|---|
| Benegal Rama Rau | 1952–1953 |
| Nagendra Singh* | 1973–1988 |
| Raghunandan Swarup Pathak | 1989–1991 |
| Dalveer Bhandari | 2012– |
(*Nagendra Singh — Deputy Chairman 1976–1979, Chairman 1985–1988 — as printed; 'Chairman' = President of the Court.)

## PART C — LUCENT MISCELLANY §22–§24 DEFENCE, INTERNAL SECURITY, TRAINING INSTITUTIONS (book pp.442–444, PDF 455–457)

### C.1 §22 DEFENCE OF INDIA (p.442) [sources printed: INDIA 2021 / 2022 / 2024, BHARAT 2025]
- Per **'INDIA 2021'**: India's landmass **32,87,263 sq. km** (printed "million sq. kms" ⚠ slip); strategically located vis-à-vis continental Asia and the **Indian Ocean Region (IOR)**; **land frontier about 15,200 km**; **coastline over 7,516.6 km**; makes India a continental or maritime neighbour of **11 countries in Asia** (as printed).
- **Supreme commander of the Armed Forces = President**; responsibility for national defence rests with the **Union Cabinet**; Defence Minister responsible to Parliament; administrative and operational control by **Ministry of Defence and the three Service Headquarters**.
- Defence Ministry = **4 departments**: (i) Department of Defence (ii) Department of Defence Production (iii) Department of Defence Research and Development (iv) Department of Ex-Serviceman Welfare.
- India has the **4th largest army, 5th largest air force and 7th largest navy** in the contemporary world (as printed; ⚠ ranks vary by source/year).
- Per **INDIA 2022**: post of **Chief of Defence Staff (CDS) created in 2019**, rank of **4-Star General**, salary and perquisites at par with a Service Chief. **Gen. Bipin Rawat** = first CDS (from January 2020), died in an air crash; **Lt Gen Anil Chauhan** = new CDS. (⚠ Recent-events: current CDS/post-holder should be verified — not in book.)
- Per **INDIA 2024**: India among **top 25 defence exporters**; defence exports increased **23-fold from ₹2,059 crore in 2015–16 to ₹15,920 crore in 2022–23** (as printed).
- Per **BHARAT 2025**: surge in women participation; women officers inducted in all branches/streams of IAF; women officers in Indian Navy **more than 750** including medical and dental; Armed Forces opened NDA for women with **19 cadets (including 10 for Indian Army)** every six months; lateral entry of girl cadets in **class XI of Rashtriya Military School from 2024–25**.

**Indian Armed Forces = 3 services**
1. **Army:** Chief = **'Chief of the Army Staff'**; HQ **New Delhi**; **seven commands**:

| Command | HQ |
|---|---|
| Western Command | Chandigarh |
| Northern Command | Udhampur |
| Army Training Command (ARTRAC) | Shimla |
| South Western Command | Jaipur |
| Eastern Command | Kolkata |
| Southern Command | Pune |
| Central Command | Lucknow |
Note: each Command is headed by a **General Officer Commanding-in-Chief of rank Lieutenant General**.

2. **Navy:** Chief = Admiral, **'Chief of the Naval Staff'**; HQ New Delhi; **three Naval Commands**, each under a Flag Officer Commanding-in-Chief of rank **Vice-Admiral**:

| Command | HQ |
|---|---|
| Eastern Command | Visakhapatnam |
| Western Command | Mumbai |
| Southern Command | Kochi |

3. **Air Force:** Chief = **Air Chief Marshal, 'Chief of the Air Staff'**; HQ New Delhi; **seven commands (five Operational, two Functional)**:

| Command | HQ |
|---|---|
| **Operational:** Eastern Air Command | Shillong |
| South-Western Air Command | Gandhinagar |
| Southern Air Command | Tiruvananthpuram |
| Western Air Command | New Delhi |
| Central Air Command | Allahabad |
| **Functional:** Maintenance Command | Nagpur |
| Training Command | Bangaluru |

4. **Commissioned Ranks** [Source BHARAT 2025]

| Army | Air Force | Navy |
|---|---|---|
| General | Air Chief Marshal | Admiral |
| Lieutenant General | Air Marshal | Vice-Admiral |
| Major General | Air Vice-Marshal | Rear Admiral |
| Brigadier | Air Commodore | Commodore |
| Colonel | Group Captain | Captain |
| Lieutenant Colonel | Wing Commander | Commander |
| Major | Squadron Leader | Lieutenant Commander |
| Captain | Flight Lieutenant | Lieutenant |
| Lieutenant | Flying Officer | Sub Lieutenant |

| Raimona | Assam | 2021 | Tiger, Clouded Leopard, Golden Langur, Indian Gaur, Asian Elephant, Spotted Deer, Wild Buffalo, Hornbill etc. |

# PART H — PARMAR SSC STATIC GK (2nd Ed., 2026): INTERNATIONAL ORGANISATIONS (book pp.498–502, PDF 531–535)

(Read from page images. Chapter is not in the calendar list — extracted as an extra. Items marked ⚠ = recent-event data as printed (2023–2025); verify before exam use. Cross-check with Lucent Part B (§14–§16) above.)

## H.1 UNITED NATIONS (UN) (p.498)
- **Established: 24 October 1945**; **HQ: New York, USA**.
- Forerunner: **League of Nations** formed by **Treaty of Versailles, 1919**.
- Formed at the **UN Conference on International Organization, 1945**.
- **Original signatories: 50 countries + 1 (Poland signed later)**.
- **Logo:** Globe, olive branches. **Term coined by Franklin Roosevelt**.
- **Secretary-General: António Guterres (Portugal)** ⚠ (as printed; recent-event item — confirm who holds the post at exam time).
- **Total countries: 193**.
- **Objective:** maintain international peace and security, develop friendly relations among nations, achieve international cooperation.
- **Official languages:** Arabic, Chinese, English, French, Russian, Spanish.

**Principal Organs**

| # | Organ | Points printed |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | UN General Assembly | All UN members; **two-thirds majority** for major decisions, otherwise **simple majority**; decisions **not binding** on members; approves UN budget, appoints the Secretary-General (⚠ as printed; on Security Council recommendation), makes recommendations through resolutions |
| 2 | UN Security Council | **15 members**; **5 permanent (veto): Britain, France, China, USA, Russia**; **10 non-permanent**, elected for **2-year term** (originally **6**); **India has been a member 8 times**; **Japan** elected as non-permanent member the most number of times |
| 3 | UN Economic and Social Council (ECOSOC) | Promotes international economic and social cooperation; **54 members** elected for **3-year terms** |
| 4 | UN International Court of Justice | Judicial organ; located in **The Hague, Netherlands**; **15 judges elected for 9-year terms**; successor of **Permanent Court of International Justice (estd 1920)** under League of Nations |
| 5 | UN Trusteeship Council | **Suspended on 1 November 1994** with independence of **Palau** |
| 6 | UN Secretariat | Headed by the **Secretary-General**, appointed for **5-year term**; implements UN policies, administers peacekeeping operations, prepares reports |

(⚠ The printed ICJ entry shows "Established: 24 October 1945 (estd: 1920) under League of Nations" — the 1920 refers to the predecessor PCIJ; as read from the scan the bullets are split across the column break.)

**UN Funds and Programmes (pp.498–499)**

| Body | Established | HQ | Other points |
|---|---|---|---|
| UNICEF | 11 December 1946 | New York, USA | humanitarian aid to children; nutrition, education, immunization |
| UNEP | 5 June 1972 | Nairobi, Kenya | founded by UN General Assembly (at **Stockholm Conference**); **UNEP + WMO = IPCC in 1988** (Inter-Governmental Panel on Climate Change) |
| UNDP | 1965 | New York | reports: **Human Development Report; Gender Inequality Index; Global Multidimensional Poverty Index**; focus: poverty eradication, inclusive growth, reduce inequalities, SDGs |
| UN-Habitat (Human Settlements Programme) | 1977 | Nairobi, Kenya | — |
| WFP (World Food Programme) | 1961 | Rome, Italy | **Nobel Peace Prize 2020** |
| FAO (Food and Agriculture Organisation) — "Twin Organisations" heading as printed | 1945 | Rome, Italy | — |
| UNFPA (Population Fund) | 1969 | New York | — |
| UNHCR (High Commissioner for Refugees) | 1950 | Geneva, Switzerland | support/protection to refugees; **Nobel Peace Prize 1954 and 1981** |
| ICAO | 1947 (**Chicago Convention**) | Montreal, Canada | — |
| IFAD | 1977 | Rome, Italy | — |

**Other Specialized Agencies of the UN (pp.499–500)**

| Agency | Established | HQ | Other points |
|---|---|---|---|
| ILO | 1919 (through Treaty of Versailles) | Geneva | **Nobel Peace Prize 1969** |
| IMO (International Maritime Organization) | 1948 | London, UK | maritime safety + environmental protection; box: established as **IMCO (Inter-Governmental Maritime Consultative Organization) in 1948**; became UN specialized agency **1959**; renamed **IMO in 1982** |
| IMF | 1944 (Bretton Woods Conference) | Washington D.C., USA | global monetary cooperation, financial stability; uses **SDR (Special Drawing Rights)** as reserve asset/currency; **SDR basket of 5 currencies: US Dollar, Yen, Euro, Pound and Renminbi** (printed "Renmibi"); box: **IMF & World Bank are known as Twin Organisations** |
| World Bank | 1944 (Bretton Woods) | Washington DC, USA | group of **5 institutions: IBRD, IDA, IFC, MIGA, ICSID** (International Centre of Settlement of Investment Disputes as printed) |
| ITU | 1865 | Geneva | regulates international communication and radio frequencies |
| UNESCO | 1945 | Paris, France | education, science, cultural heritage protection |
| UNIDO | 1966 | Vienna, Austria | supports industrial growth in developing nations |
| WHO | **7 April 1948** (celebrated as World Health Day) | Geneva | global health initiatives, disease prevention, medical research |
| UNCTAD | 1964 | Geneva | trade, investment, development issues |
| UNODC | 1997 | Vienna, Austria | against drug trafficking, corruption, international crime |
| WIPO | 1967 | Geneva | releases **Global Innovation Index** along with **INSEAD, Cornell University** |
| IAEA | 1957 | Vienna, Austria | peaceful use of nuclear energy, nuclear safety |
| UNESCAP | 1947 | Bangkok, Thailand | regional development, economic growth, social issues in Asia-Pacific |
| WTO | **1995** (through **Marrakesh Agreement, 1994**) | Geneva | regulates international trade; **replaced GATT (estd 1948)**; **GATT had 23 member countries, WTO has 166 currently** (⚠ figure as printed); **Director-General: Ngozi Okonjo-Iweala (Nigeria)** ⚠ |

## H.2 REGIONAL / OTHER ORGANISATIONS (pp.500–502)

| Organisation | Established | HQ | Members / other points |
|---|---|---|---|
| SAARC | **8 December 1985 (Dhaka, Bangladesh)** | Kathmandu, Nepal | promote economic and regional integration in South Asia; **8 members** (Afghanistan, Bangladesh, Bhutan, India, Nepal, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, Maldives); **Observers: 9 countries** |
| ASEAN | **8 August 1967 (Bangkok, Thailand)** | Jakarta, Indonesia | political, economic, security cooperation in SE Asia; **10 members** (Malaysia, Brunei, Myanmar, Singapore, Thailand, Vietnam, Indonesia, Philippines, Laos, Cambodia) |
| BIMSTEC | **1997 (Bangkok Declaration)** | Dhaka, Bangladesh | **7 members** (Bangladesh, India, Myanmar, Sri Lanka, Thailand, Nepal, Bhutan); **1st BIMSTEC Youth Summit: Gandhinagar, Gujarat, February 2025** ⚠ |
| BRICS | **2009, Russia** (as printed) | Shanghai (as printed) | **11 members** (as of January 2025); acronym B-Brazil, R-Russia, I-India, C-China, S-South Africa (**joined in 2010**); **5 new members added in 2023** — Saudi Arabia, Egypt, UAE, Ethiopia, Iran, **Indonesia (latest)** → "BRICS+" (⚠ as printed the line lists six names after saying "5 New Members"; the text below says **6 countries were invited in 2023, only 5 joined; Argentina declined in December 2023** after change of government); **Fortaleza Agreement (2014, Fortaleza, Brazil)** created **New Development Bank** (NDB, est. **2014**, HQ **Shanghai, China**); **Contingent Reserve Arrangement (CRA)** = **$100 billion** framework est. **2015**; box: **AIIB HQ: Beijing** |
| SCO (Shanghai Cooperation Organization) | **2001 (by China & Russia)** | Beijing, China | **10 members**: China, Russia, India, Pakistan (joined **2017**), Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, Tajikistan, Uzbekistan, Iran, **Belarus (joined 2024)** ⚠ |
| RATS (Regional Anti-Terrorist Structure) | 2002 | Tashkent, Uzbekistan | counter-terrorism body under SCO; **9 member countries** (as part of SCO, as printed) |
| ADB (Asian Development Bank) | 1966 | Manila, Philippines | **69 members** (India is a founding member); **India started availing ADB's assistance in 1986** |
| European Union (EU) | **1 November 1993, Maastricht, Netherlands** | Brussels, Belgium | **27 members (after Brexit 2020)**; **Nobel Peace Prize 2012** (printed "Nobel Prize in 2012"); Eurozone (Euro currency) = **20 countries**; latest member **Croatia — joined 2023** (⚠ as printed); box: **EEC**, precursor of EU, est. **1958 through Treaty of Rome** |
| NATO | **1949 (North Atlantic Treaty)** | Brussels, Belgium | **32 members**; **Military HQ: Mons, Belgium**; latest member **Sweden (March 2024)** ⚠ |
| CSTO (Collective Security Treaty Organization) | after USSR disintegration, **1991** (as printed) | — | **6 members**: Armenia, Belarus, Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, Russia, Tajikistan |
| International Solar Alliance (ISA) | **30 November 2015 (Paris, France)** | **Gurugram, Haryana** | founding members **India & France**; proposed by PM **Narendra Modi in 2015 at Wembley Stadium, UK** — called Sunshine countries **"Suryaputra" (Sons of the Sun)**; launch event: **India-Africa Summit**; opened for signature in **Marrakesh, Morocco**; **120+ member countries**; **Director General: Ashish Khanna** ⚠ |
| G20 | **1999** | no permanent secretariat | **21 members (19 countries + European Union + African Union)**; latest member **African Union, September 2023 (G20 India)**; **Presidency 2024–25: Brazil; next 2025–26: South Africa** ⚠; major event **G20 Leaders' Summit**; G20 accounts for **85% of world GDP** and **more than 2/3rd of world population**; picture lists G20 countries: Argentina, Australia, Brazil, Canada, China, France, Germany, India, Indonesia, Italy, Japan, Mexico, Republic of Korea, Russia, Saudi Arabia, South Africa, Türkiye, United Kingdom, United States of America, European Union (African Union not shown in the flag picture) |
| G7 | **1975** | no permanent secretariat | Canada, France, Germany, Italy, Japan, United Kingdom, United States and the European Union (EU participates but does not hold presidency); **TRICK: "BF JUICE"** (as printed) |
| G8 | **1997 (when Russia joined G7)** | — | G7 + Russia; **suspended 2014** after Russia's annexation of Crimea, reverting to G7 |
| FATF (Financial Action Task Force) | **1989 (initiative of G7)** | Paris, France | combats money laundering, terrorist financing; **blacklist: North Korea, Iran, Myanmar** ⚠ (as printed) |
| INTERPOL | **1923** | Lyon, France | platform for law-enforcement agencies to collaborate, even between countries without diplomatic relations |
| World Economic Forum (WEF) | **1971** | **Cologny, Switzerland** (printed "Cologny") | reports: **Global Risks Report; Global Competitiveness Report; Global Gender Gap Report; Global Travel and Tourism Report; Energy Transition Index** |

**INTERPOL Notices (picture, p.502):** Red (wanted persons), Yellow (missing persons), Blue (additional information), Black (unidentified bodies), Green (warnings and intelligence), Orange (imminent threat), Purple (modus operandi), INTERPOL–UN Security Council Special Notice (groups/individuals subject to UNSC sanctions). (Colour-to-meaning pairs as printed in the picture caption; small print — ⚠ spellings read from the graphic.)

**Bharatpol** (p.502): developed by the **CBI**; portal lets **central and state agencies** connect with INTERPOL for real-time information sharing; **replaces the previous system where only the CBI had direct access to INTERPOL**.

# STATIC GK — GOVERNMENT POLICIES / SCHEMES / INITIATIVES — RAW EXTRACTION
(Project: Testbook Notes · SSC CGL Tier-I 2026 · Round 2)

**Sources:** Lucent's General Knowledge (2026): Ch.11 Miscellany §53 "Youth Affairs, Games and Sports" — Youth Affairs part (book pp.463–465, PDF 476–478); Ch.12 Computer — "Some Important Initiatives of the Government" (book pp.496–497, PDF 509–510). Parmar SSC Fatman (2nd Ed., 2026) has **no Government Policies chapter** in its Static GK section.
**Method:** page images read directly. Fact-complete extraction (all names, numbers, dates, scheme components kept; prose condensed), not word-for-word.
**Conventions:** ⚠ = doubt / printing slip / recent statistic; spellings as printed with [sic]. No test cross-referencing done.

**Contents:** Part A — Youth Affairs (NYP, RYSK and its 8 schemes, NSS, RGNIYD) · Part B — Digital/e-Governance initiatives (e-Pramaan … PRAGATI 2.0, tele-density, internet penetration) · Part C — pointers and doubts.

**Cross-references (do not re-extract):** Sports part of §53 (Khelo India, national sports awards, SAI, LNIPE) → `sports_raw_extraction.md` Part B.1; Committees/Commissions §51–§52 → `awards_raw_extraction.md` B.15–B.16; Government schemes in Economy (poverty, employment, agriculture, banking schemes, PM schemes) → `claude/economy-raw/inflation_unemployment_schemes_raw_extraction.md`, `poverty_bop_trade_raw_extraction.md`, `agriculture_allied_raw_extraction.md`, `banking_monetary_policy_raw_extraction.md`; Polity/Acts → the Polity PDF; the digital-initiative list also sits with `sci_tech_computer_raw_extraction.md` (source chapter).

## PART A — LUCENT: §53 YOUTH AFFAIRS (book pp.463–465, PDF 476–478)

### A.1 Population and policy (p.463)
- According to **'BHARAT 2025'**, India has the **largest youth population in the world**; youth in the age group **15–29 years** comprise **more than 27.5%** of the population (⚠ statistic as printed; printed "more then").
- **National Youth Policy, 2014 (NYP-2014)** — launched in **2014**, **replacing the erstwhile National Youth Policy, 2003**; the Policy **defines 'Youth' as persons in the age group 15–29 years**.
- **NYP-2024** — "set to synergise the hopes and aspirations of youth to enable harmonious growth of society" (as printed; ⚠ recent policy — no further details printed).

### A.2 Schemes for Youth (pp.463–464)
- Department of Youth Affairs undertook a comprehensive exercise to **restructure/consolidate all its schemes into 3 flagship schemes:**
  - **A. Rashtriya Yuva Sashaktikaran Karyakram (RYSK)** — umbrella scheme (8 schemes merged)
  - **B. National Service Scheme (NSS)**
  - **C. Rajiv Gandhi National Institute of Youth Development (RGNIYD)**

**A. RASHTRIYA YUVA SASHAKTIKARAN KARYAKRAM (RYSK)** — "the following **eight** schemes have been merged into a new 'umbrella' scheme":

| # | Scheme | Points printed |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Nehru Yuva Kendra Sangathan (NYKS)** | **Launched 1972**; one of the largest youth organisations in the world; about **8.7 million youth enrolled** through **3.04 lakh youth clubs/mahila mandals**; presence in **623 districts** through Nehru Yuva Kendras (NYKs); objective: develop personality and leadership qualities of youth and engage them in nation-building activities |
| 2 | **National Youth Corps (NYC)** | Launched **2010–11**; implemented through NYKS; objectives: set up a group of disciplined, dedicated youth to engage in nation-building; facilitate inclusive growth (social and economic); act as group modulators and peer-group educators; role models for younger cohort, especially towards enhancement of public ethics, probity and dignity of labour |
| 3 | **National Programme for Youth and Adolescent Development (NPYAD)** | Introduced from **01.04.2008**; financial assistance to Government/non-Government organisations for youth and adolescent activities; **5 major components:** (a) youth leadership and personality development training; (b) promotion of national integration; (c) promotion of adventure — **Tenzing Norgay National Adventure Awards**; (d) development and empowerment of adolescents; (e) technical and resource development |
| 4 | **Youth Hostels** | Built to promote youth travel and let young people experience the rich cultural heritage; **joint venture of Central and State governments** — Centre bears cost of construction, **states provide fully developed land free-of-cost** (with water supply, electricity connection, approach roads); located in areas of historical and cultural value, educational centres, tourist destinations; good accommodation at reasonable rates |
| 5 | **Assistance to Scouting and Guiding Organisations** | Department assists scouting and guiding organisations to promote the scouts and guides movement; **international movement** aimed at building character, confidence, idealism, spirit of patriotism and service among young boys and girls; promotes balanced physical and mental development; financial assistance for training camps, skill development programmes, **jamborees** etc. |
| 6 | **National Young Leaders Programme (NYLP)** | New **central sector scheme launched 2014**; develop leadership qualities among youth; motivate youth to strive for excellence and bring them to forefront of development; components: **Neighbourhood Youth Parliament (NYP); Youth for Development Programme (YFDP); National Young Leaders Awards (NYLA); National Youth Advisory Council (NYAC); National Youth Development Fund (NYDF)**; beneficiaries: youth aged **15–29 years** |
(⚠ The book says "eight schemes" but only six numbered items are printed here (NYKS, NYC, NPYAD, Youth Hostels, Scouting & Guiding assistance, NYLP). The other two are not printed on these pages — not filled from memory.)

**B. NATIONAL SERVICE SCHEME (NSS)** (p.464)
- Introduced in **1969**; primary objective: develop the **personality and character of student youth through voluntary community service**; **"Education through service"** is the purpose.
- Ideological orientation inspired by **Mahatma Gandhi**; **motto of NSS = "not me, but you"**; NSS volunteer places the 'Community' before 'self'.
- Qualities/competencies NSS aims at: understand the community and themselves in relation to it; identify the needs and problems of the community and involve themselves in problem-solving; develop a sense of social and civic responsibility; utilise knowledge in finding practical solutions to individual and community problems; gain skills in mobilising community participation; practise national integration and social harmony.

**C. RAJIV GANDHI NATIONAL INSTITUTE OF YOUTH DEVELOPMENT (RGNIYD)** (pp.464–465)
- Located at **Sriperumbudur, Tamil Nadu**; an **'Institute of National Importance'** under the **Ministry of Youth Affairs and Sports**.
- **Set up in 1993** as a society under the **Societies Registration Act, 1975** [sic; the Act is 1860 — printed "1975" ⚠]; conferred the status of **'Deemed to be University' in 2008** by the **Ministry of Human Resource Development**.
- Functions as a vital resource centre with multi-faceted functions: academic programmes at **post-graduate level** encompassing various dimensions of youth development; **seminal research** in vital areas of youth development; **training/capacity-building programmes** in youth development; extension and outreach initiatives across the country.

(⚠ On the Societies Registration Act year: the book prints 1975; I believe the Act is of 1860 but have not verified it against a primary source — check.)

### A.3 Sports section of §53 → see `sports_raw_extraction.md` (Part B.1: Khelo India, Major Dhyan Chand Khel Ratna, Arjuna, Dhyan Chand Lifetime, Dronacharya, SAI, LNIPE).

## PART B — LUCENT'S GENERAL KNOWLEDGE (2026): CHAPTER 12 "COMPUTER" — "SOME IMPORTANT INITIATIVES OF THE GOVERNMENT" (book pp.496–497, PDF 509–510) [Digital / e-Governance schemes]

(Entries as printed; ⚠ = doubt / recent statistic; figures are as printed and change over time.)

| Initiative | Points printed |
|---|---|
| **e-Pramaan** | Framework for **e-Authentication for public services**; deliver government services to recipients in a secure manner and build citizens' trust in online environment (identity thefts etc.); **MeitY** made it available for public usage with help of **C-DAC, Mumbai** |
| **Online e-Sign (e-Hastakshar)** | Online digital signature service; **formally launched 2015**; integrated with service delivery applications via **API**; instant signing of documents online by citizens in a legally acceptable form |
| **National Knowledge Network (NKN)** | **Established 2010**; interconnect all institutions of higher learning and research with a high-speed data communication network; **only network globally that carries research & evaluation, internet and e-Governance traffic as three independent verticals under one umbrella**; connects all **SDCs (State Data Centre), NDCs (National Data Centre)**, ministries and departments |
| **UMANG** | **Unified Mobile Application for New-Age Governance**; developed and launched **2017**; single mobile platform for major government services; core platform integrated with **Aadhaar, DigiLocker, PayGov, Rapid Assessment System (RAS)**; supports **12 Indian languages** in addition to English; hosted on cloud; **over 2,033 services from 207 central/state departments** onboarded (⚠ as printed) |
| **Digital Locker (DigiLocker)** | Key initiative under **Digital India**; **paperless governance**; platform for issuance and verification of documents/certificates in digital way; Indian residents who sign up get dedicated cloud storage space; **over 29.61 crore users registered, over 675 crore documents accessible** (⚠ as printed) |
| **PRAGATI** | Video conferencing facility **PRAGATI (Pro-Active Governance And Timely Implementation)**, part of Digital India / e-Governance Reforming Government through Technology; **launched 2015**; faster implementation of central-level schemes/projects, state-level projects and resolution of grievances between state and central departments |
| **Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT)** | Switch from electronic transfer to bank accounts of beneficiary to transfer of benefits directly to **Adhaar-seeded bank accounts**; headed by **DBT Mission** (spelling "Adhaar" as printed) |
| **e-Way Bill** | Self-service platform to taxpayers and transporters to generate single e-Way bill for movement of goods from one place to another, as per **GST Rules** |
| **UIDAI (Unique Identification Authority of India)** | **Established 2009**, as an attached office to the **Planning Commission**, now attached office of **Ministry of Electronics and IT**; vision "To empower residents of India with a unique identity and a digital platform to authenticate anytime anywhere"; key objective of Adhaar: identity infrastructure for delivery of social welfare programmes |
| **National Informatics Centre (NIC)** | **Established 1976**; "Prime builder" of e-government/e-governance applications up to grassroot level; through ICT network **NICNET** has institutional linkages with all central ministries/departments, **37 state governments/UTs** and about **650+ district administrations** |
| **Software Technology Parks of India (STPI)** | **Set up 1991**, autonomous society under **MeitY**; promotion of **software exports**; single-window service to software exporters |
| **Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C-DAC)** (printed "Centre for Department of Advance Computing" ⚠ slip) | Premier R&D organisation of **IT&E (Information Technologies and Electronics)**; high-end R&D; strengthen national technological capabilities in selected foundation areas [Source: INDIA 2021] |
| **NICNET** | NIC, MeitY through its **ICT Network – NICNET** provides institutional linkages across all ministries/departments of state governments, UTs and district administrations |
| **API Setu** | **Policy on Open Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) notified 2015**; efficient sharing of data among data owners and inter-/intra-governmental agencies for interoperable systems; **portal has published around 1935 APIs** on **NDH gateway** (⚠ figure as printed) |
| **Open Forge** | Platform promoting collaborative application development through sharing and reuse of e-governance related **source code**; **home to over 2000 projects and over 9500 developers who have pushed software code 3.2 lakh times** (⚠ figures as printed) |
| **eTaal-3.0 (Electronic Transaction Aggregation and Analysis Layer)** | Tracks e-Governance transactions; **by September 2022, 109.69 crore e-Transactions with 17 e-Services** (⚠ as printed) |
| **S3WaaS** | **Secure, Scalable and Sugamya Website as a Service**; website generating and development product hosted on the **National Cloud of NIC**; generates secure websites using **GIGW**-compliant templates |
| **PRAGATI 2.0** | Strengthen central videoconferencing infrastructure; enhance videoconferencing setup at office of **Prime Minister, Cabinet Secretary, Chief Secretaries** and others |

**Tele-density** (p.497): number of telephones per 100 population; **overall tele-density 85.94% in June 2024**; **rural 59.55%**; **urban "decreased to 133.82%"** (⚠ as printed — 133.82% is higher than the overall figure; wording "decreased" looks like a printing slip; verify) [Source: BHARAT 2025].

**Internet Penetration** (p.497): Internet subscribers (broadband + narrowband) rose from **25.15 crore (March 2014) to 95.44 crore (March 2024)**, growth of **279.48%**; **Internet density** rose from **24.09% (March 2015) to 66.19% (March 2024)** [Source: BHARAT 2025] (⚠ statistics — verify from primary source).

GenAI, BharatGPT, blockchain).

## PART A — LUCENT'S GENERAL KNOWLEDGE (2026): CHAPTER 12 "COMPUTER" (book pp.488–498, PDF 501–511; last page 499 = PDF 512)

(Read from page images. Format: as printed; spellings kept with [sic]/[correction]. ⚠ = doubt/printing slip/recent.)

### A.1 INTRODUCTION (p.488)
- Computer is an **electronic machine that helps to process data**; used in administration, medicine, education, sports, defence, shops, home, markets etc.; **IT** now integral to life.
- Computer = **information-processing and information-accessing tool**: accepts data from outside world → processes → new information. "Information processing is the essence of computing."
- **Meaning:** word derived from English **'Compute' = 'to calculate'**.
- Computer = electronic device that processes input according to a given set of instructions, called **program**.
- **Blaise Pascal** developed the **first mechanical calculator in 1642 AD — 'Pascalene'** [Pascaline].
- **Charles Babbage** (British) — first person to conceive an **automatic calculator/computer in 1833**; **'Father of modern computer'**.
- Credit for developing **first computer program → Lady Ada Augusta** [Ada Lovelace], a student of Babbage.
- **Herman Holorith** [Hollerith] prepared an **electronic tabulating machine in 1880**, automatic with help of **Punch Card**.
- **Howard Ekin** [Aiken] developed the **first Mechanical Computer 'Mark-I' in 1937**.
- **J.P. Ekart** [Eckert] and **John Moschley** [Mauchly] invented world's first electronic computer **'ENIAC-I' in 1946**; paved the way for the **first revolution** in calculating machines; **Electronic Valve / Vacuum Tube** used as a switch.
- **John Van Newman** [von Neumann] invented **EDVAC (Electronic Descrete [Discrete] Variable Computer) in 1951**, using **Stored Program**; credit for using **Binary System** in computers also goes to him; his contribution gave direction to the **Computer Revolution (Second Revolution)**.

### A.2 FIVE GENERATIONS OF COMPUTER (p.488)
| Generation | Period* | Main electronic components | Main computers |
|---|---|---|---|
| I | 1940–52 | Electronic Valve / Vacuum Tube | EDSAC, EDVAC, UNIVAC |
| II | 1952–64 | Transistor | IBM-700, IBM-1401, IBM-1620, CDC-1604, CDC-3600, ATLAS, ICL-1901 |
| III | 1964–71 | Integrated Circuit | IBM-360, IBM-370, NCR-395, CDC-1700, ICL-2903 |
| IV | 1971– | Largely Integrated Circuit [Large-Scale IC as printed "Largely"] | APPLE, DCM |
| V | — | Optical Fibre, Artificial Intelligence | — |
(*printed footnote: "may vary according to university/organisation".)

### A.3 TYPES OF COMPUTER (according to size and capacity) (p.488)
1. **Micro Computer** — used by individuals → **PC / Personal Computer**; desktop, laptop, tablets.
2. **Mini Computer** — comparatively larger than micro; **5 to 50 times more powerful** than a micro computer.
3. **Main Frame Computer** — large-sized; by **Time Sharing and Multi Tasking** many people (rather more than **100**) can work at a time on different terminals.
4. **Super Computer** — very powerful, more storage; **most expensive and fastest**, process most complex jobs at very high speed.
5. **Quantum Computer** — development in final stage; "probably more advanced than that of human brain"; **Q-Bit** used instead of binary bits.

### A.4 PROGRAMMING LANGUAGES OF DIFFERENT GENERATIONS (p.488)
| Generation | Languages (as printed) |
|---|---|
| 1st (1940–52) | FORTRAN-i |
| 2nd (1952–64) | FORTRAN-ii, ALGOL-60, COBOL, LISP |
| 3rd (1964–71) | PL/I, ALGOL-W, ALGOL-68, Pascal, SIMULA-67, APL, SNOBOL, 4 BASIC, C |
| 4th (1971–90) | CLUE, ALFARD, UCLID, Reformed Pascal, MODULA, EDA, ORACLE |
| 5th (since 1990) | Artificial Intelligence Languages |
(⚠ Printed generation table for languages; the two lists in this chapter say **FORTRAN is the first programming language** and **PROLOG is the language of the fifth generation** (p.489).)

### A.5 SOME IMPORTANT FACTS RELATED TO COMPUTERS (p.489) — as printed
- **December 2** = **Computer Literacy Day**.
- India announced **New Computer Policy in 1984**.
- First computer **made in India** = **'Siddharth'**, manufactured by **Electronics Corporation of India** (ECIL).
- **First computer in India** installed in the **Main Post Office of Bangalore on 16 August 1986** (⚠ as printed; not verified).
- First **pollution-free computerized petrol pump** of India — **Mumbai**.
- First **Computer University (private sector)** in India — **Rajeev Gandhi Computer University** (as printed).
- **Bangalore (now Bengaluru)** = **Silicon Valley of India**.
- First Indian newspaper available on Internet — **The Hindu**. First Indian magazine on Internet — **India Today**. First Indian political party with website — **Bhartiya Janata Party (BJP)** (as printed).
- Three types of computers — **Digital, Analog and Hybrid**.
- **First Super Computer of the world = CRAY K-1-S** [as printed; "CRAY K1S" in table], developed by **Cray K Company, USA**.
- **'Deep Blue'** = supercomputer that defeated World Chess Champion **Garry Kasparov**; does work equivalent to **32 computers**; can think **20 crore steps of chess in 1 second** (as printed).
- **Seymour Cray** = **Father of Super Computer**.

**Super Computers developed in the World**
| Name | Manufacturer |
|---|---|
| CRAY K1S | CRAY K Research Co., USA |
| Deep Blue | IBM Co., USA |
| Blue Gene | IBM Co., USA |
| COSMOS | Cambridge University, UK |

- First Electronic Digital Computer of the world = **'ENIAC'**.
- Most popular OS: **PC → Windows; smartphones → Android; tablets → iOS**. **Android** is used in the highest number of devices across the world. **Most supercomputers use Linux**.
- **USENET** = link to connect all universities.
- First book on Personal Computer was written by **Ted Nelson**; his book **'Soul of New Machine' won Pulitzer Prize** (⚠ as printed; I believe "The Soul of a New Machine" is by Tracy Kidder — I cannot confirm the attribution; verify).
- First magazine on computer — **'Computer and Automotion'** [Automation]. First home computer — **Comodor VIC/20** [Commodore VIC-20]. First practical digital computer — **UNIVAC**.
- **FORTRAN** is the first programming language. **PROLOG** = language of fifth generation.
- **J.S. Kilbi** [Kilby] developed the **IC chips**. A computer error = **Bug**.
- **C-DAC (Centre for Development and Advanced Computing)** established in **Pune in 1988**.

**Super Computers developed in India**
| Name | Manufacturer |
|---|---|
| FLO SOLVER | NAL, Bangalore |
| PACE | DRDO |
| PARAM-10000 | C-DAC, Pune |
| CHIPP-16 | C-Dot, Bangalore |
| MULTIMICRO | IIS, Bangalore |
| MACH | IIT, Bombay |

- **PARAM-10000** — capacity **1 billion calculations per second**, made by C-DAC Pune scientists on **28 March 1998**; main credit to **Dr. Vijay P. Bhaskar**, Executive Director, C-DAC Pune (⚠ as printed — the name usually cited for the PARAM project is Vijay P. Bhatkar; I cannot confirm; verify).
- **PARAM KAMRUPA** supercomputer facility inaugurated by President **Droupadi Murmu** at **IIT Guwahati on 13 October 2022** (⚠ recent item as printed).
- **National Aeronautics Laboratories, Bangalore** [National Aerospace Laboratories] — first in India to develop a supercomputer, **FLO SOLVER**.
- **Laser printers** = fastest printers. **IBM (International Business Machine)** = American computer company.
- **Computer virus** = man-made digital parasite that corrupts (infects) the file — 'File corrupter'.
- **Modem** = device connecting computers, works on telephone lines.
- **Y-2K** = technical problem associated with the calendar (date, month, year) — **'Millenium [Millennium] Bug'**.
- Development of computer in India started **1955**.
- **Indian Institute of Science, Bangalore** developed **'Simputer'** — small palm-sized touch-screen computer.
- **First computerarium** [planetarium? as printed "computerarium"] in India — **Bengaluru (Karnataka)**.
- **Vellanad** (Thiruvananthapuram district, Kerala) = first fully computerised village of India (as printed).
- PC, Home computer, Electronic notebook = examples of micro computer.
- OS used in micro computers: **CP/M, Mac OS (Apple), DOS, Pro DOS, MS DOS/PC DOS, XENIX, Windows, Linux**.
- **Linus Benedict Torvalds** = creator and coordinator of the Unix-like OS (Linux); born in **Helsinki, Finland on 28 December 1969**.

### A.6 HARDWARE AND SOFTWARE — HARDWARE BASICS (pp.490–491)
- **Linus Torvalds:** on **17 September 1991** completed the first version of Linux; released under **General Public Licence** (open and free to all); one of the pioneers of **free software**.
- A computer has two main parts: **(i) Hardware, (ii) Software**.
- **Hardware:** three main units — **1. Input unit, 2. Processing unit, 3. Output unit** (physical units).
- Computer has its own internal 'language'; made of electronic components capable of two states — **low (0 volt) or high (say 5 volts)**. High = **'1'**, low = **'0'**; the 1s and 0s = **binary digits → 'bits'**; computers work only with bits.
- Combination of **8 bits = 1 byte**; 8 bits can represent **256 different symbols** (alphabets, numbers, special characters like $, @, +, ₹). Information represented in multiples of 8 (2³) bits; **2¹⁰ = 1024** (closest power of two to kilo = 1000).
- **Mega Byte** = 1 million bytes or 1000 KB (conventional sense); **Giga Byte** ≈ 1000 MB or 1 million KB (as printed).
- Earliest machines were **8-bit**; contemporary computers work with **16, 32 and even 64 bits** = **data width of the computer**.

**Units of Data Measurement (table, p.490)**
| Unit | Value |
|---|---|
| 4 Bit | 1 Nibble |
| 2 Nibble (8 Bit) | 1 Byte |
| 1024 Byte | 1 Kilo Byte (KB) |
| 1024 KB | 1 Mega Byte (MB) |
| 1024 MB | 1 Giga Byte (GB) |
| 1024 GB | 1 Tera Byte (TB) |
| 1024 TB | 1 Peta Byte (PB) |

- Basic elements that can signal 1 or 0 = **flip-flops** (simple electrical device); modern computers use a tiny set of flip-flops known as a **register**.
- Circuits designed on a small piece of semiconductor like **silicon**. Silicon obtained from **sand**; a poor conductor, chemically modified → **Integrated Circuits (IC)**.
- **By 1971**, engineers put the switches needed for a complete computer on a single silicon chip = **microprocessor**.
- Computer = **binary machine**; performs mathematical operations using the **binary number system**; converts decimal numbers into binary.
- Internal circuits performing mathematical operations on bits = made of **logic gates** (generate 1 or 0 depending on input); **three basic logic gates: AND, OR, NOT**.
- A computer is organised into three basic units: **1. Central Processing Unit (CPU), 2. Memory Unit (MU), 3. Input/Output Unit**.

### A.7 CENTRAL PROCESSING UNIT (CPU) (pp.490–491)
- CPU performs the main function of information processing = **'brain' of the computer**; makes all calculations and processes data.
- Three components: **(a) ALU, (b) CU, (c) Registers**
  - **ALU (Arithmetic and Logic Unit)** — performs all mathematical and logical operations.
  - **CU (Control Unit)** — directs the working of the CPU; fetches instructions (programs) from memory and controls data flow between ALU and other parts.
  - **Registers** — storage locations holding instructions/data while the CPU uses them; consist of **flip-flops**; **fastest memory elements** in the computer.
- **Main attributes of CPU:**
  - **(a) Data width** — number of bits that can be manipulated at one time; also called **word size**; **8 to 64 bits**; higher data width = more powerful.
  - **(b) Address range** — amount of memory that can be directly read/written by the CPU.
  - **(c) Clock speed** — speed of CPU; internal clock synchronises switches; more ticks per second = faster; measured in **megahertz and gigahertz**; **Hertz = unit of frequency**; **1 MHz = 1 million 'ticks' per second; 1 GHz = 1000 MHz**.

### A.8 MEMORY UNIT (MU) (p.491)
- Memory unit stores all instructions and data for the CPU; **two kinds: (a) Primary, (b) Secondary**.
- **(a) Primary memory** — working/main memory; very high speed; **temporary (volatile)** — data lost when computer is switched off; e.g. **RAM**; directly accessible to CPU; two basic kinds: **RAM (Random Access Memory)** and **ROM (Read Only Memory)**.
  - **RAM** = read/write memory; CPU can change contents anytime; volatile; capacity measured in KB/MB.
  - **ROM** cannot be altered; information stored at time of manufacture (crucial instructions); **non-volatile**.
  - **PROM (Programmable Read Only Memory)** — manufacturer may load a program into it, then it works like ROM.
  - **Cache** = primary memory storing temporary data for quick access.
- **(b) Secondary memory** — long-term storage; slower; **permanent (non-volatile)**; cheaper than primary; not directly accessible to the CPU (moved to primary first). Examples: **floppy diskettes, hard (fixed) discs, magnetic tapes, CDs, DVDs, Memory Cards, USB sticks**.
  - Floppy diskette = plastic disk coated with magnetic material; read/written by disk drives with magnetic 'head'; any information can be directly accessed.
  - **Magnetic tapes** — long plastic tapes coated with magnetic material; store much more than floppy; information **cannot be accessed directly**.
  - **Hard disks** — several discs of a hard material in one drive (text continues p.492).

### A.9 SECONDARY MEMORY (continued), INPUT/OUTPUT (p.492)
- **Compact Disk (CD)** = **'optical' medium** (properties of light used); made of special plastic; **read using a laser beam**. Secondary memory: much slower but non-volatile; for long-term storage.
- **DVD** drive — another optical drive; same size as CD but **higher storage capacity**; stores files (.doc, .docx, .pdf), audio (.mp3 etc.), video (.mp4, .mov, .AVI) (as printed with typos).
- **USB Flash drive** (USB stick, Flash Memory) — physically much smaller than optical drives, high capacity, **removable and rewritable**. **SD and Micro SD cards** — very small, very high capacity.
- **3. Input/Output:**
  - **Input devices** let users supply information (common: **keyboard and mouse**); **output devices** convey processed information (common: **monitor, printer, speakers**).
  - I/O devices are **peripheral devices** (lie on periphery of CPU, not part of it); also called an **interface** (translate information for man and machine).
  - Most popular input device = **keyboard**; **Ctrl, Shift and Alt = Modifier Keys**.
  - **OMR (Optical Mark Reader)** — reads specially prepared forms with black marks made by pen/pencil in specific positions; used by most competitive exams.
  - **MICR (Magnetic Ink Character Reader)** — used by **banks**; special numbers on cheques written with magnetic ink; MICR examines the shape of the magnetic field created by the character.
  - **Bar codes** — several parallel vertical lines of different thickness representing binary digits; bar code reader detects bars using light; can represent price, date of expiry etc.
  - **Mouse** — pointing device; movement measured and transmitted → **cursor** moves; select an option by pressing a button.
  - **Digital camera** — input device with a circuit sensitive to light.
  - Two most common output devices: **Visual Display Unit (VDU)** and **printer**. VDU uses a **cathode ray tube**; characters formed by illuminating a pattern of dots = **pixels (picture-elements)**.
  - Printers — **three popular versions: dot matrix, ink-jet, laser**. **Dot matrix** prints characters as combinations of tiny dots (printing head aligns 'pins'); **ink-jet** sprays jets of ink (smooth characters); **laser** uses a laser beam to 'burn' characters on paper.
  - **Program** = detailed sequence of instructions; may be written directly to **RAM** or stored in secondary memory and transferred to RAM when required; **execution** = data moved around in the CPU per a well-detailed sequence.

### A.10 PROGRAMMING LANGUAGE TYPES; SOFTWARE (p.493)
- Programs written using special languages = **programming languages**; each language has its own 'grammar' = **syntax**.
- **Machine language and assembly language = low-level languages**. **Assembler** = special program converting all instructions into binary format. High-level languages have **compilers or interpreters** (translation programs). Popular high-level: **C, C++, JAVA, Pascal, Fortran**.
- **Software** = set of programs controlling the hardware; divided into **Application programs** and **System programs**. Application programs = tools for specific tasks (e.g. **word processors**, **DBMS – Data Base Management Systems**). **Operating system = the most important system program**; helps users interact with the computer; popular OS: **Unix, MS-DOS, Linux, Windows, Mac OS**.

### A.11 IMPORTANT KEYBOARD SHORTCUTS (COMMANDS) (p.493) — Short-cut → Description

|---|---|

### A.12 GLOSSARY (pp.493–495) — term → meaning (as printed)
| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Active Cell | The cell in MS Excel with dark boundary |
| ALU | Arithmetic Logic Unit; all calculations in computer are done here |
| Application Software | Designed to perform specific applications such as payroll, word processing, graphics |
| Batch Processing | Data are processed in a batch |
| BIOS | Basic Input Output System; program stored in ROM |
